# The Weight of a Penny: the world of the william journal, 1595

*2026-11-15. The canon for every william journal build step.*

This is the world bible for one year in the journal of William Shakespeare, 1595, written as if preparing a drama novel of that year. It is grounded in [the source book](../research/sourcebook/) and invented in its days, with a light touch of modernity in how the year is captured. It folds three independent bibles (GPT-6 Astra, Gemini 4 Carbon and Fable 5.2, raw in `raw/`), written to the brief, into one superset under the canon decisions. Each fold file keeps its editor's notes.

Dates are Julian 1595, held on the same month and day in 2025 (identical calendar: Wed 1 January, Easter 20 April). Historical facts carry tiers T1 (documented) to T5 (invented); invented people are marked `[invented]`.

**Sections:** 1 The novel · 2 The rules of modernity · 3 The cast bible · 4 The places bible · 5 The year's arc · 6 The dated year · 7 Per-source design · 8 Specimens · 9 The canon ledger · 10 Open choices

---

## 1. The novel

**Title: *The Weight of a Penny*.** Working subtitle: *a year in the journal of William Shakespeare, 1595*.

The title names the year's governing object: the penny loaf, whose price holds while its weight shrinks. It carries the money thread and the dearth, and it knows nothing about any year but this one.

### The dramatic question

**Whose are the words?**

In January 1595 William Shakespeare is thirty, and almost nothing he has made is his. The poems carry a lord's name on the dedication page. The plays belong to the company, and printers sell them with his name left off. The Master of the Revels cuts the lines, Will Kemp adds to them, and Richard Burbage speaks them better than they were written. His wife and three children live a hundred miles away in his father's house. His one appearance in the year's records is as the middle name of three on a warrant for £20 (T1).

The year asks whether a man who lives by giving words away can come to own anything: a name, a house, a place among his fellows, his own heart. By December he has an answer, and it is not the one he expected in January.

The question has a practical twin. **Can he turn a precarious public trade into a dependable private life without making everyone he loves into material for that trade?** It also has a price. Becoming the voice of a whole hungry, quarrelling city means giving up some part of the private man, and the year makes him find out which part.

At the start of the year he has a good answer to almost every difficulty: work harder, write faster, be agreeable, wait for the money. The answer serves him well enough to become dangerous. What he wants is plain enough to write on a leaf of his tables: a house in Stratford in his own name; a play nobody can improve by talking over it; to be thought well of by a lord without needing it; to deserve Anne's dry respect; and to keep the company whole.

### Where he stands in January

To the book-buyers of Paul's Churchyard he is the honey-tongued poet of *Venus and Adonis* and *Lucrece*, dedicated to the twenty-one-year-old Earl of Southampton. To the City he is a common player in a company barely seven months old. On the Queen's warrant for the Christmas plays at Greenwich he is named second, after the jig-dancing clown Will Kemp and before Burbage (the warrant T1, the venue T2). The Lord Mayor, Sir John Spencer, lives at Crosby Place, a hundred yards from his bed, and would shut every playhouse in London if he could. His wife Anne and their children live at Henley Street: Susanna, eleven and twelve in May, and the twins Hamnet and Judith, ten at Candlemas. That household is shadowed by his father's old debts, in a town still rebuilding after the great fire of May 1594 (T1).

The year begins with £20 owed to the company, not £20 in William's purse. It ends with more money of his own, three nights before the Queen behind him and a new comedy still open on his desk. Between those two points, bread grows smaller without changing its price.

### The through-lines

**Work.**

- **The company against the City.** Eight shares, a wooden playhouse on a lease with two years left, a ground landlord (Giles Allen) who wants £24 a year against the old £14, and a hostile Lord Mayor a hundred yards from his bed. The June riots, the gallows set up at the Mayor's gate, July's martial law and the Mayor's letter of 13 September asking the Council to suppress the plays "daily shewed at the Theator and Bankside" (T1) all press on the company. The paymaster of the Chamber dies in October. The new mayor on 29 October is the year's exhale.
- **A company is a household with worse hours.** The Lord Chamberlain's Men are neither a band of penniless geniuses nor an efficient business. They are partners, rivals, dependants, creditors and craftsmen, and they know exactly how another man breathes before a hard speech. Burbage wants a king worth becoming. Kemp wants the audience to stay a party to the bargain. Heminges wants the sums to survive the poetry. The boys want to be treated as workers before their masters remember that they are children. William's first instinct is to settle every quarrel by writing a better line. The year teaches him that some quarrels need money, an apology or a different share of power.
- **Four plays, four seasons.** *Love's Labour's Lost* is finished and played in winter: language enjoying itself until it has to answer to another person. *Richard II* is written in spring: language finding out that authority cannot be talked back into existence. It is played from October with its deposition cut for the public stage, and played whole once, at a private supper before Robert Cecil (T3, the story's choice). *Romeo and Juliet* is noted in Lent, drafted from July and first played on 6 December as real dusk falls on the tomb: language running faster than anyone can keep up with. The *Dream* begins on 28 October as a page about bad weather and is still unfinished at the year's end. It is about people trying to arrange desire and finding that arrangement is not understanding, and it ends the year as a peace offering to a clown. No play comes from a one-to-one incident, and nobody "becomes" a character by saying something useful over dinner. The year supplies pressures, rhythms and contradictions, not models.
- **The ticking clock.** Nick Sorrell `[invented]`, fifteen, is the company's leading boy, and his voice is going. William writes Juliet for a voice that may not last until the play is finished.
- **The clown war.** Kemp is the star and the first name on the warrant. William keeps writing plays with less and less room for him, and *Richard II* has no fool at all. Kemp fights for every extra laugh, and he has a practical dignity William underrates: he knows what the yard paid for. The quarrel runs from February to November. It ends on 22 November, when William brings him three pages about a weaver who will wear an ass's head, and Kemp calls him *thou* for the first time.

**Money.**

- **The price stays; the portion shrinks.** The penny loaf is measured each week against the notch Mistress Ansell has cut in her bread-board, and each week it falls a little shorter. The same pattern holds for a player's protection, a patron's favour, an absent husband's promises and the word *soon* in a letter.
- **Money owed, money had, money building.** The £20 warrant comes to 37s 6d a share. There is the oak coffer with two locks, his father's old debts, the paymaster who dies in October, and a Christmas at court that will not be paid for a year. From March his account book keeps what is *expected* and what is *received* in separate columns, because expected money has a way of eating dinner. The coffer goes from £24 10s on New Year's Day to £33 10s at the year's end, with £1 of Richard Quiney's still owed to him. He is saving toward "a house" in January, and toward one particular house by August.

**Family: two households, neither an escape.** London gives him work and anonymity. Stratford gives him belonging, and obligations with names. He makes two journeys home: a ride in Lent, and a four-day walk in high summer after news of the second fire. Anne is sharp, funny and nobody's victim. She runs a household of nine on what the carrier brings, and she refuses to become the keeper of a permanent waiting room. She asks the year's question: is London a trade he is plying, or the place he lives? Their marriage is not mended by one lyrical conversation. It improves through smaller acts: a date kept, a sum delivered, a task finished without asking her to admire the effort. The year turns on 16 August, outside the great house by the Guild Chapel ("That one, then"). The children have projects of their own. Susanna tests his claims. Hamnet is interested in mechanisms, bargains and getting the better of Judith. Judith wants to know whether a rule survives being applied literally. None of them exists to explain a future play.

**Love and friendship.**

- **What cannot be entered in an account (T3, never resolved).** A young lord to whom he owes his name as a poet. He is always "my lord", and his attention is moving elsewhere, toward Mistress Vernon and the court. William's feeling for him mixes ambition, gratitude, attraction to beauty and attraction to consequence. The story never turns that mixture into an affair. Then there is an unnamed dark-eyed married woman of the parish, heard at the virginals through a window before she is ever seen. Neither is named in the journal. The private verse keeps its *you* unassigned, and its meaning may change between pages. Both feelings become sonnets that go into the bottom of the coffer. Nothing happens, and that is the story.
- **The friendships that carry the year.** Burbage, with whom he finishes half-lines. Field, the Stratford tanner's son turned printer, whose Holinshed William borrows in March and never returns. Mistress Ansell, his landlady and the first critic of every play. Heminges and his "Item". And by November, Kemp.

**Faith under weather.** Robert Southwell, a Jesuit and a poet, is hanged at Tyburn the day after the comedy opens (T1). William is not there, and he keeps what he feels for a memo in the dark. He takes Easter communion beside a merchant's tomb, with the Mayor three pews off. His father stays away from church "for debt", and his mother says an old grace under her breath. He buys the dead poet's book on 17 May and reads its rebuke of poets who write of love. In October the Earl of Arundel dies in the Tower. He attends church, and the familiar phrases sometimes comfort him and sometimes sound like surveillance. He can pray without the book handing the reader a secret denominational answer. Nothing is confessed and everything is felt: this is the guarded silence of a Warwickshire son who watches priests and peers die for the old faith.

**The news: bread, riot and sword.** England tightens like a drumhead. Ruined harvests shrink the penny loaf ounce by ounce. In June hungry apprentices seize fish at Billingsgate and butter in Southwark, and on a moonless Sunday night a thousand of them gather on Tower Hill (T1). In July provost marshals ride the City gates under martial law, five riot leaders are hanged on Tower Hill, Spanish galleys burn four Cornish towns, and a second fire burns a row of northern Stratford. Across the river, Henslowe and Alleyn outspend the company with thrones let down from the heavens and two-part *Hercules* spectacles at the Rose. William neither joins the riots nor watches the punishments. He walks wide or stays in, hears enough, and finds that not going does not mean not being implicated. Every piece of news reaches him late and secondhand: from a waterman who is wrong by a third, a tapster, the tiring-house, a stall at Paul's, or a carrier's letter days old.

**The body.** A winter cold, a bad tooth drawn in October, a back strained raising a barn frame in August. Two hundred miles on foot and two hundred on a hired horse. Sleep that follows the light.

### What changes in him, January to December

| | January | December |
|---|---|---|
| What he is | A poet with a patron who also writes plays | A playwright with seven fellows who also writes sonnets nobody sees |
| Whose voice | Marlowe's is still in his ear (*Tamburlaine* plays at the Rose on New Year's Day) | He has written a tragedy that sounds like no one else |
| What his verse does | Rhetorical fireworks: four lords swearing off women, every man forsworn before the ink is dry | A king who weeps like a beggar, and a brawler who dies with a pun in his teeth |
| The lord | Hopes for a summons | Watches him tilt from the crowd, and goes home to work |
| Patronage | Lives under one dazzling nobleman's name | Lives inside the company. Heminges and Bryan carry the court's money, and he does not mind |
| The clown | Writes around Kemp and resents him | Writes *for* Kemp and likes him |
| Home | Guilt, sent by carrier | A plan, spoken aloud to Anne |
| Money | Counts what he is owed | Counts what he is building |
| The record | Thinks a well-kept record will prove he has done enough | Knows a record can preserve a debt while disguising what it is |
| The words | Given away | Some given, some kept, and he knows which |

By December he is better at separating money owed from money imagined, news from rumour, another person's words from his use of them, and a promise from a pleasant intention. He is a little less quick to mistake fluency for generosity. He has moved out from under patronage and into the company. He ends the year still ambitious. He is not cured, and he has not become a model husband or a modern man. It is the story of a working man becoming more exact about what he owes, what he knows, what he can make, and what he should leave in another person's keeping.

### Tone

A working year, not a monument. It is warm, exact, occasionally dirty and often amused. It is brisk, sensory and tactile: carpenter's sweat, wet ink, cold mutton pies, boy players outgrowing their kirtles, and Kemp fighting for an extra laugh. Genius looks like paying attention to everything and going home early to write it down. The book is a life read through its news, its repertory and its print, written as a drama.

It is funny in the tiring-house and at the landlady's table, tense in June and July, and tender in Stratford. The comedy belongs to the business: reckonings and part-rolls; a crown whose lining smells of another man's head; a landlady who lets him the room but will not undertake that the weather stay outside the stair; a boy who refuses to swoon until someone moves the stool; a cat asleep on the book he needs; a rival manager who dates a page "the 31 of aprell". The danger belongs to a city whose appetite outruns its food, and to a trade that gathers crowds just when crowds have become suspect. It stays offstage, where the rules require it.

Everything is in the present tense of a man who does not know he is Shakespeare. No scene knows what 1596 holds. The final feeling is not triumph. It is **earned continuance**.

---


## 2. The rules of modernity

### 2.1 The governing principle

**The world is 1595 in every material, social, religious, medical and linguistic respect. Only the capture is modern.** There are no phones, cameras, screens, watches, microphones or modern idioms in the world. The capture behaves like a patient witness that was simply there, not like an assistant inside the story.

**The capture is modern; causation is not.** The journal can preserve audio, images, work surfaces, movement and body data. It can never give William news sooner, carry his words farther, make a manuscript easier to copy, or let anyone consult something unavailable in 1595. Letters, carriers and waiting still matter, because nothing in the capture shortens them.

**One vocabulary per layer.** Titles, labels and metadata are plain modern English ("Meeting", "Call", "Voice memo", "Photo"). Everything inside the capture (speech, pages, images) is period. "Call" in dialogue means only to summon or to visit.

**Speech.** Natural, interruptible, spoken 1590s English: contractions ('tis, i' faith, you'll), concrete nouns, no mock-Tudor stiffness. "Forsooth" and "prithee" are spice, not seasoning. Period oaths are allowed, since there is no statutory restraint before 1606 (T1). Obscenity is by implication only. *Thou* and *you* follow the cast table in § 3.1.

### 2.2 Dates and time

- **Storage.** Every date is stored as `2025-MM-DD`, on the same month and day as Julian 1595, and shown with the 2025 weekday. The two calendars are identical: Wednesday 1 January, no leap day, Easter Day Sunday 20 April. **Never add ten days.** Sundays matter: no public playing, and church.
- **Clock.** Times are floating local time, with no British Summer Time. If a backend needs an offset, export a fixed zero offset. Times are capture coordinates, rounded to 5 or 15 minutes. Characters never use them: they say "after dinner", "toward four", "before the play" or "at the second bell", never "at fourteen thirty".
- **Light and moons** follow the 1595 calculations in the source book's § 1, not real 2025 skies. The moon is new on 27 June, so the Tower Hill riot (29 June) happens on a dark night, and it is full on 6 December, the night *Romeo* is first played.
- **Legal year.** Headings he writes use "1594" until 24 March and "1595" from Lady Day. Documents keep their own dating ("xvto Marcij 1594", "1594/5").
- **Weeks** run Monday to Sunday, with a partial opening week (Wed 1 – Sun 5 Jan) and a closing week of three days (Mon 29 – Wed 31 Dec).

> *Example: S027, Sat 2025-03-15, is the sharers' meeting about a warrant dated "xvto Marcij 1594". It happens in March 1595, not March 1594 and not March 2025.*

### 2.3 Meeting

**What it is.** Any gathered talk with William present among two or more people that has a purpose or an occasion: sharers' reckonings, rehearsals, readings of a new play, licensing visits to Tilney, suppers, a bookshop or print-shop conversation, the family table, talk at an inn on the road. A brief exchange on a doorstep or at the foot of the stair counts as a meeting, since the person is physically there.

**Shape.**

- A 2–6 minute excerpt (25–60 lines), never the whole meal or rehearsal. Set pieces such as S027 or S112 may run to about 10 minutes.
- It begins mid-flow and ends without a summary. It is not a committee with an agenda.
- At most seven speaking voices. Every meeting contains one concrete number: a sum, a line count, an hour or a distance.
- Everyone heard is physically present. Crowds are never named speakers. Crowd audio stays indistinct and never announces facts for the listener's convenience.
- Work and objects interrupt the talk: food, tools, paper, a cue missed, a basket dropped. A rehearsal contains real attempts at the lines, not only talk about attempts. An actor may try a phrase two ways, but do not pad a script with repeated full speeches. Use short public-domain fragments only where they help.
- Sound: one room tone per place. Bells are allowed as time-marks. Room context is implied in the beats and never explained in the dialogue.
- Do not make every practical person into the same dry accountant.

**Performances are not meetings.** They appear on the calendar and in the body data, and a tiring-house meeting sometimes follows them.

**Frequency.** 3–6 a week in playing season; 1–3 a week in Lent and at Stratford.

> *Example: S027, Sat 15 Mar, 11:40, `burbage_house_holywell`, about 7 min (a set piece), seven voices. "Sharers' reckoning: the Queen's twenty pounds." Heminges opens: "The warrant has no weight until someone brings the coin." Then:*
> `will_kemp`: You heard the clerk, Will. *William Kempe* first, *William Shakespeare* second, *Richard Burbage* hindmost. Even the Queen's clerks know who fills the yard.
> `richard_burbage`: He listed us by bulk of belly, not weight of wit.
> `john_heminges`: Twenty pound, masters, and fifty shillings of it owed already to the mercer in Cheapside. Keep your thumbs off the angels till I've cast the slate.
>
> *Example: S072, Mon 7 Jul. The sharers argue on upturned property-chests whether to tour or wait out the summer. Heminges has the road in figures, on a sheet of towns and carriage costs, and his case wins, Kemp against: play on to Sat 26 Jul, rest the house in August, open again Mon 25 Aug.*
>
> *Doorstep example: Fri 22 Aug, home from Stratford. Mistress Ansell meets him at the foot of the stair with a candle, a question and something she is carrying, and does not put it down.*

### 2.4 Call

**What it is.** A short two-voice conversation with someone who is not in the room. It is the scarcest surface and the one modern touch a listener will feel.

1. **Unexplained.** The other voice is simply there: slightly close and dry, with no room tone around it, as if across a casement. Nobody comments on it. There is no ringing, no connection sound, no "Can you hear me?" and no modern sign-off.
2. **Range.** A call reaches only as far as a running boy could carry a message and come back within the hour: the City, Shoreditch, Bankside, Holborn, the Strand and Westminster. Stratford, the road, Richmond and Greenwich are out of range. **News from home always comes by letter or carrier, days late.** That lag drives the Stratford story and must never be broken. A message from someone out of range is reported as a message and never voiced as though its sender were present.
3. **Shape.** The caller names himself and his place, the way a messenger would ("Will? John Heminges. From Aldermanbury."). It raises one matter and one small human aside, and ends on a commendation ("God keep you." "And you."). A call runs 1–4 minutes. William calls out as often as he is called.
4. **Frequency.** About 34 in the year, never more than two in a week, and none in some weeks. None while he is away (26 March – 10 April, 28 July – 21 August, 24–29 December). No call is ever missed and returned, because there is no voicemail in this world.
5. **Who.** Heminges (9), Field (5), Gilbert (4), Cuthbert Burbage (4), Kettleby for Lord Hunsdon (4), Florio for Southampton House (3), Quiney while he lodges at the Bell (2), and Burbage, Hunsdon himself and Southampton himself once each. The unnamed lady never calls. The full distribution is in § 7.
6. **On the calendar** a call appears only if it was arranged. Most are not.

> *Example: S104, Fri 7 Nov, 16:50, "Call: my Lord Chamberlain", about 3 min. Hunsdon is at Somerset House; William is in the Theatre's tiring-house, still in his player's gown. "Is that Shakespeare? Hunsdon. From Somerset House. Speak up, man, I've a fire roaring here like a Scot."*
>
> *Example: W017, Wed 26 Feb, Field from Blackfriars.*
> `richard_field`: Will? Richard Field, from the Blackfriars. Have I caught thee between plays?
> `william_shakespeare`: Between a cue and a cold dinner. Go on.
> `richard_field`: I'm up to my wrists in lye-water, washing type. Harrison sent word from the Greyhound: he has scarce a dozen of the *Lucrece* left in quires.
> `william_shakespeare`: God bless the Inns of Court, then. Save me two quires of the Rochelle crown paper, Dick. My ink goes through this coarse stuff like rain through thatch.

### 2.5 Voice memo

**What it is.** William alone, speaking low: walking Finsbury Fields at dawn, at his window, behind his barred door, on the road, in an inn bed, or lying awake in the dark hour between first and second sleep.

- 30–120 seconds. It is working speech, not a diary essay read aloud. It starts mid-thought, often tries a line two or three ways, and ends abruptly ("…Enough." "…Sleep." "…No.").
- It is never addressed to anyone: not an app, not a future reader, not the journal. He never explains context to a listener and never says where a listener could find him. "Remember:" or "Mark this" are the only signposts.
- It holds what he will not trust to loose paper or company gossip: Southwell, the riots, the executions, Arundel, the two unnamed loves, fear and joy. It also holds a sum checked aloud, an answer to Anne rehearsed and then simplified, and a practical reminder that cuts into an elevated thought.
- Nothing in a memo knows the future. None reports an execution as seen: in June and July the qualifiers matter ("I heard", "Bryan says", "I did not see"). He never names the lady, and no memo reveals a T3 identity.
- Emotion does not require theatrical delivery.
- Frequency: 2–4 a week, more on the road.

> *Example: S019, Fri 21 Feb, 21:15, `ansell_house`, about 95 s, "Voice memo: the poet at Tyburn". In bed, one candle. He begins with Southwell, loses the sentence, and notices he is still holding a pen he has not used. It ends on the messenger in black for the last act of the comedy: "Let him say almost nothing and let it go through them like cold. …Enough."*
>
> *Example: S004, Wed 8 Jan, walking home from the Cross Keys under a half moon: "(low) Enter a messenger. In black, among all their colours. He needn't say much. 'The king your father—' and she has it before he can finish. The less he says… Remember: the frost on the pump tonight, how breath hangs white in the close."*

### 2.6 Notes

His writing is material: ink, paper, a hand, copying labour and physical custody. The capture makes a page legible to the product, but the world still contains all of those.

**Five streams**, each generated as its own stream:

1. **The commonplace book.** A small stitched book in a brown cover, unevenly filled, with his own headings: *Of Kings · Of Love · Of Time · Words · Faces · Prices*. It holds quotations from his reading in their original spelling with source and leaf, overheard phrases, Florio's proverbs, odd words and the Grendon constable's big words. Under "Prices" go the loaf against the notch, butter, and a quire of paper.
2. **The drafts (foul papers).** Loose folio sheets folded once. Secretary hand, with italic for names and stage directions. Speech prefixes are abbreviated and inconsistent in a believable way (*Rom.*, *Ro.*; *Iul.*; *Nur.*; the Capulets' lady as *Wife*, *Mo.*, *La.*). Whole lines are struck through and rewritten above, with few blots; neither a blotted caricature nor a miraculously clean page. There are marginal crosses where he means to return, and now and then a player's name for a character's (*Kemp* for *Peter*). Blank space means something: an unfinished question, a gap left for a word. The **sonnet papers** are a sub-stream of the drafts: single leaves, fair-copied when finished, with no titles, numbers or addressee. They are tied with a tape on 13 December and go to the bottom of the coffer.
3. **The account book.** A narrow ledger, with receipts on the left and payments on the right in his shorthand of *Item* and roman numerals. There is a total every Saturday, and a coffer count on quarter days and before and after each journey. From March it keeps *expected* and *received* in separate columns.
4. **Letters.** Drafts kept of what he sends, and the originals of what he receives. They follow period form: salutation, business, commendations, place and date by feast ("From London this Candlemas day"), and "deliver these" on the outside. To Anne he is plain and short, and gets shorter and truer as the year goes on. To each child he writes one playful paragraph. Anne's letters are in a clear, unlearned hand with strong sense; whether she writes them or dictates to Susanna is left open.
5. **The tables.** His pocket writing-tables `[ext]`: erasable leaves for lists that get wiped (errands, cues to mend, a rhyme). The same leaf may show different contents on different days.

**Text rules.**

- Where a page quotes a finished play, use the early quarto text (*LLL* Q1, *R2* Q1, *R&J* Q1/Q2, *MND* Q1) or the 1609 sonnets, in old spelling. Flag the page "public-domain text in an invented setting". Never present a composition date as a record, and never claim an original Shakespeare manuscript. Invented draft language is labelled **new fiction** in the metadata.
- Keep authentic quotation and invented connecting text separate in the data.
- Store a diplomatic image of the page and a normalised transcript separately, so the page looks right and remains searchable.
- No modern bullet glyphs, checkboxes, typed headings or folder names appear on a photographed page. Builders may group artifacts into collections (`lll_working_copy`, `richard_ii_working_copy`, `romeo_working_copy`, `dream_working_gathering`, `private_verse_leaves`, `household_letters`, `road_gathering`), but these are metadata and never visible in the world.
- **The Lent rule.** Before 5 July the *Romeo* stream holds only Brooke extracts, name lists, a plan of two houses and single images. It has no dialogue, no scene and no line from the play.

> *Example: S024, Thu 6 Mar, 19:30, commonplace book, a new heading, "Verona: first notes". Brooke's opening is copied out with "a jog-trot… a man could plough to it" beside it. "Two houses. Alike. That is the thing." A list of names ("Romeus (Romeo is better in the mouth)"), a box-plan of the two houses with the street and the Prince between them, one image (a torch brought into a vault), and "NOT YET. The King first." At the foot: "Item pd. for the booke vi d."*
>
> *Example: the account book, Tue 25 Mar, headed "Lady Day, the xxv of Marche 1595":*
> `Item pd to Mrs Ansell, the quarter's chamber, by noone ...... xx s`
> `Item a horne of her inke ................................... ij d`
> `Item a quire of paper ...................................... v d`
> `Told in the coffer this day ......................... xxvij li ij s`

### 2.7 Screen

**What it is.** The written or printed surface in front of his eyes while he works or reads, as a still or a slow scroll: what is legible, what is already done, and what is being written now. There is no desktop, browser, cursor, window or glowing rectangle anywhere in the world.

Every screen entry carries one `surface` tag:

| `surface` | What it is |
|---|---|
| `draft` | His foul papers on the board, under the quill |
| `tables` | His pocket writing-tables: small erasable leaves and a stylus |
| `part` | A player's cue-script roll, his own or one he is correcting: the cue words ranged right after a long rule, then the speech |
| `plot` | The backstage plot on its pasteboard: entrances by scene in ruled boxes |
| `book` | A printed book open on the desk or a stall: Holinshed, Brooke, Golding, Daniel, Plutarch, Southwell |
| `proof` | A wet proof sheet at Field's, with the corrector's marks |
| `ledger` | His account book, or Heminges's company book or slate |
| `letter` | A letter being read or written |
| `bill` | Playbills on posts, the Rose's and his own company's |
| `pamphlet` | Ballads, news-sheets and proclamations |
| `stage` | Watching a play (§ 2.8) |
| `almanac` | His penny almanac with his marks: the calendar's physical twin |

**Rules.**

- A `draft` screen names the play, act and scene (modern numbering in the label only), how full the sheet is, what is already done above, and what is being written.
- `proof` appears only at Field's, and he reads the proof; he never sets type.
- Bills are black-letter, title only, with "by the Lord Chamberlain his Servants" or "the Lord Admiral his Servants". No bill names an author.
- Pamphlets are real 1595 imprints only, from the ledger in § 9.
- A featured writing day has 3–8 screen segments. Most ordinary days need fewer, and he does not write continuously all day.

> *Example: S046, Thu 15 May, 10:20, `proof`. A sheet of North's Plutarch hangs damp on Field's drying line, two folio pages of the life of Caesar side by side, the ink still bright. His attention runs down the page to a turned letter circled in the margin. An inky thumb, Field's and not his, taps the ring.*
>
> *Example: W042, Sat 17 May, late, `draft`. "Richard II, 3.2 (label only); the sheet three-quarters full. Done above: the landing, the king greeting the earth. Now being written: 'For Gods sake let vs sit vpon the ground, / And tell sad stories of the death of Kings'". The page is flagged as public-domain text in an invented setting.*

### 2.8 Watching media

**Watching is a physical visit, tagged `stage`.** He must be present at a performance: at the Rose, or watching his own company from the tiring-house door. A watch-history item records the house, the play, where he stood and what he noticed.

- **A playbill read is a `bill`, not a play watched.** A report of a performance is not watch history. Never quietly expand a bill into an attendance.
- Attendance costs travel time and the whole afternoon. He cannot act and watch at the same time.
- He never watches on a Sunday. Never use one of Henslowe's disputed Sunday dates as permission for Sunday playing.
- He does not watch between 12 June and 19 July, because he does not cross the river then.
- Tilts, pageants and the Lord Mayor's show are photo and location, not watch history.
- Rose plays use only Henslowe's titles, dates and receipts, on corrected weekdays. Lost plays get staging and atmosphere, never invented plot beyond what the title allows. The few plays with known substance (*Faustus*, *Tamburlaine*) can be richer. Nothing appears on 18 May, which is the forgery.

**Canonical Rose attendances:** Fri 14 Feb *Long Meg of Westminster* (S016) · Tue 20 May *1 Hercules* (W043) · Fri 29 Aug *Longshanks* (S085) · Fri 28 Nov *Harry the Fifth* (S108). Builders may add up to four more on days he is not cast, from Henslowe's titles and receipts only. Easter Monday (S036) and the reopening on 25 August (S083) are bills, because he plays those afternoons. The 18 June *2 Caesar* is out, because it falls inside the river ban.

> *Example: S016, Fri 14 Feb, `stage`. The Rose, Lord Admiral's Men, *Long Meg of Westminster*. He stood in the yard by the stage-right post. He noticed the yard roaring at every stride of the tallest player on the stage, and the gatherer's box rattling at the gallery stair before the first speech.*
>
> *Counter-example: S036, Mon 21 Apr, `bill`. On a Bishopsgate post, the Rose's black-letter bill for* The French Doctor *beside the Theatre's for* Love's Labour's Lost. *He reads both and walks on to play.*

### 2.9 Photo

**What it is.** What his eye held, kept as a still: what he would have sketched or remembered.

- Taken from his own standing or sitting eye level, about 5 ft 7 in. Never an aerial view or an impossible vantage, and never a room he could not have entered.
- **Never of himself:** no face, mirror, reflection or shadow. His own hands may appear holding a page or writing at the desk, with ink on the right forefinger.
- Nobody poses or looks into a lens. People are caught mid-action, often from behind.
- Light comes only from the sun, fog, candle, rushlight, cresset or fire. There is no flash and no added captions. **The camera is a good phone of 2025** (design decision, 2026-11-17, choosing the sample round's Framing C): today's colour, sharpness and depth, true available light with shadows left dark, never aged, sepia, grained or painted. Everything in front of the lens is 1595.
- Period objects only. Text in frame follows the ledger exactly or is illegible. A photo shows a letter's contents only if he is holding or reading it.
- **Never an execution, a corpse or blood.** Heads on the bridge gate are distant texture at most. The limit is the empty gallows-frame before Crosby Place (S067). Never a news event he did not see: not Cornwall, not Tyburn, not a riot in progress.
- **Never frame the children against Holy Trinity or any churchyard.**
- Frequency: 3–6 a week, more at Stratford, on the road and on feast days. The 48-shot base list is in § 7.

> *Example: S006, Wed 15 Jan, 22:10, from the `ansell_house` doorway looking west. Hard frost on the cobbles and the kennel of Great St Helen's under a full moon. The church porch to the left; beyond, the long dark ridge and stone oriel of Crosby Place, with one warm light in it. A cat's prints cross the frost, and breath-mist drifts at the bottom of the frame.*
>
> *Example: S059b, Sat 14 Jun, the morning after the butter riot. Leadenhall: butter firkins set out on a stall as on any Saturday, nothing broken, a constable with a bill leaning by them, bored. Back at the lodging, a penny loaf on the bread-board, short of the notch. There is no crowd: he was not there.*

### 2.10 Body

**What it is.** Steps, sleep and heart rate, recorded every day and never remarked on.

- He never sees, says or discusses a number. He may contradict the data in a memo ("slept well enough").
- **Miles on a horse or a boat are not steps.** Walking beside a horse uphill, stable yards, on-stage movement and rehearsal pacing are.
- Sleep is an estimate with uncertainty, not a medical record. A raised heart rate is never a diagnosis of fear, love or guilt.
- Segmented "first and second sleep" (T3, used knowingly) appears on about a third of winter nights, as a waking hour around 02:00.
- All values are T5. Constants: 1,300 steps per km; resting heart rate 58–62 at baseline. Seasonal tables, special days and the illness calendar are in § 7.
- Fields per day: `steps`; `sleep` as a bed-to-wake window, duration and wakings; `hr_resting`; `hr_active_avg`; `hr_peak`, with the time and what he was doing.

> *Example: S069, Sun 29 Jun. 2,100 steps. Seated with the door barred from 21:30 to 23:15, heart 104 rising to 124, with no steps. Then 2 h 40 of sleep.*
>
> *Example: W061, Mon 28 Jul, day 1 of the walk, to Chalfont St Giles (28 miles). 58,500 steps; road average 112, peak 134 on the climb out of Uxbridge in the midday heat. Bed 21:30 in a barn-loft, up at 03:45, one waking for the alehouse dogs.*

### 2.11 Location

**What it is.** A point and a dwell time from the gazetteer in § 4, recorded in WGS84 coordinates.

- One house counts as one point. The road produces a continuous track with inn stops.
- Travel legs carry a mode: `foot`, `horse`, `wherry` or `tilt_boat`.
- Coordinates mark historical sites, not surviving buildings. Routes are historically plausible, with no later bridges. To the Rose he walks over London Bridge (1.9 km) and may come back by wherry to Old Swan Stairs if he is flush. Boats for Westminster and Richmond go from Old Swan, above the bridge; he never shoots the arches.
- **News places are never location points:** Mousehole, Paul, Newlyn and Penzance, Plymouth, Clontibret and Guiana. Nor is `tyburn`, nor `tower_hill` after 1 February, nor Greenwich, nor any house of the unnamed lady. At `southampton_house` he gets no further than the porter's lodge.
- Missing data is better than teleporting.
- **Radius of fear.** From 13 June to 5 July his tracks stay within 600 m of home, except for the Theatre road and back. He does not cross the river from 12 June to 19 July.
- Away from London: 26 March – 10 April, 28 July – 21 August, and 24–29 December at Richmond.

> *Example: S016, Fri 14 Feb. `ansell_house` 13:15 → `foot` by Gracechurch Street → `london_bridge` → `the_rose` 13:45–16:25 (1.9 km, 28 min) → home over the bridge.*
>
> *Example: the summer walk, Mon 28 – Thu 31 Jul. Four continuous road segments on the Banbury road, with nights at Chalfont St Giles, Grendon Underwood and Banbury, ending at `clopton_bridge` at 5 pm on the 31st. It is never a jump from London to Stratford.*

### 2.12 Calendar

**What it is.** His penny almanac made legible. Entries are short and period-plain.

- It holds feasts and Sundays, law-term opening and closing days, quarter days, new and full moons (with no times), playing days ("Play: *Errors*, Cross Keys, 2 of the clock", with "(I play)" on about four days in six), rehearsals, the Rose's bill, rent days, carrier days at the Bell, and family baptism and birthdays ("Twins' day").
- Nothing he does not yet know ever appears. Raleigh's sailing is not on 6 February, and 24 July reads only "No play".
- The lady never appears by any word. Small unlabelled crosses mark the days he saw or heard her: seven in the year, on 5 Jan, 28 Feb, 22 Mar, 22 May, 8 Jun, 14 Sep and 29 Oct.
- A call appears only if arranged.

> *Example: Mon 6 Jan. "Twelfth Day." "Play: Errors, Cross Keys, 2 of the clock (I play)." No Rose entry: Henslowe has no play that day, because the Admiral's Men are at court.*
>
> *Example: Sun 20 Apr. "Easter Day. Church, St Helen's, morning prayer, sermon & communion." Nothing else.*

### 2.13 News lag

An event is recorded on the day he hears of it, unless he could have seen or heard it himself. Whitehall and Westminster: the same or the next day. Greenwich and Richmond: 1–2 days. Cornwall and Plymouth: 3–6 days. Ireland: 2–3 weeks. The Indies: never within the year.

**Canonical hearing dates:** Raleigh's sailing, Tue 11 Feb (from the watermen at Old Swan) · Southwell's trial, Thu 20 Feb at supper · Clontibret, about Wed 18 Jun, in the tiring-house · the Stratford fire, Mon 21 Jul, by Anne's letter (Gilbert's garbled call comes an hour earlier) · Cornwall, Sat 26 Jul · Drake's sailing, Thu 4 Sep · Raleigh home, Mon 22 Sep · Essex and the succession book, Wed 5 Nov · the *Edward III* entry, Mon 1 Dec. He never learns of Hawkins's death.

### 2.14 Does anyone remark on the devices?

**No.** Nobody mentions recording, capturing, counting, pictures or speaking at a distance. Nobody holds, looks at or gestures toward a device. Nobody poses for a lens, checks a step count or says "I'll call you". William never says "note to self"; he says "Remember:" or "Mark this". No line of dialogue is written for the journal's benefit, and the capture never explains itself.

> *Example: on Sat 14 Jun, at the bread-board, he only says, "Remember: short of the notch again." The photo, the step count and the heart rate at the window the evening before simply exist.*

### 2.15 Builder conventions

- **IDs.** People and places use the canonical `snake_case` slugs (§ 3, § 4). Seeds keep their `S###` ids, and new entries are `W###`. Artifact ids extend their parent entry: `S006.photo.01`, `S027.audio.meeting`, `S024.notes.01`, `S046.screen.01`.
- **Tiers.** Every historical fact carries its tier (T1–T5). Invented people stay flagged `[invented]`, and real details not in the source book stay `[ext]`.
- **Voices.** Stock text-to-speech voices are cast by register, not accent.

---


## 3. The cast bible

*Fold pass 2, section 3. Merged from the GPT, Gemini and Fable bibles' § 3 under the canon, which wins wherever they disagree. Every slug here is canonical (canon § 3 and the § 6 crosswalks).*

**How to read an entry.**

- **Age** reads "on 1 January → after the birthday or baptism". A **model age** is a T5 choice for voice and appearance only, and never enters factual copy.
- **Tier** is the tier of the person's existence and role (T1 documented · T2 consensus · T3 contested · T4 tradition · T5 invented). Every spoken line in this file is T5 unless it is marked as a quotation. Invented people are flagged `[invented]`.
- **Voice casting** is a register for a stock text-to-speech voice, never a likeness or an imitation of any performer. A stock voice may double minor roles that never share a scene; the household and company principals stay distinct.
- **Sample lines** are period-flavoured and readable. Builders should vary them, not repeat them. Running jokes are listed in canon § 5.7 and are not to be used mechanically.
- **Arcs** cite the canonical spine ids (Fable's `S###` seeds and `W###` anchors, canon § 5 and Fable § 5.3). GPT's and Gemini's own W-numbers are not used here.
- **Thou and you** follow the table in William's entry. Kemp and William use *you* until Sat 22 Nov.

The groups and their order are canon § 3.

---

### 3.1 William and family

#### `william_shakespeare` · William Shakespeare · 30 → 31 · T1/T2 · the owner of the journal

**Age and tier.** Baptised 26 April 1564 (T1); the 23 April birthday is tradition (T4). Named second of the three payees on the Council warrant of 15 March 1594/5 (T1). Poet of *Venus and Adonis* and *Lucrece*, both on sale at the White Greyhound (T1). Sharer, player and house playwright of the Lord Chamberlain's Men (T2). In January he is better known as a poet than as a playwright, and "Sweet Shakspeare" reaches him in a Cambridge margin on Thu 18 Sep (T1 text).

**Where he lives.** The upper front chamber of Mistress Ansell's house in Great St Helen's, Bishopsgate (`ansell_house`; the St Helen's lodging is a T2/T3 working model for 1595, and every detail of the house is T5). At Stratford he sleeps at his father's house in Henley Street (`henley_street`).

**Parts he plays this year (T5).** He is cast on about four afternoons in six. On the others he writes, visits Field or crosses to the Rose.

| Play | His part | Why it matters |
|---|---|---|
| *The Comedy of Errors* | Egeon | Opens the play, then idle till the end |
| *Love's Labour's Lost* | The King of Navarre | "Kingly parts" (T3/T4), played knowingly |
| *Richard II* | John of Gaunt | Dies in Act 2, and watches the rest from the tiring-house door |
| *Romeo and Juliet* | The Chorus and Prince Escalus | Speaks the first words of the play himself |
| *Titus*, *Richard III* and the stock plays | Old men, dukes, messengers | — |

Cowley covers him on the worst day of the January cold and through the worst of the tooth.

**The body (T5; canon § 5.7).**

- About 5 ft 7 in (170 cm) and 70 kg. Walker's calves. He mocks his own legs in a king's hose.
- Brown hair drawing back from a high forehead and thin at the temples. A short beard he trims himself, kept close so that a stage beard sits over it. Hazel eyes that watch men's mouths when they argue. No earring.
- Ink on the right forefinger and the heel of the hand, always: blue-black oak-gall from Mistress Ansell's horn.
- A plain murrey wool doublet on workdays. A black velvet jerkin, kept for patrons and court. The grey cloak on its peg.
- Walks fast with his head slightly down and his lips moving.
- Sound teeth except one lower-left molar, which aches from Tue 9 Sep, abscesses from Fri 10 Oct and is drawn by Yaxley on Tue 14 Oct.
- A cold with a low fever, Mon 13 – Sun 19 Jan. A low back strained raising a barn frame on Tue 12 Aug, flat until the 15th and nagging until about 15 Sep. Saddle-soreness in Lent and blistered heels in July are travel, not illness.
- Sleeps well in summer and in the country. In the long winter nights he often wakes about two, lies thinking in the waking hour, and sleeps again (segmented sleep on about a third of winter nights; T3, used knowingly).
- Resting heart rate 58–62. Eats little before a play.

**Habits.**

- Writes fast and clean (the Folio's "scarse… a blot", T1 text, T3 as fact), but readiness is not ease. He can spend half a morning avoiding a passage and then write it clean in an hour.
- Two sittings: first light until dinner on days without rehearsal, and by candle after supper.
- Says lines aloud before he writes them. Mistress Ansell, a floor below, can tell comedy from tragedy by the rhythm of his feet.
- Carries the writing-tables everywhere. Reads standing at the stalls in Paul's Churchyard and buys less than he reads.
- Begs off late nights with "I am in pain" (Aubrey, T4). The company treats it as a catchphrase: "Where does it pain you tonight, Will?"
- Counts money on Saturday nights. Writes home after the carrier comes in. From March his account book keeps *expected* and *received* in separate columns.
- Uses his father's glover's paring-knife as his penknife.
- Trims a quill when a conversation turns uncomfortable. Smooths paper that is already smooth when he wants to look patient. Checks the tie of his purse after bad money news.

**Tells.**

- Rubs the inked forefinger against his thumb when he is lying or sparing someone.
- Goes very courteous when angry (Chettle's "civil demeanour", T1 text / T2 that it is him).
- Laughs silently, with his shoulders only.
- When a line arrives mid-conversation his eyes go to the middle distance and he says "Go on, I hear you," and does not.
- Repeats a word that has pleased him three or four times across a day until it turns up in a speech.
- His right thumb taps four iambs on his thigh when a phrase strikes him.
- Cornered on politics or religion, he agrees courteously with a proverb and changes the subject.

**Humour.** Dry, quick and self-mocking. Puns as reflex, in English and schoolroom Latin. His favourite joke is an adjustment of scale: a king compared to a badly run household, a magnificent promise reduced to the cost of a candle. He teases upward gently and downward never. He is the funniest man in a room that contains Kemp, and too wise to prove it. He is not permanently sparkling. At home he can be dull with fatigue, and he is irritable when asked to repeat something he believes he has already explained. When tired he mocks his own Warwickshire vowels before anyone else can.

**Worries, in his own order.**

1. Money for home, and whether what he sends is enough to be forgiven for.
2. His father's name: the debts, the church list, the gentility that slipped.
3. The lease on the Theatre, Giles Allen, and the Mayor a hundred yards from his bed.
4. Nick Sorrell's voice.
5. That the lord has tired of him.
6. That the lady has not.
7. That he is a maker of toys in a year when men are hanged for words.
8. The loaf, and what the dearth means at Stratford.
9. The steady guilt of living four days' walk from his children while he spends his best hours on invented daughters and kings.

**How he talks to whom.**

| To | Register | Thou / you | A line |
|---|---|---|---|
| Burbage | Brotherly and quick; half-lines finished for each other | *thou* both ways | "Cut me four lines from the king, Dick, not forty. Roar from the first entrance and thou'st no cellar left to fall into when they take his crown." |
| Kemp | Wary courtesy until November, then warmth | *you*, until Kemp thous him first on 22 Nov | "You have added eleven lines, Will, and nine are better than mine. Leave me the two." |
| Heminges | Plain and trusting; lets him do the sums | *you*; "John" | "Let us first discover whose money we are spending." |
| Phillips, Pope, Bryan | Easy fellowship; leaves room for interruption | *you*, first names | "That is a fine entrance, Tom. It will serve better if another man may enter after it." |
| The boys | Gentle, exact, never sarcastic | *thou*; "Nick", "lad" | "Breathe before it, not in it. The line will wait for thee." |
| Field | The home vowels come back, and Field half-mocks him for it | *thou* in private, *you* in the shop | "Thy press and my pen, Richard. A tanner's boy and a glover's, and neither of us at home." |
| Anne | Fewer words than to anyone; less ornament, and more open to being taken literally; lets silences stand | *thou* | "I meant the letter kindly." (Her answer must land before he speaks again.) |
| The children | Playful and inventing; asks them questions; enjoys a contest until they expose the terms he rigged in his favour | *thou* | "Three questions, and if I cannot answer the third thou shalt have the groat." |
| His father | Jests as armour (Plume, T4), respect under it | *you*; "sir" when it counts | "Some fairy's been at your reckoning, sir? Then the fairies keep better books than the glovers." |
| Quiney, Sadler, home men | Relaxed Warwickshire rhythm; country words come back (*kecksies*, *batlet*, *whittawer*, nine men's morris) | *thou* to Sadler, *you* to Quiney | "Thou'st flour to the elbow, Sadler. Is it bread or a siege?" |
| Hunsdon | Short and soldier-plain, as he likes it | "my lord", "your lordship" | "We have a comedy, my lord, and it is ready." |
| Southampton | Formal, warm, careful; a half-step back; any feeling hides inside a figure of speech | "my lord"; never *thou* | "My lord, what I have is your lordship's, as I once wrote, and I wrote it truly." |
| Mistress Ansell | Amused deference; tells her his plots | *you*; "Mistress" | "He is punished, Mistress. Only not in this play." |
| The unnamed lady | Almost nothing aloud | *you* | "Good morrow." |
| Himself, in memos | Low, quick, unfinished | — | "Remember: she speaks first. Not he. She. …No. Let him think he does." |

**Sample lines.**

- "I have a king, John, and no crown for him. Is there a crown in the basket, or must he reign in a hat?"
- "Put it down as charity, then. I'd liefer be thought generous than merely late."
- (Begging off) "I'd come, but I am in pain." "Where?" "Wherever you'd least like to look."
- "Nay, drink my pint for me, Tom. I have three hundred lines of kissing and sword-thrusts promised to Garnish by cock-crow, and sack makes all my lovers talk like constables."
- "Expected, and received. Two columns, and the first one eats dinner."
- (Memo, on the road) "Is it trade or dwelling. Trade, or. …She asked it better than I can answer it. Enough."

**What he wants this year.** Durable income and a house in Stratford under his own name. A play nobody can improve by talking over it. To be thought well of by a lord without needing it. To deserve Anne's dry respect, and to be welcomed home without having to plead for it. Freedom from any other man's power to stop him working. To keep the company whole.

**Arc.** See canon § 1. In one line: January's fluent mediator, half poet with a patron, becomes June's anxious employer, August's imperfect member of his own household and December's more deliberate keeper of promises, a playwright among seven fellows who writes sonnets nobody sees. He starts the year counting what he is owed and ends it counting what he is building. He writes around Kemp and ends by writing for him. He is less quick to mistake fluency for generosity. He is still ambitious, not cured, and not an exemplary husband.

**Relationships.** Burbage is his most demanding artistic partner and nearest to a brother. Kemp is his necessary corrective. Heminges is his practical conscience. Anne knows which of his explanations are explanations and which are performances. Field knows him as a tradesman's son and refuses to be dazzled. Mistress Ansell is his first critic.

**Voice casting.** A warm, light baritone in the middle register: quick and clear, with a smile in it, courteous openings, quick changes of direction and the occasional suddenly plain sentence. Never declamatory, even when he quotes himself.

---

#### `anne_shakespeare` · Anne Shakespeare (née Hathaway) · 38–39 (model 39) · T1 marriage / every word T5 · wife

**Tier.** The marriage bond of 1582 is T1. Her age comes from her monument (T2). No word of hers survives, so every line is invented (T5), and the bible says so wherever she appears. **She is never called "Agnes"**, by anyone, and no London address is ever fixed for her.

**Role and home.** She runs her half of a crowded household at Henley Street (`henley_street`): nine at table with John, Mary, Joan, Richard, Edmund and the three children. She keeps the malt and stores, the orchard and the poultry. She is a farmer's daughter from Shottery who counts in bushels and pence. She is neither an abandoned saint nor a secret oracle. She can judge cloth, provisions and an excuse, and her authority comes from repeated competence rather than from speeches about authority.

**How she talks.** Economical, dry, unhurried, practical, with a Warwickshire cadence. She wastes no words on flattery. She asks the second question, the one he hoped to avoid, and her questions often contain the fact he left out. She calls him "Will", and "husband" when she is making a point. She uses *thou*, and drops to a cool *you* when she wants him to hear the distance, or when he talks like a Londoner. Her oaths are mild: "Faith", "Lord, Will", "By'r Lady", "Marry come up", "God mend it". She dislikes being consulted after a decision has been made. Gifts that answer a real need please her; gifts that make work get a restrained curiosity. She can want him home and want him out of the kitchen within the same hour.

**Sample lines.**

- "You write that London is dear. So is Stratford, and we've no plays to pay for it."
- "Tell me not what the Queen wore at Greenwich. Tell me whether thou hast eaten aught but tavern herring since Candlemas."
- "I asked when you would come. I did not ask whether the road was long."
- "Don't promise me a house, Will. Buy one and I'll believe in it."
- "Sit there. If thou must think, think where the basket is not."
- "I liked the one you read us, with the two brothers who were the same. I laughed. Don't look so astonished."

**She wants from him:** money on the quarter and usable money, not the account of it; reliable dates; letters that say something; his presence at harvest; adult partnership and his unperformed attention; one straight answer about where he means to live; and a house of their own, away from John's moods, so that Henley Street never fears the bailiff again.

**He wants from her:** welcome, reassurance that his absence has a purpose, and absolution, which she does not deal in. He gets her judgment instead, and finds it better.

**Arc.**

- *January:* her letter arrives Sun 19 Jan while he is abed with the cold: the children's shoes, his father's cough (mild, and gone by her next letter), a question about Lent.
- *Lent:* cool at the door ("You've come, then.") on Fri 28 Mar, thawing by Mothering Sunday. The visit shows their habit of discussing logistics instead of grievance. On the Shottery walk, Sat 5 Apr (W029), she asks whether London is his trade or his dwelling. He does not answer well, and carries the question back to London and into a play about a man who mistakes a crown for a self.
- *July:* her letter about the fire, which reaches him Mon 21 Jul, is five lines long and steadier than he is.
- *August:* she watches him raise the Sadler cousin's barn frame on Tue 12 Aug and strain his back, and says nothing kind until he is flat on the bed, where she is very kind. They make a more honest bargain: he will not promise a journey until it is arranged, and she will tell him plainly what cannot wait. She pushes him to stop merely hoarding coin and to look at real Stratford freehold. On Sat 16 Aug she walks him past the great decayed house by the Guild Chapel and says, "That one, then." (W071). It is the year's turn: a wish with an address, with no inquiry and no price.
- *Autumn:* their letters grow shorter and more useful, with jokes that need no explanation.
- *December:* her Christmas letter ends with a joke at his expense, and he reads it twice.

**Relationships.** She manages John's pride tactfully until it costs the household, and Mary's quiet. She respects Mary but resists being corrected through William. She is close to Judith Sadler, and trusts Quiney with information but not with an optimistic total. Joan is her ally. She is Hobbins's clearest customer. Bartholomew is her brother at Hewlands.

**Voice casting.** A low, unhurried alto; warmth held back, then not.

---

#### `susanna_shakespeare` · Susanna Shakespeare · 11 → 12 (bapt. 26 May) · T1 / T5 character · elder daughter

**Role and home.** Henley Street. Anne's right hand. Her reading is a T5 choice: she reads and writes competently in this fiction, and that is never cited as a 1595 record. She is sharp, watchful and a little solemn, with her mother's economy and her father's ear. She is proud of the printed books her father sends by the carrier.

**How she talks.** Precise and observant, a girl of eleven, and from 26 May of twelve, trying on grown-up phrases, never precociously modern. She lets an adult finish an inaccurate statement before asking the one question that makes it collapse. She corrects his grammar in her letters, and her siblings' everywhere.

**Sample lines.**

- "You wrote *who* and it should be *whom*. Master Aspinall says so."
- "Was it paid, or did a gentleman say it would be?"
- "You said I might choose. You did not say I must choose what you had brought."
- "Judith says in a tragedy everybody is wicked and sorry by supper-time. Is it true, Father, or does somebody stay good enough to lock the doors?"
- (On the orchard scene) "She is too quick to say yes."

**She wants from him:** to be taken seriously without being made his demonstration of a clever child; books in English; his serious attention as a girl nearly grown.
**He wants from her:** conversation, approval, and evidence that the family can share his love of language. He teaches her to cast the household tallies cleanly.

**Arc.** She begins the year sending neat corrections to a household list. In Lent she is shy of him, and she catches him offering attention he is not actually giving. She turns twelve on Mon 26 May: a letter and a silver thimble. In August she reads Golding's *Ovid* aloud by Clopton Bridge on Sun 10 Aug while he makes willow boats (S081), and then, while he lies flat with his back, reads his new orchard scene aloud and tells him the girl is "too quick to say yes". He changes nothing and thinks about it for a month. In August, too, she refuses him permission to repeat a family joke to the players, and he obeys.

**Relationships.** Close to Anne. Fond of her siblings and impatient at being made responsible for them. Enjoys her grandfather's stories but checks their arithmetic. Consulted by the twins when a rule needs interpreting.

**Voice casting.** A clear, careful girl's voice, a shade solemn.

---

#### `hamnet_shakespeare` · Hamnet Shakespeare · 9 → 10 (bapt. 2 Feb) · T1 / T5 character · son, Judith's twin

**⛔ Grief care is binding.** He is alive, ordinary and well in every scene, all year. Never ill, never singled out, never the subject of a lingering look or a "last" anything, never framed against Holy Trinity or any churchyard, never given health adjectives (no "healthy", no "rosy"), never part of any mortality image or future-facing reading. He is one of three children and gets no more page than his sisters. Hamnet Sadler is a different person.

**Role and home.** Henley Street. A boy in the lower forms of the King's New School (T2 institution). Loud, kind, a runner. He likes mechanisms (strings, wheels, hinges) and transactions, and is not especially dreamy: a thing that moves wrongly interests him more than a thing that behaves. He also likes horses, willow boats and anything with a blade. He collects things: a jay's feather, a bent nail from the fire ground, and from 28 Mar Burbage's old lath sword.

**How he talks.** Quick, practical, breathless, sometimes indignant at a rule he has just exploited. He mangles Lily's Latin grammar into Warwickshire.

**Sample lines.**

- "Is the Queen fat?"
- "Can Master Kemp truly dance a jig longer than a sermon?"
- "It did turn. It turned too far."
- "If she took the larger piece yesterday, this is not the larger piece today."
- "The master kept us till five over *hic, haec, hoc*, and Judith hid my hornbook in the malt-bin. Watch how far this one goes under the arch!"

**He wants from his father:** help with objects, a fair hearing in disputes, a real wooden foil from London, stories of bears and tilting knights, and a story that is not being tested on him for an audience.
**William wants:** easy companionship, the pleasure of being useful with his hands, and Latin learned without the mirth going out of him.

**Arc.** A parcel dispute in February (by letter). In Lent: the lath sword on Fri 28 Mar; a race along the Avon bank; a wooden wheel repaired with his father on Sun 6 Apr (G026), with Joan's silent help. In August: willow boats under Clopton Bridge on Sun 10 Aug (S081); an elaborate string-and-spool contraption of his own design, brought up on Wed 13 Aug to show William flat on the chamber floor, which carries nothing and then, by dinner, works (G061); he helps his grandfather sort skins and windfall apples. On Thu 14 Aug the children act the orchard scene at William's bedside.

**Relationships.** Judith is his opponent, collaborator and preferred witness. Susanna is consulted when a rule needs interpreting. His grandfather supplies unsuitable scraps. Edmund lets him hold things he should not.

**Voice casting.** A quick, bright boy's voice, often out of breath.

---

#### `judith_shakespeare` · Judith Shakespeare · 9 → 10 (bapt. 2 Feb) · T1 / T5 character · younger daughter, Hamnet's twin

**Role and home.** Henley Street. She does not take to letters (her later mark is T1 `[ext]`), but she sings, mimics and remembers everything said in her hearing. She does her grandfather's walk, and the aldermen's, and the preacher's. She notices procedures: if someone declares a rule, she applies it beyond the point at which its inventor finds it convenient. She is given no hidden literary destiny.

**How she talks.** Fast, teasing, direct and literal, occasionally majestically wrong. She finishes Hamnet's sentences.

**Sample lines.**

- "I can't read it but I can say it."
- "You said no one was to touch it. I have not touched it. I have moved the table."
- "Why must I be the woman in his story? I was the horse yesterday."
- "Give me the groat with the Queen's ruff clearest. Hamnet bit his till her nose went flat."

**She wants from him:** participation, not observation; ribbons from Cheapside; sweetmeats from the carrier's pannier; to beat Hamnet at running.
**William wants:** delight and an uncomplicated welcome, which she refuses to supply on command.

**Arc.** She starts the year demanding that a London gift be divided by rules she devises (the Candlemas groats, "to be spent foolishly"). In April she makes William play the less interesting part in a household game. In August she stages noisy backyard pageants with Hamnet that make William laugh until his ribs ache, and on Thu 14 Aug she plays the Nurse to Susanna's Juliet from memory, after one hearing. She also redesigns Hamnet's contraption by removing the piece he thinks essential, and it works slightly better.

**Relationships.** Anne understands her impatience. Mary enjoys her singing. Hamnet finds her indispensable and impossible.

**Voice casting.** A quick, bright girl's voice that can drop into anyone else's.

---

#### `john_shakespeare` · John Shakespeare · mid-60s (model 65) · T1 · father

**Role and home.** Henley Street, which he owns. Glover and whittawer, and an unlicensed wool dealer in the informer cases (T1). Bailiff of Stratford in 1568. In money trouble since 1578. On the 1592 recusancy return among those who "come not to church for fear of process for debt" (T1; whether it was debt or faith is T3 and stays open). He once had a coat of arms drawn up: the yellowed 1568 sketch is a wish shown once, never a filing, and nobody promises anything about 1596.

**How he talks.** "Merry-cheeked" (Plume, T4): cheerful, teasing and proud, expansive while joking and evasive when a sum is named. His jests land a half-beat before the hurt shows. He tells stories as if the listener has interrupted the most important part by arriving at the end. He is full of old guild proverbs, and lowers his voice near an open window when he speaks of churchwardens, informers or the heralds. Oaths: "By my troth", "By St George", "God's my comfort", and "By the mass", an old habit caught and swallowed.

**Sample lines.**

- "A player! Well. I've seen aldermen do it for nothing and worse."
- "I don't go because Master Bailiff's man stands at the porch with paper, and I've no wish to read it. My knees ache less in mine own parlour."
- "They'd have made me a gentleman once, Will. I had the pattern drawn. Then wool fell."
- "I was bailiff of this borough when Dick Quiney was whipping tops in the kennel."
- "A man may owe fourteen shillings and yet have been bailiff."
- "I taught thee to cut a glove. The rest thou hast added at great expense."

**He wants:** the family's name mended, relief without humiliation, and evidence that William's peculiar trade has not carried the family downward. Above all, not to be pitied.
**William wants:** his blessing on the trade, pride without another obligation hidden inside it, and the truth about the church porch. He gets the first in a joke and never gets the last.

**Arc.**

- *January:* a passing cough in Anne's letter, gone by the next.
- *March:* Gilbert's call of Tue 11 Mar (W020) about his small debts.
- *Lent:* Mothering Sunday, Sun 30 Mar, and he stays home from Holy Trinity. Wed 2 Apr (S031): the talk about old debts and the arms once sought, with the old pattern shown. William quietly pays £5 of small debts in three shops without a word, including a 14s 6d glover's reckoning. John pretends not to know and jokes about it ("Some fairy's been at my reckoning").
- *July:* he steadies the Henley Street yard on the night of the fire alarm, though nothing of theirs burns.
- *August:* the malt-and-barley talk (S080, Tue 5 Aug). At sixty-odd he is up a ladder at the fire ground directing younger men. His dry aside on the fire sermon. In a modest concession, not a transformation, he lets Anne rather than William settle a small household payment.

**Relationships.** William and he genuinely enjoy one another. Mary can puncture his self-presentation with one practical recollection. He supplies the twins with materials Anne would rather he kept. He likes Quiney's respect for former office. The Lambert business, the lost Arden land at Wilmcote (T1 `[ext]`), is the sore he will not touch.

**Voice casting.** A bright, grainy tenor, with a laugh in every third line.

---

#### `mary_shakespeare` · Mary Shakespeare (née Arden) · c.55–59 (model 57) · T1 · mother

**Role and home.** Henley Street. Daughter of Robert Arden of Wilmcote (T1), gentry-born, and she never mentions it. She keeps the keys and the family's memory: who first owned a bowl, what a parcel of land was called before its present owner renamed it, the weather and harvests of thirty years back. She says a small old grace under her breath, which William recognises and does not remark on. Her prayers stay familiar and doctrinally unlabelled (the Catholic inner life is T3, felt and never shown as practice).

**How she talks.** Soft, composed, exact, economical. She answers with an example rather than an argument. She calls William "son" and her husband "master".

**Sample lines.**

- "You're thin. They don't feed players, then."
- "Your father has told the better half of that story."
- "Put the dry things above the wet. It is a rule that asks no learning."
- "Thy father talks big of spears on a shield, son, but it's good dry wheat in the garner keeps winter off the hearth."

**She wants:** peace between John and Anne under one roof, all her children safe in lean times, and less tension between money and family pride.
**William wants:** reassurance that he belongs before he has achieved anything.

**Arc.** Mothering Sunday, Sun 30 Mar (W027), is hers. In April she lets him be a son, and presses dried lavender and a flitch of salt bacon into his saddlebags. In March she sends a pair of knitted worsted hose by the carrier. In August she quietly insists that he act as a member of the working household, not a guest, and she notices the grey at his temples and says nothing.

**Relationships.** Affectionate friction with Anne over methods, never a sustained rivalry. Protective of John without being deceived by him. Enjoys Judith's singing.

**Voice casting.** A soft, low older woman's voice; never hurried.

---

#### `gilbert_shakespeare` · Gilbert Shakespeare · 28 → 29 (bapt. 13 Oct) · T1 / T3 London · brother

**Role and home.** A journeyman haberdasher lodging in St Bride's parish, near the Fleet (T3 story choice: he is recorded there in 1597). He goes home on trade now and then, but he is **never at Henley Street during William's visits and never walks the road with him**. He is the family's voice within call range, and hears Stratford news through the carrier and the Bell before William does.

**How he talks.** Blunt, brisk, unimpressed and fond. "Brother." He knows the price of felt, buttons and pack-horses to a farthing. He is practical and sociable, and less interested than William in refining an answer once it is useful. He thinks plays are a fine trade so long as they pay like one.

**Sample lines.**

- "Father owes the baker again. No, not that baker. The other baker."
- "Hobbins's grey mare cast a shoe at Shipston, else you'd have had Anne's cheeses Thursday."
- "Brother? Gilbert. From the Bell. There's been fire again. Last week. The ostler says half the High Street, a drover says Henley Street, somebody says forty houses." (Garbled and wrong: the 1595 fire was north of the centre.)
- "A player's hat, brother. I'd not sell it. I'd not buy it either."

**He wants:** capital to set up his own stall, William's London connections, and to be treated as an adult rather than the available younger brother.
**William wants:** help, without acknowledging how often he asks for it.

**Arc.** Four calls (canon § 2; Fable § 7.3). Tue 11 Mar (W020), their father's small debts. Tue 13 May (S045), Stratford news and the carrier's parcel to fetch. Mon 21 Jul (W306), the first word of the fire, an hour before Anne's letter, garbled. October, about his own birthday on Mon 13 Oct (W403), when William can barely open his jaw. He walks none of the miles and has opinions on all of them.

**Relationships.** Easy with Anne. Good at redirecting John. An old affectionate rivalry with Richard, conducted now by message, over who does the least visible work. Knows the Bell's yard and Hobbins's horses.

**Voice casting.** A brisk tradesman's baritone, flatter than William's.

---

#### `joan_shakespeare` · Joan Shakespeare · 25 → 26 (bapt. 15 Apr) · T1 / T3 background · sister

**Role and home.** Unmarried in this year (her marriage date is unknown), at Henley Street. She runs the kitchen with Mary and spins. She is candid and lively at table, where she teases William without mercy, and gentle and observant in private. She is inwardly devout: the T3 "Spiritual Testament" argument (Steggle 2024) is atmosphere only, never shown and never stated.

**How she talks.** Direct and quick. She is intolerant of being handed someone else's message to deliver.

**Sample lines.**

- "Tell her yourself. I am not a lane between two houses."
- "Thou hast ink on thy cuff again, brother, and London smoke in thy cloak. Let me brush it ere Mother sees."
- "Look, he's brought us a king's manners. Bow, Richard, or he'll put thee in a play."

**She wants:** William to notice the labour done around him without congratulating the workers as though he had invented it; a modest marriage portion when times mend; quiet room for her conscience.
**William wants:** her complicity in making his visits go smoothly.

**Arc.** In Lent she refuses to smooth over a disagreement between William and Anne, then helps him mend Hamnet's wheel without a word of commentary. She notices when he flinches at London news. She keeps the children calm and busy on the night of the July fire. At Christmas she receives a length of holland linen from him in the pack of 18 Dec. Her small, important arc: she stops being his convenient intermediary.

**Relationships.** Anne's ally. John's irreverent daughter. The twins' favourite adult for conspiracies.

**Voice casting.** A clear, lively young woman's voice.

---

#### `richard_shakespeare` · Richard Shakespeare · 20 → 21 (bapt. 11 Mar) · T1 existence / T5 · brother

**Role and home.** Henley Street. He works the glove shop with his father. Silent and capable. He takes over the Stratford errands GPT gave Gilbert.

**How he talks.** Few words, delivered after everyone else has spent theirs.

**Sample lines.**

- "It would be quicker if you stopped describing the knot."
- "If you want it carried, say carried. If you want it admired, fetch another man."
- "The skins are in. The money isn't. Same as always."

**He wants:** useful work and freedom from comparison.
**William wants:** to be helpful, but he tends to offer advice before he understands the problem.

**Arc.** In spring he quietly handles an errand William has complicated, and his competence is a rebuke to William's habit of advising. In August he makes William walk the return of a wrong order to the supplier himself. Later that month William asks him how to mend something, and follows the answer.

**Relationships.** Protective of Edmund without endorsing every ambition. Treats Anne's requests as clearer than John's. Rivals Gilbert at a distance.

**Voice casting.** A low, unhurried young man's voice.

---

#### `edmund_shakespeare` · Edmund Shakespeare · 14 → 15 (bapt. 3 May) · T1 later / T5 · youngest brother

**Role and home.** Henley Street. Stage-struck (T5 on the T1 later fact that he becomes a player). **He is not part of the company in 1595**, and no apprenticeship or journey is arranged.

**How he talks.** Eager and dramatic, trying out an adult register and dropping it. He recites scraps of *Tamburlaine* and *Richard III* into the rain-barrel.

**Sample lines.**

- "I did not say I would be a chief player. I said I would begin."
- "Nick Sorrell's scarce older than I and wears taffeta before the Queen. Why must I stay scraping pelts in Henley Street?"
- "Say it again, the bit about the horse. No, as Master Burbage would say it."

**He wants:** a route to London, and to ride back in William's stirrup to Shoreditch.
**William wants:** to encourage him without taking responsibility for an immediate move, and not to confuse being admired with being useful.

**Arc.** In Lent he asks to hold the lath sword and to hear how Burbage fights at Bosworth. William gives him a non-answer: wait till your voice settles and your hand writes a clean secretary script. He turns fifteen on Sat 3 May (a letter, W038). In August he recognises the non-answer for what it was and asks again, plainly. They agree only that he should practise reading aloud and keep at his present work, and William gives him his first taste of reading from cues in the orchard.

**Relationships.** Admires Burbage at second hand. Annoys Susanna by repeating London stories. Envies Nick.

**Voice casting.** A light young voice that cracks upward when excited.

---

### 3.2 Stratford

#### `richard_quiney` · Richard Quiney · c.38 · T1 later / T2 · mercer and borough man

**Role and home.** A mercer and alderman on the High Street side of the market (`high_cross_market`), a tireless civic fixer who lobbies gentry and Whitehall for the town. His 1598 letter from the Bell to his "Loveinge Contreyman" is T1; familiarity with William in 1595 is T2. In September 1595 he is paid for entertaining Fulke Greville and Thomas Lucy at Stratford, perhaps to view fire damage (T1 payment / T3 purpose). In London he lodges at the Bell, Carter Lane (`the_bell_carter_lane`); his being in London in November is T5.

**How he talks.** Warm, winding, solicitous and importunate, with a breathless civic gravity laced with Latin tags and money worries. He is always one favour ahead of you, and his request generally arrives after three assurances that he has not come to trouble anyone. "Loving countryman", "good Master William".

**Sample lines.**

- "I may be bold with you as with a friend. It is not for myself, Will, it is for the town."
- "It is not a great sum, considered among the men it will oblige."
- "Loving countryman, thank God the wind backed in the night, or the whole north end had gone the way of Chapel Street last year. And the Corporation's chest is bare as my palm."
- "The brief is granted, the brief is granted! Now we have only to collect it, which is the part God leaves to men."

**He wants:** money, introductions, and William's growing standing put to borough use; William's London purse and court ear behind the fire-relief brief.
**William wants:** news, borough intelligence on land and tithes, and friendship, without becoming an unresisting purse.

**Arc.**

- *Lent:* a market-day handshake, Thu 3 Apr (W028).
