- *August:* the fire and the town's plan to seek a relief brief (S080, Tue 5 Aug). On Thu 7 Aug he borrows **£2** at Stratford, with no interest. Judith Sadler makes the men say aloud whether it is a gift, a loan or a purchase.
- *September:* the Greville and Lucy entertainment, offstage.
- *November:* in London at the Bell about the brief, which is authorised on Mon 24 Nov (T1). Two calls, Sat 15 Nov (W095) and Sat 22 Nov (W413), and a supper at the Bell on Mon 24 Nov (W098). His news of home is slightly improved in the telling. He repays **£1**; William gives £2 to the collection and declines to lend more. £1 is still owed on 31 Dec. Their friendship survives the explicit terms better than William expects.

**Relationships.** Sturley is his ally. Anne trusts his information, not his totals. John likes his respect for former office. He gives Hobbins too many errands.

**Voice casting.** A warm, slightly breathless tenor.

---

#### `hamnet_sadler` · Hamnet Sadler · 30s (model 34) · T2 · baker; the twins' godfather

**Not Hamnet Shakespeare.** The twins' names are taken from the Sadlers' (T2). Use his full name or "Sadler" in all copy.

**Role and home.** The bakehouse on the High Street, which stands untouched by the fire. A boyhood friend of William's.

**How he talks.** Slow-spoken, big-handed and flour-dusted; plain, genial and affectionate. Exact about ingredients, and evasive only when the talk turns to what he has given away. The Assize of Bread worries him sick.

**Sample lines.**

- "The penny is the same penny. Ask the wheat why the loaf is smaller."
- "The loaf's small because the wheat's dear, Will, and I'm not the villain of it."
- "When wheat stands at six-and-thirty shilling the quarter, the bailiff weighs my penny loaf like gold dust, and the poor curse me for an ounce short."

**He wants:** William to remember that a household eats daily, not quarterly.
**William wants:** uncomplicated local friendship.

**Arc.** In April their humour is easy. In the July fire a cousin's malt-barn is lost. In August the talk of grain has an edge: at dinner at the Sadlers' on Sun 10 Aug (W068) he sets a penny loaf on the board, unasked. William gives **20s** toward the cousin's barn and strains his back raising its new frame on Tue 12 Aug (W069). Sadler is neither a hoarder nor an emblem of virtue: he has to keep his trade alive.

**Relationships.** Judith Sadler shares and corrects his business judgments. Anne speaks to the Sadlers directly, not through William. He is fond of both twins and makes no more of the namesake than of the other.

**Voice casting.** A slow, deep, warm voice.

---

#### `judith_sadler` · Judith Sadler · 30s (model 33) · T2 · baker's wife; the twins' godmother

**Role and home.** The bakehouse on the High Street. She is quick where her husband is slow, brisk and amused, and able to carry on an argument while working the dough.

**Sample lines.**

- "He has given you the account in flour. I will give it you in money."
- "Is it a gift, a loan or a purchase, Master Quiney? Say which aloud, the three of you, and then I'll cut the cake."
- "Sit, Will. You stand in my bakehouse like a man waiting to be paid for it."

**She wants:** fairness without theatrical generosity.
**William wants:** the welcome of an old acquaintance who needs no report on his success.

**Arc.** On Thu 7 Aug she insists the men distinguish a gift, a loan and a purchase, and her precision shapes the Quiney agreement.

**Relationships.** Close to Anne. Fond of both twins without treating either as special. More willing than her husband to say when a favour cannot be afforded.

**Voice casting.** A quick, bright, practical voice.

---

#### `abraham_sturley` · Abraham Sturley · c.40 · T2 (T1 later) · alderman, Quiney's ally

**Role and home.** Wood Street, Stratford. A scholarly alderman with an interest in malt. His 1598 letters to Quiney mention Shakespeare and money (T1 later).

**How he talks.** Precise, pious and Protestant, studded with Cicero and Scripture. He eyes William's stage-wealth with half-disapproving respect.

**Sample lines.**

- "*Dominus providebit*, Master William. Though if London hath ready money to put out at use, our burned tenements cry aloud for timber."
- "The preachers say the fire is a judgment on malting. Malt is the town's living, sir. Let them preach on what we should eat."
- "Tully says a man owes his country first. Stratford is a small country, and much owed."

**He wants:** money for Stratford's rebuilding and malting.
**William wants:** nothing much, and is wary of being enlisted.

**Arc.** He comes with Quiney to the Henley Street table on the evening of Tue 5 Aug (S080), for weight, and asks William which price for barley he has actually paid, for what, and where. The talk is of malt and the town's brief, **not of any house for William**.

**Relationships.** Quiney's ally and conscience. Cool toward John's old troubles.

**Voice casting.** A dry, precise, slightly nasal voice.

---

#### `bartholomew_hathaway` · Bartholomew Hathaway · age unknown · T2 · Anne's brother at Hewlands

**Role and home.** Hewlands farm, Shottery (`hewlands_shottery`). Later a churchwarden of Holy Trinity (T1, 1608). Used lightly.

**How he talks.** A farmer of few words who measures a man by what he grows.

**Sample lines.**

- "The ground's cold yet. You'll take a cup and go before the light does."
- "She never could abide being waited on, our Anne."
- "London. Well. Does it grow anything?"

**Wants and arc.** He wants his sister content and his rent paid. On the Shottery walk of Sat 5 Apr (W029) he gives them a cup and leaves them to the field path, which is where Anne asks her question. In August his harvest is thin, and he says so in four words.

**Relationships.** Anne's elder brother, and protective in his own silent way. Joan Hathaway, Anne's stepmother, is named offstage (`[ext]`).

**Voice casting.** A slow, broad countryman's voice.

---

#### `thomas_hobbins` · Thomas Hobbins `[invented]` · 49 · the Stratford carrier

**Role and home.** The carrier between the High Cross at Stratford and the Bell in Carter Lane, with a string of six packhorses. He lives on the Stratford end of the road and comes into the Bell **about every third Thursday**: 16 Jan, 6 Feb, 27 Feb, 20 Mar, 24 Apr, 15 May, 5 Jun, 26 Jun, 17 Jul, 11 Sep, 2 Oct, 23 Oct, 13 Nov, 4 Dec and 18 Dec. He makes one unscheduled arrival on **Mon 21 Jul** with Anne's fire letter. He brings letters, cheeses and gloves, and carries back money, ribbon and parcels: 2d a letter, 6d a parcel. (He replaces GPT's Roger Kelsett and Fable's Greenaway; canon § 3.2.)

**How he talks.** In distances and prices. Slow, weather-beaten and mud-spattered, patient with goods and protective of his beasts, less patient with anxious senders.

**Sample lines.**

- "Twelve hours to Wycombe if the way's dry."
- "Hock-deep in Tetsworth clay since Monday dawn, Master Shakespeare, and thy mistress's cheeses took never a dent."
- "I can carry the letter. I cannot make her read what you meant instead."
- "Twopence the letter, sixpence the parcel, and nothing at all for what you shout after me down the yard."

**He wants:** prompt silver pence, light bundles, clear directions and properly tied parcels.
**William wants:** a reliable lifeline home.

**Arc.** His arc is the growing exactness of their dealings: over the year the add-ons shouted after him across the Bell's yard give way to written lists and receipts. He carries the Candlemas groats north, Mary's worsted hose south in March, the summer's quarter money on 26 Jun and Anne's fire letter on 21 Jul. On the return of Mon 18 – Thu 21 Aug William walks beside his horses at their pace by the Oxford road, riding the spare pack-nag for the first stage because of his back (W073a–d). He hauls the Christmas pack north from 18 Dec.

**Relationships.** Anne is his clearest customer. Quiney gives him too many errands. Gilbert knows his horses by name. When a parcel is heavy enough to bring to St Helen's himself, Mistress Ansell feeds him in her kitchen and they talk prices.

**Voice casting.** A slow, gravelly countryman's voice.

---

### 3.3 The company

#### `richard_burbage` · Richard Burbage · 26 → 27 (bapt. 7 Jul) · T1 payee · leading player, sharer

**Tier.** Named payee on the March warrant (T1). Leading player and sharer (T2). That he plays Richard II is T3; Berowne and Romeo are T5.

**Role and home.** The family house in Holywell Street by the Theatre (T2). Younger son of James. Parts: Berowne, Richard, Romeo.

**How he talks.** Physical, generous and hot. He thinks with his hands and tests a sentence by speaking it, not by discussing it. Impatience shows as movement: standing, taking an object, trying an entrance. He is his father's son in a fight: in 1590 he took a broom-staff to a man in the Theatre yard (T1, hostile depositions). Once in a part he stays in it for weeks ("a delightful Proteus", Flecknoe, T4): he orders ale as Richard II all autumn and woos the Jackdaw's hostess as Romeo in December. He paints, as a hobby (T2 later `[ext]`). Oaths: "'Swounds", "'Sblood", "God's my life", "By this hand".

**Sample lines.**

- "Give me the long one again. No, don't read it, *give* it to me."
- "What is he doing while he says it?"
- "He's a weak king. I'll not play him weak. I'll play him a strong man at being weak."
- "You have given me a crown and then denied me a head to put it on."
- "Ned Alleyn has six foot of lung and a brass throat. Give me not louder thunder than his. Give me a man thinking out loud while his hands shake."
- (As Richard, to a tapster) "We are amazed, and thus long have we stood to watch the fearful bending of thy knee."

**He wants:** the next great part, sooner; parts big enough to change the temperature of an audience, and to eclipse Alleyn by life, not volume; protection for his father's playhouse.
**William wants:** to hear the lines alive, a brother, discipline, and the courage to make a speech less beautiful when the scene needs it.

**Arc.**

- *Winter:* Berowne, wanting to dominate the comedy's language. On Sat 11 Jan he hears Act 5 at the Jackdaw and says nothing for a full minute (W004).
- *Spring:* he reads Richard's scenes as they come, from Fri 25 Apr (W036), and learns to make the loss of command visible.
- *Summer:* his birthday supper on Mon 7 Jul (W055).
- *September:* the rehearsal of Thu 11 Sep (S093) turns when William stops explaining the king and asks him to listen to another actor.
- *2 October:* Richard, and the town talks of him.
- *December:* late on Fri 5 Dec he calls because he cannot sleep, wants one line changed, and is refused. On Sat 6 Dec he plays Romeo at 27 opposite a boy whose voice may break, and carries him through by sheer attention. At Canon Row on Tue 9 Dec both men feel the danger and pleasure of a powerful listener who gives nothing away.

**Relationships.** His father's fights are his, and he loves and quarrels with James. He depends on Cuthbert's management. He treats Kemp as weather, respecting his hold on a crowd and resenting its independence. He adores Phillips. Tipping forgives him everything. Cowley is less dazzled by him than he would like.

**Voice casting.** Strong, bright, flexible, musical baritone; the only voice in the company allowed to be big.

---

#### `will_kemp` · Will Kemp · model 35 · T1 · clown, sharer, first name on the warrant

**Tier.** Named first of the three payees in 1595 (T1). Played with Leicester's Men in the Netherlands and at Elsinore, 1585–86 (T1). Nashe called him "vice-gerent general to the ghost of Dick Tarlton" (T1). The friction with William over clowns who speak more than is set down is T3 colour, used knowingly. **No later departure from the company is foreshadowed.**

**Role and home.** Star clown, jig-maker and sharer. Hired rooms in Shoreditch or Bishopsgate Without; no fixed address. Parts: Costard, Peter, and nothing at all in *Richard II*. He owns the jig after the play.

**How he talks.** In public a breezy, boastful, rhyme-tagged patter (after his *Nine Daies Wonder*, T1 later), and he talks to a room of four as if to two thousand. In private he is sharper and more tired, and can sound foolish while making the most accurate point in the room. He calls himself "Cavaliero Kemp". He defends his right to extemporise with the groundlings. Oaths: "By my hose", "marry", "i' faith", "by'r lakin". He tells the story of his dog at Greenwich last Christmas; the dog is a story only and never appears.

**Sample lines.**

- "Set down, set down. The groundlings never paid a penny to hear what was set down. Give me sixteen lines of ink and I'll give you back forty laughs."
- "A king play! With no fool in it! Who's to tell them when to breathe?"
- "You have left me three lines and a door. I shall require a better door."
- "Pay the woman. Then tell me what we cannot afford."
- "I was first on the Queen's paper, sweet Master Shakespeare. First. Read it again."
- (Sat 22 Nov, the first *thou*) "An ass's head? With long ears that wag on a string? Now thou talk'st like a Christian playwright!"

**He wants:** room; respect for the audience's part in the play; no assumption that laughter is an inferior craft; top billing and ready money; and to be Tarlton's heir, which a playwright's theatre will not let him be.
**William wants:** Kemp's genius without Kemp's additions; his crowd-magnetism harnessed inside the play instead of wrecking its tragic scenes.

**Arc.** The year's comic war.

- *February:* Costard grows by eleven lines at the first performance (W015, Thu 20 Feb), and to twenty-four in two days (W016). Garnish tallies "Kemp's interest" in the margin of the book.
- *March:* on Ash Wednesday the Rose opens a new play, and he is bitter about the pennies left on the table. Wed 12 Mar (S025): he grumbles at a rewritten clown bit. Sat 15 Mar (S027): at the reckoning he argues that the Queen's reward should go "by desert", and is talked down.
- *Spring and summer:* Thu 8 May, he dances Phillips's jig of the slippers (W040). In June he is the one who insists that the seamstress should not finance the company's virtue.
- *August–October:* on Wed 27 Aug he finds no fool in *Richard II* (W076). He gets the jig and a grievance. From Thu 18 Sep he calls William "Sweet Master Shakespeare", daily, and by Sat 20 Sep it has stuck (W083).
- *November:* Wed 19 Nov, he reads Peter in *Romeo* and counts his lines aloud. **Sat 22 Nov (W097):** William brings him three pages of a weaver called Bottom. Kemp reads them standing, then sits, then says *thou* for the first time, and is plainly in love with the part.
- *All year:* Bartholomew Fair with William and Pope on Sun 24 Aug.

**Relationships.** Affectionate with the boys. A teasing rivalry with Burbage. Unexpectedly aligned with Heminges whenever a promised payment is being evaded. Cowley is his straight man. Tipping charges him double for spillage. Brambleby likes his custom and limits his noise. Mistress Ansell is wary of his visits, because objects in her house tend to change their purpose when he is there. Nashe is a verbal performer he respects for knowing he is performing.

**Voice casting.** Quick, nasal and musical; never still.

---

#### `john_heminges` · John Heminges · 28 → 29 (bapt. 25 Nov) · T1/T2 · sharer, man of account

**Tier.** Free of the Grocers from 1587 (T1). Sharer in 1595 (T2). The company's money man, which is T5 on later documented practice. Payee with Bryan for the Christmas 1595 court plays (T1; the warrant is dated 21 Dec 1596).

**Role and home.** St Mary Aldermanbury, within the walls (T2), with a growing family and Nick Sorrell, the leading boy, lodged in the house. He holds the eighth share, "the stock". Parts: Holofernes, York, Capulet.

**How he talks.** Steady, low, measured and itemising: "Item." He lets an inflated claim finish before naming the missing expense. He is not humourless. When money is in doubt he starts a sentence twice ("The— the reckoning stands thus"). That is the one light trace of the T4 "stuttering Heminges" ballad of 1613: never a conspicuous stammer, and not overused. He asks, now and then, for a *Love's Labour's Won*, which is a title only and which William has not written.

**Sample lines.**

- "Item, boat-hire. Item, the baskets. Item, a boy who ate four pies at the Queen's charge and three at ours."
- "The warrant has no weight until someone brings the coin."
- "We may be rich in April. It is March that requires settling."
- "I don't say we can't. I say show me where it's written that we can."
- "Show me not what the Rose took on Whit Monday. Show me what stays in the box after tallow, starch, Tilney's fee and the hired men's ten shillings."
- "Go home to your desk, Will. George and I will wait upon the Chamber clerks."

**He wants:** two plays a year on the day promised, with a part for every sharer; dates, completed copy, and accounts that tell hope from cash; civic respectability against the Mayor.
**William wants:** not to have to think about money, financial safety without being made to feel timid, and a truthful first listener.

**Arc.** He begins as the man the others allow to be tedious. Eight calls through the year (§ 7.3): among them the Princess's gown on Tue 4 Feb (W010), which becomes the 50s Cheapside mercer's debt, and the summer shares on Thu 17 Jul (W058). On Sat 15 Mar his line about the warrant opens the reckoning (S027). Through June and July his reserves become indispensable, and on Mon 7 Jul he makes the case against touring and wins (S072): play on while the Rose is dark, and rest in August. In autumn he backs paying for fresh copies, because the cheap alternative is eating the boys' nights. After Heneage dies he works out who pays at court (W090, Mon 20 Oct). He ends the year with the court paperwork outstanding and refuses to count it as received, which William notices, and copies, without rancour.

**Relationships.** Trusts Bryan's handling of great households. Relies on Phillips with the apprentices. Shares a mutual, unsentimental affection with William. Larkham trusts him to name a real payment date. Pope is loyal to him. Brambleby respects him.

**Voice casting.** Even, kind, mid-range; a careful man.

---

#### `augustine_phillips` · Augustine Phillips · 30s (model 34) · T2 · sharer, musician

**Tier.** Sharer in 1595 (T2). The jig "of the slippers" licensed in May 1595 comes from one unchecked secondary source (T2). His instruments are known from his later will (T1 later).

**Role and home.** Southwark, near St Saviour's (T2). Plays lute, cittern, bandore and bass viol, and sets the company's songs. Master of Sam Latchet, who lives in his house, and voice coach to Nick Sorrell. Parts: Boyet, Bolingbroke, Mercutio.

**How he talks.** Warm, musical and rhythmically patient. He rarely raises his voice, because people come nearer to hear. Protective of the boys' voices and of their meals.

**Sample lines.**

- "Sing it on the breath, Sam, not on the word. The word will come along."
- "Again, but save breath for the answer. The first line is not the whole play."
- "Hark to Nick's low notes, Will. Give him no shrieking at the tomb. Give him the bass viol's strings and he'll stop every breath in the house."
- "A new song is a new labour, Will. Somebody must learn it, and it is generally a boy who has not slept."
- (After Queen Mab, Tue 15 Jul) "Again."

**He wants:** the boys trained rather than merely used, the music treated as work, and richer music cues in the plays.
**William wants:** help in making language breathe.

**Arc.** He sets the cuckoo and the owl for *Love's Labour's Lost* in January and February. Thu 8 May, the jig of the slippers (W040). On Tue 15 Jul he hears the Queen Mab speech at the Jackdaw and says only "Again." He plays Mercutio, who dies on William's page on Sat 6 Sep. From September he teaches Nick to pitch Juliet lower as the voice goes, and after the crack of Fri 24 Oct he retunes him through November. In autumn he arranges the measure for the Capulet maskers. Across the year he becomes the man William consults before changing a young player's passage.

**Relationships.** Burbage adores him. A calm counterweight to Kemp. Sam's master and Nick's teacher. Sly is his stage opponent as Tybalt.

**Voice casting.** A warm, light tenor, half-sung even in speech.

---

#### `thomas_pope` · Thomas Pope · 30s–40s (model 38) · T2 · sharer

**Role and home.** Southwark (T2). Keeps the boy Arthur Meeks in his house. Big, comic, broad-shouldered and unembarrassable. Parts: Armado, the Gardener, and **the Nurse**, in a skirt.

**How he talks.** Booming and hearty, but he begins slowly and finishes decisively, and enjoys letting another man explain himself into difficulty. He is fond of fencing jargon (*punto reverso*, *stoccata*) and of roast pork.

**Sample lines.**

- "I've dandled worse. Give me the child and a cushion for my bosom."
- "I can speak it. I ask whether anyone will be listening."
- "Give me one good bout of rapier and dagger before the end, Will. My arm's stiff from playing Spanish braggarts."
- "A gardener, is it? Well. I'll prune you a kingdom and be home by four."

**He wants:** dependable parts, showy sword-work and meaty speeches, and proper credit for work that does not advertise itself as great acting.
**William wants:** a player who can ground a scene before its more brilliant people arrive.

**Arc.** Armado all winter. In *Richard II* his Gardener gives the play its plain ground. Sun 24 Aug: Bartholomew Fair with William and Kemp, roast pig and a cutpurse nearly had (S082). In autumn he helps the young players find ordinary household truth in *Romeo*, without becoming anyone's acting master. On Wed 19 Nov his Nurse reduces the first reading of *Romeo* to helplessness (W096).

**Relationships.** Loyal to Heminges. Enjoys Kemp but will not follow every improvisation. Gives Burbage unflattering but useful notes. Fences with Sly. Arthur's master.

**Voice casting.** A big, booming bass with a chuckle under it.

---

#### `george_bryan` · George Bryan · model 40 · T2 (T1 payee) · sharer

**Tier.** Sharer (T2). At Elsinore with Kemp in 1586 (T1/T2). Christmas 1595 court payee with Heminges (T1).

**Role and home.** The City, near Cripplegate (T5). Grave and courtly, he is the company's ambassador: he knows how to stand in a great chamber, and he carries its administrative errands in person. Parts: Sir Nathaniel, Northumberland, Friar Laurence.

**How he talks.** Economical, upright, unruffled, socially alert. He can repeat an instruction without implying that he agrees with it.

**Sample lines.**

- "In a presence chamber, lads, you bow to the chair even when the chair is empty."
- "They ask for readiness. They have not yet named an hour."
- "At Richmond keep your eyes below Her Grace's canopy till she laughs first. I have seen taller men than us turn to salt."
- "At Elsinore the King's men drank our health from a cup you could have bathed Sam in, and paid us in compliments."

**He wants:** the company to tell a great household's request from a promise of payment; court favour and steady savings.
**William wants:** access to court arrangements without the humiliation of waiting in every doorway himself.

**Arc.** He coaches the company for Canon Row on Tue 9 Dec and for the court rehearsal before Tilney on Tue 16 Dec. At Richmond, 24–29 Dec, he shares a chamber at the Feathers with Heminges and William and keeps the boys' eyes below the canopy. He and Heminges are the payees, and they will not be paid until the next December.

**Relationships.** Works closely with Heminges. Has a wary, practical understanding with Hunsdon's servants, Kettleby above all. Does not pretend that being near the court makes him a courtier.

**Voice casting.** A grave, polished baritone.

---

#### `richard_cowley` · Richard Cowley · model 27 · T2/T3 · hired player, **not a sharer**

**Role and home.** Shoreditch (T2). A hired player at 10s a week, halved to 5s through the dark weeks of August. A deadpan physical comedian and Kemp's indispensable straight man. Parts: Dull, Aumerle, Benvolio. He covers William on the worst days of the cold and the tooth.

**How he talks.** A high, earnest, worried voice that makes plain lines funny without trying. Anxious about his lines, and dry, occasionally aggrieved, and exact about what a principal said he would do.

**Sample lines.**

- "I have learned your part. You may now recover at your leisure."
- "If Master Kemp hits me with the bladder before my cue, Master Shakespeare, do I say my line from the rushes or get up first?"
- "You said Tuesday, Master Shakespeare. You said it on the stair, with your hat on."

**He wants:** reliability, payment for work others treat as incidental, and a half-share some day when times mend.
**William wants:** him available as cover, and learns not to describe that availability as good fortune.

**Arc.** He covers in January and October. He takes the August half-wage without complaint. In December William thanks him with a concrete service: a copied and corrected part delivered before it is needed. In William's November notes for the *Dream*, a thin worried tailor has Cowley's voice (T5, notes only).

**Relationships.** Friendly with Pope. Protective of the hired men. Larkham is his ally. Less dazzled by Burbage than Burbage would prefer.

**Voice casting.** A high, earnest, worried tenor.

---

#### `will_sly` · William Sly · age unknown · T3 for 1595 · player

**Role and home.** London. With the company by 1598 (T1 later); his membership in 1595 is T3. Young, handsome, and quick with a foil. Parts: Longaville, Mowbray, Tybalt.

**How he talks.** Brief. He would rather show you than tell you.

**Sample lines.**

- "Give me the ground, Master Shakespeare, and I'll give you the fight. Words are for after."
- "Tybalt says little and means it. I like him."
- "He steps in under the arm, so. Stand off, Sam, or thou'lt lose an ear."

**Wants and arc.** He wants a fight in every play. William wants a duel the yard believes. Mowbray in October. Tybalt from 6 Dec, and the street brawls of *Romeo* are worked out with him and Phillips.

**Relationships.** Fences with Pope. The boys copy his footwork.

**Voice casting.** A light, clipped young baritone.

---

#### `john_sincler` · John Sincler ("Sinklo") · model 29 · T2–T3 · hired man

**Role and home.** Bishopsgate Without (T5). The thin one, named in speech prefixes of the Shakespeare texts and cast whenever a walking famine is wanted. Part: the Apothecary.

**How he talks.** Dry and cheerfully macabre about his ribs. He eats like a horse without gaining an ounce.

**Sample lines.**

- "Write me a starved apothecary, Will, and save the company twopence in padding. My collar-bones play the part before I open my mouth."
- "Pad Master Pope, pad Master Kemp. Me you may hang on a peg and call a cloak."
- "Another pie, Mistress Tipping. It goes nowhere, but it goes gladly."

**Wants and arc.** Steady wages in a dear-bread year, and parts cut to his silhouette. The tireman pads everyone but him, which is a running joke. The Apothecary is written to his shape in the autumn, and the *Dream* notes have thin men in them.

**Relationships.** The hired men's wit. Tipping feeds him out of curiosity.

**Voice casting.** A dry, reedy voice.

---

#### `hired_men` · William Blackwage, Ralph Raye, Richard Hoope · ages unknown · T1 debts / T3 acting

**Role and home.** Hired men of the company, paid 10s a week (T1, a 1597 analogue), halved to 5s in August. Their small debts to Henslowe are T1; their acting in 1595 is T3. They play messengers, soldiers, servants and crowds.

**How they talk.** As an ensemble: gossip in the tiring-house, complaints about wages, jokes at one another.

**Sample lines.**

- (Raye) "I owe Henslowe five shillings, aye. A man must bank somewhere."
- (Blackwage, to Raye) "He banks across the water. We bank at the Jackdaw."
- (Hoope) "Three halberds and two of us. Who's the third soldier, the ladder?"

**Wants and arc.** Their wages, paid on time, and a speaking part now and then. Raye is teased all year for "banking across the water". In August they are the first to feel the half-wage.

**Relationships.** Cowley protects them. Garnish counts them as entrances and despairs.

**Voice casting.** Three distinct stock voices, rough to smooth.

---

#### `humphrey_larkham` · Humphrey Larkham `[invented]` · 29 · hired player, gatherer, copyist

**Role and home.** A room in Shoreditch. Hired player, gatherer when one is needed, and second copyist under Garnish. Never a sharer.

**How he talks.** Matter-of-fact and mildly sardonic. He spells out figures aloud as he checks them.

**Sample lines.**

- "If the speech is longer, the paper is longer. I have not found a cheaper rule."
- "Four-and-twenty, five-and-twenty. Five-and-twenty lines, Master Shakespeare, and a half, if the half is a word."
- "Enter it where the error was, not where it was found. Else next week we shall find it twice."

**He wants:** wages, clean copy, and an end to invisible extra work.
**William wants:** someone who can repair the practical consequences of a late revision.

**Arc.** He and Garnish agree with William a set of part-copy marks, plain identifying marks on each copy (not modern version numbers). In a late-Lent rehearsal two players turn out to hold different versions of a cue, and William finds his own correction on the wrong copy. In the counting of the gatherers' money he traces a discrepancy to a hurried transfer, not theft, and insists the correction be entered where the error occurred. He shows William how one late change runs through several parts. From Sun 19 Oct the fair bundle of *Romeo* is tied with a red thread (G080). The autumn copying dispute ends with extra paid work approved by Heminges, not with a sentimental thank-you.

**Relationships.** Cowley is his ally. Heminges is the man he trusts to name a payment date. Nick dislikes his corrections until one saves an entrance. He works the stair beside Pell when he gathers.

**Voice casting.** A flat, dry, counting voice.

---

#### `nick_sorrell` · Nick Sorrell `[invented]` · 15 · leading boy

**Role and home.** Bound to Heminges and lodged at Aldermanbury. Small, dark-eyed and small-boned, with a fierce concentration. Parts: the Princess of France, Richard's Queen, Juliet. He has played queens since he was eleven. (Merged with GPT's Nicholas Fennell and Gemini's Robin Fenn; canon § 3.3.)

**How he talks.** Grave, exact and proud of his craft, and proud above all of never dropping a line. Quiet offstage. Careful and controlled in rehearsal, quicker and less ceremonious with Sam, whom he protects while pulling rank on him. From September his voice begins to roughen, which gives his lower register a husky catch. No caricatured falsetto.

**Sample lines.**

- "I can hold it if I come in under the note, sir. If I come in on it, it goes."
- "Will she be long, Master Shakespeare? The lady. Will there be much more of her?"
- "I understand the sorrow. It is the entrance I do not understand."
- "If I fall there, the stool is beneath me."
- "I will not gabble the potion speech, Master Shakespeare. Feel my wrist. I'm not afraid of the vault, only of Master Kemp coughing in the tiring-house."

**He wants:** challenging work, to carry a whole tragedy before his beard comes in, and not to be spoken of as an interchangeable voice.
**William wants:** his emotional directness, and at first overestimates how much can be demanded without explanation.

**Arc (the ticking clock).** A winter correction makes him cautious as the Princess. Spring training restores his confidence. In autumn he has grounds to challenge William's phrasing, and does. His voice is sound to Thu 23 Oct. **Fri 24 Oct (W091):** it cracks on the potion speech in an empty rehearsal. Through November Phillips retunes him. **Sat 6 Dec:** he gets through Juliet at the Theatre, with Burbage carrying him. **Sat 27 Dec:** he plays her at Richmond. **Sun 28 Dec:** in the jig after *Errors* the voice drops for good, mid-song, before the Queen; he bows, and the hall laughs kindly. After that he does not sing. On 31 Dec William notes that the next play wants a different music. The change is accommodated, gentle and a little funny, never a catastrophe. In William's *Dream* notes a small fierce girl stands against a tall one: Nick against Arthur.

**Relationships.** Burbage carries him on stage. Phillips trains him. Heminges is his master. He protects Sam, rivals Arthur, dislikes Larkham's corrections, and is envied by Edmund at a distance.

**Voice casting.** A light, controlled boy's voice, roughening from autumn; never shrill.

---

#### `arthur_meeks` · Arthur Meeks `[invented]` · 14 · second boy

**Role and home.** Lodges with Pope in Southwark. Dark, sardonic and a mimic, and tall and shooting up, all wrists and ankles, so that his hems are let down month by month. Parts: Rosaline, the Duchess of York, Lady Capulet. He is being readied for Nick's parts and knows it. (Merged with Gemini's Kit Warden.)

**How he talks.** Good-humoured about his shins and sharp about everyone else. He does Heminges, Garnish and Kemp to the life, out of their hearing.

**Sample lines.**

- "Edith let the hem down two fingers at Easter, sir, and my shins are out in the wind again. Make the next lady tall and angry about it!"
- (As Heminges) "Item, one boy, grown. Item, no skirt to fit him."
- "When Nick's voice goes, do I get the Queen or only her gown?"

**He wants:** to graduate to young men's sword parts next year before he trips over another farthingale.
**William wants:** a second lady who can carry a scene alone.

**Arc.** Rosaline in winter. The Duchess of York in October. Lady Capulet in December. Edith lets down his hems at Easter, and Mistress Pope lets them down twice more after Michaelmas, until by Tue 25 Nov there is no more hem (W414). His height against Nick's smallness is a running comedy in the tiring-house, and in November it turns up in the *Dream* notes.

**Relationships.** Pope's boy. Nick's rival and friend. Sam's tormentor and protector. Edith's best customer.

**Voice casting.** A dry boy's voice, already lower than Nick's.

---

#### `sam_latchet` · Sam Latchet `[invented]` · 12 · the small boy

**Role and home.** Bound to Phillips and lives in his Southwark house. Sings like a bird and forgets where to stand. His troubles are ordinary: shoes, sleepiness, a lost cue, a costume a size too tight, and a strong wish for a second helping. Parts: Moth, Juliet's page. William begins to write Puck with Sam's feet in mind. (Merged with GPT's Toby Bellam.)

**How he talks.** Quick, clear, and impatient to show he has understood.

**Sample lines.**

- "I can remember it better when everyone stops telling me I shall forget."
- "Is there more pie? I am not hungry. I only ask if there is."
- "Master Phillips, the doublet's eating me."
- (Richmond) "It was gold, Master Shakespeare, with pearls on. I saw it go. It went under the rushes."

**He wants:** a useful entrance and permission to ask questions.
**William wants:** obedience at first, and comes to value the questions.

**Arc.** From an over-tight costume in the winter to a court part he has learned securely by December. Edith adjusts the fastening he cannot reach. On Sat 27 Dec at Richmond he searches the rushes for the Queen's lost gold button, set with five pearls (T1 loss), and does not find it.

**Relationships.** Admires Nick and competes with him. Trusts Edith's seams more than an actor's promise. Phillips is his master and his care.

**Voice casting.** A high, clear, quick child's voice; sings well.

---

#### `abel_garnish` · Abel Garnish `[invented]` · 53 · book-keeper

**Role and home.** Curtain Road, Shoreditch. Holder of the book, copier of the parts, maker of the plot that hangs on its peg in the tiring-house. He keeps Tilney's licensed books like relics. (Merged with Gemini's Peter Bramwell.)

**How he talks.** In corrections, fussy, whispered and ink-smudged, with pins in his lapel. He loves William's clean copy and loathes Kemp's margins.

**Sample lines.**

- "You have Navarre exit at the hundred-and-tenth line and speak at the hundred-and-twelfth. Shall he shout it from the stairs?"
- "Give me clean entrances in the left margin, Master Will. 'Enter two or three' tells me naught when six hired men are eating herring behind the arras."
- "Kemp's interest this week: fourteen lines. Last week eleven. Master Kemp is a growing concern."
- "A fair copy three weeks before, or I prompt from your foul papers and God help the King of Navarre."

**He wants:** fair copies three weeks before an opening, with no tavern blots.
**William wants:** a book that never fails a player in the dark.

**Arc.** He takes the book of *Love's Labour's Lost* on Tue 21 Jan (W007) and gives out the parts on Tue 28 Jan. The tally of "Kemp's interest", each added line ticked in the margin and totalled at the week's end, runs from February to November and stops, without comment, after Kemp reads Bottom. He copies all three new plays into parts, with Larkham as second hand, and keeps the three Richmond nights moving without a missed cue.

**Relationships.** Kemp's natural enemy and secret admirer. Larkham's master in copying. Sincler and the hired men are his "six men behind the arras".

**Voice casting.** A dry, precise, whispering older voice.

---

#### `margery_pell` · Goodwife Margery Pell `[invented]` · 47 · gatherer at the gallery stair

**Role and home.** She takes the gallery money at the stair, knows every face and what it paid, and reports the house in one line. She is the company's best intelligence on the City's mood in June.

**Sample lines.**

- "Lawyers, 'prentices, three bad women and a bishop's man. Forty-one shillings in my box."
- "Quiet house. Too many caps pulled down. I don't like a quiet house in June."
- "The Inns men are back, Master Shakespeare. You can hear their Latin on the stair."

**Wants and arc.** She wants a full stair and no trouble on it. She counts the boxes with the sharers on Sat 22 Feb (S117). In June she reads the yard for the company. On Thu 9 Oct she hears the Inns men come back for *Richard* (S096).

**Relationships.** Larkham gathers beside her. Heminges trusts her count.

**Voice casting.** A flat, carrying, unimpressed voice.

---

#### `edith_kettlewood` · Edith Kettlewood `[invented]` · 27 · seamstress and mender to the company

**Role and home.** A rented room in Shoreditch (no separate location point). An independent seamstress who supplies and mends for the company.

**How she talks.** Brisk and precise, with a silence that makes men hear their own foolishness.

**Sample lines.**

- "I have made him a prince. The prince still owes eight shillings."
- "Do the gesture again, Master Burbage. Slowly. There. That is a yard of cloth you have just spent."
- "Payment on delivery. The weather is your trouble, Master Shakespeare, not my thread's."

**She wants:** payment, usable measurements, and players who do not tear a garment and call it passion.
**William wants:** economy, and transformations made from finite cloth.

**Arc.** January's unpaid **8s** is an early test of the company's priorities, and the running joke of the prince who still owes Edith for his sleeves. It is paid on Thu 20 Mar from the stock share, not from William's purse. In June she refuses more unsecured work and asks for payment on delivery, and Kemp backs her. She lets down Arthur's hems. In December she adjusts the fastening Sam cannot reach.

**Relationships.** Kemp supports her demands. Sam trusts her. James believes she uses too many pins until she shows him why.

**Voice casting.** A brisk, clear young woman's voice.

---

#### `james_burbage` · James Burbage · c.64 · T1/T2 · owner of the Theatre

**Role and home.** Holywell Street, Shoreditch (`burbage_house_holywell`), whose hall is where the sharers meet out of season and where the £20 is split. A joiner by trade, once Leicester's man, builder of the Theatre in 1576 (T1). He leased the ground from Giles Allen on 13 April 1576 for 21 years, so the lease runs out in April 1597 (T1). In 1584 he refused the Recorder's summons, standing on being Lord Hunsdon's man (T1).

**How he talks.** Gravelly, blunt, stubborn and litigious; a builder who speaks of rights as though they were timber lengths. Every conversation bends to the lease, Giles Allen or timber, and he curses Allen every third breath. He smells of oak shavings and law-parchment. Oaths: "God's body", and "By the mass", an old habit from Queen Mary's days.

**Sample lines.**

- "Twenty-one years, and two to run, and he wants the house when the ground falls in. My house! I'll carry it off on my back first."
- "I raised every post of this house with mine own adze, and Giles Allen shan't have a splinter of it without law to the teeth."
- "A man may argue with me. He shall not argue my wall off its ground."
- "There's a great stone hall in the Blackfriars would hold a winter. I only say there is one."

**He wants:** to beat Allen, to keep his building valuable and his son indispensable, and a roofed winter house.
**William wants:** a workplace that is not forever turning into a lawsuit in James's head.

**Arc.** In February he frets over rotting sill-timbers. The lease darkens every meeting. He repaints in the August break. On Wed 3 Dec, at the dress rehearsal, he first speaks of the Blackfriars hall (W099), and in mid-December he confides the plan and its £600 to William (both T5, ahead of the T1 purchase of February 1596; nothing is bought in 1595). In December he concedes that one repair can wait until the company is back from court.

**Relationships.** Affectionate combat with Richard. Trusts Cuthbert with documents while claiming not to need help. Sees Kemp as a valuable disturbance. Thinks Edith uses too many pins. Ellen Burbage, "Mistress Burbage", keeps the kitchen and is named offstage.

**Voice casting.** Gravel, impatient; an old man who has shouted at carpenters for forty years.

---

#### `cuthbert_burbage` · Cuthbert Burbage · c.30 · T2 · the business son

**Role and home.** Holywell, Shoreditch. Elder son of James, not a player. He manages gatherers, repairs and the legal letters, and is the family's envoy to Giles Allen and the lawyers.

**How he talks.** Restrained, courteous, lawyerly; dry enough to be mistaken for cold. He tells his father no by describing the consequences, not the prohibition.

**Sample lines.**

- "My father says he'll pull the house down. I say let's first ask what the timber's worth."
- "My father has agreed in all respects except the agreement."
- "My father would answer Allen with a crowbar and Dick with a broom-staff. Somebody in Holywell must write civil English."

**He wants:** a peaceful renewal of the lease or an orderly way out; decisions that can be put in writing.
**William wants:** access to James without having to fight him.

**Arc.** Four calls. **Sat 10 May (W041):** Allen's demand of £24 a year against the present £14. Tue 17 Jun (W137): the house in the riots. Sat 13 Sep (W335): the Mayor's letter. Fri 12 Dec (W417): the Blackfriars hall. He begins as a negotiator of repairs and ends as a quiet advocate for keeping company cash in hand. He is not a villain skimming the profits.

**Relationships.** Devoted to Richard without his appetite for performance. Allen's only correspondent in the family.

**Voice casting.** A cool, even, lawyerly baritone.

---

#### `giles_allen` · Giles Allen · c.60 · T1 · ground landlord of the Theatre

**Role and home.** A gentleman landlord whose ground the Theatre stands on. **William sees him once, at the ditch, and never speaks with him.** His demands reach William only through Cuthbert. To James he is a name to spit.

**How he talks (as Cuthbert reports him).** Pious, icy and legalistic. He smiles while he raises the rent and threatens to put the timber to "better uses".

**Sample lines (reported).**

- "A lease of one-and-twenty years hath an end, like all earthly vanities, Master Burbage."
- "Truly the parish complains sore of your trumpets."
- "Four-and-twenty pound, and the house to me at the term's end. I think it very reasonable."

**Wants and arc.** He wants £10 more a year and control of the building. His screws tighten in spring (the £24 demand, reported Sat 10 May) and drive James toward the Blackfriars plan by December.

**Voice casting.** If ever heard at a distance: thin, polite and cold.

---

### 3.4 Patrons, court and civic

#### `henry_carey_hunsdon` · Henry Carey, 1st Lord Hunsdon · 68 → 69 (4 Mar) · T1 · patron, Lord Chamberlain

**Role and home.** The Queen's cousin and Lord Chamberlain since 1585 (T1). Keeper of Somerset House (`somerset_house`, never entered); also at Blackfriars and at court. His letter of 8 October 1594 asked the Mayor to let "my nowe companie" play at the Cross Keys that winter (T1). **He is hale and loud all year, and nothing hints otherwise.**

**How he talks.** Naunton's portrait (T4): downright, loud and loyal, "his custom of swearing and obscenity in speaking made him seem a worse Christian than he was". Short soldier's sentences. He hates Latin and flattery equally. Oaths: "God's wounds", "God's death", "by God's body", "body o' me", "a pox on it". He laughs like a cough.

**Sample lines.**

- "God's wounds, man, don't *my-good-lord* me by the yard. Have you a play or no?"
- "I asked whether you could play it, not whether you could explain why you could not."
- "The Mayor's a ropemaker's conscience in a furred gown. Let him write. I answer to one woman, and she likes a play."
- "No deposing. Not in that house, not this winter. Kill your king offstage and bring her something to laugh at."
- "And Shakespeare. Clean hose. All of you. Last year one of your fellows had a hole in his heel a man could have put a fist through, and I saw her see it."

**He wants:** comedies that please the Queen, no Privy Council trouble, and his name worn decently.
**William wants:** the one shield that makes a player not a vagabond. The year teaches him that protection is not immunity from every civic complaint.

**Arc.** Mostly a presence through Kettleby. After the Mayor's letter he sends a blunt "play on" (W081, Tue 16 Sep). He has seen *Richard II* at the Theatre (T5). **Fri 7 Nov (S104):** his one call, commanding three nights at Richmond, with the joke about the hose. Through his son-in-law Hoby he summons *Richard* to Canon Row for Tue 9 Dec. No wedding hint and no commission for the *Dream*.

**Relationships.** Father of Margaret Hoby. Kettleby is his voice. Bryan knows his household's practical channels. William always observes rank with him.

**Voice casting.** A big, rough bass.

---

#### `ambrose_kettleby` · Ambrose Kettleby `[invented]` · 44 · gentleman usher to the Lord Chamberlain

**Role and home.** Hunsdon's household. The patron's voice on most calls. Smooth, exact, and faintly amused by players; a man who has stood behind chairs for twenty years.

**Sample lines.**

- "My lord bids me say (and I soften it) that he is content."
- "You will be sent for. Whether you will be *paid* for is a question for another office."
- "My Lord Treasurer has the gout, Master Shakespeare, and when he has it the realm limps."
- "Sir Edward Hoby will receive you on Tuesday. Bring the king. My lord bids me say: the whole king."

**Wants and arc.** He wants things done properly and without his master being troubled. Four calls: Tue 2 Sep, Burghley's gout (S087); Tue 16 Sep, "play on" (W081); Mon 20 Oct, the Christmas warrant (W405); Sun 7 Dec, the Hoby summons (W101).

**Relationships.** Bryan's opposite number. Kettleby and William like each other a little more than either shows.

**Voice casting.** A smooth, dry, faintly amused courtier's voice.

---

#### `henry_wriothesley_southampton` · Henry Wriothesley, 3rd Earl of Southampton · 21 → 22 (6 Oct) · T1 / contact T3 · dedicatee of both poems

**Tier.** The dedications of 1593 and 1594 are T1. Any 1595 contact is T3, used as the story's choice and kept thin. **Intimacy is never stated, and no sonnet is ever addressed or delivered to him.**

**Role and home.** Southampton House, by Chancery Lane in Holborn (`southampton_house`), where William gets no further than the porter's lodge, and the court. Burghley's former ward, said to have been heavily fined for refusing the de Vere match (T1/T2 for the refusal; the £5,000 figure is an unchecked talking point). A follower of Essex. Courting Elizabeth Vernon by September (T1, Whyte). His stepfather Heneage dies on 17 Oct.

**How he talks.** Young, quick, cultivated and fashionable, used to time being made for him. Impulsive, kind without thinking and forgetful in the same way, switching in a heartbeat from warmth to stung hauteur. He treats debt as weather and love as a charge down the tiltyard. Not a languid caricature.

**Sample lines.**

- "Master Shakespeare! They tell me you've left off poems for players. Is it so? I call that a desertion."
- "Florio preaches thrift to me in three tongues, and my Lord Treasurer counts my manors. Come, have you turned wholly player for sixpences?"
- "You have brought me a question when I had hoped for a compliment."
- "Have you seen Mistress Vernon when she turns her head in the gallery? Tell me not of caution. Write me men who throw their caps over the windmill."
- "I'm to run at the tilt on the Queen's day. Come and see me unhorsed."

**He wants:** admiration, distinction in Essex's circle, Elizabeth Vernon without losing the Queen's favour, and William's pen when he snaps his fingers.
**William wants:** continued regard, and perhaps more than he can safely name even to himself. What he gives is deference, real warmth, and a growing private sorrow at watching a golden youth spend himself carelessly.

**Arc (four touches only).**

1. *Winter and spring:* silence. In February William leaves a paper for Florio at the lodge and hears nothing; Florio is the go-between.
2. **Tue 23 Sep (S091):** one call, on the day Whyte writes of him and Mistress Vernon. The earl is happy and distracted, and asks for "something for a lady", which William understands is for another man's love. The call is short enough to show their unequal command of time.
3. **Mon 6 Oct:** the birthday William does not mark (W087).
4. **Mon 17 Nov:** seen in armour across the tiltyard (S106), far off.

**Rule.** No source says what William feels beyond gratitude, dazzle and a private sting. The year's sonnets have no addressee. Always "my lord".

**Relationships.** Florio serves and instructs him, and considers him gifted and distractible. His courtship is public news, not access to his private life.

**Voice casting.** A light, bright tenor, with laughter under it.

---

#### `john_florio` · John Florio · 41–42 · T1 tie to the earl / T3 contact · Southampton's Italian tutor

**Role and home.** Southampton's household (T1 from his 1598 dictionary; T3 for 1595). "Resolute John Florio" (T1), lexicographer and proverb-hunter, gathering words for his great dictionary.

**How he talks.** Proud, prickly and formal; a fountain of proverbs in two languages and an ornate cascade of synonyms in three. He can make a correction sound like a favour, and is touchy when players borrow his phrases without bowing. He keeps words on slips.

**Sample lines.**

- "*Chi va piano va sano.* Who goes softly goes safely, Master Shakespeare, and you go at a gallop."
- "Verona? I'll tell you Verona. But you'll put a sea-coast on it, I know you poets."
- "A word may cross the sea and still arrive badly dressed."
- "Mark this one for your slip-box: *puntiglio*, the nice point of honour on a rapier's tip. Your young Veronese will die of it."
- "They tell me there is a pedant in your new comedy. Does he speak Italian? No? Then he is not I."

**He wants:** recognition for learned work, patronage security, and no casual borrowing of his learning.
**William wants:** linguistic precision without sitting an examination, and Verona's local colour.

**Arc.** Three calls, with no meeting. Tue 18 Mar (W120): the paper William left at the lodge has been received, and nothing more. Fri 11 Jul (W302): Verona, and Italian colour for *Romeo*. **Fri 17 Oct (S098):** Heneage is dead. He is not the man who secretly teaches William all of Italy.

**Relationships.** Southampton's tutor and gatekeeper. Finds William quick enough to be both gratifying and troublesome.

**Voice casting.** A precise, ornate, slightly accented voice; never a caricature.

---

#### `elizabeth_i` · Queen Elizabeth · 61 → 62 (7 Sep) · T1 · the court's audience

**Role and home.** The court: Greenwich, Whitehall and Richmond in the source book's itinerary, at Richmond from 23 Dec. She watched the Christmas 1594 plays at Greenwich (T1 payment / T2 venue). **She is seen, almost never heard.** No invented intimate monologue, no private friendship with the playwright, and no access to her thoughts.

**How she talks.** Measured and exact, used very sparingly.

**Sample lines.**

- "Proceed."
- (To a lady beside her, overheard) "The small one sings well."
- (After the voice breaks, 28 Dec; the hall laughs, and so does she) "Let him bow. He has earned the bow."

**Wants and arc.** She wants service, entertainment and proper order. William wants approval, payment and the reassurance of having been seen. She is an absent authority in January's unpaid account, a distant figure at the tilt on Mon 17 Nov, and a visible but unreadable audience at Richmond on 26–28 Dec, where a gold button set with five pearls is lost from her kirtle "at a play at Richmond" (T1), searched for and never found. Her birthday bells ring on Sun 7 Sep.

**Relationships.** Hunsdon's patronage works inside her household. Cecil and Essex belong to a political field William cannot see.

**Voice casting.** A dry, clear older woman's voice, very still.

---

#### `edward_hoby` · Sir Edward Hoby · 34–35 · T1 · host at Canon Row

**Role and home.** Canon Row, Westminster (`canon_row`). A scholar-courtier and Hunsdon's son-in-law. On Sun 7 Dec 1595 he asks Sir Robert Cecil to supper on Tuesday, where "K. Richard [shall] present him selfe to your vewe" (T1). That this means *Richard II* is T3, the story's choice.

**How he talks.** Hospitable, assured, silky and watchful. He enjoys the daring of his own supper and is lightly proprietary about the experience he is arranging. He speaks in double meanings fit for Cecil's table.

**Sample lines.**

- "My father-in-law's men, Sir Robert. And a king, as I promised. He will present himself to your view."
- "We shall have room enough, provided no man brings his whole importance with him."
- "Sir Robert dines lightly and listens hard, Master Shakespeare. Give us King Richard whole, the parliament and all, after the marchpane."

**Wants and arc.** He wants an evening that displays taste and connection and pleases Cecil. William wants the performance valued without becoming an instrument he cannot control. Hoby's courtesy never quite answers how the entertainment will be used. Tue 9 Dec (S112a): the supper, *Richard* played whole for the only time, and a reward of £5 to the company (12s 6d a share).

**Relationships.** Margaret's husband. Hunsdon's son-in-law. Host to Cecil.

**Voice casting.** A smooth, cultivated baritone.

---

#### `margaret_hoby` · Margaret, Lady Hoby (née Carey) · 30s (model 36) · T1 · hostess

**Role and home.** Canon Row. Hunsdon's daughter and Hoby's wife. She makes the room work.

**How she talks.** Composed, practical and socially exact.

**Sample lines.**

- "The gentlemen may wait. The candles cannot."
- "Put the chair there, and the king may sit in it. My father's men know where to stand."
- "Supper, I think. The king has had his say."

**Wants and arc.** She wants an evening that works, not merely one her husband has imagined. William wants her clear instructions. On Tue 9 Dec she makes physical space for the performance, sets a real limit on delay, notices the labouring arrangements before the men discuss their meaning, and ends a dangerous drift of talk by turning to supper.

**Relationships.** Understands her father's bluntness and her husband's elaboration. Treats the players as skilled workers without pretending that rank has disappeared.

**Voice casting.** A clear, composed woman's voice.

---

#### `robert_cecil` · Sir Robert Cecil · 31 → 32 (1 Jun) · T1 · the still man by the fire

**Role and home.** Privy Councillor, of the court and London. Hoby's invited guest (T1); his attendance in the scene is T5.

**How he talks.** Quiet and precise; he asks little. His force lies in what other people supply to fill his silences.

**Sample lines.**

- "And which part do you mean to show us?"
- "Go on. I am listening."
- (The one courteous sentence at the end, a candidate for the halves) "You have shown us a king very exactly, Master Shakespeare. I shall remember which parts were shown."

**Wants and arc.** He wants information, and control of what an occasion signifies. William wants to finish the work without being made to explain his political soul. On Tue 9 Dec he sits small and still in a chair by the fire, watches a king unmake himself, and at the end says one courteous sentence that William turns over for a week (W102, memo, Thu 11 Dec). He is not villainous omniscience. He may simply be tired, attentive and hard to read, and William must not take the absence of objection for approval.

**Relationships.** Hoby wants to please him. Bryan wants the practical terms understood.

**Voice casting.** A quiet, light, precise voice.

---

#### `edmund_tilney` · Edmund Tilney · 58–59 · T1 · Master of the Revels

**Role and home.** St John's, Clerkenwell (`st_johns_clerkenwell`). Master of the Revels since 1579 (T1), who licenses the plays and selects the court's Christmas programme. His fee is 7s a play (T1 `[ext]`, from Henslowe's later payments).

**How he talks.** Courteous, bored, administrative and dangerous by habit; unastonished by artistic necessity. He reads with a pen in his hand and dips it in red ink before he speaks.

**Sample lines.**

- "It is a pretty play. The fourth act is prettiest where it is shortest."
- "You may require it for your purpose. I require to know what purpose it will be heard to serve."
- "Your King Richard talks very prettily of graves and worms, Master Shakespeare. Yet a crown does not come off an anointed head before penny prentices while the marshals ride the gates. Amend me this, and you shall have my hand for seven shillings."
- (His marginal note, T5 modelled on his T1 note on *More*) **"Leave out the deposing of the King in the Parliament, & only report it."**

**Wants and arc.** No Privy Council scandal, prompt fees, and three polished, safe plays for the Queen. William wants latitude without provoking a refusal. Three licensing visits, all T5: **Fri 24 Jan**, *Love's Labour's Lost* passes with two oaths trimmed (W008); **Thu 10 Jul**, *Richard II* passes with the deposition cut (W056); **Thu 13 Nov**, *Romeo* passes untouched, which William finds almost insulting (W094). And **Tue 16 Dec**, the court rehearsal before him (W104). These are negotiations over what is shown, never a heroic confrontation with censorship.

**Relationships.** Garnish carries the books to him. Heminges understands the fees. Burbage dislikes the cuts more openly than William can afford to.

**Voice casting.** A dry, unhurried, cultivated older voice.

---

#### `thomas_heneage` · Sir Thomas Heneage · c.63 · T1 · Treasurer of the Chamber

**Role and home.** The court, and the Savoy in the report of his death. His office made the payment of March 1595 (T1). He is Southampton's stepfather. **He dies on 17 October** (T1), and his widow submits his accounts. He is an offstage administrative presence, never a speaking acquaintance. If his name is read from a document, a present character reads it.

**Lines said about him.**

- (Florio, Fri 17 Oct) "Sir Thomas is dead, Master Shakespeare. This morning. My lord's mother is in black and my lord is in a temper."
- (Heminges, Mon 20 Oct) "The office does not die with the man. But the man signed, and now who signs?"
- (William, memo) "He paid us in March. I never saw his face."

**Arc.** First an office that processes money, later a death that reminds the company that offices are held by mortal men and paperwork can outlast them. It is why the Christmas money is uncertain (W090).

---

#### `robert_devereux` · Robert Devereux, Earl of Essex · 29 → 30 (10 Nov) · T1 · seen at the tilt; news

**Role and home.** Essex House on the Strand, and the court; heard of, never visited. His Accession Day device of 17 Nov 1595 is T1, and the Queen confronts him in November over the succession book dedicated to him (T1, from Whyte), which William hears of on Wed 5 Nov (S103a, S103b). **No captured dialogue.** He is public confidence seen from a distance and private embarrassment known through rumour.

**Lines said about him.**

- (Davy Gannet) "My lord of Essex has a book writ to him about who's to be king after, and the Queen's that angry the river's gone back up to Kingston."
- (Bryan) "A device at the tilt, they say, with a hermit and a secretary and a soldier. A good deal of speaking for a man on a horse."
- (William, memo) "A book named for him, and he never asked for it. Or so he says. …A deposing play feels warm in the hand this week."

**Wants and arc.** He wants royal favour and public distinction. William wants nothing from him except to understand how performed confidence differs from actual security. November's book and the tilt remain news and spectacle, not access to Essex's inner chamber.

**Relationships.** Southampton is his follower. William must never be made an intimate participant.

---

#### `john_spencer` · Sir John Spencer · c.58 (est.) · T1 · Lord Mayor to 28 Oct

**Role and home.** Crosby Place (`crosby_place`), a hundred yards from William's bed, in St Helen's parish. "Rich Spencer", Lord Mayor from October 1594 to October 1595, the target of the June riots, and author of the 13 September letter complaining that plays are "daily shewed at the Theator and Bankside" (T1). **He never speaks to William.** He is seen as a furred gown passing the church door, a coach, a lit window in the oriel, and his carved pew at St Helen's. He is heard directly only in church, giving the litany its responses from his pew, short and cold (W011, W079, W336).

**How he sounds.** Austere, magisterial, a merchant-autocrat's bark, and above all the voice of his paper: precepts read aloud by beadles and ministers, the letter to the Council. The lines below are invented and are heard only as precept or report, never in conversation with William.

**Sample lines (precept or report).**

- "The charge of the streets does not fall upon those who profit by gathering them."
- "Where drums beat at two of the clock, masterless men gather by four and break pillories by dusk."
- "The City is no nursery for stage-players."

**Wants and arc.** He wants the City governable, cheap-grain discipline, and every playhouse padlocked. William wants his trade not to be made the explanation of every urban trouble. There is no personal confrontation: proximity is the point. A distant neighbour in winter, and the Gray's Inn Prince rides past William's door to dine with him on Sat 4 Jan (W001). Easter communion three pews off on Sun 20 Apr. The blue-starch precept read on Sun 1 Jun. Under pressure in June: on Fri 27 Jun rioters set up a gallows at his gate, and the photo is the empty frame. The September letter (S088, Sat 13 Sep). He steps down on 28–29 Oct (S101), and the light in Crosby Place's oriel goes out. His going does not end the City's hostility. After him Sir Stephen Slany is Mayor, who is not a neighbour and not a character.

---

### 3.5 London household and the town

#### `dorcas_ansell` · Mistress Dorcas Ansell `[invented]` · 52 · his landlady

**Role and home.** A scrivener's widow who holds the lease of the narrow three-storey house on the north side of Great St Helen's close (`ansell_house`; canon § 5.1). She lets the upper front chamber to William at £4 a year (20s each quarter day, by noon) and the lower back room to Gillis Verbeke. Firing and washing are 6d a week from Michaelmas to Lady Day, board at her table 3d a meal, and candles are his own. She makes and sells oak-gall ink to her late husband's receipt, at 2d the horn. Callers are stopped at the foot of the stair. (She absorbs Gemini's Widow Kempster and GPT's Nettlefolds; canon § 3.5.)

**How she talks.** A sermon-goer who disapproves of plays in general and demands the plot of each in particular, then rules on it. Brisk, nosy and London-sharp. Suspicious of players, and fiercely defensive of "my Master Shakespeare, who makes less noise than a mouse and pays on quarter day before noon". She counts candles. She judges the penny loaf against a notch her husband cut in the bread-board in a good year. She asks her questions while carrying something. She is mildly legalistic about the house and proud of a useful distinction. "Mark me." "As I am a Christian woman."

**Sample lines.**

- "The loaf wants a full thumb of the notch. Mark me, by Michaelmas we'll be eating the board."
- "You burned three candles to the socket Tuesday. Three! I heard you walking. Was it the lovers again?"
- "I let you the room. I did not undertake that the weather should remain outside the stair."
- "Tallow's up a halfpenny the pound, and if you burn till two after midnight your eyes'll look like boiled gooseberries come Sunday."
- "If you will not eat it hot, I cannot promise to keep it patient."
- "That is not how you ask for bread." (At her window, Fri 27 Jun.)

**Her verdicts** (running joke 12; a new one for each play he tells her):

- *Love's Labour's Lost:* "Four young men swear off women and keep it till dinner. That I believe."
- *Richard II:* "A king put down by his cousin! And is the cousin punished? No? Then I don't call that a play, I call it a bad example."
- *Romeo and Juliet:* "She should have minded her mother."

**She wants:** rent on the quarter day by noon, quiet after nine, no candle accidents, no company men sleeping on her floor without agreement, the plots, and now and then some gossip about court ladies' ruffs.
**William wants:** ink, discretion, privacy, a little credit, and an audience of one who cannot be flattered, with the illusion that his late work disturbs nobody. She refuses that last contradiction without making a scene of it.

**Arc.** His first critic all year. She nurses his cold with a posset and a lecture (W006, Sun 19 Jan). In June she hides her Flemish lodger and makes William swear to say Verbeke is from Norwich (S058, Thu 12 Jun). On Fri 27 Jun, when the gallows goes up at the Mayor's gate, she stands at her window, says "That is not how you ask for bread," and shuts the shutter (S067). On Sun 29 Jun she bars the street door beside him (S069). In August she welcomes him back by handing him something to carry. At Martinmas, Tue 11 Nov, he helps carry in the household's winter stores. On Sat 6 Dec she comes to the Theatre for the first time in her life, sits in the gallery in her church hat, and will not discuss it until the next morning. On Mon 22 Dec she has the troublesome latch mended and does not charge for it, and he pays the Christmas rent early. Their December reconciliation is domestic, not emotional: he tells her before he changes his plans.

**Relationships.** Alse's mistress and Verbeke's protector. Gib's owner. Wary of Kemp's visits. Respects Field's trade. Feeds Hobbins when he comes to the house. Sits under Thwaite with a sharp face.

**Voice casting.** Brisk, carrying, mid-pitch; a woman used to being heard through a floor.

---

#### `alse_pinder` · Alse Pinder `[invented]` · 16 · the maid

**Role and home.** Mistress Ansell's maid, from Hertfordshire, sleeping in the closet off her mistress's chamber. She cannot read. Quick. She brings his water and washing, and lingers for a speech. She is the measure of whether a line lands on someone who has never seen a play.

**Sample lines.**

- "Is it true they're boys? All of them? Even the one that dies?"
- "Say the bit about the moon again, sir. Mistress says I'm to come down, but say it again."
- "At home we'd have the hog in by now. Here they buy it in pieces."

**Wants and arc.** She wants to see a play, and on Sat 6 Dec she is allowed to, from the lower gallery beside her mistress (W100; T5). She hears whole scenes through the floor all year and repeats them wrong to the water-carriers.

**Relationships.** Mistress Ansell's charge. Gib's enemy. Verbeke teaches her a Flemish counting-rhyme.

**Voice casting.** A young country girl's voice, quick and curious.

---

#### `gillis_verbeke` · Gillis Verbeke `[invented]` · 34 · Flemish silk-weaver, fellow lodger

**Role and home.** The ground-floor back room of `ansell_house`. A journeyman silk-weaver, a "stranger" from Flanders, working at a master's loom in Norton Folgate. Gentle and devout, with careful English. He carries the year's anti-alien fear (T1, 12 June). **He is never harmed.**

**Sample lines.**

- "In Ghent also they burned the houses of the wrong people. Always it is bread, and then it is us."
- "Norwich. Yes. I am from Norwich. I say it so: *Norwich*."
- "The shuttle goes, and comes, and goes. A weaver thinks with his feet, like you."

**Wants and arc.** He wants to work and be left alone. On Thu 12 Jun he is hidden in the lumber garret over William's chamber (S058). After June he teaches William three words of Dutch and a weaver's knot, and some of his talk of looms and shuttles goes into a certain weaver in November.

**Relationships.** Mistress Ansell's protégé. William's neighbour across the stair. Alse's teacher of rhymes.

**Voice casting.** A soft, careful voice with a light Flemish cadence; never a caricature.

---

#### `josias_thwaite` · Master Josias Thwaite `[invented]` · 60 · reader and curate at St Helen's

**Role and home.** St Helen's church (`st_helens_church`). Long sermons, with the sand-glass turned twice.

**Sample lines.**

- "And *fourteenthly*…"
- "We shall be brief, brethren." (He never is.)
- "Starch, brethren. Blue starch. Consider the lilies: they toil not, neither do they starch."

**Wants and arc.** He wants an attentive parish and a godly City. On Sun 1 Jun he reads the blue-starch precept with real feeling. Trinity Sunday and the June pulpits preach against disorder. The Lambeth Articles (T2) reach William through Field on Thu 20 Nov (M045), not from the pulpit. On Advent Sunday, 30 Nov, he begins on the four last things and reaches the second (S110).

**Relationships.** Spencer's minister and Mistress Ansell's trial.

**Voice casting.** A thin, reedy, untiring older voice.

---

#### `gib` · Gib · the cat

**Role and home.** Mistress Ansell's grey cat: heavy, and always on the book William needs (running joke 7). No lines.

**Lines said to Gib.**

- "Off Holinshed, sirrah. He's Master Field's, and Master Field counts."
- (Mistress Ansell) "Gib, come down. Master Shakespeare is writing a king and you are not in it."
- (William, memo) "The cat has chosen the deposition. A critic."

---

#### `agnes_tipping` · Agnes Tipping `[invented]` · 45 · hostess of the Jackdaw

**Role and home.** The Jackdaw in Holywell Lane (`the_jackdaw`), the ordinary where the company eats. Dinner is 6d. She gives credit by the slate, forgives Burbage everything and charges Kemp double for spillage. Her sign feeds a running joke about Greene's "upstart crow".

**Sample lines.**

- "Dinner's sixpence, wit's extra."
- "That's a jackdaw on my sign, Master Shakespeare, not a crow. A crow would never give credit."
- "Master Burbage, you've been a king at my table since October. Kings pay."

**Wants and arc.** She wants full benches and settled slates. On Tue 15 Jul Queen Mab is first heard at her table (W057). In December Burbage woos her as Romeo, and she tells him to sit down and eat.

**Relationships.** The company's hostess. Sincler's despair and delight.

**Voice casting.** A warm, loud, landlady's voice.

---

#### `alice_brambleby` · Alice Brambleby `[invented]` · 41 · keeper of the Split Hazel

**Role and home.** The Split Hazel, a modest ordinary near Bishopsgate (`split_hazel`), with rooms above it. It is the public room near home where he can find talk without compulsory carousing.

**How she talks.** Hospitable but not intimate by default. She can stop an argument by asking who is paying.

**Sample lines.**

- "You may dispute the government after you have moved your elbow from the gravy."
- "Say where you heard it."
- "Master Nashe, wit is not money. I have tried paying the brewer in it."

**Wants and arc.** She wants solvent customers and a house that survives a nervous year. The mid-May supper with Nashe is at her house. June makes her careful about strangers and repeated political claims. By autumn she has a rule: a man may tell news, but he must say where he heard it.

**Relationships.** Likes Kemp's custom and limits his noise. Respects Heminges. Does not indulge Nashe's belief that wit is payment.

**Voice casting.** A measured, dry woman's voice.

---

#### `davy_gannet` · Davy Gannet `[invented]` · 58 · his waterman

**Role and home.** Old Swan Stairs (`old_swan_stairs`), with a tilt-boat for long fares. A veteran seaman of '88. He shouts the news with his fares and is wrong by a third every time (running joke 13), but he is encyclopaedic on tides, winds, drowned apprentices and ships at Deptford and Plymouth. Wherry 1d across, 2d to Whitehall. (Merged with Gemini's Wat Tredway, who gives him the tides, the 1588 service and the Richmond tilt-boat.)

**Sample lines.**

- "Westward ho! Raleigh's gone for gold, the Spaniard's in Kent, and it'll rain."
- "Sit trim amidships, Master Will. The ebb's running like a mill-race under the starlings today."
- "I was off Gravelines in '88, master, and I tell you the Spaniard's a poor sailor and a worse cook."
- "Drake and Hawkins, both, sailed Thursday with a hundred ship. Or twenty. Many."

**Wants and arc.** He wants steady fares and safe passage. Tue 11 Feb: Raleigh gone for Guiana (S014a). Thu 4 Sep: Drake and Hawkins have sailed (S084). Sat 19 Jul he rows William past the Swan going up (W059). **Wed 24 Dec:** he takes the company up to Richmond by tilt-boat on the flood, about four hours (W107), and brings them down on **Mon 29 Dec** (W108).

**Relationships.** Everyone's waterman, William's in particular.

**Voice casting.** A salt-cured, carrying old voice.

---

#### `barnaby_yaxley` · Barnaby Yaxley `[invented]` · 50 · barber-surgeon

**Role and home.** A shop in Bishopsgate Street. Cheerful about pain. He draws the tooth on Tue 14 Oct for 12d (W089), after 2d of cloves.

**Sample lines.**

- "Hold the stool, sir, and think on your sins. It distracts."
- "A good root. Three prongs, like the devil's fork. I'll keep it, if you've no use for it."
- "Broth for a week, and no oaths, and no speeches. I know you players."

**Wants and arc.** A steady trade in teeth, beards and bleeding. After the tooth he shaves William's beard close once, on 16 Dec, for court.

**Relationships.** The parish's barber. Mistress Ansell sends for him.

**Voice casting.** A hearty, unfazed middle-aged voice.

---

#### `the_lady_unnamed` · never named · late 20s · T5 on a T3 tradition

**Who she is.** A married woman of the parish who plays the virginals. Dark hair, dark eyes, a low laugh. She plays very well and knows it. Amused by him. Not safe. (Canon § 7 #2: the descriptors "kin to court musicians" and "a musician's kin" are dropped.)

**Rules for every builder (binding).**

1. She has no name, initial or surname anywhere: not in his notes, not in a filename, not in a transcript. His notes say "she", "the black-eyed", or leave a gap.
2. She is not identified with any historical person. A builder must not make her Emilia Lanier, and must not rule it out.
3. They are never alone indoors. There is no touch beyond a hand at a church door.
4. She speaks at most a dozen lines in the year.
5. No verse is ever addressed or delivered to her. The private verse keeps its *you* unassigned.

**Sample lines.**

- "You are the one that writes." (The six words at the church door, 22 May, W044: her only speech in the year.)
- Unused, and free for a later pass: "I had thought you taller." · "Don't put me in a play. I'd know." · "Your hat, sir. It is raining."

**Arc (the crosses on his calendar). Seven in the year, and no more.**

- *Sun 5 Jan:* music from an upper window on Twelfth Eve; the window is Morley's (W002).
- *Fri 28 Feb:* a face at St Helen's, and the first sonnet with "black" in it (W018).
- *Sat 22 Mar:* a music evening at Morley's, and an unsigned quatrain (M009).
- *Thu 22 May:* six words at the church door, "You are the one that writes." (W044).
- *Sun 8 Jun:* Whitsunday; a dark head in the north aisle turns once, and away (W048).
- *Summer:* he writes his way toward her, and twice she is elsewhere when he looks (W301, 6 Jul; W305, 20 Jul; no cross).
- *Sun 14 Sep:* a dark head in church, and at evening the pavan's hard place gone over behind an open shutter (W336), the first cross since June.
- *Wed 29 Oct:* at the Lord Mayor's show she laughs with someone else, and it is nothing, and it is enough (S101).
- *Sat 13 Dec:* he ties the year's sonnets in a tape and puts them at the bottom of the coffer (W103). Advent Sunday, 30 Nov (S110), has no sighting.

Nothing happens. That is the story.

---

#### `thomas_morley` · Thomas Morley · c.37 · T2 (unchecked as a neighbour) · composer

**Role and home.** Great St Helen's, near the church. Composer and organist, a Gentleman of the Chapel Royal (T2), and host of evening music in a candlelit room where the lady plays.

**How he talks.** Witty and mercurial, obsessed with Italian canzonets, the balance of air and words, and breath.

**Sample lines.**

- "Give me vowels a singer can open his throat upon, Will. Your English 'strengths' and 'twelfths' chew like gravel in a madrigal."
- "Four lines, no more, and every one ending open. A song is not a sermon."
- "You hear that? She takes the turn a hair late on purpose. She'll never tell you why."

**Wants and arc.** He wants fresh lyrics and cultivated company in the parish. William gets from him an education in harmony, and the room where the lady plays. His window is the Twelfth Eve music of Sun 5 Jan; the music evening of Sat 22 Mar is at his house; at most, otherwise, he is a man with music under his arm in Bishopsgate Street.

**Relationships.** Phillips knows his songs. The lady plays at his evenings.

**Voice casting.** A quick, light, musical voice.

---

### 3.6 Print, pens and rivals

#### `richard_field` · Richard Field · 33 → 34 (Nov) · T1 · printer, Blackfriars

**Role and home.** His house and shop in Blackfriars (`fields_printing_house`). A Stratford tanner's son (T1) who married his master Vautrollier's widow Jacqueline and took over the press. He printed *Venus* and *Lucrece* and, in 1595, North's *Plutarch* (T1). Careful, respectable and Protestant. He disapproves of playhouses and reads every play William will lend him. He supplies William's Rochelle crown paper at trade price, 4½d the quire.

**How he talks.** Dry, exact and measured, with home vowels under a London trade manner, smoothed by Huguenot Blackfriars sobriety. More amused than he first sounds. He dislikes a correction described as small when it means real labour. He smells of linseed ink and damp rag paper. He uses *thou* in private and *you* in the shop.

**Sample lines.**

- "Thou'rt wasted on players. I say it as thy friend and as a man with a press standing idle on Fridays."
