- "My Holinshed, Will. The second volume. I see the gap on the shelf every morning."
- "The word is yours. The trouble is now mine."
- "A page does not become fair because the author has ceased looking at it."
- "Feel the impression on that leaf. North's Plutarch, clean pica Roman top to tail. Books outlive playhouses, and never draw prentices to break heads."
- "It's market day at home. I never pass a Thursday but I know it."

**He wants:** another poem for the press, dependable copy, and respectability for his household.
**William wants:** books on loan, a hearth that sounds like Stratford, honest counsel, and the regard of someone who knew the town before his success.

**Arc.** Five calls: Wed 26 Feb, Harrison and the octavo *Venus* (W017); Mon 28 Apr, the gap on his shelf (W127); Thu 5 Jun, Millington's octavo and a Sunday (W134); Wed 17 Sep, a Cambridge man's margin (W337); Mon 15 Dec, Burby, a Sunday and the gap (W418). **Wed 19 Mar: he lends the Holinshed** (W022), volume two, which is not returned by 31 Dec (running joke 9). Sun 4 May: dinner at the Fields' (W039). **Thu 15 May: the Plutarch sheets coming off the press** (S046), the year's fullest hour of friendship, two craftsmen discussing what survives the hand; he sells William the Plutarch at trade price, 14s unbound. In July he refuses to romanticise dangerous speech. **Thu 18 Sep: he shows William Covell's "Sweet Shakspeare"** (W082). In November they talk over the Lambeth Articles (M045, Thu 20 Nov). **Mon 1 Dec: Burby enters *Edward III*** (S111); Harrison reports it that day, and Field calls about it on Mon 15 Dec (W418). In December they can disagree about playhouses without treating it as betrayal. On Tue 30 Dec William reads him pages of the new comedy by his hearth.

**Relationships.** Jacqueline is his working partner. Harrison is in the commercial chain of the poems; William must not assume that sales pay him a royalty each time. Mistress Ansell respects his trade.

**Voice casting.** A dry, measured, tradesman's baritone.

---

#### `jacqueline_field` · Jacqueline Field · model 42 · T1 · his wife

**Role and home.** The Blackfriars house. Vautrollier's widow (T1), French, who runs the shop's accounts (T5). She feeds William at Sunday dinners and corrects his French for the Princess's court.

**How she talks.** Controlled and decisive, with a light French rhythm only if a stock voice can do it without caricature.

**Sample lines.**

- "*Non, non.* You make them speak French as an Englishman dreams it."
- "If both of you have finished discussing paper, one of you may move it."
- "Sit by the fire and rest your back before you walk into the fog."

**Wants and arc.** She wants the shop's work to go on and visitors to understand that hospitality costs space. William wants to be treated as an old friend rather than an interruption, and over the year learns to ask whether it is a useful hour before he starts talking.

**Relationships.** Richard's partner in all things. A friendly recognition of Anne's competence, from letters. She refuses to be a decorative foreign presence.

**Voice casting.** A clear, decisive woman's voice.

---

#### `john_harrison` · John Harrison (the elder) · c.60 · T1 · publisher at the White Greyhound

**Role and home.** The White Greyhound in Paul's Churchyard (`pauls_churchyard`). He entered *Lucrece* in 1594 and holds the *Venus* rights from Field (T1). *Venus* is moving into a handy octavo this year.

**How he talks.** A genial, shrewd bookseller's purr, interested in a book's movement rather than its author's feelings. He knows to the copy what the Inns-of-Court men buy in term. He tells William how his poems sell as if reporting on a third party.

**Sample lines.**

- "Your Venus moves, Master Shakespeare. The Lucrece is… respected."
- "They ask for the poem. That is the better sort of asking."
- "Young gentlemen buy *Venus* to tuck in a doublet and *Lucrece* to leave open on the parlour table. Bring me a third poem and name your earnest."

**Wants and arc.** Saleable stock, another narrative poem, and no confusion over rights. William wants evidence that readers value him, preferably without an account of the costs. He appears sparingly: winter trade, spring browsing at his stall, autumn praise in print. He is not William's agent.

**Relationships.** Works with Field. Tolerates Nashe as one tolerates weather coming in at an open door.

**Voice casting.** A smooth, genial older voice.

---

#### `thomas_nashe` · Thomas Nashe · 27 → 28 (Nov) · T1 / contact T3 · satirist

**Role and home.** Shifting garrets about Fleet Street and Blackfriars. Brilliant and broke; the satirist and pamphleteer in a running quarrel with Gabriel Harvey (T1). Friendship with William is unproved (T3), so every meeting is invented. Exactly **three suppers** in the year.

**How he talks.** Fast, extravagant and combative; one image too many, and then another because he knows it. He coins words every sentence and laughs loudest when his pockets are empty. Sober, he is haunted by Marlowe and Greene.

**Sample lines.**

- "A man may be ruined for a sentence. It is a discouragement to brevity."
- "Lend me half a crown till Michaelmas and I'll swear thy blank verse cures the toothache."
- "Harvey! Harvey writes like a man walking on his own beard."
- "Kit would have liked thee. He'd have hated thee first, but he'd have liked thee."

**Wants and arc.** He wants an audience, fellowship, his reckonings paid and ears for his feud. William enjoys his electric tongue in small doses, and goes home before midnight. **Sun 2 Mar**, Shrove Sunday at the Bull: a loud roast-beef supper, quarrelling with a man who is not there (S021). **Mid-May**, at the Split Hazel: exuberant, and Alice makes him pay. **Mid-July**, a supper of literary and street gossip whose fantastical rush leaves a cadence in William's ear just before Queen Mab. July's news makes William less willing to enjoy recklessness at another man's expense. They stay capable of laughter, and are not a literary club.

**Relationships.** Alice makes him pay. Field declines to be recruited into his battles. Kemp appreciates a verbal performer who knows he is performing. Harrison tolerates him.

**Voice casting.** A fast, bright, cracking young voice.

---

#### `samuel_daniel` · Samuel Daniel · 32–33 · T1 / contact T3 · poet of the *Civil Wars*

**Role and home.** Holborn and the Savoy liberties. A grave, philosophical court poet whose *First Fowre Bookes of the Civile Wars* (T1, 1595) is a likely source for *Richard II* (T2/T3). William reads it standing at a stall for weeks in March and buys it on Tue 15 Apr for 2s (W033).

**How he talks.** Quiet, measured and scholarly; faintly wary of the playhouses' vulgarity.

**Sample lines.**

- "I wrote the Queen's grief for a quiet chamber, Master Shakespeare. I confess I had not thought a boy in Shoreditch could make two hundred prentices hold their breath with it."
- "History is a stern master. You have been kinder to Richard than I was, and less kind to the truth."
- "I hear you put a gardener in. I should not have dared."

**Wants and arc.** Literary permanence and enlightened patronage. One supper, in October, after *Richard II* has opened: a gracious mutual reckoning.

**Relationships.** A rival William admires and does not envy.

**Voice casting.** A soft, grave, careful voice.

---

#### `michael_drayton` · Michael Drayton · 31–32 · T3/T4 · a Warwickshire voice at Paul's

**Role and home.** London and Warwickshire. A fellow Warwickshire man, met at the stalls in Paul's Churchyard. Friendly and a little competitive about sonnets. **Never the "Rival Poet" as fact.** The later "merry meeting" story (Ward, 1662) is T4 and out of period.

**Sample lines.**

- "Arden! You say it as we do. Half London thinks it's a lady's name."
- "Fourteen lines, Will, and I've sixty sonnets to your — how many? You'll not say."
- "Buy the Daniel. Then come and tell me why mine's better."

**Wants and arc.** He wants a fellow countryman's admiration. Four meetings at the stalls in Paul's, two in spring and two in autumn: Tue 15 Apr (W033), Sat 31 May (W047), Mon 22 Sep (S090) and Mon 29 Sep (S092).

**Voice casting.** A warm Midlands voice, close to William's own.

---

#### `george_peele` · George Peele · 38 → 39 · T1 / T3 · old playwright

**Role and home.** London. Shabby and witty. Seen on Accession Day, Mon 17 Nov, with a manuscript under his arm (his *Anglorum Feriae* on that day's festivities, T1). He and William once shared a play (*Titus*, T3), and they nod like old soldiers. Nothing hints at what follows for him.

**Sample lines.**

- "Shakespeare! Still alive and still in Shoreditch. I've a poem on the tilt. Every horse in it rhymes."
- "Titus is still playing, they tell me. Do they still laugh in the wrong place?"
- "A groat for a poor poet? No? Wise man."

**Wants and arc.** A patron for the poem, and a drink. One meeting, at the tilt.

**Voice casting.** A rough, humorous, tired voice.

---

#### `edward_alleyn` · Edward Alleyn · 28 → 29 (1 Sep) · T1 · the Rose's star

**Role and home.** Bankside, near the Rose (`the_rose`). The tall, six-foot tragic star of the Admiral's Men, a payee for them on the same March account (T1), married to Henslowe's stepdaughter Joan, to whom he writes as "my good sweete harte and loving mouse" (T1, 1593). Onstage a deep organ of a voice; offstage mild, pious and domestic over a pot of small beer.

**Sample lines.**

- "You need not admire it, Will. Only tell me whether the back row heard."
- "Father Henslowe laid out seven pound two shillings on our throne in the heavens. Come over the water and watch Jupiter come down without creaking."
- "My wife sends to know whether you are married. I said yes. She says then why are you always here?"

**Wants and arc.** Full galleries and peaceful respect between the two licensed companies. William wants the stimulation of rivalry and would like not to envy him; he studies Alleyn's command from the Rose yard and invents the opposite style for Burbage. He watches him on Fri 14 Feb (*Long Meg*), Tue 20 May (*1 Hercules*), Fri 29 Aug (*Longshanks*) and Fri 28 Nov (*Harry the Fifth*). February's spectacle irritates; May's *Hercules* brings honest professional admiration; by autumn William can admire a rival's success without translating it into his own failure. The Rose is dark from Thu 26 Jun to Mon 25 Aug. Two brief exchanges in the year, both at the Rose: a cordial word outside on Fri 14 Feb (S016), and "Come to count my inches?" at the tiring-house stair on Fri 29 Aug (S085).

**Relationships.** Henslowe's family and business. A respectful rivalry with Burbage. No feud with William.

**Voice casting.** A deep, resonant, gentle bass.

---

#### `philip_henslowe` · Philip Henslowe · c.40 · T1 · owner of the Rose

**Role and home.** Bankside, in the Clink liberty. Dyer, pawnbroker, financier and owner of the Rose, whose diary gives the daily takings (T1) in phonetic spelling. Three of the Chamberlain's hired men owe him small sums (T1).

**How he talks.** Affable, paternal and calculating. Sums first. He turns art, grief and thunder instantly into shillings and elm boards. Not stupid because his spelling is irregular.

**Sample lines.**

- "You've a pretty wit, Master Shakespeare. Does it pay?"
- "It was a good house. The carpenter has already heard."
- "Sixteen new books in a twelvemonth keeps the yard fresh. Why sweat six months polishing one diamond when painted glass shines at two o'clock?"

**Wants and arc.** Receipts that exceed obligations, and players bound to him by debts. He repairs the Rose in Lent (T1: elm boards, carpenters, the painter, £8 19s in all). He dates a page "the 31 of aprell" (T1; it reaches Shoreditch as a joke on Wed 30 Apr, S039). On Wed 4 Jun he pays £7 2s for "the throne in the heavens" (T1, S052). He shuts on Thu 26 Jun (S066) and reopens Mon 25 Aug. **One exchange with William, at the bridge foot on Tue 20 May (W043):** civil, appraising, all money. He never hands William his diary for exposition, and his 18 May is blank. The summer silence of his house matters more to William than any conversation.

**Relationships.** Alleyn's father-in-law and banker. Creditor to Raye, Blackwage and Hoope.

**Voice casting.** A comfortable, shrewd, middle-aged voice.

---

#### `francis_langley` · Francis Langley · 46–47 · T1 · offstage; the Swan rising

**Role and home.** Paris Garden, Southwark. A goldsmith and builder of the Swan, whose new playhouse rises across the water in 1595. **No contact in 1595** (any contact is T3). Nothing of 1596 is touched.

**Lines said about him.**

- (Davy Gannet, Sat 19 Jul) "That's Langley's. A goldsmith building a playhouse, master. He'll gild the groundlings."
- (James Burbage) "Another house! Another! He'll empty Bankside and then wonder why the ground's cold."
- (Heminges) "A new house wants new players. It's not ours I'd fear for. It's the Rose's."

**Arc.** Seen once, as timber and scaffolding from a wherry on Sat 19 Jul (W059).

---

#### `robert_southwell` · Robert Southwell · 33 (d. 21 Feb) · T1 · never met; a memo and a book

**Role.** A Jesuit poet, tried at Westminster on Thu 20 Feb, which William hears of at supper after the first *Love's Labour's Lost*, and hanged at Tyburn on Fri 21 Feb (T1). **William is never there**: he speaks a memo into the dark that night (S019). No kinship is adopted (T3) and no private conversation is fabricated. On **Sat 17 May** he buys *Saint Peters Complaint* for 6d and reads its epistle, on poets, lovers and liars, standing up.

**His words, and words about him.**

- (His own text, pre-1700, from the epistle; verify against EEBO before use) "…a Poet, a Lover, and a Liar, are by many reckoned but three words of one signification."
- (At supper, 20 Feb, the news) "They tried the priest today. Westminster Hall was full to the doors."
- (William, memo, 21 Feb) "He made verses too. …What is a poet for. Enough."

**Arc.** An execution reported, then a living force in printed language. William wants from the book what he cannot ask its author: a way to understand conviction without reducing it to a faction's label. No gore, no scaffold, no imagined last thoughts.

---

#### Named offstage, no slug needed

Mentioned in talk, letters or news, never on stage: Alexander Aspinall, the Stratford schoolmaster, quoted once by Susanna (T1 `[ext]`; fall back to "the master"). Ellen Burbage, "Mistress Burbage", in her kitchen. Sir Stephen Slany, Mayor from 29 Oct. Rowland Whyte, the newsletter writer. William Covell, a margin. Cuthbert Burby, who enters *Edward III* on 1 Dec. Sir Thomas Wilford, the provost-marshal of July. The five executed on 24 July (Graunt, Pearson, Stokeley, Tuley, Brough). Elizabeth Vernon. Lord Burghley, and his gout. Raleigh, Drake and Hawkins (whose death is never heard). The Earls of Arundel (dead in the Tower, 19 Oct; memo Wed 22 Oct) and Huntingdon. Joan Hathaway, Anne's stepmother (`[ext]`). Christopher Marlowe, a memory whenever the Rose plays *Tamburlaine* or *Faustus*.

---

### 3.7 The relationships map

**The inner ring.** William's closest daily people are **Burbage** (brother and instrument), **Heminges** (conscience and purse) and **Mistress Ansell** (first critic and keeper of the stair). Each sees a different William: Burbage the maker, Heminges the partner, Ansell the lodger who walks the floor at two in the morning.

**The company's alliances.**

- **The money pair:** Heminges and Bryan, who handle the great households and the Chamber, and will be paid a year late.
- **The music and boys pair:** Phillips and Pope, who keep Sam and Arthur, train Nick, and make the boys' work bearable.
- **The Burbage house:** James, Cuthbert and Richard, bound by the lease and divided by temperament. Cuthbert translates his father to the world and his brother to the accounts.
- **The workers:** Cowley, Larkham, Edith, Pell, Sincler and the hired men, who share a practical solidarity about wages and invisible labour. Kemp, unexpectedly, is their champion.

**The company's frictions.**

- **The clown war:** Kemp against William, refereed by Garnish's tally, from February until the truce of Sat 22 Nov.
- **Kemp and Burbage:** the crowd's man against the play's man.
- **James and everyone:** the lease, Allen and timber.
- **The boys' ladder:** Nick against time, Arthur waiting for Nick's parts, Sam wanting a second helping of everything.

**The household.** Mistress Ansell, Alse, Verbeke and Gib, with William on the top floor: a small, safe world that the June riots threaten and do not breach. Thwaite and Spencer press on it from the church and from Crosby Place.

**Stratford.**

- **Anne and William:** the year's love story, in two visits and a dozen letters.
- **Anne and her allies:** Joan, Judith Sadler and Mary, the women who run the house and see through the men.
- **John and his pride:** managed by Anne, punctured by Mary, quietly paid for by William.
- **The children:** three equal voices, Susanna precise, Judith procedural and Hamnet mechanical, all fond of their father on their own terms.
- **The borough men:** Quiney and Sturley, always one favour ahead. The Sadlers anchor friendship in bread.
- **The links:** Hobbins carries the line between the two worlds. Gilbert stands at the London end of it.

**Patronage, the ladder above him.**

- Hunsdon is the shield, and Kettleby his voice.
- Margaret and Edward Hoby are the bridge to Cecil.
- Tilney is the gate.
- Southampton is the old light moving away, and Florio is the doorkeeper who lets nothing through.
- The Queen is at the top, seen and unreadable.

**The rivals and the trade.** Field is the Stratford friend in London, with Jacqueline beside him; Harrison is the chain of the poems. Henslowe and Alleyn are the rival house across the water, respected and envied. Nashe is the dangerous pleasure. Daniel, Drayton and Peele are fellow pens: Daniel and Peele seen once each, Drayton four times at the stalls.

**The two unnamed weathers.** The lord and the lady. Neither is ever named in any way that settles anything, and neither is in any way an affair. Both end the year at the bottom of the coffer, tied in a tape.

---


## 4. The places bible

*Fold pass 2, section 4. It merges the three bibles' § 4 (`gpt`, `gem`, `fab`), plus the place detail scattered through their cast, dated-year and per-source sections, under the canon § 4 and § 5. Coordinates come from the source book's gazetteer (`research/sourcebook/03-places-and-itinerary.md` § 2) unless marked* est. *Distances come from its § 3.*

**How to read an entry.** Each place gives its canonical slug, what it is, its coordinates and tiers, what it looks, sounds and smells like by season, who is found there, how William uses it and when, and its photo opportunities. Dates are Julian 1595, stored as the same month and day in 2025, with the 2025 weekday. Entry ids are the shared seeds (`S###`) and Fable's `W###` anchors from `fab` § 5.3, which is the canonical spine. GPT and Gemini entries are cited by date, not by their own `W` numbers, which collide with Fable's.

**Standing rules for every place** (canon § 2, collected here so a location or photo builder has them in one spot):

- Coordinates mark historical sites, not surviving buildings. One house is one point. The road produces a track with inn stops, and miles on horse or boat are not steps.
- **News places are never location points.** Nor is `tyburn`. `tower_hill` has a point only on Sat 1 Feb. He never has a point at Greenwich in 1595, inside Southampton House, or at any house of the unnamed lady.
- **The radius of fear.** From 13 June to 5 July his tracks stay within 600 m of home, except the road to the Theatre and back. He does not cross the river between 12 June and 19 July. `southwark_market` is never visited from 12 June to 19 July.
- He is never present at a riot as it happens or at an execution. Photos never show an execution, a corpse or blood. The heads on the bridge gate are distant texture at most, and the empty gallows-frame before Crosby Place is the limit.
- Photos are taken from his standing or sitting eye level (about 5 ft 7 in). They never show his face, a mirror, his reflection or his shadow, though his hands may appear. The only light is sun, fog, candle, cresset or fire. Text in frame follows the canon or is illegible.
- Never frame the children against Holy Trinity or any churchyard.

---

### 4.1 His lodging: one house, one household

#### `ansell_house` · Mistress Ansell's house, Great St Helen's `[invented]`

*51.5150, −0.0815 (est., ±50 m of the church point) · T3 that he lodged in St Helen's parish in 1595 (the first record is the default roll of 15 Nov 1597, T1); T5 for the house, the household and every detail below · `gpt` `nettlefold_house` and `gem` `st_helens_lodging` map here.*

This is the most-used place in the journal. Most of his nights, most of his writing, most of his voice memos and nearly all his calls happen inside it. Write it as a real house with a real household, and let it change with the year.

**The close.** Great St Helen's is the old nunnery close, opening off the east side of Bishopsgate Street a few minutes inside the gate. It is a quiet pocket a stone's throw from the City's loudest road. St Helen's church stands thirty yards off across the cobbles, with its porch, its twin gables and its churchyard elms where the rooks build. A hundred yards to the south-west, above the lower roofs, rises the long dark ridge and the tall stone oriel of Crosby Place, the Lord Mayor's house. Thomas Morley the composer lives in the parish, in a lane off Bishopsgate Street a few steps from the close (T2, unchecked as neighbour), and his upper window is the one that has music in it on Twelfth Eve (W002, M009). About seventy householders live in the parish, a pocket of theatre men among them (T3, Marsh). A kennel runs down the middle of the close, frozen in January and stinking in July.

**The house** (canon § 5.1). A narrow three-storey timber house on the north side of the close, jettied over the lane and backing on a garden wall. Its front is plaster between dark studs, with small leaded panes, a lead spout that drums in rain, and a lantern bracket by the door.

- **Ground floor.** The *hall*, where Mistress Ansell keeps her table, her settle and her fire, and where he boards when he boards. The *kitchen* behind it, with the bread-board her late husband notched in a good year, and the copper where she boils her oak-gall ink on Mondays from his receipt: galls, copperas and gum. *Verbeke's back room* opens off the kitchen toward the garden. Behind is a narrow yard with a privy against the garden wall and the brine-tubs at Martinmas.
- **First floor.** Mistress Ansell's chamber at the front, over the hall, and Alse's closet behind it.
- **Second floor.** William's front chamber. Above it is the lumber garret, under the tiles, where the oiled canvas road-wallet hangs between journeys among old bedsteads and a scrivener's chest of dead men's indentures.

**The stair.** Narrow, steep, turning twice, and cold as a well. It lets the draught up into his room all winter (`gpt`). Every tread has its own voice, and Mistress Ansell knows each one. From her chamber below she can tell comedy from tragedy by the rhythm of his feet as he walks his lines (`fab`). **Callers are stopped at the foot of the stair.** Heminges or Gilbert may stand at the street door for a doorstep word, but no company man climbs it uninvited, and Kemp least of all, because objects in a room Kemp has visited tend to change their purpose (`gpt`). Alse lingers on the half-landing with his water for the end of a speech. Gib sleeps on the third tread from the top, exactly where a man carrying a candle puts his foot.

**His chamber.** About six paces from the window to the door: that is the length he walks when he says lines aloud before writing them (`gem`'s six-pace closet).

- **The west window** looks over the close to the church porch, with the elms on the left and Crosby Place's roof-line and oriel beyond. The desk stands in its light. Small leaded panes in a casement that opens outward. A shutter on the inside that he latches at night. Apples on the sill in autumn.
- **The small north light** at the back looks over the garden wall toward the backs of Bishopsgate Street. Frost flowers form on it in January.
- **The desk:** a board on trestles under the west window. On it are his brass inkhorn, the round pounce-box (`gem`), the quills, his father's glover's paring-knife used as a penknife, the penny almanac, the writing-tables, a quire of Field's Rochelle crown paper, a tallow candlestick, and whatever book Gib is lying on.
- **The joined stool** with the cushion Anne made. From 27 Feb a folded paper wedge comes out from under its short leg and is kept as a private joke (`gpt`).
- **The bed:** a standing bed with a flock mattress and faded green say curtains (`gem`), with two bolsters for the days of the cold.
- **The coffer:** oak, two locks, at the bed's foot. This is `gem`'s "locked table-box" and `gpt`'s "small cash box". It holds the money, his letters from home and, from 13 Dec, the sonnets tied in a tape at the very bottom.
- **One shelf of books** (below), a candle-box, a pewter basin and ewer, and a peg by the door for the grey cloak.
- **The latch** on his door is unreliable all year and sticks in wet weather. Mistress Ansell has it mended on Mon 22 Dec and does not charge for it (`gpt`).

**The shelf, with dates** (canon § 5.1). Golding's Ovid, battered. His own *Venus* and *Lucrece*. Brooke's *Romeus*, worn, from Thu 6 Mar. Field's Holinshed, volume two, on loan from Wed 19 Mar, with Field asking for it monthly, and **not returned by 31 Dec**. Daniel's *Civil Wars*, bought Tue 15 Apr after weeks of reading it standing at a stall. North's Plutarch, unbound, at trade price from May. Southwell's *Saint Peters Complaint*, bought Sat 17 May. A Chaucer borrowed through Florio, from October.

**What he hears.**

- *Bells.* St Helen's bells, thirty yards off, for every service and every passing. Bow bell faint at nine for the curfew. The ringers in their shirtsleeves on the Queen's birthday, Sun 7 Sep. On Thu 24 Jul he hears the bells and does not go (S077).
- *The house.* Mistress Ansell's brisk, carrying voice through the floor: "a woman used to being heard through a floor" (`fab`). Alse singing in the kitchen when she thinks nobody is listening. The copper on Mondays. Verbeke coming in late from his master's loom in Norton Folgate and praying low in Flemish. Gib's weight landing on the boards at two in the morning.
- *The close.* Water-bearers' cries. Carts in Bishopsgate Street like distant surf. The watch calling the hour. Rooks in spring and swifts screaming round the gables in summer (`gem`). Rain drumming in the lead spout. Music from Morley's upper window. Crosby Place's servants and the sword-bearer's comings and goings.
- *The year's bad nights.* Apprentices' voices at night in June. On Sun 29 Jun, the noise from Tower Hill, 0.9 km away, coming over the roofs in the dark.

**Smell and light by season.**

- *Winter (Jan–Feb, Nov–Dec).* Sea-coal smoke curling under the casement lead, tallow, damp wool, the stair cold as a well. Breath white before the candle warms the room. Frost flowers on the north light. Daylight from about eight to four, so most writing is by candle. The posset in January: hot ale and nutmeg in a pewter pot at his door (`gem`).
- *Spring (Mar–May).* The window open. Rooks in the churchyard elms. The smell of ink boiling on Mondays. Light lengthening past seven by May.
- *Summer (Jun–Aug).* Light till after eight. Stifling heat under the tiles in July (`gem`), warm tallow and dry rushes, a bluebottle at the casement. The stink of Houndsditch on an east wind. An open casement lets in other people's business, and he closes it and immediately misses the air (`gpt`).
- *Autumn (Sep–Oct).* Apples on the sill. Damp in the plaster. The first fire, from Michaelmas, when the firing charge begins. Mistress Ansell's toasted crabs and small beer rising through the floorboards (`gem`). Earlier candles from St Luke's day.

**The household** (canon § 3.5; one landlady, folding `gem`'s Widow Kempster and `gpt`'s Nettlefolds).

- **Mistress Dorcas Ansell** `[invented]`, 52, a scrivener's widow who owns the lease. She makes and sells ink at 2d the horn. She is a sermon-goer who disapproves of plays in general and demands the plot of each in particular, then rules on it. She counts candles and judges the penny loaf against the notch. She asks her questions while carrying something (`gpt`). To the neighbours he is "my Master Shakespeare, who makes less noise than a mouse and pays on quarter-day before noon" (`gem`). To his face he is a man who burned three candles to the socket on Tuesday.
- **Alse Pinder** `[invented]`, 16, her maid from Hertfordshire. She cannot read and is quick. She brings his water and washing and is the measure of whether a line lands on someone who has never seen a play.
- **Gillis Verbeke** `[invented]`, 34, a Flemish silk-weaver journeyman in the back room, gentle and devout, with careful English. He is hidden in the June riots and never harmed. After June he teaches William three words of Dutch and a weaver's knot.
- **Gib**, the landlady's heavy grey cat, always on the book he needs.

**The routine.**

- *A playing day.* He wakes with the light, which is about a quarter to seven in winter and before five in summer. Alse knocks with the water. He writes for an hour if he can. He leaves at half past eight: down the stair, across the close, into Bishopsgate Street. Rehearsal is at nine and the play at two, and he is home by dusk or after. If he boards, he takes supper at her table, and then writes by candle until she raps on the ceiling with a broom after nine.
- *A writing day.* He works at the desk from first light to dinner, with one loop out to Leadenhall or Finsbury Fields, and works again by candle after supper.
- *The week.* The ink is boiled on Monday. On a Thursday carrier day he writes home after the play. Saturday night he counts at the coffer. On Sunday it is church across the close, with the Ansell household in the south aisle and Master Thwaite's sand-glass turned twice. He boards at her table two or three times a week, irregularly, and the talk there is plot, verdict, loaf and candles.
- *The night.* On about a third of winter nights he wakes around two, between first sleep and second (T3, used knowingly), and lies in the dark with the waking hour. Many memos come from that hour.

**Terms and rent** (canon § 5.1; `gpt`'s 26s and `gem`'s 15s a quarter yield).

| Item | Canon |
|---|---|
| Rent | £4 a year: **20s on each quarter day by noon**, paid on Tue 25 Mar (Lady Day, S028), Tue 24 Jun (Midsummer, W052) and Mon 29 Sep (Michaelmas, S092). The Christmas quarter is paid early, on **Mon 22 Dec** (W106), before Richmond. His almanac reads "Rent, Mistress Ansell, xx s., by noon" |
| Firing and washing | 6d a week, Michaelmas to Lady Day |
| Candles | His own |
| Board | 3d a meal at her table, when he takes it |
| Ink | 2d the horn, of Mistress Ansell |
| Martinmas | No coal charge. On Tue 11 Nov he helps carry in the household's winter stores (S105, W093) |

**The year in the house** (dated; the dated year adds more).

- **Wed 1 Jan (S001).** The year's book is opened at the desk at first light, with frost on the pane. He writes one line owed: "of the Queen, for two plays at Greenwich at Christmas, xx li., not yet had". Downstairs something is dropped, and he notes "the year begins with demands from below" (`gpt`, re-homed).
- **Mon 6 Jan.** After the Burbages' bean-cake (W003), she has kept him back a slice of her own Twelfth-cake, and stands over him while he eats it (`gem`, re-homed).
- **Mon 13 – Sun 19 Jan.** The cold. She nurses him with a posset and a lecture (W005–W006). On Wed 15 Jan he stands at the street door at ten at night in the frost under a full moon (S006). On the worst days he lies propped on two bolsters with the *Love's Labour's Lost* papers across his knees, striking out stale conceits (`gem`). Anne's letter comes on the 19th.
- **Fri 21 Feb (S019).** In bed by one candle, he speaks the memo about the poet at Tyburn. He has it from a tapster, a waterman and Mistress Ansell's account of a neighbour's account.
- **Thu 27 Feb.** The paper wedge comes out from under the stool.
- **Fri 28 Feb (W018).** Sonnet night: the first poem with "black" in it.
- **Thu 6 Mar (S024).** Brooke's *Romeus* is open on the desk under a candle, and Gib's grey flank lies across the right-hand page. "Gib hath sat upon it already."
- **Sat 8 Mar (W019).** He re-orders the desk for Lent.
- **Sat 22 Mar (M009).** He comes back across the close in the dark from the music evening at Morley's, murmuring a memo, then strikes a light and writes four unsigned lines on a scrap.
- **Tue 25 Mar (S028).** Lady Day rent at noon. The year turns 1595. She gives him her blessing on the road, and a cup of small beer (`gem`).
- **Sat 17 May (W042).** The hollow crown, written by one candle, with a second candle guttered beside it. Bed at ten past one. Southwell's book was bought that day.
- **Tue 10 Jun (S094).** He drafts the deposing scene at the desk.
- **Thu 12 Jun (S058).** Fish, and the cry against strangers. Verbeke is hidden in the lumber garret over William's chamber. She makes the household swear, William first, that if anyone asks, the back room is let to a Norwich man.
- **Sat 14 Jun (S059b).** The penny loaf on the bread-board is a full thumb short of the notch.
- **Tue 24 Jun (S065b).** Midsummer morning in the close at 04:50: every doorway dressed in the night with green birch, fennel, St John's wort and white lilies, and a lamp in a bracket over the Ansells' door still burning against the daylight (Stow's custom, `[ext]`; `gem`'s birch and lilies, re-homed). He stands under his own lintel a quarter of an hour and goes no further. Photo 26. The same noon, the Midsummer rent (W052).
- **Fri 27 Jun (S067).** When the gallows goes up before the Mayor's gate, she stands at her window, says "That is not how you ask for bread," and shuts the shutter.
- **Sun 29 Jun (S069).** Tower Hill, on a moonless night. She and William put the bar across the street door together, making it sound more ordinary than it feels (`gem`, `gpt`). She asks whether anyone has remembered the upstairs candle (`gpt`). Then he bars his own chamber door too and sits from half past nine until a quarter past eleven, with his heart at 104 rising to 124.
- **Sat 5 Jul (W054).** *Romeo* begins with a street brawl, written by a man who has just watched one from behind a shutter.
- **Thu 24 Jul (S077).** No play. He stays in and hears the bells.
- **Sun 27 Jul (W060).** He packs the oiled canvas road-wallet on the bed.
- **Fri 22 Aug (W075).** Home from Stratford with a limp. She welcomes him by handing him something to carry (`gpt`).
- **Thu 28 Aug (G068).** A late-summer casement: the lovers across a distance, a neighbour's voice through the window (`gpt`).
- **Mon 29 Sep (S092).** Michaelmas rent, accounts in two columns, and the firing begins.
- **Fri 10 – Mon 13 Oct.** The fierce nights of the tooth, with clove oil and wool. He paces the six paces between one and three in the morning. In that half-waking, at a quarter past two on Tue 14 Oct, the night before the drawing, a weaver with an ass's head comes to him (M038; T5 colour, `gem`).
- **Tue 28 Oct (S100).** An autumn gale rattles the casement as he writes the first page of the *Dream*, Titania's ruined seasons (`gem`).
- **Sat 8 Nov (W092).** Fog. The tomb, by one candle.
- **Tue 11 Nov (W093).** Martinmas. He writes the last line of *Romeo* by a quarter past ten, goes to Leadenhall with Mistress Ansell for the winter stores, and back in the close before noon takes a turn with the colliers' sea-coal sacks beside the brine-tubs of salted beef (S105).
- **Sat 6 Dec.** She goes to the Theatre for the first time in her life and sits in the gallery in her church hat. She will not discuss it until the next morning.
- **Sat 13 Dec (W103).** He ties the year's sonnets in a tape and puts them at the bottom of the coffer.
- **Mon 22 Dec (W106).** Foul weather (T1). The Christmas rent is paid early, and the latch is mended while the passage is clear.
- **Mon 29 Dec (W108, M056).** Back from Richmond, home from the Bull at four. She tells him where his letters are and what time the household will eat. The mended latch works without requiring a metaphor (`gpt`).
- **Wed 31 Dec (S116).** The year's book is closed at the desk, with the coffer at £33 10s.

**Sample lines.**

- Ansell, on the stair: "I let you the room, Master Shakespeare. I did not undertake that the weather should remain outside the stair." (`gpt`'s Giles, re-homed)
- Ansell, at his door: "If you will not eat it hot, I cannot promise to keep it patient." (`gpt`'s Margery)
- Ansell: "Tallow's risen a halfpenny the pound, and if you burn dips till two after midnight your eyes'll look like boiled gooseberries come Sunday." (`gem`'s Kempster)
- Ansell, at the bread-board: "The loaf wants a full thumb of the notch. Mark me, by Michaelmas we'll be eating the board." (`fab`)
- Verbeke, in June: "In Ghent also they burned the houses of the wrong people. Always it is bread, and then it is us." (`fab`)

**Who is found there.** William, Mistress Ansell, Alse, Verbeke and Gib. Heminges and Gilbert come to the street door only. Callers never come up the stair.

**How he uses it.** Sleeping, writing in two sittings a day, the Saturday counting, letters home, his two illnesses, most voice memos and almost all of his calls. The house is his shelter in June and July. Expect about 320 nights here: every night of the year except 26 Mar – 9 Apr (the Lent ride and stay), 28 Jul – 21 Aug (the walk, the stay and the return, ending with a night at the Bell) and 24 – 28 Dec (Richmond).

**Photo opportunities.**

- The desk at first light: the account book at a fresh leaf, quill, inkhorn, almanac, frost on the pane (S001, Wed 1 Jan). The expected and received columns are visibly distinct from March (`gpt`).
- The frosted close from the doorway at 22:10 under the full moon, looking west, with the church porch and elms on the left, Crosby Place's oriel beyond with one lit window, a cat's prints across the frost and breath-mist at the bottom of the frame (S006, Wed 15 Jan; `fab` prompt 1).
- The inked paper wedge, out from under the stool (Thu 27 Feb, `gpt`).
- Brooke open under a candle with the cat's flank across the page (S024, Thu 6 Mar).
- One candle, a page three-quarters written, the glover's knife, a guttered second candle (W042, Sat 17 May).
- The penny loaf on the bread-board, short of the notch (S059b, Sat 14 Jun).
- The inside of his chamber door, the bar across it, one candle, nothing else (S069, Sun 29 Jun).
- Casement light across a draft, with the neighbour's house beyond (Thu 28 Aug, G068, `gpt`).
- The close in pale November sun with sea-coal sacks and brine-tubs of salted beef (S105, Tue 11 Nov, `gem`).
- Rain-dark window and the latch under repair (Mon 22 Dec, `gpt`).
- The returned pack below the mended latch (Mon 29 Dec, `gpt`).
- The closed coffer beside an unfinished *Dream* page (S116, Wed 31 Dec).

### 4.2 The City and Shoreditch

**St Helen's and Bishopsgate: home ground**

#### `st_helens_church` · St Helen's, Bishopsgate

*51.5148, −0.0817 · T1 church and parish (from 1597); T2/T3 as his parish in 1595.*

- **What it is.** The old nunnery church, two naves side by side, the nuns' and the parish's, crowded with merchant monuments (`gem`). Sir Thomas Gresham's great chest-tomb (T1) stands there, plainer than his fortune. Sir John Spencer's carved pew is at the front. The church survives.
- **By season.**
  - *Winter and Lent:* cold stone, damp, wet wool cloaks, the sand-glass on Master Thwaite's pulpit turned twice.
  - *Easter:* lilies on the tomb ledge, rosemary, fresh rushes and communion wine.
  - *Summer:* rushes, dust in the shafts of light, the doors open for air.
  - *September:* bell-ropes streaking up into sunlit dust in the tower on the Queen's birthday.
  - *Advent:* beeswax and bay boughs, and the parish dole laid out at the south porch on St Thomas's day.
- **Who is found there.** The whole parish. Spencer and his household, with the sword-bearer at the pew's end until 28 October. The unnamed lady with her kin in the north aisle. The Ansell household in the south aisle. Thomas Morley and the parish musicians. **Master Josias Thwaite** `[invented]`, 60, the reader and curate, whose sermons run long: "And *fourteenthly*…"
- **How he uses it, and when.** Every Sunday he is in London, as statute and the parish roll require. Spencer is heard directly once, in church, giving a short cold response in the litany, and never speaks to him.
  - *Fri 28 Feb (W018):* from his west window, a face on the porch step as the Friday litany lets out.
  - *Sun 20 Apr (S035):* Easter communion beside Gresham's tomb, with the Mayor three pews off.
  - *Thu 22 May (W044):* six words with the lady at the church door.
  - *Sun 25 May (W045):* Rogation. The parish beats its bounds, with boys and peeled willow wands bumping the boundary stones round Crosby Place.
  - *Sun 1 Jun (W133):* Thwaite reads the Mayor's blue-starch precept with real feeling (the precept is heard of on Wed 28 May, W046).
  - *Sun 7 Sep (W079):* the Queen's birthday bells.
  - *Sat 1 Nov (S102a):* All Saints, morning and evening. The old prayers and the new doctrine, kept general. All Souls, next day, is only a memo at his window (S102b).
  - *Sun 30 Nov (S110):* Advent Sunday.
  - *Sun 21 Dec (S113):* St Thomas's day. The shortest day, the dole, and his 2s.
  - After 29 October Spencer still sits in his pew, but without the sword-bearer, and the pew is a private man's pew again.
- **Photo opportunities.** Gresham's tomb on Easter morning, a shaft of sun across the stone, lilies and rosemary on the ledge (S035). The nave from a parishioner's ordinary pew, at the height of a seated man (`gpt`). Boys with willow wands at a wall, the curate with his book (W045). Taut hemp bell-ropes blurring upward through dust-shafts, the ringers' shirtsleeves at the edge of the frame (Sun 7 Sep, `gem`). The St Thomas's dole table at the south porch: wheaten loaves, grey frieze cloth and stacks of pence, with the donor not centred (S113, `gem`, `gpt`).

#### `crosby_place` · Crosby Place (Crosby Hall)

*51.5143, −0.0826 · T1 neighbour; T5 any encounter · `gem` `crosby_hall`.*

- **What it is.** The great fifteenth-century merchant mansion, tall stone and timber with a soaring oriel. Richard of Gloucester once lodged there (T1 `[ext]`), and it is the Crosby Place of William's own *Richard III*. It is now the house of Sir John Spencer, Lord Mayor until 28 October, the City's richest man and the playhouses' enemy, a hundred yards from William's bed.
- **By season.** Hoarfrost on the stone mullions under the January full moon. Torches, trumpets and halberds when the Gray's Inn Prince comes to dine. Lit windows late into June and July nights while the City sits in council. In the autumn, after 29 October, the light in the oriel goes out at a civic hour.
- **Who is found there.** Spencer in his furred gown and his coach. Sheriffs' sergeants, link-boys, servants, the sword-bearer. Muttering apprentices in June.
- **How he uses it, and when.** He passes it twice daily and never enters. It is the year's emblem of the City's power and the contrast to his rented room (`gpt`).
  - *Sat 4 Jan (W001):* the Prince of Purpoole's mock-court rides up Bishopsgate Street to dine with the Mayor here (T1). William watches from the mouth of the close with Burbage, and the two of them laugh over the Night of Errors (`gem`).
  - *Wed 15 Jan (S006):* frost on its roof-line under the full moon.
  - *Fri 27 Jun (S067):* a gallows is set before its gate (T1). He sees the raw frame at evening, after the crowd has gone.
  - *Wed 29 Oct (S101):* Spencer is out, and Slany is Mayor (Slany is not a neighbour and not a character).
- **Photo opportunities.** The Prince's plumes, tabards and a trumpeter passing in torch-smoke, seen over citizens' backs (W001). Moonlit frost on the stone mullions and the frozen kennel (S006; `gem`, `gpt`). **The empty gallows-frame in the lane before the gate at evening: raw timber, nobody near, shutters closed, chalk scrawls illegible on the gatepost** (S067; this is the canon limit).

#### `bishopsgate` · Bishopsgate Street and the gate

*Gate 51.5165, −0.0812 (est.) · T1 geography · `gem` `bishopsgate_bedlam`.*

- **What it is.** His daily road north. First the street with its inns, then the gate with its statue-niches, its chains and its watch-house, then Bishopsgate Without. Beyond lie Bedlam on the left, Norton Folgate and Shoreditch. Barnaby Yaxley's barber-surgeon's shop is in the street, and so is the Bull.
- **By season.**
  - *November to March:* mud ankle-deep, churned kennel mud, and the Houndsditch stink in thaws.
  - *May Day:* white hawthorn and ribbons across the arch at dawn, and a maypole in a suburb lane off the street.
  - *Midsummer Eve:* bonfires in the street under the watch's cressets.
  - *July:* dust and flies. The proclamation nailed at the gate, and the provost-marshal's mounted men beside it.
  - *Accession night, 17 November:* a bonfire at the corner and every steeple ringing.
  - *Always:* carts, the water-bearers' cry, the Bull's ostlers, a pie-woman at the gate.
- **Who is found there.** Gate-warders, carters, silk-weavers going out to Norton Folgate, apprentices, beggars, Yaxley in his doorway, and William twice a day.
- **How he uses it, and when.** It is the barometer of London's temper on his commute (`gem`).
  - *Thu 1 May (S040):* May Day.
  - *Mon 23 Jun (S065a):* the Midsummer bonfires.
  - *Fri 4 Jul (S070):* the proclamation of martial law at the gate (T1), with Sir Thomas Wilford's men by it.
  - *Tue 14 Oct (W089):* the tooth drawn at Yaxley's, 12d, with 2d for cloves before. "Hold the stool, sir, and think on your sins. It distracts." He hits a heart-rate peak of 148 in the chair at 10:05.
  - *Mon 17 Nov (S106):* the bonfire night.
- **Photo opportunities.** Hawthorn garlands across the arch at dawn (`gem`), and a maypole with ribbons and girls with green boughs in a lane off the street (S040). A Midsummer bonfire, faces lit from below, the watch's cressets passing behind (S065a). The proclamation at the gate, the royal arms at its head, a mounted provost's man beyond (S070). The Accession Day bonfire at a street corner at night (S106).

#### `bedlam` · Bethlem Hospital

*51.5175, −0.0815 (est.) · T1 geography and incident · `gem`'s Bedlam beats (S054) map here.*

- **What it is.** A low range with a gate and a low stone wall just outside Bishopsgate on the west side, right on his path to Shoreditch.
- **By season.** Sounds from within that he has learned not to hear. They carry more in summer, when the windows are open.
- **Who is found there.** Keepers at the gate and visitors who pay to look, which he does not.
- **How he uses it, and when.** He passes it on every Theatre day. **Fri 6 Jun (S054):** the apprentices carry a man out. He sees the aftermath and the running, not the rescue.
- **Photo opportunities.** The gate standing open, a wrenched hasp on the wicket, a dropped cap in the road, backs running north (S054; `fab` shot 23 with `gem`'s hasp).

#### `bull_inn` · The Bull, Bishopsgate Street

*51.5157, −0.0833 · T1 site (an inn-yard playing place); T5 his use · `gem` `bull_inn_bishopsgate`.*

- **What it is.** A galleried carriers' and players' inn three minutes from his door (0.2 km), with a horse-yard and a sixpenny dinner ordinary.
- **By season.** A roaring sea-coal fire, a spit and boiled-beef steam in winter. A cool dark taproom and ale in summer. A spiced cup in December.
- **Who is found there.** Ostlers, carriers, sharers dining between rehearsal and play, Hunsdon's grooms, Nashe when someone else is paying.
- **How he uses it, and when.** Hiring horses, dinners near home, and tavern news without walking to Cheapside.
  - *Sun 2 Mar (S021):* the Shrove Sunday supper with Nashe: roast beef before the fast, the year's first Nashe supper.
  - *Mon 17 Mar (W021):* he bespeaks his Lent horse here at 12d a day plus fodder, and collects it on Wed 26 Mar.
  - *Thu 10 Apr (W032c):* the bay goes back at three, sixteen days at 12d.
  - *Sun 13 Jul (M022):* Nashe's mid-July supper of roast mutton and Rhenish. Nashe talks for two hours of the terrors of the night and falls silent once, over a third cup, remembering Marlowe. William slips home at nine, "in pain", before the fourth pint (`gem`, re-dated to before Queen Mab).
  - *Mon 29 Dec (M056):* the sharers, landed from Richmond, take roast capon and lamb's-wool here at half past two. Nobody argues, and nobody says what everyone at the board feels (`gem`).
- **Photo opportunities.** A roast chine with a mustard pot, manchet crusts and pewter tankards on a scarred board (S021, `gem`). The hired bay in the inn-yard on the morning of 26 Mar, with saddlebags and the road bundle of separate small parcels (`gpt`).

#### `split_hazel` · The Split Hazel `[invented]`

*51.5155, −0.0830 (est.) · T5 · from `gpt`.*

- **What it is.** A modest ordinary near Bishopsgate, a few doors from the Bull. It is a low room with two long tables and a bench against a wall that gives off yesterday's warmth. Alice keeps rooms above it. This is the house near home, where William goes on Sundays and evenings. The Jackdaw is the company's weekday ordinary in Shoreditch, and the two are different houses.
- **By season.** Winter: onions, smoke and wet wool. Summer: ale, flies and hot washing water.
- **Who is found there.** **Alice Brambleby** `[invented]`, 41, hospitable but not intimate by default. She can stop an argument by asking who is paying. She likes Kemp's custom and limits his noise, and she respects Heminges. She does not indulge the assumption that wit is payment. After June she is more careful about strangers and repeated political claims, and by autumn she has a rule: a man may tell news, but he must say where he heard it. Kemp, Bryan, Pope, Nashe once, and parish regulars also come.
- **How he uses it, and when.** A public room where talk is available without compulsory carousing (`gpt`). It is the year's news-room near home.
  - *Fri 3 Jan (G001):* Kemp's story needs more room than the table. Alice makes him move his elbows. The court reward is "expected", and Alice remarks that expected money has eaten several dinners in her house.
  - *Tue 11 Feb (S014b):* the evening of Davy Gannet's news, the room argues Raleigh's gold. Certainty increases with distance from the ship.
  - *Sun 18 May (G038):* Nashe's mid-May supper. Nashe spends the table, and Alice ends the evening's most elaborate argument by setting down the actual bill.
  - *Sat 12 Jul (G051):* under martial law, a supper kept quieter than usual. Kemp demonstrates a comic hesitation using only a cup and finds that restraint improves it.
  - *Sat 26 Jul (S076):* after the last play before the break, the evening the news reached the tiring-house, the room is all Cornwall and Tower Hill: Mousehole, Paul, Newlyn and Penzance, with varying confidence. Alice asks each teller where his account began.
  - *Fri 12 Sep (G074):* a parcel from home, come by the carrier on the 11th, tied with a knot of such confidence that Kemp makes opening it an exhibition. Alice produces a knife and ends the contest.
  - *Wed 5 Nov (S103b):* Bryan brings the Essex news, had from a Carey groom come down from Richmond, and distinguishes the reported event from the increasingly confident accounts of Essex's private feelings.
- **Sample line.** Alice: "You may dispute the government after you have moved your elbow from the gravy."
- **Photo opportunities.** The long table after supper: trenchers, a cup Kemp has turned into a prop, elbows (Sat 12 Jul). The over-confident parcel knot on the board (G074, Fri 12 Sep).

#### `cross_keys_inn` · The Cross Keys, Gracechurch Street

*51.5122, −0.0852 · T1 Hunsdon's letter of 8 Oct 1594; T3 that the company played here in winter 1594/5, taken as fact in this canon.*

- **What it is.** The company's winter house, from 1 January to Shrove Tuesday, 4 March. It is a deep galleried inn-yard on the west side of Gracechurch Street, next to the Bell Inn yard. A scaffold stage on trestles stands against the stable side under three tiers of balustraded chambers (`gem`), and a parlour serves as the tiring-room. Hunsdon's promise holds: start at two, done by half past four, no drums or trumpets (T1). It is a six-minute walk from home (0.45 km).
- **By season.** Only winter: horse, wet straw, hot ale and mulled sack from the taproom, braziers in the galleries, packed woollen bodies steaming in air near freezing, breath smoking. The light goes by the fifth act. Deliveries and the stage's needs compete for the yard (`gpt`).
- **Who is found there.** Citizens and their wives, Inns men in term, the innkeeper's ostlers who know every cue, all the sharers and boys, and Abel Garnish with the plot pasted up backstage.
- **How he uses it, and when.** Rehearsals at nine and plays at two. In winter the Jackdaw is too far, so he eats at the inn.
  - *Fri 17 Jan (S007):* a rehearsal from parts and a play in cold light, played with a fever. Cowley comes in twice on a doubled cue, and William alters three cue-words.
  - *Tue 18 Feb (W014):* full rehearsal.
  - *Thu 20 Feb (W015):* ***Love's Labour's Lost* first played**, £7 11s in the boxes. The last scene is played in blue dusk with two torches. At supper he learns what happened at Westminster.
  - *Sat 22 Feb (S117):* the gatherers' boxes broken open on the parlour table.
  - *Tue 4 Mar (S022):* Shrove Tuesday, the last play.
  - *Mon 10 Mar (W118):* the baskets are carted to Shoreditch, and after that the company never returns. The Mayor's hostility closes the door for the next winter (T5 on a T3 gap).
- **Photo opportunities.** The yard at half past four with the stage torches just lit and breath smoking in the galleries (S007). A cue roll in a boy's hands with cold boards beyond (`gpt`). A playbill on a post in Gracechurch Street, black-letter and rain-spotted, title only (W015). The boxes broken open: a drift of pennies and halfpence, with Heminges's hands sorting (S117).

#### `leadenhall` · Leadenhall market

*51.5127, −0.0834 · T5 errands · `gem` `leadenhall_market`.*

- **What it is.** The quadrangular stone market-hall and civic granary at the top of Gracechurch Street: meat, meal, butter, poultry, cheese, leather, candles. It is the nearest market to home, five minutes south.
- **By season.**
  - *Lent:* salt ling and stockfish.
  - *May and June:* dear Cheshire cheese, shrinking penny loaves, assize weighers and anxious housewives under the dearth.
  - *August, on his return:* thin stalls.
  - *Martinmas:* beef for salting.
  - *Always:* cold iron and animal breath in winter, stronger smells in summer.
- **Who is found there.** Bakers, butchers, assize weighers, Mistress Ansell and Alse with baskets, a constable in June.
- **How he uses it, and when.** On a writing day he walks a loop here. It is where he sees the price of the year, and he observes prices through purchases, not lectures (`gpt`).
  - *Thu 29 May (S050):* Ascension. He buys cheese, and the wheat looks thin.
  - *Sat 14 Jun (S059b):* **the morning after the Southwark butter riot.** Butter is guarded by a constable. He did not cross the river. Only this aftermath is his.
  - *Tue 11 Nov (S105):* the Martinmas errand with Mistress Ansell, because he has offered an opinion about household expense. He is useful for carrying and less useful for predicting what things should cost (`gpt`).
- **Photo opportunities.** A shrunken penny loaf in his hand beside the assize scale (S050; `gem`'s balance). Butter firkins on a stall, a constable with a bill leaning by them (S059b, `fab` shot 24). Winter provisions being assessed, not a decorative still life (S105, Tue 11 Nov, `gpt`).

#### `tower_hill` · Tower Hill

*51.5098, −0.0767 · T1 events; **a location point on Sat 1 Feb only**.*

- **What it is.** The wide sloping ground north-west of the Tower moat, with the river stairs below. It is 0.9 km from his door.
- **The one day: Sat 1 Feb (S011).** He walks down from the Cross Keys before dinner with Burbage, Kemp and Phillips coming down too, to see the Gray's Inn Prince of Purpoole come up on the tide in fifteen bannered barges with trumpets, shawms and culverin salutes, and land at the Tower stairs (T1). His view is restricted, from the bank among other people.
- **After that he walks wide of it.** On Sun 29 Jun (S069) he hears the riot from his room. On Thu 24 Jul (S077), when five men die there, he stays in. There is no location point either day, and nothing is shown.
- **Photo opportunities.** Fifteen barges with streamers coming up on grey flood-water below the Tower, gun-smoke hanging, an obstructing shoulder at the frame edge (S011; `fab` shot 6, `gpt`, `gem`). The scaffold stays out of frame.

**Shoreditch and the fields**

#### `the_theatre` · The Theatre, Holywell

*51.5248, −0.0795 · T2 his workplace.*

- **What it is.** James Burbage's timber polygon of 1576, standing in open ground outside the City's jurisdiction. It has a yard, three thatch-and-tile galleries, a thrust stage under painted heavens held up by two oak pillars painted as marble (`gem`), a tiring-house behind and a hut above. It is the company's house from Easter Monday. **It is nineteen years old**: its sill-beams are spongy with rot, and the ground lease has two years left (`gem`, `gpt`). On playing days it flies a flag with the Chamberlain's badge, a white swan from the Hunsdon crest (T2 `[ext]`). The tiring-house smells of old velvet, size, lamp-black, sweat and rabbit-skin glue. The plot hangs on its peg by the stage door. Garnish's stool and desk stand behind the arras. It is an 18-minute walk from home (1.4 km), and a playing day there and back is about 2.7 km.
- **By season.**
  - *Late February:* wet grass at the sills, the rot showing.
  - *April:* raw wind across Holywell fields for the first rehearsals.
  - *Spring:* green fields to the north, larks, archers at Finsbury, afternoon sun on two thousand faces.
  - *June:* a restless yard, Margery Pell's eyes everywhere.
  - *July:* thin houses, flies, the marshal's rider at the lane end.
  - *August:* dark and padlocked, then the smell of fresh paint on the return.
  - *Autumn:* full houses for *Richard*, nutshells underfoot, wet cloaks, and from late October torches for the last act.
  - *December:* a brazier in the tiring-house, court doublets airing over a copper charcoal-pan, the last act in dusk, torches lit for the tomb.
- **Who is found there.** The whole company. The sharers are Burbage, Kemp, Heminges, Phillips, Pope and Bryan. The hired men are Cowley, Sly, Sincler, Larkham, Blackwage, Raye and Hoope. The boys are Nick Sorrell, Arthur Meeks and Sam Latchet. Garnish has the book, Edith Kettlewood her needle, and Goodwife Pell is at the gallery stair: "Lawyers, 'prentices, three bad women and a bishop's man. Forty-one shillings in my box." James and Cuthbert Burbage come and go. Giles Allen is seen at the ditch, never in talk with William. Inns men come in term.
- **How he uses it, and when.** Rehearsal at nine, play at two, reckonings on Saturday. He plays on about four afternoons in six. When Gaunt is dead he watches the rest from the tiring-house door.
  - *Tue 25 Feb (G013):* James walks William and Cuthbert round the damp sills and jabs his joiner's knife two inches into a spongy oak post laid in 1576 (`gem`). James's hand indicates a damp joint (`gpt`).
  - *Fri 11 Apr (S033):* the first rehearsal of the spring, with a cue rewritten overnight.
  - *Mon 21 Apr (S036):* Easter Monday. **The Theatre reopens.**
  - *Thu 8 May (W040):* Phillips's jig of the slippers.
  - *Mon 30 Jun, Thu 24 Jul:* no play.
  - *Mon 7 Jul (S072):* in the tiring-house, the sharers weigh a tour against waiting out the summer. Heminges's case wins: play on to Sat 26 Jul, dark in August, open at Bartholomew. That evening is Burbage's 27th birthday supper at the Jackdaw (W055).
  - *Sat 26 Jul (S076):* the last play before the break, *Errors*. Cornwall's news reaches the tiring-house.
  - *28 Jul – 23 Aug:* dark.
  - *Mon 25 Aug (S083):* reopens, the same day as the Rose.
  - *Wed 27 Aug (W076):* the first *Richard* rehearsal. Kemp finds no fool in it. William's back has a bad day lifting a property basket.
  - *Thu 11 Sep (S093):* rehearsing *Richard* with Burbage.
  - *Fri 26 Sep (W084):* the *Richard* dress rehearsal.
  - *Thu 2 Oct (W085):* ***Richard II* first played**, £11 6s. A mirror is laid on the throne's seat. His heart is 138 as Gaunt and 112 standing at the door through Act 4.
  - *Sat 4 Oct (W086):* the week's reckoning.
  - *Thu 9 Oct (S096):* Michaelmas term brings the Inns men to *Richard*.
  - *Mon 20 Oct (W090):* the sharers, behind the tiring-house door: who pays at Christmas, now Heneage is dead?
  - *Mon 17 Nov:* no play (Accession Day).
  - *Wed 3 Dec (W099):* the *Romeo* dress rehearsal. James speaks of a great hall.
  - *Sat 6 Dec (W100):* ***Romeo and Juliet* first played**, £12 13s, the year's best house. Dusk on the tomb, torches, Mistress Ansell in the gallery, and the full moon over Shoreditch on the walk home.
- **Photo opportunities.** A close view of the rotted mortise beside wet grass (G013, Tue 25 Feb, `gem`, `gpt`). Kemp's oversized yellow slippers propped by Phillips's cittern on the boards (Thu 8 May, `gem`). Burbage's hands holding the crown before rehearsal (S093, `gpt`). Heminges's sheet of towns beside a jug and a heel of bread, the tour weighed and set aside (S072, `gpt`). The stage before the house fills: a throne on its dais, a mirror laid on the seat (W085). The gatherers' boxes spilling pence, groats and sixpences onto the baize at the Saturday reckoning (W086, `gem`). Cressets casting orange light across packed gallery tiers at an October dusk (`gem`). Workers at the Theatre after a performance, tired and intent (`gpt`). The plot on its peg beside a boy's rolled part tied with thread (late November, `gem`). Court doublets airing over the charcoal-pan (December, `gem`). Torches on the stage at four in a December dusk, a small figure laid on a bier, the yard's faces lifted (W100).

#### `the_curtain` · The Curtain

*51.5229, −0.0804 · T2/T3; not the company's house in 1595.*

- **What it is.** The other Shoreditch playhouse (1577), about 200 m south of the Theatre across muddy Curtain Close. It is rectangular, as MOLA found (T1), and its acoustic is rougher. It is the first playhouse on his walk (1.2 km from home).
- **By season.** A muddy lane in spring. Afternoon sword-prizes and overflow crowds in autumn. Its noise leaks into the street.
- **Who is found there.** Masters of Defence and their prize-fighters, a tumbling troupe, hired players. Cuthbert Burbage sometimes eyes it as a fallback, should Giles Allen ever make good his threats (T5, `gem`).
- **How he uses it, and when.** A landmark he passes daily, whose bills he reads. In late August and early September he stops for a fencers' prize to watch how a man falls when the point goes in under the arm, with Mercutio's death written on Sat 6 Sep. The company does not play here in 1595, and there is no invented move.
- **Photo opportunities.** A fencers' bill on the gatepost, black-letter, title only. The prize stage from the back of the crowd, two blades crossed.

#### `burbage_house_holywell` · The Burbage house, Holywell Street

*51.5251, −0.0790 (est.) · T2 that the Burbages lived by the playhouse; T5 detail.*

- **What it is.** James's house by the playhouse, with a joiner's shop behind. Its hall has a long table where the sharers meet out of season. Mistress Burbage's kitchen is beyond it.
- **By season.** A big fire in winter and the smell of shavings from the shop. In summer the door stands open to the yard.
- **Who is found there.** James, now about 64, Cuthbert and Richard. Ellen Burbage, "Mistress Burbage", in her kitchen. The sharers.
- **How he uses it, and when.** The company's private room for money and decisions.
  - *Mon 6 Jan (W003):* Twelfth Day, the bean-cake.
  - *Tue 28 Jan (W009):* parts given out, and the first reading of *Love's Labour's Lost*.
  - *Sun 26 Jan (S009):* supper, on the day of the Derby wedding at Greenwich.
  - *Sat 15 Mar (S027):* **the £20 split** at the long table: 37s 6d a share, eight parts. Heminges opens with "the warrant has no weight until someone brings the coin".
  - *Mon 30 Jun (W053):* the morning after the riot, he reads *Richard* to the fellows here.
  - *Wed 19 Nov (W096):* *Romeo* read to the company, with Pope's Nurse.
  - *Fri 19 Dec:* James confides the £600 plan for the Blackfriars hall (see `fields_printing_house`).
  - Out of season, on reckoning Saturdays, the sharers meet here.
- **Photo opportunities.** The company's allocation sheet before any coin is out (S027, `gpt`). Then the £20 in gold and silver on the long table, a ledger beside it, seven men's hands at the edges (S027, `fab` shot 10).

#### `the_jackdaw` · Tipping's ordinary, at the sign of the Jackdaw, Holywell Lane `[invented]`

*51.5243, −0.0788 (est.) · T5.*

- **What it is.** The company's ordinary, a minute from the Theatre. One long room, with a settle by the fire that is Burbage's by conquest and slates behind the door. Dinner is 6d: mutton on flesh days, ling and buttered eggs on fish days. In the dearth the portions shrink and the price holds. The sign feeds the running joke about Greene's "upstart crow".
- **By season.** Winter fug and wet wool. In summer the door is propped open on the lane and the flies come in. In autumn it is crowded and loud after *Richard*.
- **Who is found there.** **Agnes Tipping** `[invented]`, 45. She gives credit by the slate, forgives Burbage everything and charges Kemp double for spillage. "Dinner's sixpence, wit's extra." The whole company eats here.
- **How he uses it, and when.** Dinner on playing days from 11:00 to 12:00, and sometimes supper from 17:00 to 18:30. It is the company's talk-shop. In winter it is too far from the Cross Keys and he eats at the inn.
  - *Sat 11 Jan (W004):* he reads Act 5 of *Love's Labour's Lost* to Burbage, who says nothing for a full minute.
  - *Mon 7 Jul (W055):* Burbage's 27th birthday supper.
  - *Tue 15 Jul (W057):* Queen Mab.
  - *Sat 22 Nov (W097):* three pages for Kemp, and the truce. Kemp says *thou* for the first time.
- **Photo opportunities.** Modest food shared after work, costume pieces nearby (`gpt`). The slates behind the door, the marks illegible. Burbage's empty settle by the fire.

#### `st_leonards_shoreditch` · St Leonard's, Shoreditch

*51.5268, −0.0774 · T2 milieu.*

- **What it is.** The parish church of the playhouse district. Its bells carry to the tiring-house.
- **Who is found there.** The players' households. William is not a parishioner.
- **How he uses it.** As a sound and a landmark. The tiring-house keeps time by its bells.
- **Photo opportunities.** Its tower under the full moon on the walk home on Sat 6 Dec (W100).

#### `finsbury_fields` · Finsbury and Moorfields

*51.5205, −0.0870 (est.) · T2 milieu; T5 his walks.*

- **What it is.** Open ground west of the Bishopsgate road, marshy toward Moorfields. It has archers' butts, laundresses' lines and windmills on the rise.
- **By season.** Hoarfrost and standing water in winter. Larks and drying linen in spring. Dust and the archers in summer.
- **Who is found there.** Archers, laundresses, idle apprentices on holidays.
- **How he uses it, and when.** His walking-and-muttering ground for memos and for learning lines, and a dawn walk when a speech will not come. *Sun 9 Feb (W011)*: lines on the tables. *Fri 21 Mar (W023)*: a memo of Verona names. It lies about 700 m from home, **outside the radius of fear**, so there are no Finsbury walks from 13 Jun to 5 Jul.
- **Photo opportunities.** Laundresses' lines flapping, windmills on the rise. A single archer at the butts in a low winter sun.

**West of the City: Paul's, Blackfriars, Holborn, Clerkenwell**

#### `pauls_churchyard` · St Paul's Churchyard and Paul's Walk

*51.5136, −0.0992; Paul's Cross 51.5142, −0.0977 · T2 milieu; T5 a given visit · `gem` `st_pauls_churchyard`.*

- **What it is.** The pre-fire cathedral (never the later dome) and the booksellers' signs crowded against its buttresses. Harrison's **White Greyhound** sells *Venus* and *Lucrece* (T1). The nave is the news exchange of England, Paul's Walk. The open-air pulpit of Paul's Cross stands in the north-east yard for sermons and proclamations. It is 15–17 minutes from home by Cornhill, Poultry and Cheapside (1.2 km).
- **By season.** Paste, new paper, damp stone, ink and dust. A hundred men talking at once in the nave, from ten to noon and from three to five. Ballad-sellers singing their wares. Crisp newly stitched quartos in spring. Rain on the stall-boards in autumn.
- **Who is found there.** John Harrison, stationers and their prentices, news-mongers, masterless soldiers, lawyers, post-riders, Inns students. Michael Drayton, a Warwickshire voice at the stalls. Field on his errands.
- **How he uses it, and when.** He comes twice a week when he can, reads standing, and buys less than he reads.
  - *Thu 6 Mar (S024):* a worn Brooke, 6d.
  - *Tue 15 Apr (W033):* Daniel's *Civil Wars*, 2s, after weeks of reading it at the stall.
  - *Sat 17 May (S047):* Southwell's *Saint Peters Complaint*, 6d. He reads the epistle on poets, lovers and liars standing up.
  - *Sat 31 May (W047):* the *True Tragedy* in octavo: his old play, another company's name on it, and no author's.
  - *Mon 16 Jun:* a crowd is broken up near Paul's. **He hears of it and does not walk there** (canon amendment 2; Paul's is outside the radius of fear).
  - *Sat 26 Jul (S078):* the riot-execution pamphlet on a stall (T1 imprint, Allde for Blackwall).
  - *Thu 18 Sep (W082):* Field shows him Covell's "Sweet Shakspeare" in a Cambridge book at a stall.
  - *Mon 29 Sep (S092):* talk of the Golden Pen contest (T1), which he calls the only prize-fight he could win.
  - *Thu 23 Oct (M040):* the supper with Samuel Daniel at an ordinary in Paternoster Row, hard by (`gem`). It is Daniel's one supper of the year, the two poets talk craft, and they part at Paul's Chain.
  - *Mon 17 Nov (S106):* Bishop Fletcher's Accession sermon at the Cross (T1). William passes the throng on his way west to the tilt and does not stop.
  - *Mon 1 Dec (S111):* Burby's entry of *Edward III* is heard of.
- **Photo opportunities.** A bookstall in rain, quartos under a cloth. A ballad-seller mid-song. Southwell's book opening in his hands, with no supernatural lighting (S047, `gpt`). The *True Tragedy* octavo face-up on a stall (W047). The Covell page, legible only if checked against the 1595 imprint, otherwise illegible (W082). The crowd at Paul's Cross from its edge.

#### `fields_printing_house` · Richard Field's house and shop, Blackfriars

*51.5126, −0.1029 · T2 friendship; T5 visits · `gpt` `field_printing_house`, `gem` `field_printshop_blackfriars`.*

- **What it is.** The old Vautrollier house in the Blackfriars liberty, a quiet sunlit precinct inside old Dominican stone walls (`gem`). On the ground floor are two oak screw-presses, cases of type under the windows, sheets drying on cords overhead like washing, and a corrector's desk. The family lives above, and there is a French pot on the fire. Sir George Carey's house stands nearby in the same precinct, and so does the empty stone hall of the old friary. It is 1.6 km from home, 20–25 minutes.
- **By season.** Year round: the sharp smell of ink (varnish and lamp-black), lye, turpentine, boiled linseed oil and damp Rochelle paper. The thump and creak of the bar. Jacqueline calling the apprentices in two languages. In December, a warm hearth of sea-coal.
- **Who is found there.** Richard Field, and Jacqueline, who runs the accounts and controls access and pace (`gpt`). Compositors and apprentices.
- **How he uses it, and when.** A sanctuary from the playhouse, a place to be a Stratford man, the only place he sees a `proof`, and his source of good paper at trade price (4½d the quire).
  - *Wed 19 Mar (W022):* Field lends him Holinshed, volume two.
  - *Sun 4 May (W039):* dinner with the Fields.
  - *May:* the Plutarch at trade price, 14s unbound.
  - *Thu 15 May (S046):* the Plutarch sheets coming off the press.
  - *Fri 19 Dec (M052):* in the lane outside, in a thin snow, on the eve of the new moon, James Burbage and Richard come out of the old stone hall with a carpenter's rule under James's cloak. Over a hot gill James swears him to secrecy about £600 and a roof (`gem`; T5 ahead of the T1 purchase of Feb 1596).
  - *Tue 30 Dec (M057):* by Field's fire he reads him loose pages of the *Dream*, Bottom among them. Field laughs until he wipes his eyes (`gem`; pages only, about twelve leaves, unfinished and untitled).
  - Field asks for the Holinshed every month.
- **Photo opportunities.** The press-room: Plutarch sheets hung to dry, a pressman's arm on the bar (S046). Proof, type and a paper stack (`gpt`). Sea-coal glow on loose *Dream* leaves and two pewter cups on the settle, with no title page (M057, `gem`, amended).

#### `the_bell_carter_lane` · The Bell, Carter Lane

*51.5131, −0.1000 (est., ±150 m) · T1 for 1598 (Quiney's letter); T5 for 1595 · `gem` `bell_carter_lane`.*

- **What it is.** The Stratford men's inn just south of Paul's and the carrier's London end. It has a yard of packhorses and a taproom full of Warwickshire. Letters are left with the host. It is 1.4 km from home, about 18 minutes.
- **By season.** Wet pack-saddles, Warwickshire hay and bacon. Mud-caked panniers in February and March. Dusty summer bundles. Frost on the Christmas hampers.
- **Who is found there.** **Thomas Hobbins** `[invented]`, 49, the carrier, with his six packhorses, who talks in distances and prices: 2d a letter, 6d a parcel. Also Richard Quiney in November, Gilbert, and Warwickshire drovers.
- **How he uses it, and when.** He sends letters, groats, gloves and silver home, and goes on carrier Thursdays after the play. Over the year his shouted add-ons give way to written lists and receipts (`gpt`). He drinks Warwickshire ale when homesick (`gem`). **Carrier in:** 16 Jan, 6 Feb, 27 Feb, 20 Mar, 24 Apr, 15 May, 5 Jun, 26 Jun, 17 Jul, 11 Sep, 2 Oct, 23 Oct, 13 Nov, 4 Dec and 18 Dec, all Thursdays.
  - *Thu 20 Mar (W121):* worsted hose knitted by his mother, and word that the Oxford road is drying in the March wind (`gem`).
  - *Thu 26 Jun (S066):* the quarter's 60s goes home. The day falls inside the radius of fear and the Bell is 1.4 km off, so he does not walk that far: Heminges's man carries the canvas and a line to the Bell for him.
  - *Mon 21 Jul (S074):* **Hobbins's one unscheduled arrival**, with Anne's four lines about the second fire. Gilbert's garbled call came an hour before.
  - *Thu 21 Aug (W074):* he walks in from the road with the packhorses, and sleeps the night here in a shared bed, 2d.
  - *Wed 1 – Thu 2 Oct (W400):* the Michaelmas 60s goes home by Thursday's carrier, on the day *Richard II* opens. Gilbert, who lodges hard by in St Bride's, carries the canvas to the Bell the evening before.
  - *Sat 15 Nov (W095):* Quiney calls from here.
  - *Mon 24 Nov (W098):* supper with Quiney. The brief is authorised (T1), William gives £2 to it, Quiney repays £1 of the August £2, and William declines to lend more.
  - *Thu 18 Dec (W105):* the Christmas pack: 60s, spices, oranges, cloth, and holland linen for Joan.
- **Photo opportunities.** Anne's fire letter, with carrier's dust on the wrapper, opened in the yard (S074, `gpt`). Mud-caked panniers (February, `gem`). The Christmas pack and remittance list on the taproom board (W105, `gpt`).

#### `st_johns_clerkenwell` · The Revels Office, St John's

*51.5222, −0.1026 (est.) · T2 · `gpt` `revels_office`, `gem` `clerkenwell_revels`.*

- **What it is.** The old priory of St John of Jerusalem, its gatehouse and panelled chambers, where Edmund Tilney keeps the Revels. There are chests of court apparel, masque drawings, tinsel properties, ledger shelves, waiting benches, and a long room where plays are heard before Christmas. Tilney's authority shows in procedure, not menace (`gpt`). It is about 2.4 km from home by Moorgate and Smithfield.
- **By season.** Cool, dusty quiet: wax, parchment and stored cloth. Work is always interrupted by other work (`gpt`).
- **Who is found there.** Tilney, Revels clerks, Garnish, rival book-holders on the bench.
- **How he uses it, and when.** Licensing at 7s a play.
  - *Fri 24 Jan (W008):* *Love's Labour's Lost* licensed.
  - *Thu 10 Jul (W056):* *Richard II* licensed with the deposition cut: "Leave out the deposing…"
  - *Thu 13 Nov (W094):* *Romeo* licensed untouched.
  - *Tue 16 Dec (W104):* the court rehearsal before Tilney.
- **Photo opportunities.** The foot of the *Richard II* book with Tilney's hand and the Revels seal, the signature illegible (W056, `gem`, amended). Masque drawings pinned on a panel, and the apparel chests.

#### `southampton_house` · Southampton House, Holborn

*51.5181, −0.1115 (est.) · T3 contact · coordinate corrected by canon § 4 (Holborn, by Chancery Lane; `gpt` and `gem`'s −0.1205 is the later Bloomsbury house).*

- **What it is.** Great gates on Holborn with gardens behind. **He gets no further than the porter's lodge.** What lies inside, the gallery, the lute, the Italian books in Florio's study and the creditors waiting in the outer hall (`gem`), he knows only from Florio and a glimpse through the gate: a court, a groom with greyhounds, a visitor kept waiting on a bench (`gpt`).
- **By season.** A coal brazier in the lodge in February. Shutters closed in the autumn when the Earl is at court, which he hears of and does not see.
- **Who is found there.** The porter. Florio and the Earl, at the far end of calls: Florio's three and my lord's one.
- **How he uses it, and when.** *Mon 10 Feb (W115):* in the morning he leaves a paper for Florio at the lodge, and hears nothing until Florio's call of Tue 18 Mar (W120). No sonnet ever goes there (canon § 7 fix 1). Florio's calls come from here (W120, W302, S098), and so does the one call from my lord of Southampton on *Tue 23 Sep (S091)*.
- **Photo opportunities.** The gates from Holborn, with a groom leading a horse out. The lodge window with the brazier glow.

#### `grays_inn` · Gray's Inn hall, High Holborn

*51.5191, −0.1126 · T1 the Night of Errors (*Gesta Grayorum*); T3 him · from `gem`.*

- **What it is.** The hammer-beam Tudor hall of the most ambitiously literary Inn of Court, where *The Comedy of Errors* played amid glorious chaos on Innocents' Night, 28 December 1594. It is 2.5 km from home.
- **By season.** Torchlit revels in January, the tail of the Prince of Purpoole's reign. Black-gowned students in term.
- **Who is found there.** Young barristers and the Purpoole revellers, the sharpest and quickest-witted young audience in London.
- **How he uses it, and when.** **Remembered more than visited.** *Thu 9 Jan (S003)*: two Gray's Inn students come round to the Cross Keys parlour and retell the Night of Errors, and nobody tells it the same way. He does not enter the hall in 1595. In term the students stream up to Shoreditch for *Richard* (S096), and he is cultivating them.
- **Photo opportunities.** The hall's hammer-beams in torchlight, the screen at the far end where a stage once stood.
