#### `smithfield` · West Smithfield, Bartholomew Fair

*51.5187, −0.1008 (est.) · T2; the fair's exact 1595 days are not established.*

- **What it is.** The vast trampled livestock ground outside Newgate and Aldersgate: cattle-pens and horse-coursers most weeks. At Bartholomewtide it becomes a canvas city of clothiers, puppet-motions, tumblers, ballad-singers and cutpurses, with Bartholomew pigs on the spit. He crosses its edge on the way to Clerkenwell.
- **By season.** Mud and horse-trading on ordinary days. On 24 August: smoke, grease, gingerbread, trumpets and a sea of hats.
- **Who is found there.** Drovers, horse-dealers, fairground folk, cutpurses. On the fair day, Burbage, Phillips, Pope, Arthur Meeks and little Sam Latchet with him; Kemp dancing in a ring; Gilbert behind a stall of points.
- **How he uses it, and when.** **Sun 24 Aug (S082):** after church, two days home from Stratford. There are booths, cloth, roast pig and puppets. A cutpurse is taken. He buys three gingerbread men before he thinks, and they go home to Alse. Sam is allowed a small choice and spends longer making it than the men expected (`gpt`). No public play is attended, and it is 14,200 steps of crowd-shuffling.
- **Photo opportunities.** A pig turning on a spit over charcoal under striped canvas, a puppet booth, a sea of hats (S082). A narrow view between moving people (`gpt`).

---

### 4.3 Bankside and the river

#### `old_swan_stairs` · Old Swan Stairs

*51.5094, −0.0889 (est., ±100 m) · T5 modelling point.*

- **What it is.** A slippery timber watermen's landing just above London Bridge, Davy Gannet's stair. From here he takes a wherry west (2d to Whitehall) or across (1d). Boats land and embark above the bridge and never shoot the arches (the source book flags the practice as unverified; keep it as the rule). It is 1.0 km from home, 12 minutes.
- **By season.**
  - *January:* cakes of ice at the stair's foot and a freezing wind off the water.
  - *Midsummer:* wherries decked with green birch and lilies (he does not see them this year; on 24 Jun he keeps to the close).
  - *July:* stinking low-tide mud, stranded wherries, a dog asleep on the stair, a copper moon over the bridge.
  - *Autumn:* mist, and the Lord Mayor's barges.
  - *December:* squalls, then frost on the tilt-boat's canvas hoops.
  - *Always:* mud exposed and covered by the tide, competing watermen shouting "Westward ho!"
- **Who is found there.** **Davy Gannet** `[invented]`, 58, a veteran of '88 who knows the tides and every drowned apprentice. He shouts news with his fares and is wrong by a third every time. Competing watermen.
- **How he uses it, and when.** River travel, with an allowance for waiting (`gpt`).
  - *Tue 11 Feb (S014a):* Raleigh gone for Guiana, from Davy, wrongly.
  - *Sat 15 Mar (W119):* by Davy's boat to Whitehall with Kemp and Burbage to fetch the Queen's coin, 2d a man; the tide turns and they walk back.
  - *Tue 20 May:* back from the Rose by wherry, being flush.
  - *Sat 12 Jul (S071):* heat and stink at low tide. Davy tars an upturned keel by lantern.
  - *Sat 19 Jul (W059):* in Davy's boat with James and Cuthbert Burbage, past Paris Garden.
  - *Thu 4 Sep (S084):* Drake and Hawkins have sailed, from Davy, a third too big.
  - *Mon 22 Sep (S090):* Raleigh home "with a ship so deep in gold she'd hardly steer", from Davy, on the way to Paul's.
  - *Wed 29 Oct (S101):* the Lord Mayor's show on the water.
  - *Tue 9 Dec (S112a):* by water to Westminster for Canon Row.
  - *Mon 22 Dec (M053):* the squall (T1 foul weather) on the day the Queen's move to Richmond is put off. He helps Davy lash the tilt-boat's canvas and confirms Wednesday's flood.
  - *Wed 24 Dec (W107):* up to Richmond in Davy's six-oar tilt-boat, packed with property trunks, on the flood, about four hours.
  - *Mon 29 Dec (W108):* down again on the ebb.
- **Sample lines.** Davy: "Westward ho! Raleigh's gone for gold, the Spaniard's in Kent, and it'll rain." Davy again: "Sit trim amidships, Master Will. The ebb's running like a mill-race under the starlings."
- **Photo opportunities.** The bridge at first light from the lowest dry step, nearly a silhouette with light barring through the arches, one sculler with oars lifted and dripping, wet weed and a mooring ring in the foreground (any morning outside 12 Jun – 19 Jul; no dated shot). Low tide in heat: mud, stranded wherries, a dog asleep on the stair (S071). The new Mayor's barge with the companies' streamers in silver mist, seen past a working boatman. At the edge of the frame a woman's dark head is turned away, laughing (S101; the lady's cross for 29 Oct). Frost on the tilt-boat's hoops, trunks and covered work bundles aboard (Wed 24 Dec; `gem`, `gpt`).

#### `london_bridge` · London Bridge

*N end 51.5093, −0.0862 · S end 51.5060, −0.0882 (est., ±50 m) · T2 that he crossed it.*

- **What it is.** The only bridge: nineteen stone piers on boat-shaped starlings, with a wall of tall timber houses and shops along its whole length. Nonesuch House stands on it (T1 `[ext]`), and the waterwheel clanks at its northern end (T1 `[ext]`). The water roars through the arches. At the Southwark end stands the gate, and what stands on it is seen far off, never counted and never dwelt on. **It is a route, not a viewing platform** (`gpt`). From home to the Rose by the bridge is 1.9 km, 23–29 minutes, free.
- **By season.** A freezing gale off the river in January. Holiday crowds on May Day. In June, armed watchmen, which he hears of only.
- **Who is found there.** Pedlars, carts, watchmen, everyone crossing dry-shod to Southwark.
- **How he uses it, and when.** On foot to Bankside on his Rose days: Fri 14 Feb, Tue 20 May, Fri 29 Aug and Fri 28 Nov. **Tue 20 May (W043):** Philip Henslowe stops him at the bridge foot. The exchange is civil, appraising and all money: "You've a pretty wit, Master Shakespeare. Does it pay?" He does not cross between 12 June and 19 July.
- **Photo opportunities.** The bridge from Old Swan Stairs at sunrise (undated; never 12 Jun – 19 Jul). The narrow street between the overhanging houses, carts jammed, a shop shutter let down as a counter.

#### `the_rose` · The Rose, Bankside

*51.5072, −0.0954 · T1 venue; T5 his visits.*

- **What it is.** Philip Henslowe's fourteen-sided timber, thatch and plaster playhouse off Park Street, Southwark (T1 archaeology `[ext]`), the home of Edward Alleyn and the Admiral's Men. It is fresh-wainscoted and painted after the Lent repairs (T1), with a new throne in the heavens from June (T1, £7 2s). The Bear Garden is next door.
- **By season.**
  - *February:* muddy ditch-lanes and a roaring *Long Meg* crowd.
  - *Lent:* sawdust, lime-tubs and wet paint behind the shutters.
  - *May:* packed *Hercules* afternoons.
  - *27 Jun – 24 Aug:* bolted and dark.
  - *Late August:* renewed traffic.
  - *November:* *Harry the Fifth*'s cannon-smoke drifting over the yard.
  - *Always:* the bears' roar from next door, tar and river mud, oranges, the stair-woman's cry. Alleyn's voice fills the house like water.
- **Who is found there.** Henslowe at his door, counting. Alleyn, tall, as Tamburlaine and Hercules. The Admiral's Men. William in his grey cloak in the gallery.
- **How he uses it, and when.** His watch history: what works on two thousand Londoners, and how to do it otherwise. **Canonical attendances (surface `stage`):** Fri 14 Feb *Long Meg* (S016), Tue 20 May *1 Hercules* (W043), Fri 29 Aug *Longshanks* (S085) and Fri 28 Nov *Harry the Fifth* (S108). Builders may add up to four more on days he is not cast, using Henslowe's titles and receipts only. On other days he reads the bill. Easter Monday is a bill, because he plays that afternoon (S036). **The Rose's 18 May is blank.**
- **Photo opportunities.** The Rose from the river with the flag up and wherries thick at the stairs (W043). Its entrance before a performance, with ordinary crowd movement (S016, `gpt`). The penny yard gazing up at the stage in crisp winter light (S016, `gem`). The dated year never puts him on Bankside in Lent (the Lent closing reaches him as a bill at Bishopsgate, S026), so `gem`'s shot of new boards by the lime-tubs is unused.

#### `southwark_market` · Borough market and the Bankside walk

*51.5060, −0.0885 (est.; the bridge's south end) · T1 the butter riot; T5 his presence · `gem` `southwark_market_bankside`. **Never visited 12 Jun – 19 Jul.***

- **What it is.** The crowded market approach south of the bridge along Borough High Street, by St Saviour's. From it runs the willow-lined Bankside path past the Clink, the Bear Garden and the Rose, and, from midsummer, Francis Langley's Swan timbers rising in Paris Garden (`gem`).
- **By season.** Butter, fish, baskets, mud and delivery traffic (`gpt`). Bear-dogs barking across the water all summer. Wet willows in autumn.
- **Who is found there.** Butter-women, fishwives, apprentices, Langley's masons. Phillips and Pope are said to lodge on the Southwark side (T3 for 1595, `gem`).
- **How he uses it, and when.** The walk to the Rose on his four Rose days, and an errand on the way. **The butter riot of Fri 13 Jun (S059a)** happens here without him. He hears of it, and the photo is Leadenhall the next morning (S059b).
- **Photo opportunities.** The Bankside path in autumn with the Swan's frame closed in beyond the willows (Fri 29 Aug or Fri 28 Nov). Fish baskets stacked at the bridge foot.

#### `paris_garden_swan` · Paris Garden: the Swan a-building

*51.5073, −0.1027 · T1 venue (built c.1595–96); T3 any link (the Langley writ of 1596 is outside the year).*

- **What it is.** A ring of raw timber rising in Paris Garden, bigger than the Rose. He knows only that a goldsmith is building a playhouse while the Mayor writes letters against such things. Francis Langley stays offstage, with no contact in 1595.
- **By season.** Raw scaffolding in July. By the autumn, a frame closing in.
- **Who is found there.** Langley's carpenters and masons.
- **How he uses it, and when.** **Sat 19 Jul (W059):** seen from Davy Gannet's boat with James and Cuthbert Burbage, his first time on the river since May. His track passes it, and there is no dwell.
- **Photo opportunities.** A ring of new timber scaffolded against the sky, seen from the water (W059, `fab` shot 31).

---

### 4.4 Westminster and the court

#### `somerset_house` · Somerset House, the Strand

*51.5117, −0.1174 · T1 patron (Hunsdon wrote from here in 1594); T5 him.*

- **What it is.** The Tudor riverside house on the Strand, not the later building, kept by the Lord Chamberlain. It has a water-gate on the river.
- **By season.** Liveried grooms with the Carey badge year round. Barges bumping the water-gate. Torches at the gate on winter nights.
- **Who is found there.** Hunsdon, hale and loud. Ambrose Kettleby, his gentleman usher, whose voice carries most of the patron's calls. Household clerks.
- **How he uses it, and when.** **He never enters it this year.** It is where Hunsdon and Kettleby call from: Kettleby on Tue 2 Sep (S087), Tue 16 Sep (W081) and Mon 20 Oct (W405), Hunsdon on Fri 7 Nov (S104), and Kettleby with the Hoby summons on Sun 7 Dec (W101). He passes it on the Strand on 17 Nov and on the midnight walk home of 9 Dec.
- **Photo opportunities.** The water-gate from a passing wherry on 9 Dec at dusk, with torches. The Carey badge on a groom's sleeve in the Strand.

#### `whitehall_palace` · Whitehall Palace

*51.5046, −0.1260 · T1 the Council's warrant of 15 Mar 1594/5, issued here; it does not place him · never inside the palace; once at the outer precinct, 15 Mar · `gpt` `whitehall`.*

- **What it is.** The sprawling royal precinct of red brick, tennis courts and the Holbein Gate (T1 `[ext]`), not the later Banqueting House. It is all gates, waiting, liveries, water traffic, expensive fabric and ordinary service work (`gpt`). Court is here from 1 Mar (the Shrovetide tilt, Lent sermons, the Garter on 23 Apr), from 18 Aug, from 14 Nov to 27 Nov, and from 19 Dec until the move to Richmond.
- **By season.** Wet stone and smoke in March. Torches and crowds in November.
- **Who is found there.** Chamber clerks, guards, petitioners. The Queen in court seasons, unseen.
- **How he uses it, and when.**
  - *Sat 15 Mar (W119):* the three named men go up by wherry in the morning to fetch the coin (T5). He goes only as far as the outer precinct, to the office where the Chamber pays out, and waits in its passage with Kemp and Burbage while a clerk counts. He walks back by the Strand and Holborn to Shoreditch for the split. He is never inside the palace and never before the Council.
  - *Mon 3 Mar (S020):* the court's Shrovetide is heard of.
  - *Mon 22 Dec (W106):* by dusk Heminges has it from Somerset House that the Queen's move to Richmond is put off "by reason of the foul weather" (T1), until tomorrow.
- **Photo opportunities.** The Holbein Gate from the street, liveries passing under it. A clerk's hands counting coin on a board in the Chamber's office (Sat 15 Mar).

#### `whitehall_tiltyard` · The Whitehall tiltyard

*51.5047, −0.1283 · T1 the event; T5 his presence · `gem`'s tilt beats inside `whitehall_palace` map here.*

- **What it is.** The sandy tiltyard by the palace, now Horse Guards: the barrier, the stands, the Queen's gallery.
- **The day: Mon 17 Nov (S106), Accession Day.** No play. He walks there and back (15,800 steps). Entry is 12d (T2 `[ext]`). There are ten pairs (T1), Essex's device, Essex against Cumberland, and Southampton in armour against Scudamore (T1), far off. The weather is clear and cold. He stands among citizens and cannot hear the speeches. The Queen is a far gallery and a glitter.
- **By season.** One day only: trampled ground, horses, barriers, controlled sightlines, trumpets, distant elite display (`gpt`), and thousands of spectators.
- **Who is found there.** The Queen, Essex, Southampton (his one sighting of the earl), the tilters and their pages, citizens.
- **How he uses it.** A spectator. He cannot photograph private royal reactions (`gpt`).
- **Photo opportunities.** Two riders meeting at the barrier, splinters in the air, the stands a blur of colour (S106, `fab` shot 44). The tilt seen from behind a barrier and other spectators' shoulders (`gpt`).

#### `canon_row` · Sir Edward Hoby's house, Canon Row

*51.5014, −0.1255 · T1 Hoby's letter of 7 Dec; T3 that the play was his *Richard II* (the story's choice) · `gem` `canon_row_westminster`.*

- **What it is.** A narrow, well-born Westminster lane between Whitehall and the river. Hoby's house has a panelled great chamber with a screen at one end, wainscot, green floor-rushes, silver plate, a fire, and forty white wax candles, some in silver branches. Twelve sit at table. The company plays after supper in a space six paces by four.
- **The night: Tue 9 Dec (S112).** Iron frost. The letter said "K. Richard present himself to your view" (T1). They play *Richard II* whole, with the deposing, once (T3). Lady Hoby makes the room work. Sir Robert Cecil is small and still in a chair by the fire, and says one sentence that William keeps for a memo on Thu 11 Dec (W102). The reward is £5 to the company, 12s 6d a share. He goes to the Theatre and home first, then by water from Old Swan, and walks home along the Strand, Fleet Street and Cheapside at midnight (3.7 km), in bed at 01:20.
- **Who is found there.** Sir Edward and Lady Hoby, Cecil, their guests, the sharers who play.
- **Photo opportunities.** The cleared playing space before the guests settle (`gpt`). Forty candles, the screen, a chair by the fire with a small still figure in it, seen from behind the players' entrance (S112, `fab` shot 47; `gem`'s silver branches and wainscot). The Strand at midnight, empty and iron-cold.

#### `westminster_hall` · Westminster Hall

*51.4999, −0.1254 · T2 milieu (the law terms).*

- **What it is.** The cavernous Norman hall under the great hammer-beam roof raised for Richard II (T1 `[ext]`; it is the king he is writing). It houses Common Pleas, King's Bench and Chancery, with stationers' and law-scriveners' stalls along the walls.
- **By season.** The roar of parchment and pleading in term: Hilary from Thu 23 Jan, Easter from 7 May to 2 Jun, Trinity to 9 Jul, Michaelmas from Thu 9 Oct to Fri 28 Nov. It is silent out of term.
- **Who is found there.** Judges, serjeants, barristers, country litigants, scriveners, cutpurses.
- **How he uses it, and when.** Term on the calendar means full sixpenny galleries. *Thu 23 Jan (S008a):* Hilary term opens, and he walks west with Bryan for a single morning to watch language at work, as an observer of competing explanations and money changing hands, never a party to any suit (`gpt`, `gem`). *Thu 20 Feb (S018):* **Southwell is tried here (T1), and William hears of it at supper. He is never here that day.**
- **Photo opportunities.** Papers held against a chest amid the foot traffic (`gpt`). Black gowns and country suitors under the hammer-beams (`gem`).

#### `greenwich_palace` · Greenwich Palace

*51.4822, −0.0067 · T1 for the company's Christmas 1594 plays; T2 venue · **no location point in 1595**.*

- **What it is.** The red-brick riverside palace downstream, about 7 km by river, where the company played before the Queen on 26 and 28 December 1594. The court is here again in summer 1595, from 3 May to 18 Aug, and the proclamation of 4 July is dated here.
- **How he uses it.** **Remembered only.** It appears on the first page of the year's book (S001: "of the Queen, for two plays at Greenwich at Christmas, xx li., not yet had"). Then there is the tavern story of the day of two *Errors*, 28 December 1594: Greenwich in the afternoon and the scramble upriver to Gray's Inn that night, told a different way by every teller. The Admiral's Men play at court there on 1 and 6 Jan (T1, S002, W003). It is the year's benchmark: two plays last Christmas, three at Richmond this one.
- **Photo opportunities.** None; he is not there.

#### `richmond_palace` · Richmond Palace

*51.4612, −0.3089 · T1 the company's plays of 26, 27 and 28 Dec; T2 him.*

- **What it is.** The turreted royal palace ten miles up-river in Surrey, with towers, vanes, onion cupolas and long galleries. The hall is dressed for plays, with a state under a canopy, degrees of seating, and a stage on trestles with a traverse. There are roaring hearths, sweet-flag rushes, and wired chandeliers of white wax (`gem`). The Yeomen of the Revels bring the apparel chests. Light is costly and uneven, and the performers wait in colder service spaces than their audience (`gpt`). Court is here from Tue 23 Dec.
- **The nights.** Fri 26 Dec *Love's Labour's Lost*: his heart is 140 before the Queen. Sat 27 Dec *Romeo and Juliet*: 150 as the Chorus. That night a gold button set with five pearls is lost from the Queen's kirtle "at a play at Richmond" (T1), and it is searched for and never found. Sun 28 Dec *The Comedy of Errors*, the only Sunday performance of the year. Nick Sorrell's voice breaks in the jig after it.
- **By season.** Only the frost of Christmas week, after the foul weather of the 22nd.
- **Who is found there.** The Queen, almost wordless. Hunsdon, Tilney and his men. The whole company.
- **How he uses it.** The year's last performances, and its summit.
- **Photo opportunities.** The towers and vanes across the frosted Green. The hall an hour before, dressed, with the canopy of state empty. A boy on his knees searching the rushes with a candle, with no button in frame (S115b; `fab` shot 48, `gpt`). Players waiting in a service passage (`gpt`). Packed costume at the end of the third night (`gpt`).

#### `the_feathers_richmond` · The Feathers, Richmond Green `[invented sign]`

*51.4616, −0.3060 (est.) · T5 · `gpt` `richmond_lodging`; `fab`'s unslugged Feathers.*

- **What it is.** An inn on the Green, 200 m from the palace, where the company lodges from 24 to 29 December. It has shared storage, clothes drying before the fire, and beds allocated by practicality. Nothing about it is royal by association (`gpt`). **He shares a chamber with Heminges and Bryan, three to a bed (W107, S114).**
- **When.** Up on Wed 24 Dec. *Thu 25 Dec (S114):* Christmas Day in a strange town, with church on the Green and dinner at the inn's long table. Three nights of court hours, then down on Mon 29 Dec. Heminges keeps the court paperwork distinct from money received, because the court will not pay until next year (T1: the warrant is dated 21 Dec 1596).
- **Photo opportunities.** The hired room with drying clothes and separated papers (`gpt`). The Green in frost from the inn door.

---

### 4.5 The roads

Both journeys leave the City westward by Ludgate, the Strand and Charing Cross, then keep west by Knightsbridge and Kensington and cut north by a lane to the Uxbridge way, a mile longer, so as not to pass **Tyburn** (S030a, W061), which has its own entry below. Only the summer return comes in by the carriers' road past it and down Holborn (W073d). Distances are the source book's computed road miles (§ 3.3: haversine × 1.12 between towns), counted from the City. Inns on both roads are T5 until a source names them. The Oxford tavern stays unnamed by rule.

#### `tyburn` · Tyburn

*51.5133, −0.1603 · T1 events · **never a location point**.*

**He is never here.** Southwell is hanged here on Fri 21 Feb (T1). William does not go, and it exists in the journal only as a word in that night's memo (S019). The Lent ride out on Wed 26 Mar, the ride back on Thu 10 Apr and the summer walk on Mon 28 Jul all go round it by Knightsbridge and Kensington, a mile out of the way (S030a: "Not by the tree. Not five weeks after."; W032c, W061). Only Hobbins's string, coming in on Thu 21 Aug, keeps the carriers' road past it by Holborn, and the track does not pause (W073d). No point is recorded and nothing is described, not even crows (canon § 7 fix 10). No photo.

#### `oxford_road` · The Oxford road (route A, about 102 miles)

*Track, no single point · T2 route; T5 his use · `gem` `oxford_road_inns`; `gpt`'s `wheatley` is a waypoint here, with no stop.*

- **What it is.** The southern road to Stratford. It runs from the City to Uxbridge (20 mi), Beaconsfield (29), High Wycombe (34), up through the Chiltern beechwoods to the ridge at Stokenchurch (42), down to the yellow clay of Tetsworth (48) and on to Wheatley (55). Then Oxford, Carfax (61), Woodstock (70), Enstone (77) and Chipping Norton (81). Over the Cotswold edge by Long Compton (86) to Shipston-on-Stour (92) and Alderminster (98). Stratford, at Clopton Bridge, is at 102.
- **By season.** *Late March:* lambs and plough-teams, mud to the hocks in the Chiltern bottoms, primroses in cold ditches, a bright cold ridge at Stokenchurch with the Vale of Oxford blue below, smoke-blackened inn rafters and oat-bins, rain at Long Compton, and the Rollright stones off to the left in drizzle. *Mid-August:* white dust, stubble and thin harvest, and the slow bells of packhorses.

**The Lent ride out** (horse hired at the Bull, 12d a day plus fodder; about 38s for the whole journey with inns).

| Day | Date | To (night) | Miles | Note |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Wed 26 Mar | High Wycombe, the Catherine Wheel `[invented sign]` | 34 | By Ludgate, the Strand, Knightsbridge and Kensington (not by Tyburn), Uxbridge and Beaconsfield (S030a) |
| 2 | Thu 27 Mar | Woodstock, the Bear `[ext]` | 36 | Up over the ridge at Stokenchurch in the morning. Bait at Oxford in an unnamed Cornmarket tavern, Oxford at dusk, and Woodstock by night (W025) |
| 3 | Fri 28 Mar | Stratford | 32 | By Chipping Norton, Long Compton (rain) and Shipston. **Clopton Bridge at dusk** (W026) |

**The Lent ride back.**

| Day | Date | To (night) | Miles | Note |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Tue 8 Apr | Woodstock, the Bear | 32 | By Shipston, Long Compton (dry this time) and Chipping Norton (W032a) |
| 2 | Wed 9 Apr | High Wycombe, the Catherine Wheel | 36 | Past Oxford without stopping; a memo on Anne's question on the rise toward Wheatley, and a line or two of Gaunt's rhythm at the Catherine Wheel that night (W032b; `gem`, re-homed from Tetsworth) |
| 3 | Thu 10 Apr | London, home | 34 | By Kensington and Knightsbridge, not by Tyburn; the bay back at the Bull at three (W032c). Rehearsal the next morning (S033) |

**The summer return, with Hobbins's packhorses at the horses' pace** (W073a–d). He rides the spare pack-nag for the first stage out of Stratford because of his back, and walks the rest.

| Day | Date | To (night) | Miles | Note |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Mon 18 Aug | Chipping Norton | 21 | The nag as far as Shipston, then on foot. About 30,000 steps (canon § 5.4) |
| 2 | Tue 19 Aug | Oxford | 20 | On foot with the packhorses. About 43,500 steps |
| 3 | Wed 20 Aug | Beaconsfield | 32 | On foot, every mile. About 66,500 steps, the year's longest day on foot |
| 4 | Thu 21 Aug | London, the Bell, Carter Lane | 29 | On foot, by Uxbridge and Tyburn; about 60,500 steps. Home to St Helen's on Fri 22 Aug |

- **Who is found there.** Ostlers, carriers, scholars near Oxford, cloth-carriers, drovers, Hobbins and his string.
- **Photo opportunities.** Chalk-and-clay cart ruts shining in low sun among the beeches toward High Wycombe (Wed 26 Mar, `gem`). A string of packhorses with bells on the leader and the carrier's back (W073b, `fab` shot 40).

#### `uxbridge` · Uxbridge

*51.5450, −0.4817 · T2 route.*

- **What it is.** A market town 20 miles out, where both routes leave London's road and separate. It is full of carts, horses, provisions and traffic choosing its way (`gpt`). There is no named historic inn.
- **When.** Passed on Wed 26 Mar and Thu 10 Apr on horseback, on Mon 28 Jul on foot (the Banbury road turns off north here), and on Thu 21 Aug with the packhorses. There are no stops.
- **Photo opportunities.** The fork in the road, with a carter choosing.

#### `beaconsfield` · Beaconsfield

*51.6083, −0.6571 · T2 route; T5 stop.*

- **What it is.** A road town 29 miles out, and **night 3 of the summer return (Wed 20 Aug)**. An unnamed inn gives him a shared room, and there are boots that must be dealt with before supper (`gpt`). Road dust in August, and no romantic coaching-inn imagery.
- **When.** Passed in the Lent ride both ways. Slept on Wed 20 Aug.
- **Photo opportunities.** The road pack and boots on the inn floor at Beaconsfield (`gpt`, re-dated).

#### `high_wycombe` · High Wycombe

*51.6290, −0.7480 · T2 route; T5 stop.*

- **What it is.** The Chiltern valley town 34 miles out, with an inn in the high street at the sign of the Catherine Wheel `[invented sign]`. Woodsmoke, stable noise, and bowls of water carried upstairs (`gpt`).
- **When.** Lent nights only: Wed 26 Mar and Wed 9 Apr. Passed on Wed 20 Aug.
- **Photo opportunities.** The hired horse and tied luggage in the inn-yard at first light on 27 Mar (`gpt`).

#### `oxford` · Oxford

*51.7520, −1.2579 (Carfax) · T2 route; T5 stop.*

- **What it is.** The halfway town, 61 miles out: stone, bells, passing scholars and tradespeople, cramped traffic at Carfax. He has no acquaintance with any innkeeper here. The Davenants and the Crown are T4 and anachronistic for 1595, so the tavern stays unnamed.
- **When.** *Thu 27 Mar:* a bait in an unnamed Cornmarket tavern, and the town at dusk. *Tue 19 Aug:* night 2 of the summer return.
- **Photo opportunities.** The Cornmarket at dusk: gowned scholars, a tavern door spilling light, the sign out of frame (W025, `fab` shot 12).

#### `woodstock` · Woodstock

*51.8475, −1.3545 · T2 route; T5 stop.*

- **What it is.** A small town by the royal park, 70 miles out, with an inn at the sign of the Bear `[ext]` (a long-established inn of that name; its existence in 1595 is unverified, § 9.4). Spring mud and early stable activity (`gpt`).
- **When.** Lent nights only: Thu 27 Mar and Tue 8 Apr. Passed on foot with the packhorses on Tue 19 Aug (W073b).
- **Photo opportunities.** Dusk outside the Bear, with travel mud on the shoes in the foreground (`gpt`). Hired tack hung in the stable (`gpt`).

#### `chipping_norton` · Chipping Norton

*51.9383, −1.5424 · T2 route; T5 stop.*

- **What it is.** The Cotswold wool town 81 miles out, the last town before the edge. It is **night 1 of the summer return (Mon 18 Aug)**. A stool, warm water and an inconveniently placed bed matter more to a stiff back than picturesque architecture (`gpt`). `gpt`'s pack strain here is dropped.
- **When.** Passed on the Lent ride (Fri 28 Mar and Tue 8 Apr). Slept on Mon 18 Aug.
- **Photo opportunities.** The pack on the inn floor at Chipping Norton (`gpt`).

#### `shipston_on_stour` · Shipston-on-Stour

*52.0630, −1.6247 · T2 route.*

- **What it is.** A waypoint 92 miles out, and the last substantial stop before Stratford, with food, water and the temptation to say the journey is nearly done before it is (`gpt`).
- **When.** Passed on Fri 28 Mar and Tue 8 Apr. On Mon 18 Aug he gets off the pack-nag here and walks.
- **Photo opportunities.** The Stour bridge with the road beyond.

#### `banbury_road` · The Aylesbury–Banbury road (route B1, about 100 miles)

*Track · T2 corridor; T5 his use · `gem` `banbury_road_grendon`.*

- **What it is.** The northern clay-and-ridgeway corridor. It runs from the City to Uxbridge (20 mi), Chalfont St Giles (28) and Amersham (32) through Chiltern flint lanes. It crosses the heavy Vale of Aylesbury pastures by Wendover (41), Aylesbury (46) and Waddesdon (52) to the straggling thatch of Grendon Underwood (57). Then come Bicester (64), Aynho (73) and Banbury (79) among red ironstone hills. Wroxton (82) and Upton lead to the escarpment of Sun Rising Hill (87) and the drop into Warwickshire by Pillerton (91) and Ettington (93). Stratford is at 100. This is the line that fits Aubrey's Grendon (T4).
- **By season.** Only high summer: white road-dust, standing barley thin and short in the ear, harvesters who look at the sky and say nothing, dog-roses over, larks, wild thyme on the Chiltern banks, and his own feet. There are blistered heels by day 3 (travel texture, not illness).

**The summer walk out** (about 11s on the road).

| Day | Date | To (night) | Miles | Note |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Mon 28 Jul | Chalfont St Giles | 28 | By Ludgate, the Strand, Knightsbridge and Kensington (not by Tyburn) and Uxbridge. Night in a barn-loft over an alehouse (W061) |
| 2 | Tue 29 Jul | Grendon Underwood | 29 | By Amersham, Wendover and Aylesbury. An alehouse with no name, and the constable (W062) |
| 3 | Wed 30 Jul | Banbury | 22 | By Bicester and Aynho. A thunderstorm over Edge Hill to the north-west in the late afternoon (W063) |
| 4 | Thu 31 Jul | Stratford | 21 | By Wroxton, Sun Rising Hill and Ettington. Edge Hill behind him by noon. **Clopton Bridge at five** (W064) |

- **Who is found there.** Reapers, drovers, alehouse keepers, the Grendon constable.
- **Photo opportunities.** Thin barley close up, reapers small beyond (W063, `fab` shot 34). From Sun Rising Hill, the whole Vale of the Red Horse with Stratford's spire a pin in the haze (W064, `fab` shot 35).

#### `chalfont_st_giles` · Chalfont St Giles

*51.6330, −0.5700 (est.) · T5 stop.*

- **What it is.** A Chiltern village 28 miles out, among beechwoods, and **night 1 of the summer walk (Mon 28 Jul)**. He sleeps in a barn-loft over an alehouse, with hay, swallows and a ladder.
- **Photo opportunities.** The white road through beeches near Chalfont in the long evening light, with no shadow of his in frame (W061; `fab` shot 32, amended).

#### `grendon_underwood` · Grendon Underwood

*51.8826, −1.0075 · T4 (Aubrey's constable "humour" village, "on the road from London to Stratford"), played knowingly.*

- **What it is.** A straggling thatch village in the Vale, 57 miles out, and **night 2 of the summer walk (Tue 29 Jul)**. An alehouse with no name. A parish constable of great dignity and small sense mangles big words by lantern-light ("auspicious vagroms"). No play comes from him one-to-one, but the big words go into the running jokes. William's memo that day, at twenty past three on the road between Aylesbury and Waddesdon, is W062's. (`gem`'s Grendon-on-Lammas moves here, to 29 Jul.)
- **On the way that morning.** The flint road past Amersham, with wild thyme and a lichened parish marker (`gem`, re-dated from 31 Jul).
- **Photo opportunities.** An alehouse bench, a constable's staff propped against the wall, its owner's legs only (W062, `fab` shot 33). The flint road at Amersham in the morning (`gem`).

#### `banbury` · Banbury

*52.0602, −1.3403 · T2 route; T5 stop.*

- **What it is.** The market town among the ironstone hills, 79 miles out, and **night 3 of the summer walk (Wed 30 Jul)**. A clean inn, **unnamed** unless the Reindeer `[ext]` is verified.
- **By season.** The storm over Edge Hill that afternoon, which catches him the last mile in and has him wet to the shirt in forty paces (W063).
- **Photo opportunities.** Thunderheads over the Edge Hill ridge from the Banbury road.

---

### 4.6 Stratford-upon-Avon

#### `henley_street` · The Henley Street house

*52.1939, −1.7080 · T1 property; T2 that the family lived there in 1595 · `gem` `henley_street_stratford`.*

- **What it is.** John Shakespeare's double house in the north of the town. The glover's and whittawer's shop and the wet-house are on one side, smelling faintly of alum, lime, kidskin and wool-fells. The hall and kitchen are on the other, with a low-beamed hearth. Chambers are above. Anne keeps a neat buttery and a malt-loft. Behind runs a garden and orchard strip down to the Guild Pits, with pearmain apples, bees, herbs, a dog-rose hedge, and a low chamomile bench under the boughs (`gem`). The house is crowded and functional, full of objects whose place everyone but William knows (`gpt`). **The 1595 fire does not touch it.**
- **Sleeping (T5).** William and Anne have the chamber over the hall. The girls sleep in a truckle. Hamnet is in with Edmund over the shop.
- **By season.**
  - *Lent visit:* wood-smoke, simnel spice, wet garden earth on a spade, the dog-rose hedge in bud, and rebuilding sounds from the streets near by (`gpt`).
  - *August:* open doors, apples swelling, drowsy wasps, warm bread, children crossing adult business, and a thread of old smoke in the thatch from the fire up the town.
- **Who is found there.** **Nine at the table:** John, Mary, Joan, Richard, Edmund, Anne and the three children. Gilbert is never here during William's visits.
- **How he uses it, and when.** Being husband, father and son, and sleeping without London bells.
  - *Fri 28 Mar – Tue 8 Apr:* the Lent stay. On Fri 28 Mar Burbage's old lath sword goes to Hamnet, with ribbons, a riddle-book and gloves he did not make. In April he repairs Hamnet's wooden wheel, imperfectly (`gpt`). *Wed 2 Apr (S031):* his father on debts and the arms once sought. *Mon 7 Apr (W031):* the evening before he rides (canon § 7 fix 6: never "last").
  - *Thu 31 Jul – Mon 18 Aug:* the summer stay.
  - *Fri 1 Aug (S079):* Lammas, with the Lammas loaf on the table.
  - *Tue 5 Aug (S080):* harvest, malt and barley at the evening table, with Quiney and Alderman Sturley.
  - *Sat 9 Aug (W067):* the orchard scene, written on the garden bench at night under a full moon. Bed at half past two.
  - In August the children build a string-and-spool contraption (`gpt`).
  - *Tue 12 – Fri 15 Aug:* flat on his back after the barn frame. *Thu 14 Aug (W070):* the children play the orchard scene at his side while he lies on the chamber floor.
  - *Sat 16 Aug (W071):* Anne names the great house by the chapel.
  - *Sun 17 Aug (W072):* the supper before the road.
- **Photo opportunities.** A Lammas loaf on the table with nine wooden trenchers (S079, `fab` shot 36). The garden under a full moon: the orchard wall, apple boughs, a bench with paper weighted by a stone (W067, `fab` shot 38). The same bench the next morning in apple-shade, the brass inkhorn, two quills and blotted *Romeo* papers (Sun 10 Aug, `gem`, re-dated). The imperfect repaired wheel (April, `gpt`). A string-and-spool construction under three children's competing hands (August, `gpt`). An unpacked household list (`gpt`).

#### `holy_trinity` · Holy Trinity

*52.1866, −1.7075 · T1 · `gem` `holy_trinity_stratford`. **Never frame the children against it.***

- **What it is.** The collegiate parish church on the Avon's lip at the south end of the town, reached by the walk down through Old Town under the limes, with elms by the river. William and his children were christened here (T1).
- **By season.** Rooks cawing in the bare elms at the end of March. Green river-reeds and river damp in August.
- **Who is found there.** The family, except John, who stays home "for debt" (T1 basis). Neighbours, the vicar and the parish clerk.
- **How he uses it, and when.** Sunday service on his visits.
  - *Sun 30 Mar (W027):* Mothering Sunday, with Mary.
  - *Sun 6 Apr (W030):* John stays home.
  - *Sun 3 Aug (W065):* a sermon on the fire as God's judgment on a town that malts on the Sabbath, with his father's dry aside afterward.
  - *Sun 10 Aug and Sun 17 Aug:* church.
  - The register is an outside historical source, not a prop he consults (`gpt`).
- **Photo opportunities.** The lime walk from the Old Town end, empty, in March. Rooks in the elms against a pale sky. The nave from his pew, adults only. **Never the churchyard, and never the children framed against the church.**

#### `guild_chapel_school` · The Guild Chapel and the King's New School

*52.1901, −1.7077 · T2 his schooling; T1 the buildings · `gpt` `guild_chapel`. Moved 2026-11-17 by the editor from 52.1905, −1.7078 to the school end of the Guildhall, more than 50 m from New Place, so the journal's map does not fold a walk past the chapel into the great house.*

- **What it is.** The chapel on Chapel Street with its whitewashed walls (the wall paintings were limed over in 1563, in his father's year as chamberlain, T1 `[ext]`). The Guildhall stands beside it, with the school upstairs where Hamnet learns his Latin. The great house stands opposite.
- **By season.** The chapel bell and boys' voices through the school windows. In August the lane is quiet in the heat.
- **Who is found there.** The master (fall back to "the master"; Alexander Aspinall is quoted once, by Susanna, T1 `[ext]`), the boys, Hamnet among them, and the chapel clerk.
- **How he uses it.** A landmark on his walks and a route he remembers, not a flashback factory (`gpt`). It is the corner where the town's past and his wish stand face to face.
- **Photo opportunities.** The chapel's whitewashed wall in low sun. The school stair from the street.

#### `new_place` · The great house by the chapel (New Place)

*52.1907, −1.7076 · T1 for its purchase in 1597; T5 for 1595.*

- **What it is.** The second-largest house in the town, opposite the Guild Chapel. It is big, brick-and-timber and decayed, with a broken pane or two and a garden gone to nettles. **It is not his.** In this canon it is a wish with an address.
- **How he uses it, and when.** He passes it in Lent and in August. **Sat 16 Aug (W071):** Anne names it: "That one, then." He makes no inquiry, pays no deposit, uses no lawyer and names no price in 1595.
- **Photo opportunities.** The great house in brick and timber, one broken pane, nettles at the gate (W071, `fab` shot 39).

#### `high_cross_market` · The High Cross and the Thursday market

*52.1923, −1.7062 (±40 m) · T2 milieu · `gpt` `stratford_market`, `gem` `stratford_high_cross` (its bridge, New Place and fire beats map to their own slugs).*

- **What it is.** The market cross at the junction of Bridge Street and High Street, the borough's spine. The Thursday market sells corn, malt, butter, cheese and gloves on John's stall. **The Sadlers' bakehouse is on the High Street**, within this point.
- **By season.** *April:* fresh adze-chips and new thatch on the rebuilt houses, butter-women. *August:* anxious farmers haggling over dear barley and malt, and thin sacks.
- **Who is found there.** Anne, for whom it is working ground, so that William is the less informed buyer. Richard Quiney, Hamnet and Judith Sadler, Abraham Sturley, country-women, John at his stall.
- **How he uses it, and when.**
  - *Thu 3 Apr (W028):* he walks the market with a twin on each hand, and Quiney stops him. Anne judges goods while William holds the basket (`gpt`).
  - *Fri 4 Apr (W123):* he goes quietly round three shops and pays £5 of his father's small debts, without a word at Henley Street.
  - *Thu 7 Aug (W066, G059):* market day. At the Sadlers' bakehouse Quiney borrows £2 with no interest. Judith Sadler makes the men say aloud whether it is a gift, a loan or a purchase.
  - *Sun 10 Aug (W068):* dinner at the Sadlers'.
- **Photo opportunities.** The High Cross on market day: butter-women, a glove stall, two small figures running ahead (W028, `fab` shot 15). A grain measure and hands discussing a purchase (`gpt`; S080's frame is a bushel of thin barley at a malt-house door).

#### `stratford_fire_ground` · The burned streets

*1594 scar: Chapel Street 52.1911, −1.7075 · 1595 row north of the centre: 52.1945, −1.7068 (est.) · T1 both fires; T3 dates and extent · `gpt` `stratford_north_lane`.*

- **What it is.** Two fires, two places.
  - **The 1594 scar.** The great fire of Mon 13 May 1594 burned the town centre, Wood Street among it, and along Chapel Street (T1 annal; T3 exact day). In Lent it is half rebuilt: black stumps, a brick stack standing alone, new green oak pale against the char.
  - **The 1595 row.** The second, smaller fire falls in this canon on Tue 15 Jul (T5 date on a T1 fire; the source book's "probably July"). About twenty houses and malt-barns go in a row north of the centre. No one of his is hurt, and Henley Street is untouched. In August there is ash, charred wattle, soot-blackened malt-kiln stone, collapsed rafters with weeds already through the ash, men on ladders, reused timber being sorted, and a smell of smoke that lingers only locally (`gem`, `gpt`, `fab`).
- **Who is found there.** Carpenters, the burned-out families, the Sadler cousin, neighbours lending a hand.
- **How he uses it, and when.**
  - *Tue 1 Apr (S032):* the family day on the 1594 fire ground, with the children walking ahead past the new frame.
  - *Thu 7 Aug (W066):* he walks the burned row on market day.
  - *Tue 12 Aug (W069):* **raising the Sadler cousin's new barn frame** on the burned row. He strains his back on the frame. His 20s toward its timber was given on market day, Thu 7 Aug (W066).
  - Tue 13 May, in London, is a year since the first fire (S045).
- **Photo opportunities.** Chapel Street in early April: black footings, a lone smoke-stained chimney-stack, a new oak frame raised pale and roofless with a carpenter astride the wall-plate, three children half-turned toward it and busy, the boy with a thin wooden sword over his shoulder and the younger girl pointing (S032; `fab` prompt 3 and shot 14). The burned row in August: charred wattle, men on ladders, an old man directing from a rung (W066, `fab` shot 37). Soot-blackened kiln stonework with weeds in the ash (`gem`).

#### `clopton_bridge` · Clopton Bridge

*52.1916, −1.7003 · T2 his arrival point · `gem`'s bridge beats inside `stratford_high_cross` map here.*

- **What it is.** The long stone bridge of fourteen arches over the Avon, where both London roads arrive. It is stone, carts and water, the place where a journey becomes a town (`gpt`).
- **By season.** *March:* the Avon high and brown at dusk. *August:* slow, reed-green water, and arches mirrored clean.
- **Who is found there.** Carters, drovers, boys on the parapet, Richard Shakespeare come down to meet the walker.
- **How he uses it, and when.**
  - *Fri 28 Mar (W026):* arrival at dusk on a tired horse.
  - *Tue 8 Apr (W032a):* departure.
  - *Thu 31 Jul (W064):* arrival at five in the afternoon on his own feet, with blistered heels. Richard takes his pack (`gpt`, re-homed from Gilbert).
  - *Sun 10 Aug (S081):* in the afternoon, the children race willow-bark boats from the bridge (`gem`, moved here by canon § 7 fix 5).
  - *Mon 18 Aug (W073a):* departure with Hobbins's string.
- **Photo opportunities.** The long line of arches in last light, the Avon high and brown (W026, `fab` shot 13). Luggage in the foreground at arrival (`gpt`). Three small willow-bark boats with twig masts in sunlit reeds below the arches (S081, Sun 10 Aug, `gem`, moved). Richard's hands taking the parcel (`gpt`).

#### `hewlands_shottery` · Hewlands, the Hathaway farm, Shottery

*52.1906, −1.7315 (corrected) · T1 family; T2 visits · `gpt` `shottery`, `gem` `shottery_hewlands`.*

- **What it is.** A long thatched timber farmhouse a mile west of the town across field-paths and stiles. It has sheep-folds, a cider-press, an old orchard and the brook. It is Anne's brother's farm, and a place where Anne's history does not begin with her marriage to William (`gpt`).
- **By season.** *April:* pink-tipped apple buds, lambing pens, wet meadow on the path. *August:* warm hedges, domestic work, shade.
- **Who is found there.** Bartholomew Hathaway, Anne's brother. Joan Hathaway, her stepmother (`[ext]`). Anne, at home there.
- **How he uses it, and when.** *Sat 5 Apr (W029):* the Shottery walk alone with Anne, away from Henley Street ears, and the hard question: "Is London where you work, or where you live?" He does not answer well. *Tue 5 Aug (S080):* a day binding behind Bartholomew's sickles, six till four. *Sun 10 Aug (G060):* a second, easier walk out by the field path, Anne naming the land as they pass it (`gpt`).
- **Photo opportunities.** The path across wet meadow, a woman's back in a grey gown ten paces ahead (W029, `fab` shot 16). Apple buds framing the long thatched roof and the sheep-hurdles (`gem`). Anne in the lane in August, occupied with the place and not posed (`gpt`); the path through stubble toward the long thatched roof, her back a few paces ahead (G060).

---

### 4.7 News places (heard of, never visited)

None has a slug or a location point. They appear only in talk, letters, pamphlets, and one map-like image a builder may place on a `pamphlet` screen. Coordinates are in the source book § 2.7, for that image only.

| Place | Event (T1) | Heard |
|---|---|---|
| Mousehole · Paul · Newlyn · Penzance | The Spanish raid, 23 Jul | Sat 26 Jul in the tiring-house; the Split Hazel is full of it that evening (S076) |
| Plymouth (the Hoe) | Drake and Hawkins sail, 28 Aug | Thu 4 Sep (S084). Hawkins's death is never heard within the year |
| Clontibret, Co. Monaghan | O'Neill's victory, 25–27 May | About Wed 18 Jun, in the tiring-house (W050) |
| Guiana | Raleigh's voyage: sailed 6 Feb; home "with little riches" by 21 Sep | Tue 11 Feb (Davy Gannet, S014a); Mon 22 Sep (S090) |

**Counts.** 57 canonical places, the lodging included, matching canon § 4: 1 in § 4.1, 22 in § 4.2, 5 in § 4.3, 8 in § 4.4, 13 in § 4.5 and 8 in § 4.6. The news places are listed separately and have no slugs.
