### December

#### Week 49 · Mon 12-01 – Sun 12-07

**S111 · 2025-12-01 (Mon) · 10:45 · pauls_churchyard · *Edward III* entered to Burby**  
people: william_shakespeare, john_harrison  
sources: screen (book), notes, meeting, memo  
At the White Greyhound, Harrison has it fresh from Stationers' Hall and reports it, as he reports everything, as of a third party: Cuthbert Burby entered a book this morning, a play of King Edward the Third (T1), that has been played about the City. "I am told there are scenes in it of a king and a countess that are thought to be in your manner, Master Shakespeare." They are in his manner because they are his: made three or four years gone for another company, for a fee, and never thought of since (his share is T3, taken here as the story's choice). "Did Master Burby ask leave?" "Of whom?" Sixpence to the Hall, Harrison says, buys a title its place in the book; and a title, he adds, must promise something quickly, whatever is made of the promise after, which William thought himself at a bill-post in October. William thinks of what a printed title gathers in and what it leaves out: no company, no hands, no name. He remembers one line of it for certain, of lilies that fester, because he has since used it again in a thing that lies in his folder at home. The memo, going north: "The line's in Burby's book and in my coffer. Which of us has it? He has paper from the Hall. I have only the making." Then, wryly: "The *True Tragedy* in May, this in December. I am being published by everybody but myself."

The whole tragedy in apparel on a stage rimed with frost, a brazier in the tiring-house and the new plot on its peg. Nick's Juliet, pitched low, comes through the cold like a bell under water; Burbage never takes his eyes off him. Pope's Nurse has to be told to leave something for Saturday. They try the vault: the tiring-house door for its mouth, a trestle bier, and the question of when the torches are lit. By the fifth act on Saturday it will be dusk in earnest. "Then light them when he says light," says William, "and not before." Garnish times it at two hours and fifty minutes and says so. James Burbage has stood the whole morning in the yard in two cloaks. "There's a great stone hall in the Blackfriars would hold a winter. I only say there is one." Raye has news to warm them: by Henslowe's book, Monday was the thirty-first of November (so the page is read; T1, to be checked). Kemp: "Thou seest, Will? There's a man keeps the calendar we're to be paid by."

**G091 · 2025-12-04 (Thu) · 09:00–10:30 · the_theatre · The last inexpensive revision**

The copying pass for the court rolls begins, the one after which, by his own undertaking of the twenty-sixth, nothing changes. Larkham has the rolls in a row and his pen wiped. William finds one change that matters, a cue in the vault that two men heard differently yesterday, and makes it. Then he finds two more that are merely attractive, a better word for the Friar and a turn in the Nurse's lament, and lets them go, which costs him more than writing them would have. Sam, holding the ink, observes aloud that the author has now obeyed the rule that is mostly applied to boys. Heminges does not praise the restraint; he simply proceeds without another argument, and the benefit is plain to everyone in the room. It is carrier day; William sends four lines down to the Bell by Larkham's boy, with the letter of the sixteenth and the Advent six: the lovers are played on Saturday. Photo: the rolls laid out in order on the trestle, each with Larkham's small sign in the corner.

"Will? Richard. From Holywell. I cannot sleep and I've woken the house trying." He wants one line changed. At the last, in the vault, he has the drug and the kiss and then five words, and five is not enough to die on; Richard had a prison, a groom and four murderers. Could he not have ten lines there? "No." "Six?" "Thou hast had two hours of them, Dick. He's done with words. Drink, kiss her, fall." A silence on the other side, and then the real matter: "And if the boy's voice goes in the vault?" "Then she wakes hoarse, as a girl would that had slept two days in a grave. Hold him as thou didst Wednesday. He'll follow thine eyes." "…Five words." "Five." "God keep thee, then, thou flint." "And thee. Sleep." William does not: the body shows 4 h 10.

**W100 · 2025-12-06 (Sat) · 14:00–17:05 · the_theatre · *Romeo and Juliet* first played**  
people: william_shakespeare, richard_burbage, nick_sorrell, thomas_pope, augustine_phillips, will_kemp, margery_pell, dorcas_ansell, alse_pinder

Clear and cold (invented weather); the swan flag stiff, the yard full by one. In the lower gallery, upright in her church hat with Alse beside her, sits Mistress Ansell, who has never been inside a playhouse. He walks out alone and speaks the first words of his own play, "Two housholds both alike in dignitie", with heart 148. Pope's Nurse stops the play three times. Phillips's Mercutio is laughed with and then, very suddenly, not, and the yard takes a long while to forgive it. Kemp carries the fan, speaks what is set down and one line more. Burbage plays a boy at a run. Nick comes in under every note and holds, and he is clearest exactly where William stopped asking him to show what he feels. The sun is gone before four, and the tiremen light the iron cressets round the galleries. When Romeo calls for light it is brought, real fire into real dusk, and photo 46 is that: torches on the stage, a small figure laid on a bier, the yard's faces lifted. In a house of two thousand not one nut is cracked through the vault, and in the upper rooms women weep aloud into their mufflers. William stands behind the arras through the fifth act with heart 126, then goes on as the Prince and shuts it. Nobody moves at first. The boxes hold £12 13s, the year's best, and the share is 40s. Margery Pell: "I've no line, masters. I watched it. I never watch." Walking home, the second frame: the full moon over Shoreditch church. Mistress Ansell has gone ahead and says nothing at the door but "Good night." Steps 13,400; slept 6 h 30.

**W416 · 2025-12-07 (Sun) · 07:45 · ansell_house · Mistress Ansell's verdict**

Before church, at the table, with the loaf unmeasured. She has had a night to prepare it. "She should have minded her mother." He waits. "And the mother should have minded her. And the friar is a meddler, and the nurse wants whipping, and the old men are fools, and I'd not have had the prince's office for a pension." He waits still. "The torches smoked. I had something in mine eye at the end. That is all I shall say of the end." Verbeke asks, carefully, whether the two houses were truly friends after. "For the length of a speech." Alse has held her question since four o'clock yesterday: "Is it true they're boys? All of them? Even the one that dies?" "Even she. He's fifteen, and lodges in Aldermanbury, and ate two pies after." Alse thinks it the most wonderful part of all. Mistress Ansell: "I shall not go again. Once is knowledge. Twice is appetite."

**W101 · 2025-12-07 (Sun) · 15:00 · ansell_house · Call: Kettleby, the Hoby summons**

"Master Shakespeare? Kettleby. From Somerset House, and on the Sabbath, for which I ask your pardon and not my lord's." My lord's son-in-law, Sir Edward Hoby, keeps a supper on Tuesday at his house in Canon Row for a guest he is particular to please, and has this day written to bid him (T1 letter; what was shown is T3, and this story's choice). Sir Edward desires the company, after supper, with the king play. "The king, sir? My lord said in plain words—" "My lord said not at court. This is a gentleman's parlour, behind a gentleman's door, and the gentleman is his daughter's husband." A pause. "Sir Edward has heard there is more of the play than Shoreditch has been let see. He desires it whole." "Whole." "I repeat a word; I do not advise upon it. Come by water at five. Twelve at table, and a small room." "Does my lord know whose guest it is?" "My lord knows whose house it is. God keep you." "And you, sir." He sends the Ansells' errand-boy to Aldermanbury with four lines for Heminges, and goes up to find the three leaves.

**G092 · 2025-12-07 (Sun) · 17:30 · ansell_house · Tuesday, Canon Row: the work of it**  
people: william_shakespeare, george_bryan, dorcas_ansell  
sources: meeting, calendar, notes  
George Bryan comes to the foot of the stair after evening prayer, sent by Heminges with the arrangement, because Bryan knows how a gentleman's house is entered. Mistress Ansell lets him as far as the third step. They do not talk about what Sir Edward's guest wants to see, or why; they talk about bodies, paper, candles and time. Nine players and two boys on a floor the size of a counting-house; the three leaves out of Garnish's chest, and who has not spoken them since June; the screen for a tiring-house; candles to be kept off the players' feet; the tide, which serves at five and will not serve at midnight. "K. Richard" in Sir Edward's letter is to be their king and nobody else's, and neither man says anything more about it than that. William writes the address on his tables, and under it the work: "Canon Row. v of the clock, by water. the 3 leaves. a glass." Bryan, going: "And bow to the chair by the fire, whoever is in it." 

#### Week 50 · Mon 12-08 – Sun 12-14

**G086 · 2025-12-08 (Mon) · 09:00–10:15 · the_theatre · The correction after success**

Saturday's success has not made every cue secure. Nick asks, carefully, for a clearer lead into one exchange in the orchard, where Romeo's last word and his own first come too close and he has twice gone in a breath early. Burbage treats the request at first as unnecessary caution: it went, did it not, and the yard wept. William backs the boy. He has seen a fraction of doubt spread through a scene like a crack through a pane, and the orchard is all pane. The lead is changed by two words. Larkham marks the book and both rolls before anyone carries off a rival memory of the old ones, and the copies go on with his small sign in the corner. Burbage, being fair, tries it and says it is better. Then Phillips calls Nick away to the lute, and the rest of the morning goes on the king for tomorrow night.

**S112a · 2025-12-09 (Tue) · 17:00–22:30 · canon_row · The Hoby supper: a king whole**  
people: william_shakespeare, edward_hoby, margaret_hoby, robert_cecil, richard_burbage, augustine_phillips, thomas_pope, john_heminges, george_bryan, nick_sorrell, abel_garnish  
sources: location, meeting, photo, body  
*Errors* at two in Shoreditch, then home for a clean band, Old Swan Stairs at five, and up to Westminster on the last of the flood in an iron frost (invented weather). Sir Edward Hoby's great chamber is panelled in polished oak, with a screen at one end and green rush-matting on the boards; the marchpane and hippocras are being cleared as they come in. Photo 47: forty white wax candles in branched silver doubled in the panelling, a fire, the screen, and by the hearth a chair with a small still figure in it, slight and a little crooked in the shoulder, seen from behind the players' entrance. Twelve at table; Lady Hoby, my lord's daughter, among them; my lord is not. It is Lady Hoby who makes the room work, the table drawn back, the candles moved off the players' floor, a stool found for Garnish behind the screen, while Sir Edward manages what the evening means. Sir Edward presents them himself: "My father-in-law's men, Sir Robert. And a king, as I promised. He will present himself to your view." They have a floor six paces by four. William dies as Gaunt close enough to a lady's skirt to smell the orris, and gives "inkie blots, and rotten parchment bonds" to the air a foot past Sir Robert's ear. Sir Robert sits three yards from their boots and does not move. Then the three leaves no public house has heard. Burbage gives the crown away with his own hands, washes off his own balm, sends for the glass, looks, and dashes it on Sir Edward's boards: four shillings, the first and only time. No one at the table stirs. At the end Sir Robert Cecil says one courteous sentence (W102). William refuses himself the comfort of deciding what it meant; nobody asks the players anything, and no errand comes of it. The steward puts a purse in Heminges's hand, £5, which is 12s 6d a share.

**S112b · 2025-12-09 (Tue) · 23:00–01:10 · ansell_house · The cold walk home**  
people: william_shakespeare, richard_burbage  
sources: location, body, photo, memo  
The tide has gone and the watermen with it; Heminges takes the boys and the baskets in Sir Edward's cart. William walks, and Burbage with him, by King Street and Charing Cross. Second frame of photo 47: the Strand at midnight, empty, iron-cold, every door shut, their breath going up straight. Burbage has been the king since the second of October and has broken a glass tonight. Somewhere about the Savoy he comes out of it, for the first time in ten weeks, and is only cold. "Did he like it? The little man?" "He said he'd remember." "Is that good?" "It's what he said." By Fleet Street, Ludgate and Cheapside, under a moon three nights past the full. They part at the mouth of the close and Burbage goes on north alone, whistling a jig. Body: steps 16,900 for the day; heart 96 on the walk; bed 01:20; slept 5 h 05.

**W102 · 2025-12-11 (Thu) · 02:20 · ansell_house · Voice memo: Cecil's one sentence**  
people: william_shakespeare  
sources: memo, body  
The dark hour between the first sleep and the second, very quiet, a house-creak. He has it by heart and says it over as he would a cue: "'It is very well penned, Master Shakespeare. I had not thought to be made to pity him; I shall remember that it can be done.'" A breath. "That what can be done? That a man can be made to pity a king put down. …Or that a king can be put down. The same four words." Another. "'Very well penned.' He looked at the hand, not the face. They all know me by the hand." He tries it as praise, as warning, and as a secretary's habit of saying nothing in a full sentence. "Two nights since I had forty candles and the Queen's own councillor, and I'm lying here construing him like Ovid." Then, drier: "He saw the three leaves. Hunsdon's daughter saw them. The Master cut them. All three are content. Nobody is ever told what the others know. …Sleep." Resting 60. Yesterday the lovers played a second time to a house nearly as full, and he said the Prologue with a steady heart.

**G093 · 2025-12-11 (Thu) · 09:00–10:00 · the_theatre · Was he pleased?**  
people: william_shakespeare, richard_burbage, george_bryan  
sources: meeting, notes, memo  
Burbage asks again, by daylight and with Bryan by, whether Sir Robert was pleased. It is his way of asking whether the work was good. Bryan can report only what he saw: the steward's courtesies, the purse given before and not after the last speech, Lady Hoby's thanks to the boys by name, and Sir Robert rising when the women rose and not before. William says that a powerful man's face is not a verdict, and that he has decided not to read it. So they go back to the work they can judge. Was the Queen's entrance late in the garden? It was. Did the shortened groom scene hold, played on a floor six paces wide? It did. The memo after is three words: "A late entrance." 

**W417 · 2025-12-12 (Fri) · 08:15 · ansell_house · Call: Cuthbert on a hall in the Blackfriars**  
people: william_shakespeare, cuthbert_burbage  
sources: call  
"Master Shakespeare? Cuthbert Burbage. From Holywell, before my father is up." His father went on Wednesday to look at a hall in the Blackfriars: stone, roofed, the old friars' dining-room, dry as a bone and out of the Lord Mayor's reach. He came home walking like a man of thirty. "He'll not tell me what's asked. He holds up six fingers and I pray it's shillings." No one is to speak of it; there is nothing to speak of. But the poet has a friend in that precinct, the printer. What manner of neighbours are they? William thinks of Field's table: grace before and after, the catechism below. "Respectable. They like quiet, and a sermon, and to be abed by nine." "Then we'd be as welcome as a drum in Lent." "I didn't say so." "You didn't need to. God keep you." "And you. Tell him the king's at two, and to wear both cloaks."

**M049 · 2025-12-12 (Fri) · 17:30 · ansell_house · The shortest day: a weaver wakes**  
people: william_shakespeare  
sources: notes, screen (draft), memo  
By the sun this is the shortest day of the year, whatever the almanac says of St Thomas: up at eight, gone a little before four, not eight hours of grey light between (T1·calc). He is home from the king by half past four in pitch dark and lights two dips. He does not write the queen and the weaver yet, which he is saving. He writes what comes after, out of order, because it comes: the weaver waking alone on the turf with his own head back on his shoulders, feeling for his ears, and trying to tell himself what has happened to him, and failing in the most beautiful way, in prose, getting every sense in the wrong place. "It shall be call'd Bottomes Dreame, because it hath no bottome" (Q1 1600; re-key). He says it aloud to the window and laughs with his shoulders. The memo tries the waking speech twice in Kemp's cadence and once in his own: "No. His. It's his." Then he puts the leaf under the others, where the queen's waking will go on top of it when he gets there.

**W103 · 2025-12-13 (Sat) · 20:30 · ansell_house · The sonnets put away**  
people: william_shakespeare, gib  
sources: notes, screen (draft), memo, photo  
After the reckoning (37s, and Sir Edward's 12s 6d laid beside it), both locks, and the Saturday count. Then the folder. He turns the leaves over once, the year's and a few older, single sheets fair-copied, without titles, numbers or names; he does not count them and reads only first lines. The weary one from February. The one that argues with painted beauty. One copied in October on a night the jaw would not let him sleep. One with a line about lilies that Master Burby now also owns. He squares the edges, ties them across and across with a length of tape from the property basket, and lays them at the very bottom of the coffer, under the canvas and under the account books of two years. Then everything else goes back on top. Nothing happened. No one is named, and no one is to be. The memo is six words, with the lid down: "Kept. Not given. That's a kind."

**M050 · 2025-12-14 (Sun) · 02:30 · ansell_house · Two sleeps in midwinter; the Christmas letter**  
people: william_shakespeare  
sources: body, notes (letter), screen (letter)  
A midwinter night in two halves: first sleep from about half past nine, awake at a quarter to two, second sleep from three to half past seven. In the waking hour he gets up in his bed-gown, lights one candle from the banked fire, and writes the Christmas letter while the house is silent: to Anne first, then a paragraph to his father and mother together, then one to each child. It is warmer than his day letters and he lets it be. He tells them he will be at Richmond and not at home, plainly, and promises no surprise arrival he cannot make. He tells them what is coming in the pack on Thursday, all but one thing each, so that something will still be a surprise. He seals nothing; the pack will be corded at the Bell. Body: first sleep 4 h 15, waking 1 h 15, second 4 h 30; resting 58.

**G094 · 2025-12-14 (Sun) · 17:00–19:00 · ansell_house · An ass's head on paper**  
people: william_shakespeare  
sources: notes, screen (draft), photo  
After evening prayer he works on the weaver's change, but mostly as a joiner would. What can a man wear on his head and still be heard in the galleries? How much of his face must show? Can he eat, or must he be fed, and is being fed funny? He draws a head on the back of a spoiled leaf, a crude thing with the ears too long and a hinged jaw, and finds the crude sketch more use than any elegant description: the jaw must move or the voice will be lost in it. Beside it in the margin: "ask Burbage's father. canvas and paste. light." He imagines the player's pride surviving the absurdity rather than being cancelled by it: a man in an ass's head who is still entirely sure he is the best actor in Athens. The page is funny before it is beautiful, and that pleases him. Photo: the sketch on the board by candle, the ears overrunning the margin, his own inked forefinger at the edge of the frame.

#### Week 51 · Mon 12-15 – Sun 12-21

**W418 · 2025-12-15 (Mon) · 12:20 · the_theatre · Call: Field on Burby, a Sunday, and a gap**  
people: william_shakespeare, richard_field  
sources: call  
"Will? Richard Field. From the shop, and I've a thing to say I'd liefer have said a fortnight since." He saw Burby's entry in the Hall book with his own eyes. "That's two of thine gone to press by other men's hands inside a twelvemonth, Millington's and now this. And thou'lt tell me again they're not thine to print." "They're not." "Then write me what is. A third poem. The press stands idle Fridays, and I'm tired of saying it." William says he has a duke, four lovers and a weaver in hand. "A weaver. God help us." Then: Sunday, St Thomas's, dinner after sermon, before he goes up to the court; Jacqueline will have a goose. "And the king is played, Will. Thou canst have no more use for the second volume." "There's more in it than one king, Richard." "More kings?" "It's a long book." A silence that has a shelf in it. "Sunday. God keep thee." "And thee."

**W104 · 2025-12-16 (Tue) · 09:00–12:30 · st_johns_clerkenwell · Court rehearsal before the Master**  
people: william_shakespeare, edmund_tilney, george_bryan, nick_sorrell, sam_latchet, thomas_pope, john_heminges, richard_burbage  
sources: meeting, location, notes  
The long room at St John's, a chair set at one end for the Master and understood by everyone to be a greater chair. Bryan drills the entry before a word is spoken: "In a presence chamber, lads, you bow to the chair even when the chair is empty." Sam bows to it four times to be safe. They give the lovers entire and pieces of the two comedies. Tilney hears Pope's Nurse with no change of face and lets every word stand: "Her Majesty was nursed. Leave it." Of the whole he says, "It is a pretty play. The fourth act is prettiest where it is shortest," and with his own pen takes forty lines of lamentation over a girl who is not dead. William finds, to his annoyance, that it is better. The Yeomen of the Revels open a chest and lend two cloaks for the Prince and Paris, to be returned on Innocents' night, counted. Nick speaks low and holds. Before they go, the Master signs the three books for Richmond, and says nothing at all of the king. On the tables: "ix of the clock, after supper. bow to the chair."

**G095 · 2025-12-17 (Wed) · 09:00–11:00 · the_theatre · Hands before court**  
people: william_shakespeare, edith_kettlewood, augustine_phillips, nick_sorrell, sam_latchet, humphrey_larkham  
sources: meeting, photo, notes, body  
Apparel, parts and properties are checked together this morning, all at once in the tiring-house, so that one department's certainty cannot hide another's problem. Edith Kettlewood, sent for at the stock's expense, adjusts a fastening at the back of Sam's page's coat that he cannot reach himself and has been pretending he can, and lets out a seam of Nick's at the chest without remark. She has her account written out before she threads a needle, and Heminges has promised it paid before Twelfth Night. Larkham identifies every current copy by its mark, not by who insists loudest that his is the right one; two stale rolls are found and burned in the brazier. Phillips counts the boys' songs. William's whole work is to answer questions at once and not to create fresh ones, and he finds it harder than writing. Photo: Edith's hands at the back of a small coat, a pin in her mouth, the needle's thread catching the window light. Steps 9,800.

**W105 · 2025-12-18 (Thu) · 12:00 · the_bell_carter_lane · The Christmas pack**  
people: william_shakespeare, thomas_hobbins  
sources: notes (letter, ledger), location, meeting, photo  
"Carrier in at the Bell", the carrier's final run of the year. A pack corded and sealed: the Christmas quarter's 60s in canvas; raisins of the sun and a paper of sugar for his mother; prunes and a ballad of the Queen's day for his father; a grey wool kerchief for Anne, with no verse; six Seville oranges, a sugar-loaf and a paper of cinnamon for the house; a length of holland linen for Joan, and a remnant of russet cloth that Mistress Ansell chose and he paid for. For Susanna a book of fables with no riddles in it; for Judith a ballad-sheet with its tune named at the head, and whoever reads it to her must sing it; for Hamnet a leather ball, stitched hard. A groat each, on the old terms. Hobbins writes every item into his list and reads the list back before he will cord it; nobody shouts anything down the yard after him this time. He has Anne's letter. All are loud. Susanna has found two faults in his last. Judith has the whole street informed that her father plays before the Queen at Christmas. Hamnet would know whether the Queen will see the sword that killed four Richmonds. His father says the bailiff's man has given up the porch for the winter. Last: "The great house by the chapel lost another pane in the wind on St Nicholas' night. I tell you so that you may know it is getting cheaper." He reads it in the yard, laughs with his shoulders, and reads it again.

**M052 · 2025-12-19 (Fri) · 10:30 · fields_printing_house · James Burbage's six hundred**  
people: william_shakespeare, james_burbage, richard_burbage  
sources: meeting, location  
Coming out of Field's shop with a quire under his arm, into Blackfriars Lane in a thin snow, he meets James Burbage and Dick coming the other way from the old friars' buildings, James with a carpenter's rule badly hidden under his cloak. There is no pretending, so James stops pretending. Over a hot spiced gill at the nearest door, and with an oath of secrecy taken on the gill, he confides it: the great stone hall, the Upper Frater, can be had of its owner outright, and he means to have it for six hundred pounds, "come the spring, when the money's found", and roof the company against the winter and the Lord Mayor and Giles Allen together. Six fingers, then, and not shillings. William says only that it is a great deal of money. "It's a great deal of roof." Dick says nothing and looks as if he has heard it every night for a month. William looks up at the stone lancets and thinks of what he told Cuthbert of Field's table and quiet neighbours, and keeps it to himself. He promises the secrecy and keeps that too. The day's other news, that the Queen has come up suddenly to Whitehall and nobody can say why, reaches them only at the Theatre at two.

**W419 · 2025-12-20 (Sat) · 08:00 · ansell_house · Call: Heminges, is it still Richmond?**  
people: william_shakespeare, john_heminges  
sources: call  
"Will? John. From Aldermanbury, with eleven pair of hose drying before the fire." The— the court is at Whitehall. The Queen came up from Richmond yesterday of a sudden (T1): my lord of Huntingdon is dead in the north (T1), and she would break it to the Countess herself. Heminges has Davy Gannet's tilt-boat bespoken for Wednesday's flood and a deposit down. Is Christmas still kept at Richmond, or are they to play at Whitehall, or nowhere? William has had it from Kettleby's boy an hour since: the Queen goes back Monday, and the players are looked for at Richmond as appointed. "Monday. Good. Then the tilt-boat stands." A breath. "And if she doesn't go Monday?" "Then we follow the Queen, John, like everybody else." "Item, a second deposit. God keep you." "And you." The lovers play today for the fourth time; a new moon tonight.

**S113 · 2025-12-21 (Sun) · 08:00 and 16:00 · ansell_house · St Thomas: the shortest day**  
people: william_shakespeare, dorcas_ansell, alse_pinder, richard_field, jacqueline_field  
sources: calendar, body, location, meeting, photo, notes (ledger)  
St Thomas's Day, which the almanac's custom calls the shortest, though by the sun the turn was a week since. Before church, poor women of the parish come to the door a-gooding, and Mistress Ansell stands on her step with a measure of wheat-meal and a bag of halfpence and gives to each by name, in a year when the loaf is two fingers and a thumb short. "It is not charity if you don't know whom you're giving it to." Alse carries the bag. After service the churchwardens have the dole-table out by the south porch, wheaten loaves, grey frieze and a dish of pence, and he puts two shillings of his own in the box without a word, because Mistress Ansell is watching to see whether he will make a show of it. Photo: the long dole-table by the porch, the loaves heaped, hooded women's breath going up. In the ledger, among church and gifts: "St Thomas, ij s." After sermon he walks to Blackfriars for Jacqueline's goose and is told over it that a man going to court should take a second shirt. Field asks nothing more about the second volume, which is worse. Home by half past three with the light already going; candle at four; bed by eight. Body: steps 7,300; slept 8 h 20 in two parts, with an hour awake about two in which he thought of nothing but where the Prince stands.

#### Week 52 · Mon 12-22 – Sun 12-28

**W106 · 2025-12-22 (Mon) · 11:30 and 14:00 · ansell_house · Foul weather; the rent paid early**  
people: william_shakespeare, dorcas_ansell, margery_pell, john_heminges  
sources: notes (ledger), calendar, body, meeting, photo  
Wind and sleet out of the east since before light (T1: foul weather in London this day). He pays the Christmas quarter three days early, twenty shillings counted twice, since on the quarter day he will be up the river. Mistress Ansell writes the receipt in her husband's law-hand and promises to keep the chamber aired, the coffer unvisited and the cat off the lord's book. The latch on his door, which has stuck and sprung by turns all year, has been mended this morning by her joiner, and when he offers to pay she says it is the house's latch and not the lodger's. It shuts the first time. *Errors* is played at two because it was billed, to a yard of some sixty souls and a few wet gowns. Margery Pell: "Four shillings and tenpence, and I'd have given them their pennies back to go home." It is the Theatre's last play of the year. By dusk Heminges has it from Somerset House that the Queen has put off her going to Richmond for the weather (T1), until tomorrow. "Item, she has sense. The tilt-boat stands." Steps 6,900; the walk home is into the wind the whole way.

**M053 · 2025-12-22 (Mon) · 12:15 · old_swan_stairs · Foul weather at the stairs: Gannet's tilt-boat**  
people: william_shakespeare, davy_gannet  
sources: location, body, meeting  
Between the rent and the play he goes down to Old Swan in the sleet, because Heminges's bespoken boat is Gannet's and Gannet must be looked at. The river is a brown chop under the bridge and the gusts come up it like a bellows. They lash the canvas down over the tilt-boat's hoops together, William holding and Gannet knotting, and Gannet confirms it for Wednesday's flood, six oars and himself, "if the Queen leaves any water in the river". He has it already that she will not go to Richmond at all, that the river is to freeze by Friday, and that the Spaniard has heard of the weather and is coming. One of the three is a third true. William walks up Fish Street Hill to the Theatre with his cloak soaked through on the windward side. Heart 92 on the hill.

**W420 · 2025-12-23 (Tue) · 09:30 · the_theatre · Hose, baskets, and the order of going**  
people: william_shakespeare, john_heminges, george_bryan, will_kemp, thomas_pope, abel_garnish, sam_latchet, nick_sorrell  
sources: meeting, notes  
The sleet has blown through and left it hard and bright. In the tiring-house Heminges holds a muster such as my lord might: every man to show both heels. New hose for the whole company, out of the stock, 26s. Kemp presents his feet like a duchess. "Find a hole in that, grocer. Thou'lt find none; I've walked on my hands since November." Pope's old pair is produced, with a heel a fist could go through, and the mystery of Greenwich is at last solved, to Pope's entire satisfaction. Garnish packs three books and sixty rolls in a chest and sits on it. Bryan gives the order of going: sharers, hired men, boys; nobody to speak to a yeoman of the guard first; nobody to touch a hanging. Sam asks whether the Queen is truly there or whether it is the chair again. The court went up to Richmond this morning (T1). Nick has not sung a note for four days, by Phillips's order, and looks as if he were carrying a full cup.

**W107 · 2025-12-24 (Wed) · 07:00–15:30 · old_swan_stairs · Upriver to Richmond**  
people: william_shakespeare, davy_gannet, john_heminges, richard_burbage, will_kemp, augustine_phillips, thomas_pope, george_bryan, abel_garnish, hired_men, nick_sorrell, arthur_meeks, sam_latchet  
sources: location, notes (ledger), body, memo, photo  
Before light, both locks: he keeps nine shillings out for the journey and counts the rest, £33 0s. At Old Swan, Davy Gannet's tilt-boat, canvas-hooped, with six oars and Gannet himself at the stern, takes on the baskets, the chest with Garnish on it, seven sharers, six hired men and three boys, on the first of the flood. The paper goes in first and driest, under the hoops, with the rolls in oiled cloth. Photo: white frost on the gunwale and the scarlet of my lord's livery coats folded on a basket. Gannet casts off with the news: "Westward ho! The Queen's at Windsor, there's ice at Kew, and they've made peace with Spain." She is at Richmond, there is rime on the reeds and no ice, and there is no peace. Past the Swan's raw ring, Lambeth, Chelsea, Putney; bread and cold beef at the oars' second rest; Kemp sings, and Burbage tells Sam the name of every great house and is right about half. Four hours and a little. Then round the last bend, the towers and their gilt vanes standing out of a white meadow. They are lodged at the Feathers on the Green, three to a bed. At the Feathers the lodging is settled by what is practical: the dry room for the paper, the baskets where a man carrying them need not climb, the boys under Phillips's eye. The memo, at the inn window, short: "A year ago, Greenwich. Same cloak." Steps 6,200.

**S114 · 2025-12-25 (Thu) · 09:00–20:00 · the_feathers_richmond · Christmas Day at the Feathers**  
people: william_shakespeare, george_bryan, thomas_pope, will_kemp, augustine_phillips, nick_sorrell, arthur_meeks, sam_latchet  
sources: calendar, meeting, body, photo  
Christmas Day, quarter day, a strange town under frost. The company goes to the parish church in a body, in clean bands, and is stared at as players are; the court keeps its own chapel behind the wall two hundred yards off. Dinner at the inn's long table on the stock: brawn and mustard, a goose, a minced pie as broad as a drum-head. Pope carves. Phillips has the boys sing a carol in three parts and gives Nick the lowest and fewest notes. Kemp proposes the health of the grocer who will be first on the Queen's next paper and wait for his money till his beard is grey. Bryan forbids the ale after six and is obeyed, which is itself a wonder. William sits a while thinking of another table, nine at it and his own stool carried back to the shop, and of a grey kerchief that should be there by now. Sam asks whether a court laughs differently from a yard, and the grown men give him four answers that cannot all be true; William says the only answer worth having will be heard tomorrow at about ten. Then Sam asks him how a fairy runs, and he gets up and shows him. Steps 4,800; slept 5 h 50 in a shared bed.

**S115a · 2025-12-26 (Fri) · 10:00, 17:30 and 21:00–23:50 · richmond_palace · St Stephen's night: Navarre before the Queen**  
people: william_shakespeare, elizabeth_i, henry_carey_hunsdon, edmund_tilney, richard_burbage, will_kemp, thomas_pope, nick_sorrell, sam_latchet, john_sincler, george_bryan  
sources: location, meeting, photo, body, calendar  
In the morning, a frame of Richmond's towers and gilt vanes across the frosted Green. Then the waiting, which is longer than any of them would like and matters less than all of them are tempted to think. Photo 48, first frame (the prompt is in § 8), at half past five: the plain service room the players are given to tire in, a few candles and a small fire, a rolled part on a trestle, the gilt crown set out of harm's way, someone's hands finishing a fastening; and through the half-open door a slice of the great hall, a hundred white wax tapers lit in their wired branches over sweet-flag rushes, and the corner of a canopy of state with nobody under it. Sam bows to it through the door. At nine it is not empty. The Chamberlain's Men play before the Queen this night (T1; the title is invented). He enters first, in a paper crown and the old velvet, on "Let Fame, that all hunt after in their lyues", with heart 140. He does not look at the state and sees it the whole time: my lord standing by it with his white staff, and a stillness in the chair. She laughs at Pope's Armado, aloud, once, from deep in the chest. Kemp's Costard speaks what is set down and three lines over, to her face, and gets her hand lifted for it. At the last Sincler comes on in black, and the hall goes quiet and stays so through the owl's song, "Tu-whit to-who", until the chair stirs. Bryan, after: "She kept her seat to the songs. That's as good as a purse, and likelier." Toward midnight, in the yeomen's buttery over a cup of hippocras, my lord comes through and claps William on the shoulder hard enough to rattle what teeth he has left, and goes out again without a word. Steps 7,500.

**S115b · 2025-12-27 (Sat) · 21:00–00:20 · richmond_palace · St John's night: the lovers; a button lost**  
people: william_shakespeare, elizabeth_i, henry_carey_hunsdon, nick_sorrell, richard_burbage, thomas_pope, augustine_phillips, will_kemp, john_heminges, sam_latchet  
sources: location, meeting, photo, body  
The Chamberlain's Men again (T1; the title is invented): the lovers, ten days short of a month old. He walks out between the degrees and speaks the Prologue with heart 150, the highest the year has shown. Indoors every hour is night, and the vault needs no dusk. Pope's Nurse does what my lord said a nurse would do. Nick comes in under every note in a hall where a whisper carries, grave and low, and holds to the dagger; Burbage does not once look away from him. There is no hush like the Theatre's at the end; there is a rustle, and one cough, and then the chair rises. Afterwards a stir at the upper end. A gold button set with five pearls is gone from the Queen's kirtle since the play began (T1). Photo 48, second frame (§ 8), at twenty to midnight: a boy on his knees in the rushes with a short candle held low, a yeoman's scarlet hose at the edge of the light. Sam searches the length of the stage-front and under the trestles for half an hour and does not find it. Nobody ever does. Kemp, aside, as the yeomen sweep with their tapers: a little gold thing may raise a bigger search than a missing player ever did. Heminges tells him to keep the observation to himself, and he does, until the inn. Steps 8,200.

**S115c · 2025-12-28 (Sun) · 21:00–23:10 · richmond_palace · Innocents' night: *Errors*, and the jig**  
people: william_shakespeare, elizabeth_i, nick_sorrell, will_kemp, augustine_phillips, arthur_meeks, richard_burbage, john_heminges, george_bryan  
sources: location, meeting, body, calendar  
Holy Innocents, a Sunday, and the one Sunday in the year they play (T1 for the night; the title is invented). It is a year to the day since the two *Errors*. Morning prayer keeps its place in the day; the night is the Queen's command, and the only exception to the Sunday rule. He gives Egeon's long tale to a hall with cups in its hands, heart 118, and has two hours to stand behind the traverse and listen to people laugh at a thing he made when he was younger. Everyone is tired, and small courtesies count: he spends the second act helping a hired man find the right roll in Garnish's chest, instead of rehearsing his own nerves aloud. Then the jig, by my lord's leave: Kemp, Phillips on the cittern, and the boys for the burden. Nick has a verse. He comes in on the note, not under it, because he is happy. The voice drops out of the tune and comes back a man's, a fifth lower, in front of the Queen of England. He stops, considers, and makes her a bow, exactly, as a queen would. The hall laughs, and kindly; so, by Bryan's account, does the chair. Arthur Meeks takes up the verse on the next beat as if it had been set so. Behind the traverse, afterwards: "It's gone, sir." "It's come, Nick. Sly shall teach thee to fence." Steps 7,900.

#### Week 53 · Mon 12-29 – Wed 12-31

**W108 · 2025-12-29 (Mon) · 09:00–14:00 · the_feathers_richmond · Downriver home; my lord is content**  
people: william_shakespeare, ambrose_kettleby, john_heminges, nick_sorrell, will_kemp, davy_gannet  
sources: location, meeting, notes (ledger), body, photo  
Kettleby finds them at the Feathers among the baskets, in a furred gown, with a purse. "My lord bids me say (and I soften it) that he is content." The purse is my lord's own, not the Chamber's: four pounds, "for hose", which Heminges divides on a barrel-head into eight parts of ten shillings. They are looked for again at Twelfth Day. Of the Queen's money, nothing; an office is still empty. Heminges enters my lord's purse on one leaf and the Queen's three nights on another, received and expected, and William does not argue with the distinction. Two borrowed cloaks go back to the Yeomen, counted. Then Gannet's tilt-boat, on the ebb, in thin sun. Nick sits by William in the stern and asks, in the new voice he does not yet trust, what a queen does when she is a man. "He learns to stand differently. Thou'st time." Kemp wants the ass's head made by Candlemas and has views on the ears. Old Swan by two. Steps 5,400 for the day.

**M056 · 2025-12-29 (Mon) · 14:30 · bull_inn · Spiced ale at the Bull; home to the stair**  
people: william_shakespeare, richard_burbage, will_kemp, john_heminges, augustine_phillips, thomas_pope, george_bryan, dorcas_ansell, gib  
sources: meeting, location, body  
Up Fish Street Hill from Old Swan with the baskets gone on to Shoreditch in a cart, and the seven sharers turn in at the Bull in Bishopsgate Street as if by agreement, though nobody proposed it. Roast capon and a bowl of lamb's-wool, the roasted apples bursting in the hot ale. Nobody argues. Nobody threatens to go to the Admiral's. Kemp tells the button story at last, at length, with the yeomen's tapers done in spoons. Heminges sets my lord's purse on one side of his book and the Queen's three nights on the other and will not be teased out of the distinction. Nobody at the board says aloud what every man there thinks about where the company stands at the year's end, between Easter and Innocents' night, and it is better unsaid. At four William walks the two hundred yards home. Mistress Ansell has the parish's five days ready for him on the stair, all of them, and Gib is on the Chaucer. He puts my lord's ten shillings in behind both locks without counting the rest. Steps for the walk 2,100.

**W109 · 2025-12-30 (Tue) · 06:30–11:00 · ansell_house · A letter to Anne; a page for Bottom**  
people: william_shakespeare, dorcas_ansell  
sources: notes (letter), screen (draft)  
No play till the new year. First the letter, shorter than any he has sent and with more in it. They played three nights and were liked; the Queen laughed at Pope; they will be paid when an office is filled. Then: "You asked me a thing at Shottery brook and I answered it ill. I work in London. I live where you are. The rest is arithmetic." And the arithmetic: what is in the coffer; that it is nine pounds more than at New Year, after all that went down by the carrier; that he has asked nobody the price of any house and will not until the sum is not laughable. "Tell me when it loses another pane." A paragraph to each child, by name, the same length. Then a clean sheet, and the two beginnings of the autumn meet. First the workmen at their rehearsal in the wood, the weaver correcting everyone, the bellows-mender piping "Most radiant Pyramus, most lilly white of hue" in a voice that will have to be a girl's (Q1 1600; re-key), and the weaver going off into the brake a man and coming back not. Then the queen from the page of ruined seasons wakes among her flowers, "What Angell wakes me from my flowry bed?", and what she sees is a weaver in an ass's head, singing to show he is not afraid. He writes it to the foot of the leaf, laughing without sound, and says the weaver's lines over in Kemp's own lordly cadence to be sure of them. Mistress Ansell, at dinner: "Comedy. I heard the feet."

**M057 · 2025-12-30 (Tue) · 19:30 · fields_printing_house · Pages by Field's hearth**  
people: william_shakespeare, richard_field, jacqueline_field  
sources: meeting, screen (draft), photo  
After dark he carries the morning's leaf to Blackfriars, with the three pages of workmen and the queen's ruined seasons folded inside it: pages, not a play, a dozen leaves of a thing with no last act. Field has a sea-coal fire and Jacqueline has mulled wine in two pewter cups on the settle. Photo, before he unfolds anything: the firelight on the top leaf, the ink brown and fresh, the two cups on the oak beside it. Then he reads. Field, who is sober about playhouses and suspicious of fairies on doctrine, gets through the casting of the play with a straight face and is undone by the bellows-mender's "Most radiant Pyramus, most lilly white of hue". He laughs till he has to wipe his eyes on his sleeve. "Thou hast put all Warwickshire into Athens, Will. Thistles, weavers, asses and moonshine." Jacqueline wants to know whether the queen is punished for loving an ass. "Not yet. I haven't got that far." "Then don't." Field asks, at the door, whether the second volume of his Chronicle is to see the new year in Great St Helen's. "It is." "God keep thee, then, and it."

**S116 · 2025-12-31 (Wed) · 20:30 · ansell_house · The year's book closed**  
people: william_shakespeare  
sources: notes (ledger), screen (ledger, almanac), calendar, memo, photo  
The light went before four, and outside the frost is hardening on the stones of Crosby Place, where a man lives who is no longer Mayor. After supper he rules a line under the last entry and casts the year on one leaf; the page is the notes specimen in § 8, and every figure here is that page's. The coffer: £33 10s, counted twice, where it stood at £24 10s when he opened the book. Received in the year, £55 14s: his shares of the weeks played; my lord's ten shillings of Monday, on a line of their own; his part of the Queen's warrant of March, his part of Sir Edward's purse, and a pound of Quiney's. Laid out, £46 14s. Sent home, four quarters, the Lent quarter by his own hand and lighter by 42s, which went with the rest to his father's shops, and thirty shillings over the quarter's put into Anne's lap in August. Paid at Stratford for his father, £5; toward the Sadler cousin's barn, 20s; to the brief, £2; lent to Richard Quiney £2 and had again £1, "he oweth j li." Of what is left he marks twenty pounds in his head as not to be spent, and writes no reason beside it. Owed: "of the Queen, for three plays at Richmond at Christmas, not yet had." The same line began the year, with Greenwich in it. Made: "Navarre ended. The King ended. Verona ended. A thing of a wood near Athens, xij leaves, not ended." Then a list in no column. The lease, two years less. The loaf, two fingers. A new Mayor, who has not yet written to anybody. Field's Chronicle, still here. Two poems on Harrison's board, still selling. "Sweet Shakspeare", printed at Cambridge, by a man he has never met. The play of the wood: go on with the workmen, and let them be men before they are asses. Under *Words*, the last note of the year: "N. S. his voice gone at Richmond, the 28th. The next wants a different music." He does not write down what lies at the bottom of the coffer. He has a new penny almanac, unmarked, and puts it on the old one. Of what he made this year, some is the company's, some is Burby's, some is the Master's to cut, and some is under two locks. He knows which is which. Toward the end of the candle, with St Helen's bell going for the old year, the memo is short and low and goes nowhere: "Counted. …Tired. Not done. Good." He lights a fresh candle and takes up the twelfth leaf.

---


## 7. Per-source design

The canon's rules of modernity (canon § 2) govern every surface. This section gives the recurring *patterns*: what a builder generates on an ordinary day, how often, and with what numbers.

### 7.1 Calendar

**The shape of a playing week** (Monday to Saturday in season).

| Time (sundial) | What |
|---|---|
| First light to about 08:30 | Waking by daylight; private writing or reading (§ 7.6). In midwinter he rises by candle |
| 09:00–11:00 | "Rehearsal, 9" on two or three mornings a week, daily in the week before a new play. Other mornings: company business, copying decisions, or more writing |
| 11:00–12:00 | Dinner: at the Jackdaw from Easter, the Bull or the Cross Keys in winter, or Mistress Ansell's board at 3d |
| 14:00–about 16:30 | "Play: *title*, *house*, 2 of the clock", with "(I play)" on about four days in six |
| 16:45–18:00 | Reckoning or talk, repairs, the walk home |
| About 18:00 | Supper |
| Evening to 21:00–22:00 | Letters, reading, writing by candle. Not every night is a masterpiece in progress |

**The other shapes.** *Sunday:* church at St Helen's, morning prayer and sermon, then a long walk, a letter, and dinner at the Fields' about monthly; never a public play. *Lent and August in London:* writing mornings, walks, reading at the stalls, no "Play" entries; rehearsals from Fri 11 Apr. *Stratford:* "Holy Trinity" on Sundays, "Market" on Thursdays, family days, no company entries. *Road days:* departure and arrival, with "Bait at…" and the night's town. *Court:* "Richmond: *title*, before the Queen" on 26, 27 and 28 Dec, the 28th being the year's only Sunday performance.

**Standing layers** (generate for every applicable day).

| Layer | Rule |
|---|---|
| Feasts and Sundays | From source book § 1: Septuagesima 16 Feb · Shrove Tuesday 4 Mar · Ash Wednesday 5 Mar · Lady Day 25 Mar · Mid-Lent (Mothering) Sunday 30 Mar · Palm Sunday 13 Apr · Good Friday 18 Apr · Easter Day 20 Apr · St George 23 Apr · Low Sunday 27 Apr · May Day · Rogation Sunday 25 May · Ascension 29 May · Whitsunday 8 Jun · Trinity Sunday 15 Jun · Corpus Christi 19 Jun (reckoning only) · Midsummer 24 Jun · St Peter 29 Jun · St James 25 Jul · Lammas 1 Aug · St Bartholomew 24 Aug · the Queen's birthday 7 Sep · Michaelmas 29 Sep · St Luke 18 Oct · SS Simon and Jude 28 Oct · All Saints 1 Nov · Martinmas 11 Nov · Accession Day 17 Nov · Advent Sunday and St Andrew 30 Nov · St Thomas 21 Dec · Christmas 25 Dec · St Stephen 26 Dec · St John 27 Dec · Holy Innocents 28 Dec. Add "evening prayer" on feast days |
| Law terms | Open and close days only: Hilary 23 Jan / 12 Feb · Easter 7 May / 2 Jun · Trinity 20 Jun / 9 Jul · Michaelmas 9 Oct / 28 Nov |
| Quarter days | 25 Mar, 24 Jun, 29 Sep: "Rent, Mistress Ansell, xx s., by noon". Christmas is paid early, on 22 Dec. Firing and washing 6d a week from Michaelmas |
| Moons | New and full, as the almanac prints them, with no times (§ 5.1) |
| His company | The playing days and titles in § 5.3; dark days written "No play" |
| The rival bill | "Rose: *title*" on each playing day, from § 5.3 (Henslowe's titles, corrected weekdays, never a Sunday; 18 May blank) |
| The carrier | "Carrier in at the Bell" on the fifteen Thursdays in § 5.1. The unscheduled Mon 21 Jul is *not* on the calendar, because he does not know it is coming |
| Family days | 2 Feb "Twins' day" · 11 Mar Richard · 15 Apr Joan · 23 Apr unlabelled ("St George" only) · 26 Apr "my christening day" · 3 May Edmund · 26 May "Susanna, xij" · 13 Oct Gilbert |
| Company days | Saturday: "Reckoning". 4 Mar: "My lord's birthday" (T1 date). 7 Jul: Burbage. 25 Nov: Heminges |
| Legal year | Headings read "1594" until 24 Mar and "1595" from Lady Day |
| Private marks | A small cross with no label on the lady's days, seven in the year: 5 Jan, 28 Feb, 22 Mar, 22 May, 8 Jun, 14 Sep, 29 Oct (§ 5.1). None on any other day |

**Generation rules.**

1. Calendar titles stay period-plain ("Rehearsal at the Theatre", never "creative sprint").
2. Do not turn every featured entry into an all-day block. Add routine entries on ordinary days within the itinerary and repertory.
3. Sunday worship recurs every week. The start time may vary within the morning.
4. Travel days include loading, meals, baits and arrival.
5. Future obligations may appear as expectations ("Court at Christmas?"), never as knowledge of what happens.
6. **Never on the calendar:** an event he does not yet know; an execution as an appointment (24 Jul reads only "No play"); the lady by any word; any tour.

### 7.2 Meetings

**Recurring groups.**

| With | Where | How often | Length | Texture |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| The sharers' reckoning | The tiring-house, or the Burbage hall out of season | Every Saturday in season; fortnightly otherwise; more in crises (S027, S068, S072, S088, W090, S107) | 4–10 min | Heminges itemises, Kemp disputes, Burbage is bored, Bryan knows how court works, William jokes it shut |
| Rehearsal | The Cross Keys parlour, then the Theatre stage | 2–3 mornings a week; daily before a new play | 4–8 min | Garnish prompting, boys corrected, a cue changed, Kemp extemporising. Real attempts at lines, not talk about attempts |
| Burbage, working a part | The stage, the Jackdaw settle, walking home | Weekly, more in spring and autumn | 3–8 min | Two men finishing each other's lines; embodied disagreement |
| Boy-player work with Phillips | The tiring-house | Two or three times a month | 3–6 min | Breath, cues, fatigue, hems; Nick's voice watched from October |
| Heminges and Bryan | The tiring-house door | A weekly short contact | 2–4 min | Exact instructions and custody of money and papers |
| First readings | The Jackdaw or the Burbage hall | Four: 28 Jan (*LLL*), 30 Jun (*Richard*), 19 Nov (*Romeo*), and a *Dream* fragment on 22 Nov | 6–10 min | William reads all the parts, badly on purpose in the clown's |
| Tilney | St John's, Clerkenwell | Three licensings (24 Jan, 10 Jul, 13 Nov) and one court rehearsal (16 Dec) | 3–5 min | Courtesy with a knife in it |
| Field | The shop; the Sunday table | About twice a month, not all captured | 4–9 min | Stratford vowels, books, the trade, reproach |
| Mistress Ansell's table | The hall at `ansell_house` | 2–3 a week (he boards irregularly) | 2–5 min | The plot, her verdict, the loaf, candles. Verbeke, Alse and Gib in the room |
| Paul's talk | The Walk and the stalls | Weekly | 2–4 min | News in fragments, three voices at once |
| Suppers | The Bull, the Split Hazel, the Jackdaw, the Bell, Fields' | One or two featured a month: Nashe ×3 (2 Mar, 18 May, 13 Jul), Daniel ×1 (23 Oct), Quiney at the Bell (24 Nov) | 5–10 min | News, humour, interrupted eating |
| The family table | Henley Street | Daily while home; selectively captured | 4–12 min | Nine voices, written overlapping; work mixed with talk |
| On the road | Inns and alehouses | Each night of travel | 2–5 min | Strangers. He listens more than he speaks |
| Court and great houses | Canon Row; Richmond | 9 Dec; 26–28 Dec | 4–10 min | Players at the edge of rooms, Bryan's coaching, whispers |

**Doorstep meetings.** A brief exchange at a door counts as a meeting (canon § 2), and GPT's threshold calls survive here: Mistress Ansell at the door on Fri 22 Aug, Gilbert collecting the carrier money on Wed 1 Oct, Bryan at the Theatre gate with court messages, Davy Gannet at Old Swan Stairs with news wrong by a third.

**Set pieces** run up to about ten minutes: S027 (the £20), S072 (tour or wait), S088 (the Mayor's letter), W096 (the *Romeo* reading), S112a (Canon Row) and S115a–c (Richmond).

**Counts.** Three to six a week in season, one to three in Lent and at Stratford.

**Rules.** At most seven speaking voices, and every meeting contains one concrete number (a sum, a line count, an hour, a distance). Everyone heard is present; crowds are texture and never named speakers. Food, tools and paper interrupt dialogue. Not every practical person is the same dry accountant. Short public-domain fragments are used sparingly, and an actor may repeat a phrase differently without the full speech being padded in. Period oaths are allowed (no restraint until 1606); obscenity is by implication only. A performance is never a meeting.

### 7.3 Calls

**The limits** (canon § 2). A short two-voice conversation, slightly close and dry, with no room tone, 1–4 minutes. Range: only as far as a running boy could carry a message and come back within the hour, so the City, Shoreditch, Bankside, Holborn, the Strand and Westminster. **Never** Stratford, the road, Greenwich or Richmond, so news from home always comes by letter, days late. Never more than two in a week. None while he is away (26 Mar – 10 Apr, 28 Jul – 21 Aug, 24–29 Dec). No call is ever missed and returned: there is no voicemail in this world. Nobody comments on the means.

**The shape.** The caller names himself and his place ("Will? John Heminges. From Aldermanbury."). One matter, one small human aside, a commendation to end. William calls out as often as he is called.

**The year's 34 calls**, re-synced to § 6 in the final pass: these are exactly the § 6 entries whose sources include `call` (checked by script: no week holds more than two, and none falls in an absence). Where William places the call, the row says so.

| # | Date | Caller ↔ William | The matter | The aside |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Mon 27 Jan | `john_heminges`, Aldermanbury (W112) | Four ladies and a dairymaid against three boys: two boys hired by the week as Maria and Katharine, Sincler for the messenger | His wife says Nick is growing through his sleeves |
| 2 | Tue 4 Feb | `john_heminges`, from the Cheapside mercer's (W010) | The Princess's gown: 50s, on credit against the Queen's £20 | Nick, being measured, bids him say the sleeves must be long |
| 3 | Wed 26 Feb | `richard_field`, Blackfriars (W017) | Harrison and the octavo *Venus* | A press stands idle on Fridays; when is the third poem? |
| 4 | Tue 11 Mar | `gilbert_shakespeare`, St Bride's (W020) | Their father's small debts: three shops, near £5 on a shop-ticket | It is Richard's birthday; neither has sent |
| 5 | Tue 18 Mar | `john_florio`, Southampton House (W120) | The paper left at the lodge was received; my lord is glad he prospers | *Chi va piano va sano*, offered as a kindness |
| 6 | Sat 19 Apr | `john_heminges` (W125) | Sam is hoarse before Easter Monday's cuckoo; Arthur learns the owl in case | "He sings it as if it were a judgment" |
| 7 | Mon 28 Apr | `richard_field` (W127) | A gap on his shelf where the Holinshed was; dinner on Sunday | "The cat reads only the second volume" |
| 8 | Sat 10 May | `cuthbert_burbage`, Holywell (W041) | Giles Allen asks £24 for the £14 lease | His father says he'll pull the house down |
| 9 | Tue 13 May | `gilbert_shakespeare`, St Bride's (S045) | A year since the first fire: news from his trade visit home | Hamnet won twopence at the butts |
| 10 | Thu 5 Jun | `richard_field` (W134) | Millington's octavo: "come Sunday and be angry at my table" | "Sunday. Not the Holinshed." |
| 11 | Sat 14 Jun | `john_heminges` (W136) | The morning after the butter riot: do we play? *The Shrew*, and come by the fields | "What Fleming? There's a man from Norwich lodges here." |
| 12 | Tue 17 Jun | `cuthbert_burbage` (W137) | Two hired men to sleep in the tiring-house; the gates chained after the play | The flag: "a white swan is a thing to throw at" |
| 13 | Fri 11 Jul | William calls `john_florio` (W302) | Verona: the river, the Roman playing-place, tombs in the open street | *Amor, tosse e fumo* |
| 14 | Thu 17 Jul | `john_heminges` (W058) | August dark: the hired men on half wages of 5s, out of the stock | *Labour's Won* "on the thirty-first of April" |
| 15 | Mon 21 Jul | `gilbert_shakespeare`, from the Bell (W306) | Fire again at Stratford, garbled; Anne's letter is at the Bell | He has run from the shop |
| 16 | Fri 22 Aug | `richard_burbage`, Holywell (M030) | Home and hungry: the house opens Monday; he wants Verona read | He has worn a hole in the king's roll at the crown |
| 17 | Tue 2 Sep | `ambrose_kettleby`, Somerset House (S087) | My lord will come to the king on Thu 2 Oct; Burghley's gout | Sent up for my lord's linen and his letters |
| 18 | Sat 13 Sep | `cuthbert_burbage`, from a scrivener's in Cheapside (W335) | The Lord Mayor's letter against plays, by hearsay: Surrey, "and of Middlesex" | "Middlesex is my father's house and two miles of fields" |
| 19 | Tue 16 Sep | `ambrose_kettleby` (W081) | My lord's answer: play on | A ropemaker's conscience in a furred gown, softened |
| 20 | Wed 17 Sep | `richard_field` (W337) | A Cambridge man has him in his margin: come to Paul's tomorrow at ten | Field read it "for the workmanship, which is middling" |
| 21 | Tue 23 Sep | `henry_wriothesley_southampton`, Holborn (S091) | Brief and unequal; my lord's attention is elsewhere | "Come and see me unhorsed" |
| 22 | Thu 25 Sep | `john_heminges` (W338) | Items for the dress rehearsal: the crown new gilt, 4s; Nick's gown let down, 18d | Nick eats like two |
| 23 | Mon 13 Oct | William calls `gilbert_shakespeare` (W403) | His twenty-ninth birthday; the money went safe and came back as a cheese | "I'd have done it for six with the shop pliers" |
| 24 | Fri 17 Oct | `john_florio` (S098) | Heneage is dead at the Savoy | A proverb in two tongues: a remedy for everything but death |
| 25 | Mon 20 Oct | `ambrose_kettleby` (W405) | Who signs for the Christmas money now | The court removes to Richmond today, among the hampers |
| 26 | Fri 7 Nov | `henry_carey_hunsdon`, Somerset House (S104) | Three nights at court, and not the king | Hunsdon has seen *Richard* at the Theatre |
| 27 | Sat 15 Nov | `richard_quiney`, the Bell (W095) | The fire brief, "two hundred houses, one fire with another" | His boots not yet dry |
| 28 | Sat 22 Nov | `richard_quiney`, the Bell (W413) | Supper at the Bell on Monday at six | "Then come alone and bring your own" |
| 29 | Tue 25 Nov | William calls `john_heminges` (W414) | His twenty-ninth birthday; the court hose and the boys' suits | Nobody is to startle Nick |
| 30 | Fri 5 Dec, late | `richard_burbage`, Holywell (W415) | The night before *Romeo*: he wants one line changed, and is refused | "God keep thee, then, thou flint" |
| 31 | Sun 7 Dec | `ambrose_kettleby` (W101) | The Hoby summons for Tuesday, with the king whole | On the Sabbath, for which he asks pardon |
| 32 | Fri 12 Dec | `cuthbert_burbage`, Holywell (W417) | His father's hall in the Blackfriars | Six fingers held up: "I pray it's shillings" |
| 33 | Mon 15 Dec | `richard_field` (W418) | Burby's entry in the Hall book; dinner on Sunday, St Thomas's | Jacqueline will have a goose |
| 34 | Sat 20 Dec | `john_heminges` (W419) | The Queen is at Whitehall of a sudden: is it still Richmond? | Eleven pair of hose drying before the fire |

**By caller:** Heminges 8 · Field 5 · Cuthbert 4 · Gilbert 4 · Kettleby 4 · Florio 3 · Quiney 2 · Burbage 2 · Hunsdon 1 · Southampton 1. By half-year: 12 in January–June, 10 in July–September, 12 in October–December. No call comes from Southampton in February, and no call from Florio carries a sonnet.

### 7.4 Voice memos

**Sound.** William alone, close and low. Sometimes walking (footfalls, breath, a cart going by), sometimes at his window, on the road, or in bed in the waking hour between first and second sleep (very quiet, a house creak, Gib). 30–120 seconds. He starts mid-thought, before its purpose is clear. He tries a line, stops, and tries it two or three ways. A practical reminder intrudes on an elevated thought ("…and candles. Remember: candles."). He never explains context to a listener. It ends abruptly: "…Enough." "…Sleep." "…No."

**Kinds and counts for the year** (about 2–4 a week, more on the road).

| Kind | About | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| A line being found | 45 | Walking to Shoreditch: three versions of a couplet, then silence. The *Romeo* feast sonnet tried in July. Gaunt in the Catherine Wheel |
| Private reaction to news | 20 | S019 Southwell (21 Feb) · S059a butter (13 Jun) · S069 Tower Hill (29 Jun) · S077 the executions (24 Jul) · S097 Arundel (22 Oct) · S103a Essex (5 Nov) |
| The road | 14 | W032b (9 Apr) · W062, the Grendon constable (29 Jul) |
| Home and Anne | 12 | After Shottery (5 Apr); on the ride back with her question; after her letters |
| The unnamed loves | 8 | Oblique; no names, and no pronoun that settles anything in the lord's case. After the call of 23 Sep. After 29 Oct |
| Money muttered | 10 | Saturday sums said aloud; a sum checked twice |
| The dark hour | 12, winter only | Between first and second sleep: prayers that stop halfway, fears, lists |
| Body | 6 | The tooth; the back; "I am too old to walk to Warwickshire" (he is 31) |
| Anniversaries and turnings | 6 | S004 the messenger in black (8 Jan) · S038 St George (23 Apr) · S045 the fire's year (13 May) · W102 Cecil's sentence (11 Dec) · S116 the year closed (31 Dec) |

**The register by season.** *Winter:* nimble, amused, impatient with his body. *Spring:* longer pauses; testing what a character can know. *June–July:* the qualifiers matter: "I heard", "Bryan says", "I did not see". *August:* domestic specifics, less self-explanation. *Autumn:* concern with what other workers must do after his revisions. *December:* quieter, less inclined to make a final statement.

**Rules.** He never names the lady. He never says where a listener could find him. He never reports an execution as seen, and no memo knows the future. Nothing reveals a T3 identity or supplies a fact he could not have. He never gives the journal instructions, and says "Remember:" or "Mark this", never "note to self". He may contradict the body data ("slept well enough").

### 7.5 Notes and writing

He keeps five kinds of written thing, and builders generate each as its own stream. Store a diplomatic image and a normalised transcript separately.

**1. The commonplace book** (`commonplace_book`). A small stitched book in a brown cover, unevenly filled, for extracts and observations under his own heads: *Of Kings · Of Love · Of Time · Words · Faces · Prices*. Quotations from his reading in the original spelling, each with source and leaf (Holinshed, Daniel, Brooke, Golding's Ovid, North's *Theseus*, Southwell's epistle). Overheard phrases and odd words: Florio's proverbs (March and July), the meal-market's dearth sayings (June), the Grendon constable's big words (29 Jul), Kemp's new name for him (September). *Prices:* the loaf against the notch, butter at 5d and at 3d, a quire of paper at 5d (4½d at trade for Field's Rochelle crown), Brooke 6d, Daniel 2s, Plutarch 14s, Southwell 6d, cloves 2d, the tooth 12d. *Faces:* one line about his father's hand on the old sketch, and nothing about the lady that names her.

**2. The drafts, his foul papers** (`lll_working_copy`, `richard_ii_working_copy`, `romeo_working_copy`, `dream_working_gathering`). Loose folio sheets, folded once.

- Secretary hand, with italic for names and stage directions.
- Speech prefixes abbreviated and inconsistent in a believable way: *Rom.*, *Ro.*; *Iul.*; *Nur.*; the Capulets' lady as *Wife*, *Mo.*, *La.*; *Rich.*, *Bul.*; *Bot.*
- Whole lines struck through and rewritten above, few blots, marginal line counts. Neither a blotted caricature nor a miraculous clean page.
- Marginal crosses where he means to return. Now and then a player's name for a character's (*Kemp* for *Peter*).
- Blank space is meaningful: an unfinished question, room for a sum, a page deliberately left unfilled.
- **Text rule:** where a draft quotes the finished play, use the early quarto text (R&J Q1/Q2, R2 Q1, LLL Q1, MND Q1) in old spelling and flag the page "public-domain text in an invented setting". Variant or abandoned lines invented for the draft are flagged "new fiction, not Shakespeare's text". No composition date is ever presented as a record.
- **The Lent rule:** before Sat 5 Jul the *Romeo* stream holds **only** Brooke extracts, lists of names, a plan of two houses, and single images. No dialogue, no scene, no line from the finished play.
- *Richard II* has two states from July: the working draft with the deposition, and the licensed book with it cut. *Romeo* is tied with red thread from 19 Oct, with unresolved marks kept for rehearsal. The *Dream* is twelve leaves on 31 Dec, unfinished, with an ass's head sketched on the back of a spoiled leaf (14 Dec).

**3. The account book** (`company_account_gathering` and his own ledger). A narrow ledger: receipts on the left, payments on the right, in his own shorthand of *Item* and roman numerals (li. s. d.). From March, *expected* and *received* in separate columns. A total every Saturday night, when he counts and checks the tie of his purse. A coffer count on quarter days and before and after each journey, matching § 5.1 exactly. Legal-year headings: "1594" until 24 March, "1595" from Lady Day (S028). Typical lines: "Item, of the Queen, for two plays at Greenwich, xx li., not yet had" (1 Jan) · "Item, my part of the Queen's money, xxxvij s. vj d." (15 Mar) · "Item, Mrs Ansell, the quarter, xx s." · "Item, ink, ij d." · "Item, lent R. Q., xl s., to be had again" (7 Aug) · "Item, of R. Q., xx s." (24 Nov) · "Item, to the brief, xl s." (24 Nov) · "Item, of the court, nil" (31 Dec).

**4. Letters** (`household_letters`). Drafts kept of what he sends and the originals of what he receives. Period form: salutation, business, commendations, place and date by feast ("From London this Candlemas day"), and "deliver these" on the outside. To Anne he is plain and short, and grows shorter and truer as the year goes (the letter of 31 Aug says what he is thinking). To the children, one playful paragraph each, equal weight. Anne's letters come in a clear, unlearned hand with strong sense, and from September under agreed heads: money, supplies, the household. Whether she writes or dictates to Susanna is left open. Letters move only by carrier or by hand.

**5. The tables** (`road_gathering` is a sub-use). Erasable leaves for lists that get wiped: errands, cues to mend, a rhyme, three words before a writing morning. The same leaf may show different contents on different days. On both journeys the tables and a small portable gathering are his whole desk.

**The sonnet papers** (`private_verse_leaves`) are a sub-stream of the drafts. Single leaves, fair-copied when finished, with no titles, no numbers and no addressee. Use the 1609 text. Which sonnets is the builder's choice, but none may be presented as written to anyone, and no verse is ever delivered to a named person. They are kept apart from the patron calls. On Sat 13 Dec they are tied with a tape and go to the bottom of the coffer.

These are artifact slugs for builders, not folders anyone sees in the world.

### 7.6 Screen

Every screen entry carries one `surface` tag: `draft` · `tables` · `part` · `plot` · `book` · `proof` · `ledger` · `letter` · `bill` · `pamphlet` · `stage` · `almanac`. A featured writing day captures three to eight segments; most ordinary days need fewer. Source books lie pinned open under a brass weight beside the sheet.

**A typical writing day: Tuesday 13 May, not cast.**

| Time | Surface | What shows |
|---|---|---|
| 05:10–05:40 | `tables` | Yesterday's errand list, wiped; three words written: "sit — ground — stories" |
| 05:40–07:30 | `draft` | *Richard II* 3.2, the sheet half-full from Saturday. About forty lines in two runs, with a long halt between |
| 07:30–08:15 | `book` | Holinshed open at Richard's return from Ireland, his finger in Daniel at the same place; the two read against each other |
| 08:15–10:30 | `draft` | Continues. One speech struck entire and rewritten shorter |
| 10:30–10:45 | `ledger` | A stray payment entered: candles, 4d |
| 11:00 | — | Dinner (no screen) |
| 12:30–13:30 | `part` | Egeon's roll, conned for Thursday |
| 13:40 | `bill` | The Bishopsgate post: the Theatre's bill and the Rose's (*Long Meg*) side by side |
| 14:00–16:30 | `stage` | From the tiring-house door: *Two Gents*. Kemp's Launce plays to an empty rope's end, because the dog is a story Kemp tells and never comes on stage. A watch-history item |
| 18:30–21:00 | `draft`, then `letter` | Thirty more lines; then the first paragraph of a letter home on the fire's anniversary, after Gilbert's call |

**A second day: Saturday 11 October, the tooth, not cast.**

| Time | Surface | What shows |
|---|---|---|
| 04:50–05:10 | `tables` | "cloves · Yaxley? · lark" |
| 05:10–07:00 | `draft` | *Romeo and Juliet* 3.5, a fresh sheet. The dawn parting: the lark and the nightingale exchange tried three ways in the margin, and the Q2 lines written out clean beneath |
| 07:00–07:20 | `book` | Brooke at the lovers' parting, a thumb on the page; Gib on the other half |
| 07:20–08:00 | `draft` | Continues to the Nurse's entrance, eighty lines in all; a cross in the margin |
| 12:30–12:40 | `ledger` | "Item, cloves, ij d." |
| 16:45–17:30 | `ledger` | Heminges's sheet at the Saturday reckoning in the tiring-house: *Richard II* twice this week |
| 20:30–21:30 | `draft` | *Richard II* 4.1 by candle: the deposition the Master struck, copied fair from his foul papers onto three clean leaves for the coffer |

**Rules.**

- Every `draft` screen names the play, act and scene (modern numbering in the label only), how full the sheet is, what is already done, and what is being written now.
- `stage` means he is physically present and not acting: at the Rose, or watching his own company from the tiring-house door. Never on a Sunday, and never at the Rose between 12 Jun and 19 Jul. **Canonical Rose attendances:** 14 Feb *Long Meg* (S016) · 20 May *1 Hercules* (W043) · 29 Aug *Longshanks* (S085) · 28 Nov *Harry the Fifth* (S108). Builders may add up to four more on days he is not cast, using Henslowe's titles and receipts only. Lost plays get staging and atmosphere, never invented plot beyond what the title allows; *Faustus* and *Tamburlaine* can be richer. Easter Monday (S036) is a `bill`, not a watch.
- `bill`: black-letter, title only, "by the Lord Chamberlain his Servants" or "the Lord Admiral his Servants". No author is ever named. Reading a bill is not watching a play. Tilts, pageants and the Lord Mayor's show are photo and location, not watch history.
- `proof` appears only at Field's.
- `pamphlet`: real 1595 imprints and public notices only. The *True Tragedy* octavo (31 May, his old play, no author) · Southwell's *Saint Peters Complaint* (bought 17 May) · Covell's *Polimanteia* with its "Sweet Shakspeare" margin (18 Sep) · Daniel's *Civil Wars* (bought 15 Apr) · North's Plutarch (Field's, from 15 May) · Spenser's *Amoretti* and *Colin Clouts Come Home Againe* and *Locrine* "By W. S." on the stalls · the riot-execution pamphlet (Allde for Blackwall, T1), seen and not bought on Sat 26 Jul · the Woburn fire tract (heard of 17 Sep, unverified) · the blue-starch precept read in church (1 Jun) · the 4 July proclamation nailed at Bishopsgate.
- `almanac`: the penny almanac, consulted for moons, terms and fairs; the *Dream*'s first page is written with it open at October.

### 7.7 Photo shot list (103 shots)

All from his eye, standing or seated (about 5 ft 7 in). Never of himself: no face, mirror, reflection or shadow; his own hands may appear holding a page, a basket or a pen. Nobody poses or looks into a lens. Light is only sun, fog, candle, cresset or fire. Text in frame follows the canon or is illegible. Never an execution, a corpse or blood, never a news event he did not see, never an impossible vantage. Expect three to six a week, more at Stratford, on the road and on feast days.

**Numbering.** This list numbers its own 103 shots. § 6 and § 8 cite photos by Fable's 48-shot base numbering ("Photo 26", "Photo 27"), which is not this list's: match a § 6 or § 8 photo to its row here by entry id, never by number. Every row's entry carries `photo` in its § 6 sources; § 6 also has photos with no row here, and those stand.

Shot 48 shows a door and nothing else; that is its point. Shot 47 is the limit of what the year shows of its violence.

### 7.8 Body

**Constants (T5).** About 170 cm and 70 kg (seasonal drift 68.5–71 kg; never a product claim). **1,300 steps per km**, with no second stride model. Resting heart rate 58–62 at baseline, ordinary range 55–70. Miles on horse or boat are not steps; on-stage movement and rehearsal pacing are. Sleep is an estimate. A raised heart rate is never a diagnosis of fear, love or guilt. Everything here is recorded and never remarked.

**London daylight, 1595** (computed for 51.51°N in apparent solar time, the sundial time he lives by; Stratford is within 4 minutes of these).

| Writing day at the lodging | 2,500–6,000 | Church or Paul's, a meal out, one loop to Leadenhall or Finsbury Fields |

| Household and rest | 2,000–5,500 | |
| Stratford ordinary day | 5,000–10,500 | The market, the fields, Shottery and back |
| Riding day | 4,500–7,000 | Leading the horse uphill, inn yards, stables |
| Foot road day | 44,000–68,000 | From the day's miles (table below) |
| Boat day | 3,000–7,000 | River distance is never converted to steps |
| Court day | 7,500–8,500 | Waiting in passages; late nights |

**The illnesses: the only ones in the year** (canon § 5.5).

| Episode | Dates | Effects in the data |
|---|---|---|
| Cold with a low fever | Mon 13 – Sun 19 Jan | Resting HR 66 → 76 at the peak (17–18 Jan), back to 66 by the night of the 19th and 62 by the 21st. Steps fall to 900–5,000 on the quietest days. Sleep disrupted 13–16 Jan, then long (9 h) on 17–18 Jan. Played through on the 17th; Cowley covers the 18th. Ansell's posset and lecture. No high fever |
| Low-back strain | From Tue 12 Aug, raising the Sadler cousin's barn frame | Flat 12–15 Aug (steps 400–1,200, resting 66–70). Stiff on the road 18–21 Aug: the spare pack-nag on the first stage, more stops. A bad moment shifting the armour-basket on Wed 27 Aug. Eased by about 15 Sep. No collapse, nothing radiating |
| Tooth, lower-left molar | Ache from Tue 9 Sep; abscess from Fri 10 Oct; drawn Tue 14 Oct by Yaxley | Wakings from 9 Sep; resting 64–70 from 25 Sep. The fierce nights, 10–13 Oct, with clove oil and wool: 3 h 30 – 4 h 45 sleep and midnight pacing. Peak 148 in the chair. Three bad days and a week of broth after; jaw tender to 21 Oct; resting 59–62 by the 22nd. Cowley covers on the worst days |
| Not illness | — | Saddle-soreness in Lent and blistered heels in July are travel texture. John's one passing cough is in a January letter and gone by the next. **No child is ever ill**, and no boy player either |
