In all: about 204 miles on a hired horse in Lent, and about 202 miles of road in summer, of which about 192 are walked.

### 7.9 Location

A point and a dwell time for each place in canon § 4; one house is one point. The road produces a track with inn stops. Coordinates mark historical sites, not surviving buildings. Routes are historically plausible, with no later bridges. Missing data beats teleporting.

**London distances on foot** (from the lodging; source book § 3.1).

| To | Distance | Walk |
|---|---|---|
| The Bull | 0.2 km | 3 min |
| The Cross Keys | 0.45 km | 5–7 min |
| Tower Hill | 0.9 km | 11–13 min |
| Old Swan Stairs | 1.0 km | 12 min |
| The Curtain | 1.2 km | 14–17 min |
| Paul's Churchyard | 1.2 km | 15–17 min |
| The Theatre | 1.4 km | 16–20 min |
| The Bell, Carter Lane | 1.4 km | 17–19 min |
| Field's, Blackfriars | 1.6 km | 20–25 min |
| The Rose, over the bridge | 1.9 km | 23–29 min |
| St John's, Clerkenwell | about 2.4 km, by Moorgate and Smithfield | 30 min |
| Gray's Inn | 2.5 km | 30–37 min |
| The Theatre → the Rose | 3.1 km | 37–47 min |
| Whitehall or Canon Row | 3.7 km, by Fleet Street and the Strand | 44–55 min |

**Bridge or wherry.** From the lodging to the Rose he walks over London Bridge (free, and no slower). From Paul's or Blackfriars to Bankside, or to Westminster, he takes a wherry from Old Swan Stairs or Paul's Wharf: 1d across, 2d to Whitehall. Boats embark and land above the bridge and never shoot its arches. Richmond is by tilt-boat with Davy Gannet on the flood.

**Dwell patterns.**

| Pattern | Points and typical dwell |
|---|---|
| Playing day, winter | `ansell_house` (night to 08:30) → `cross_keys_inn` (09:00–17:00) → home. In winter the Jackdaw is too far, so he eats at the inn or the Bull |
| Playing day, from Easter | `ansell_house` → `bishopsgate` → `bedlam` (pass) → `the_theatre` (09:00–17:00) → `the_jackdaw` (dinner 11:00–12:00; sometimes 17:00–18:30) → home |
| Writing day | `ansell_house` nearly all day; one loop to `leadenhall` or `finsbury_fields`; `st_helens_church` on Sundays |
| Book day | Home → `pauls_churchyard` (60–90 min) → `fields_printing_house` → home by Cheapside |
| Rose day | Home → `london_bridge` → `the_rose` → back by wherry to `old_swan_stairs` if he is flush |
| Reckoning Saturday | As a playing day, plus `burbage_house_holywell` out of season |
| Carrier day | Home → `the_bell_carter_lane` after the play, on the Thursdays in § 5.1. On Thu 26 Jun Heminges's man goes instead |
| Licensing day | Home → `st_johns_clerkenwell` (24 Jan, 10 Jul, 13 Nov, 16 Dec) |
| The warrant, 15 March | Home → `old_swan_stairs` → by wherry to Whitehall Stairs → `whitehall_palace`, the outer precinct only, to receive the coin from the Treasurer's clerk → on foot by the Strand and Holborn → `burbage_house_holywell` |
| Court night, 9 December | `the_theatre` → home → `old_swan_stairs` → by water to Westminster → `canon_row` → home on foot by the Strand, Fleet Street and Cheapside |
| Stratford day | `henley_street` → `high_cross_market` (Thursdays) → `guild_chapel_school` → `hewlands_shottery` (a mile west) → `clopton_bridge`; `holy_trinity` on Sundays, never framed with the children |
| Richmond, 24–29 December | By tilt-boat from Old Swan to Richmond stairs; lodged at `the_feathers_richmond` on the Green, sharing a chamber with Heminges and Bryan; `richmond_palace` 200 m away |

**Where he sleeps: all 365 nights** (checked: 320 at the lodging, 29 at Henley Street, 5 at Richmond, 10 on the road, 1 at the Bell).

| Nights | Place |
|---|---|
| Wed 1 Jan – Tue 25 Mar | `ansell_house` (84) |
| Wed 26 Mar | `high_wycombe` |
| Thu 27 Mar | `woodstock` |
| Fri 28 Mar – Mon 7 Apr | `henley_street` (11) |
| Tue 8 Apr | `woodstock` |
| Wed 9 Apr | `high_wycombe` |
| Thu 10 Apr – Sun 27 Jul | `ansell_house` (109) |
| Mon 28 Jul | `chalfont_st_giles` |
| Tue 29 Jul | `grendon_underwood` |
| Wed 30 Jul | `banbury` |
| Thu 31 Jul – Sun 17 Aug | `henley_street` (18) |
| Mon 18 Aug | `chipping_norton` |
| Tue 19 Aug | `oxford` |
| Wed 20 Aug | `beaconsfield` |
| Thu 21 Aug | `the_bell_carter_lane` |
| Fri 22 Aug – Tue 23 Dec | `ansell_house` (124) |
| Wed 24 Dec – Sun 28 Dec | `the_feathers_richmond` (5) |
| Mon 29 Dec – Wed 31 Dec | `ansell_house` (3) |

No other overnight travel may be generated without a canon change.

**Never a location point.** `tyburn` (the Lent ride both ways and the summer walk out go round it by Knightsbridge and Kensington, S030a, W032c, W061; only the 21 Aug return comes in past it, W073d, and nothing is recorded or described). `tower_hill` after 1 February. Greenwich (he is not at court there this year). `southampton_house` beyond the porter's lodge, and only once, on Mon 10 Feb. `whitehall_palace` beyond its outer precinct. Any house of the unnamed lady. The news places: Mousehole, Paul, Newlyn, Penzance, Plymouth, Clontibret and Guiana.

**The radius of fear.** From 13 June to 5 July his tracks stay within 600 m of home, except for the walk to the Theatre and back by the same road. He does not cross the river from 12 June to 19 July, and is not at `southwark_market` in that time.

## 8. Specimens

*Fold pass 2, sections 8 and 9. This merges the three bibles' § 8 and § 9 (`gpt`, `gem`, `fab`) under the canon, using Fable's dated-year first half (`fab-2a`) for the entry beats it fixes. Where a bible's specimen broke a binding rule or the canon, it was rewritten, and the fix is named under each specimen.*

**How to use the specimens.** Each one sets the voice for one journal source and is tied to a canon entry id, date and weekday. Every slug is canonical. Every line and number is T5 unless a tier is shown. Quoted play and poem text is public-domain early-print text in an invented setting, and must be re-keyed from the early edition before use (§ 9.4 C). Audio is given as JSON so a builder can lift it straight into the script format.

| § | Kind | Entries |
|---|---|---|
| 8.1 | Meeting script | S027 |
| 8.2 | Calls | S091 · S104 |
| 8.3 | Voice memos | S019 · S069 · W062 · W073a |
| 8.4 | Notes pages | S024 · S100 · S116 |
| 8.5 | Screen descriptions | S046 · M020 |
| 8.6 | Photo prompts | S006 · S011 · S032 · S059b · S065b · S115a · S115b |

### 8.1 Meeting script: S027

**`S027` · 2025-03-15 (Sat) · 11:40 · `burbage_house_holywell` · "Sharers' reckoning: the Queen's twenty pounds" · about 7 min (a set piece)**

People: `john_heminges`, `will_kemp`, `richard_burbage`, `william_shakespeare`, `james_burbage`, `thomas_pope`, `augustine_phillips` (seven speaking voices, the cap). `george_bryan` is present and silent.

*Context for the builder.* Kemp, William and Burbage fetched the £20 this morning from the office where the Chamber pays out, in the outer precinct at Whitehall (never inside the palace, never before the Council), and walked back by the Strand and Holborn (`fab-2a` W119). The hall is cold, and it is Lent, so there is no play this afternoon. Coin goes on a cloth on James Burbage's long table, with the company's book beside it. Photo 17 (§ 7.7) shows the gold and silver with seven men's hands at the edges. In the ledger that night: "of the Queen, my part, xxxvij s. vj d. had."

*Merged from.* `fab` § 8.1 is the base. Folded in: the canon's opening line from `gpt` ("the warrant has no weight until someone brings the coin"); Heminges's count of the coin and Burbage's Alleyn line from `gem`; Pope's "even when spoken slowly", Edith's eight shillings and the Kemp–Heminges exchange about false report from `gpt`. *Fixes:* the dog no longer appears on a stage, only in the Christmas boat (canon § 5.7). Kemp's added lines now match Garnish's tally: 35 and a song, nine of them written into the book on 12 March (`fab-2a`). `gpt`'s version, in which the coin is not yet collected and seven £2 advances are paid, and `gem`'s version (£14 among seven, plus a 10s earnest to William) both yield to the canon split (§ 5.2). `gem`'s lines that know about Bottom or a clownless *Richard II* in March are cut. Its mercer, "Master Campion", is left unnamed, since the name collides with the poet Thomas Campion.

```json
{
  "id": "S027",
  "kind": "audio",
  "title": "Sharers' reckoning: the Queen's twenty pounds",
  "lines": [
    {"speaker": "john_heminges", "text": "Set it down there. No, on the cloth, Will, on the cloth, I'll not chase an angel under the settle. A warrant has no weight until someone brings the coin. You've brought it. So. Twenty pound. Count it with me, somebody honest."},
    {"speaker": "will_kemp", "text": "Count it with me. I'm first on the Queen's paper. William Kemp, William Shakespeare and Richard Burbage. I heard the clerk read it. He read me loudest."},
    {"speaker": "richard_burbage", "text": "He read you first because he began at the wrong end and was too proud to turn about. And we stood an hour in his passage while he trimmed his quills."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "He read us as servants to the Lord Chamberlain, all three, and looked at us as a man looks at three fish. I never felt so paid and so little."},
    {"speaker": "james_burbage", "text": "What's the date on it?"},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "Whitehall, the fifteenth of March, fifteen ninety-four."},
    {"speaker": "will_kemp", "text": "Ninety-four! There. We played at Christmas, they pay us in Lent, and they date it a year gone. That's the Queen's arithmetic. But I'm first on it."},
    {"speaker": "john_heminges", "text": "It's ninety-four till Lady Day, as you well know, and it's twenty pound in any year. Sixteen angels at ten shillings, that's eight pound. Four sovereigns, twelve. And eight pound in white money, shillings and sixpences. Twenty, sound and unclipped."},
    {"speaker": "thomas_pope", "text": "That makes twenty, even when spoken slowly."},
    {"speaker": "richard_burbage", "text": "And Alleyn's crew had thirty for three nights at Greenwich. Next Christmas we play three, or I eat my buskins."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "Let them have their thirty, Dick. They'll spend it on elm boards. Henslowe has carpenters coming in on Monday."},
    {"speaker": "john_heminges", "text": "Thirteen, six and eight for the two plays. Six, thirteen and four of Her Majesty's reward. Now. Item."},
    {"speaker": "thomas_pope", "text": "Here it comes. Hide your purses, lads, John has said Item."},
    {"speaker": "john_heminges", "text": "Item, boat-hire to Greenwich and home, two days, and the carriage of the baskets. Sixteen shillings."},
    {"speaker": "richard_burbage", "text": "Sixteen! Did we go by way of Calais?"},
    {"speaker": "john_heminges", "text": "We went against the tide, at Christmas, with a dog. Item, rewards. Five hired men at four shillings. Three boys at two. The book-keeper and the tireman, four apiece. Thirty-four shillings."},
    {"speaker": "augustine_phillips", "text": "Give the boys three, John. Nick stood two hours in that farthingale and never once scratched."},
    {"speaker": "john_heminges", "text": "Two is written. I'll give them the third in pies, and it'll please them better. Item. The— the mercer in Cheapside. For the taffeta, and the copper lace, and the Princess's gown that is still not paid for. Fifty shillings."},
    {"speaker": "will_kemp", "text": "Fifty shillings on a gown for a play the Queen's never seen!"},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "The town has seen it three times, Will. You were in it. You spoke five-and-thirty lines more than I wrote, and a whole song, and I've put nine of them in the book."},
    {"speaker": "will_kemp", "text": "Only nine?"},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "The rest were about the gown."},
    {"speaker": "john_heminges", "text": "Sixteen, thirty-four and fifty. That is five pound, to the penny. Which leaves fifteen. Fifteen pound in eight parts is thirty-seven shillings and sixpence the part."},
    {"speaker": "will_kemp", "text": "Eight parts. I count seven men at this board. Who's the eighth, the dog?"},
    {"speaker": "john_heminges", "text": "The stock. As always. The apparel, the books, the licence money. The stock takes a share and I keep it, and when your motley splits, Will Kemp, the stock will buy you another."},
    {"speaker": "will_kemp", "text": "Then let the stock pay Mistress Kettlewood first. She's owed for a prince's sleeves since the autumn, and she looks at me in the tiring-house as if I wore them."},
    {"speaker": "john_heminges", "text": "Thursday. Out of the stock. Eight shillings."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "Eight shillings. She required me to remember it."},
    {"speaker": "augustine_phillips", "text": "Sensible woman."},
    {"speaker": "will_kemp", "text": "Then I say this. The Queen's reward, the six, thirteen and four, that's a reward for pleasing. Who pleased? When her ladies were nodding on Innocents' night and the candles guttered, who gave them the jig and woke the whole Presence? I say the reward goes by desert."},
    {"speaker": "richard_burbage", "text": "By desert! God's my life. Then give it all to the dog."},
    {"speaker": "james_burbage", "text": "By desert I'd have the rent of my house out of it before any of you saw a groat. Who keeps a roof over this company? And two years left on the ground, and Giles Allen sniffing at my timber…"},
    {"speaker": "richard_burbage", "text": "Father. Not the lease. Not before dinner."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "Will, hear me. If we pay by desert we must first agree what each man deserves, and we shall be here till Easter and come out with nothing but opinions. Pay it by the share and we'll all go to dinner equally wronged."},
    {"speaker": "thomas_pope", "text": "Equally wronged! Write that over the door."},
    {"speaker": "will_kemp", "text": "You've a smooth tongue, Master Shakespeare. I've noted it. It goes round a man like a cat."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "It's my whole living. I have no dog."},
    {"speaker": "augustine_phillips", "text": "Take your thirty-seven and six, Will, and I'll set you a new jig for Easter, and you shall have the whole stage after the play and nobody's lines to trouble you."},
    {"speaker": "will_kemp", "text": "…With the slippers?"},
    {"speaker": "augustine_phillips", "text": "With the slippers."},
    {"speaker": "will_kemp", "text": "Hm. Well. Count it out, then. But I was first on the paper."},
    {"speaker": "john_heminges", "text": "You were first on the paper. I'll write that in the book, if it will content you. Kemp. First on the paper. Thirty-seven and six. Richard. Thirty-seven and six. Will."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "Thank you, John. …It's lighter than I thought the Queen would be."},
    {"speaker": "will_kemp", "text": "John, you will never be troubled by a false report from your own mouth."},
    {"speaker": "john_heminges", "text": "I am troubled sufficiently by the rest of yours."},
    {"speaker": "richard_burbage", "text": "Come to the Bull and drink the Queen's health, all of you. Lent or no Lent."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "One cup. Mistress Ansell can smell a tavern through two oak doors, and she has opinions on sack in Lent."},
    {"speaker": "richard_burbage", "text": "And what will you do with the rest of yours?"},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "Put it on a horse."},
    {"speaker": "thomas_pope", "text": "A wager? You?"},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "A hired one. As far as Warwickshire. I'm going home for Lent, and I mean to arrive like a man who has been paid."},
    {"speaker": "james_burbage", "text": "And come back, mind. Easter Monday. There's a house to open."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "I'll come back. I've a king wants writing, and nowhere in Stratford to put him."}
```

*Notes for builders.* 53 lines, which is inside the set-piece allowance. The concrete numbers are £20, 16s, 34s, 50s and 37s 6d, and they reconcile: 16s + 34s + 50s = £5, and £15 ÷ 8 = 37s 6d. Rewards: 5 × 4s + 3 × 2s + 2 × 4s = 34s. Coin values (an angel at 10s, a sovereign at 20s) are T2 (source book 05 § 1). Alleyn's £30 is T1 (06 S002). Kemp says "first on the paper" three times. Burbage's "next Christmas we play three" is ordinary hope, and it comes true at Richmond; it carries no other freight. Kemp and William use *you* (canon § 5.7).

### 8.2 Calls

#### 8.2.1 Call: S091

**`S091` · 2025-09-23 (Tue) · 10:40 · William at `ansell_house` (his chamber, a writing morning); the earl at `southampton_house` · "Call: my lord of Southampton" · about 3 min**

*Context.* This is the earl's only call of the year (canon § 3.4). It falls on the day Rowland Whyte writes that he "doth with too much familiarity court the fair Mistress Vernon" (T1; William does not know of the letter). The court has been at Nonsuch since late August (T1). The earl is happy and distracted, and he asks for "something for a lady", without saying which. William understands that the something is for another man's love. He answers with advice and a friar, and promises nothing; the earl, not listening, says "Make it good" and is gone. William writes nothing for it that night. The lady's name reaches him only by evening, as Bryan's gossip from a Carey groom (§ 6 S091). Mood in the week table: *stung; busy*.

*Merged from.* The beats are `fab`'s (§ 3, Southampton arc 2). The wit about the court's knowledge is `gpt`'s threshold scene, recast from a doorway into a call. The return from Nonsuch, Paul's Walk "whispering everything twice" and the line about the Queen forgiving a broken lance come from `gem`. *Fixes:* `gem` has the earl call him "Will" and "my poet" and use *thou*, asks for songs to be sent, and has William promise "everything my hand makes". All of that is cut, because it reads as intimacy and as verse delivered to a named real person (rule 6). Here William gives no verse and promises none. The final pass conformed the call to § 6 S091: a morning call, the lady unnamed in it, no refusal spoken, and the earl's "Make it good" to close. Florio, present in `gpt`'s scene, becomes an offstage aside, because a call has two voices. The £5,000 fine for refusing the Vere match is left out because the source book has not checked it (02, open questions).

```json
{
  "id": "S091",
  "kind": "audio",
  "title": "Call: my lord of Southampton",
  "lines": [
    {"speaker": "henry_wriothesley_southampton", "text": "Master Shakespeare? Southampton. From Holborn. I have found the one hour today in which nobody has found me, and I spend it on you."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "My lord. Then I'm much honoured, and I'll not make it longer."},
    {"speaker": "henry_wriothesley_southampton", "text": "That is what they all promise before they begin. Have I drawn you from your dinner?"},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "From a quill, my lord, and two lovers in Verona who will not stay where I put them."},
    {"speaker": "henry_wriothesley_southampton", "text": "Then they are wiser than I am. I'm an hour back from Nonsuch, splashed to the thigh. Tell me, have you heard that the whole court knows my mind better than I do?"},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "I have heard that courts are well supplied with knowledge, my lord."},
    {"speaker": "henry_wriothesley_southampton", "text": "Especially of other people."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "It costs less to come by."},
    {"speaker": "henry_wriothesley_southampton", "text": "Ha. There. That was worth the mud. Now hear the matter. There is a lady. No, I'll not tell you which; Paul's will tell you soon enough, for Paul's hears everything."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "Paul's Walk hears everything twice, my lord, and half of it backward. Is she fair?"},
    {"speaker": "henry_wriothesley_southampton", "text": "She is— yes. And she mocks me. I want something for her. A song. A few lines a man could leave in a glove. You do it better than all the Italians Florio sets me to construe."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "My lord, a thing made for one lady by another man's hand is known for borrowed the moment she reads it. She will want your hand, not mine."},
    {"speaker": "henry_wriothesley_southampton", "text": "My hand writes like a horse falling downstairs."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "Then let her see it fall, my lord. A lady forgives a blot that is a man's own sooner than a fair line that is not."},
    {"speaker": "henry_wriothesley_southampton", "text": "Then she will laugh at the blot. She laughs at everything I make."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "My lord, I have a friar in Verona who bids his lovers love moderately. He is not much heeded."},
    {"speaker": "henry_wriothesley_southampton", "text": "Then he is a friar. You're a sly one under all that 'my lord'. Her Majesty does not love to have her ladies courted, you know."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "So I have heard, my lord. Her Grace forgives a broken lance at the tilt far sooner than a whisper in the gallery."},
    {"speaker": "henry_wriothesley_southampton", "text": "Then I'll break lances in November and whisper in September. Come and see me unhorsed on the Queen's day."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "I'll stand among the citizens and cry for your lordship."},
    {"speaker": "henry_wriothesley_southampton", "text": "Do. Florio has been at my door this quarter-hour with a Petrarch open, looking at me as if I were a verb he cannot conjugate. Make it good. God keep you, Master Shakespeare."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "And your good lordship. I commend me to you."}
  ]
}
```

*Notes for builders.* The canon shape is all here: he names himself and his place, raises one matter (the lady), gives one aside (the mud, then Florio) and ends on a commendation. William says "my lord" throughout and never *thou*. Nothing in the call says what William feels. The sting is for the memo afterwards (§ 6 S091), and stays unexplained. That the Queen disliked her ladies being courted is `[ext]` (§ 9.4 A). The earl's "unhorsed" line is `fab`'s sample line, and it sets up the S106 sighting.

#### 8.2.2 Call: S104

**`S104` · 2025-11-07 (Fri) · 16:50 · William at `the_theatre` (the tiring-house); Lord Hunsdon at `somerset_house` · "Call: my Lord Chamberlain" · about 3 min**

*Merged from.* `fab` § 8.2, kept whole: it is the strongest call in the three bibles. *Fixes:* none were needed. `gem`'s version of S104 had Hunsdon hint at the Carey–Berkeley wedding as an occasion for the *Dream* ("keep some moonshine in thy inkhorn"), which turns a T3 link into a commission (canon § 7 #8), so none of it is used. Hunsdon's choice of plays matches the canon's court titles (*LLL*, *Romeo*, *Errors*).

```json
{
  "id": "S104",
  "kind": "audio",
  "title": "Call: my Lord Chamberlain",
  "lines": [
    {"speaker": "henry_carey_hunsdon", "text": "Is that Shakespeare? Hunsdon. From Somerset House. Speak up, man, I've a fire roaring here like a Scot."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "My lord. Your lordship's servant. We are this moment come off the stage."},
    {"speaker": "henry_carey_hunsdon", "text": "What was it?"},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "Richard the Third, my lord."},
    {"speaker": "henry_carey_hunsdon", "text": "The crookback. Good. He's dead and nobody minds him. Now hear me, for I've no breath to say it twice. Christmas. The court keeps it at Richmond. You are sent for. Three nights. St Stephen's, St John's, Innocents'."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "Three, my lord. We are much bound to your lordship."},
    {"speaker": "henry_carey_hunsdon", "text": "You're bound to be good, that's what you're bound to. She was merry at Greenwich last year. I'd have her merry again. What have you that's new?"},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "The comedy of the King of Navarre, my lord, that Her Majesty has not seen. And a tragedy of two lovers, that will be ready before Advent."},
    {"speaker": "henry_carey_hunsdon", "text": "Lovers. Do they die?"},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "They do, my lord."},
    {"speaker": "henry_carey_hunsdon", "text": "Hm. Well, she likes a death if it's young and it's Italian. Is there bawdy in it?"},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "There is a Nurse, my lord."},
    {"speaker": "henry_carey_hunsdon", "text": "Ha! Then there's bawdy. Good. Bring the lovers. Bring the Navarre thing. And the third night something old, something they can laugh at with a cup in their hand."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "And the other king, my lord? Richard the Second draws the town."},
    {"speaker": "henry_carey_hunsdon", "text": "God's wounds, no. Are you mad? There's a book come out of Antwerp about who shall wear the crown after, with my lord of Essex's name on the front of it, and the Queen has had him by the ears for it this very week. I'll not carry a play to court where a king is talked out of his chair. Not this winter. Not in that house."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "I understand your lordship."},
    {"speaker": "henry_carey_hunsdon", "text": "See you do. Play it in Shoreditch till the boards wear through, and the Master has cut it. I've no quarrel with it. I saw it, did they tell you? I stood at the back like a carter. Your Burbage made me weep, the villain. But there's a time for a thing."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "My lord, one matter more. Sir Thomas Heneage being dead, God rest him, my fellow Heminges would know, who signs for the players now?"},
    {"speaker": "henry_carey_hunsdon", "text": "Ha. Your grocer would know who pays. A pox on it, so would I. The office is not filled. You'll be paid. You'll be paid slow. You were paid slow when he was alive. Tell your grocer the Queen's debt is better than another man's coin."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "I'll tell him so, my lord. He'll write it in his book and look at it."},
    {"speaker": "henry_carey_hunsdon", "text": "Ha! Let him. Kettleby will send you the days and the hours. And Shakespeare. Clean hose. All of you. Last year one of your fellows had a hole in his heel a man could have put a fist through, and I saw her see it."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "It shall be mended, my lord."},
    {"speaker": "henry_carey_hunsdon", "text": "Mend it. God keep you."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "And your good lordship."}
  ]
}
```

*Notes for builders.* "A book come out of Antwerp" is Doleman's *Conference* (T1 event, 3 November; the Antwerp imprint is `[ext]`). William heard of the confrontation on 5 November (canon news lag). Hunsdon having seen *Richard II* at the Theatre is T5 (`fab` open choice 19). "Paid slow" sets up the T1 fact that the Richmond plays are not paid until a warrant of 21 December 1596. The hole in the hose is running joke 14.

### 8.3 Voice memos

#### 8.3.1 Memo: S019

**`S019` · 2025-02-21 (Fri) · 21:15 · `ansell_house` · "Voice memo: the poet at Tyburn" · about 110 s**

*Context.* *The Shrew* plays without him and he does not leave the house. He was not at Tyburn and was never going. By supper he has the story from three mouths: a tapster, a waterman and Mistress Ansell's account of a neighbour's account. He speaks low, in bed, by one candle. Body that day: 2,700 steps, resting heart rate 66, 4 h 50 of sleep (`fab-2a`).

*Merged from.* `fab` § 8.3 is the base. `gpt`'s S019 adds "I have not been there. I should say that first", the distrusted "fine thought" about the voice, and the empty pen. `gem`'s adds the dead-king turn, softened. *Fixes:* `gem` names Topcliffe and the rack, describes a heart quartered over a brazier and claims Arden kinship with Southwell ("my mother's cousins… knew his kin's name"). The first two are execution and torture imagery, and the third is a kinship claim, which is T3 and held unused. All three are cut. `gem`'s 13:30 timing, written as the hanging happens, is replaced by `fab`'s evening hour.

```json
{
  "id": "S019",
  "kind": "audio",
  "title": "Voice memo: the poet at Tyburn",
  "lines": [
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "They hanged the Jesuit this morning. Southwell. I did not go. …I was never going."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "I should say that first. There are men who talk as if hearing a thing gave them the eyes of everyone who saw it."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "Three men told me of it before supper and no two alike. One says he prayed for the Queen. One says the people would not let the hangman cut him down alive. They cried out, let him hang, let him be dead first. I'd like to believe that one. …I do believe that one."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "He was a poet. That's the thing I can't get round. They say it as though it were another offence, or an excuse. It is neither. Men have his verses by heart that would not own to it."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "And yesterday I stood on a scaffold in Gracechurch Street in a paper crown and swore to study three years and see no women. And they laughed. God knows they laughed. I was glad of it. I am glad of it."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "What is it for, then? The making. Put a thing in Latin and they call it treason. Put it in the mouth of a king three hundred years dead and they pay a penny at the door and let you go home to supper."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "…No. That's too neat. He was not hanged for a poem. He was hanged for a priest. I must not make a likeness where there's none. It is only that he was thirty-three, and I am thirty, and we both…"},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "I was thinking of the voice. How long a voice may stay in words when the mouth is stopped. That is a fine thought, and so I distrust it a little. It makes a thing seem mended when nothing is mended."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "My father says a prayer at night that I am not supposed to know. I heard it through a wall when I was nine. I could say it now."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "I shan't."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "…Remember: the messenger. In the last act. He comes in black among them all in their colours, and he has to say only a little. The less he says. Let him say almost nothing and let it go through them like cold."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "I have been holding this pen all the while. There's no ink in it."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "I had that right. I had that right before today. …Enough."}
  ]
}
```

*T-notes.* Southwell's age (33) and the dates are T1. The crowd's cry is T2 `[ext]` from Catholic accounts, given as hearsay. No kinship is claimed and nothing is seen. The father's prayer is T5 and deliberately left unspecified. The messenger is Marcade, whom Sincler plays (`fab-2a`).

#### 8.3.2 Memo: S069

**`S069` · 2025-06-29 (Sun) · 22:20 · `ansell_house` (his chamber, the bar across the door) · "Voice memo: Tower Hill, through the wall" · about 100 s**

*Context.* It is St Peter's Day, and there is no moon (new moon 27 June). From about nine, a sound to the south-east that is not bells: a great many voices on Tower Hill, under half a mile off. "A thousand apprentices, with some of the Tower's own warders among them" (T1). He is not there and sees none of it. Mistress Ansell bars the street door and allows no light in the front of the house. Verbeke goes up to the garret without being told, and Alse sleeps in her mistress's chamber. The body record shows no steps from 21:30 to 23:15 and a heart rate of 104 rising to 124 (canon § 5.8). Photo 28 is the inside of his door, the bar across it, one candle. He whispers.

*Merged from.* `gem`'s S069 memo is the base; `fab-2a`'s beats and memo lines ("It comes and goes like a sea… They'd pass this door") are folded in. *Fixes:* "Thirty paces from my bed" becomes the canon's hundred yards. "Wat's boy" becomes a boy in the close: there is no Wat, and Davy Gannet is not a St Helen's neighbour. `gem` has him strike no flint at all, which would contradict photo 28, so the candle is now on the floor and shaded. `gem`'s "when I open Brooke's book on Monday" would put *Romeo* drafting on 30 June, against the canon's 5 July start, so it becomes a note for later with no line of the play in it. Detail with no source is cut: discharged pikemen from Ostend, a drum, the Mayor's watch stoned, "Rich Spencer".

```json
{
  "id": "S069",
  "kind": "audio",
  "title": "Voice memo: Tower Hill, through the wall",
  "lines": [
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "The bar's down across the street door. She'll have no light in the front of the house, lest a lit pane draw a stone. I have the one candle on the boards behind the bed, and my hand round it."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "A thousand of them on Tower Hill, the boy cried, running through the close. And some of the Tower's own warders with them. A thousand. I can't see one of them. I can only hear them."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "It comes and goes like a sea. …There. And now nothing. And now there."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "Friday they set a gallows at the Mayor's own gate. A hundred yards from this bed. All for fivepenny butter and a loaf a man could hide in his fist."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "Verbeke went up into the garret without a word said to him. He knows what a crowd says to a stranger. He knew it before I did."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "…If they come up Bishopsgate they come for him, not us. They'd pass this door. They'd pass it."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "My heart goes like a fuller's mill. Ridiculous. I am in my own chamber with the bar down."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "Remember this dark. When I come to the two houses, begin there. Not in a palace. In the street, among the serving-men, with the heat on them and one word too many."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "…Enough. Let it go by."}
  ]
}
```

*T-notes.* The riot, its size, the warders and the 27 June gallows are T1 (source book 01, 27 and 29 June). The butter price (3d against 5d) is T1. "Fivepenny butter" is his phrase. The "two houses" note is an image, not a line, so it obeys the canon's *Romeo* rule.

#### 8.3.3 Memo: W062

**`W062` · 2025-07-29 (Tue) · 15:20 · `banbury_road`, between Aylesbury and Waddesdon (est. 51.835, −0.880) · "Voice memo: on the road, second day" · about 80 s**

*Walking.* Footfalls on a dry road throughout, larks, breath. Heart rate about 114.

*Merged from.* `fab` § 8.4, kept whole. *Fixes:* none needed. It reaches toward the orchard scene but speaks no finished line of it, and the scene is written on 9 August (W067).

```json
{
  "id": "W062",
  "kind": "audio",
  "title": "Voice memo: on the road, second day",
  "lines": [
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "Two-and-twenty miles since four this morning, and the left heel has an opinion."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "Barley. All this side of Aylesbury. It should be to a man's belt by now and it's to his knee. Nobody says anything. They look up and they look down and they go on reaping what there is."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "She'll ask why I walked. I'll say the horse was dear. It was dear. …It's not why."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "I wanted to be tired when I got there. I wanted to have paid something."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "Now. The boy. Under the wall. He's come over the wall, he's in the dark, and she's above, and she doesn't know he's there. So she can say it. She can say the thing nobody says when they know they're heard."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "That's the whole of it. She thinks she's alone."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "And he mustn't answer too soon. If he answers too soon it's a wooing. If he waits it's… he's overhearing a soul. Let him wait. Let him stand in the dark and be afraid to breathe."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "What's in a name. What's… a name. Something with a flower. Any flower. …No, not any."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "I shall lose this before Grendon. Say it again tonight. Wall. Dark. She thinks she's alone. He waits."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "…There's a church tower. Is that Waddesdon? God send it's Waddesdon and they have ale."}
  ]
}
```

*Notes.* The thin barley is the dearth, with no cause given (`fab` weather rule). The Grendon constable that evening is T4 (Aubrey).

#### 8.3.4 Memo: W073a (night 1 of the return)

**`W073a` · 2025-08-18 (Mon) · 18:00 · `oxford_road`, the inn yard at Chipping Norton, among the horses · "Voice memo: Chipping Norton, the back" · about 90 s**

*Context.* This is the first night of the return with Hobbins's packhorses. He rode the spare pack-nag for the first stage out of Stratford (bridge to Shipston, ten miles) because of the back he strained raising the Sadler cousin's barn frame on 12 August. The rest of the way he walked, about 30,000 steps (canon § 5.4, § 5.5). The week-table thread is "notes for Act 2".

*Merged from.* `gpt` § 8.4 (W056). *Fixes:* `gpt` sets it at Chipping Norton on its own summer walk out, with a back strained lifting the pack. The canon drops that strain because Chipping Norton is not on the outward route. So the memo is re-homed to the first night of the canon return, and the back is the barn frame's. The wit (the back as a creditor, the paper not always among the needful things, the strap) is kept. The *Romeo* note is an image only, which is allowed after 5 July. The final pass moved the memo from an inn chamber at 21:40 to the yard at six, where § 6 W073a has it, folded in § 6's yard lines ("too old to walk to Warwickshire", the house), and made Hobbins's nag the day's (to Shipston), since 19 Aug is on foot.

```json
{
  "id": "W073a",
  "kind": "audio",
  "title": "Voice memo: Chipping Norton, the back",
  "lines": [
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "At Chipping Norton. The inn's dog has taken my stool, and I have let him, which tells you how the back does."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "I walked a hundred miles to Stratford with nothing worse than a raw heel, and raised one barn frame for a man's cousin, and was undone by it. This deserves to be remembered, though not repeated to Kemp."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "It is not much. It complains when I rise and is content when I sit, which gives it the advantage of most creditors."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "Hobbins put me up on the spare nag as far as Shipston, on condition I carried nothing. I said I'd carry nothing but a thought. He said thoughts weigh nothing on a horse. He has never carried mine."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "Tomorrow the paper goes nearer the top of the wallet. No. The things I need go nearer the top, and I'll own that paper is not always among them."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "There was a sentence on the road this morning. I thought I should keep it. …I remember instead that the left strap wants shortening."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "Very well. The strap is useful."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "I am too old to walk to Warwickshire. …Three years. What's a house worth? Forty pound? A hundred? I don't know. I know what a play is worth."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "Mark this, though. The friar. He should come on with a basket, at first light, gathering. And in the one basket the herb that heals and the herb that kills, and he knows which is which, and says so. A man who knows that much ought to be careful. He won't be."},
    {"speaker": "william_shakespeare", "text": "…Enough. Sleep on the left side."}
  ]
}
```

*Notes.* The inn is unnamed, as the canon names none at Chipping Norton. The friar's basket is a T5 image. Hobbins is `[invented]`.

### 8.4 Notes pages

#### 8.4.1 Notes page: S024 (the Lent fix)

**`S024` · 2025-03-06 (Thu) · 19:30 · `ansell_house` · commonplace book, new heading; one opening · "Verona: first notes"**

*Rule.* This is the canon's Lent rule: early notes only. Brooke's poem is open; the page holds extracts, names, a plan and one image, with no dialogue and no line of the play. Brooke's text is pre-1700 and public domain. **Re-key the two Brooke couplets from EEBO-TCP before use** `[ext]`. Store a diplomatic image and a normalised transcript separately (canon § 2, Notes).

*Merged from.* `fab` § 8.5, kept whole; it is the canonical form of S024. *Fixes:* none needed. The blank after "alike in" is a gap, not a line, and is deliberate.

```
                    VERONA  — out of Brooke, bought this day in
                    Paules ch:yard, vi d., lacking the last leaf
                    but one. Gib hath sat upon it already.

  ¶ He begins:
      There is beyonde the Alps, a towne of auncient fame,
      Whose bright renoune yet shineth cleare, Verona men it name
  — a jog-trot. xiiij syllables & then xij. A man could plough to it.

  ¶ The matter:
      There were two auncient stockes, which Fortune high dyd place
      Above the rest, indewd with welth, and nobler of their race
  — two houses. Alike. *That* is the thing. Not one good one bad.
    Alike in — worth? state? name?
                                      alike in   [left blank]

  ¶ Names he gives:   Romeus  (Romeo is better in the mouth)
                      Iuliet           Capilet / Capelet
                      Montagew         Tybalt       Paris, a Countie
                      Mercutio — hath but one cold hand in Brooke
                          & no more.  A man with a cold hand. Hm.
                      a Nurce — he lets her talk.  GOOD.  let her talk more.
                      Fryer Lawrence

  ¶ His time:  moneths. They are wedded & meet in secret a whole
    winter in Brooke.   too long.   ?? how short can it be made
           a moneth       a weeke        — dayes —

  ¶ He preaches. To the Reader he sayth they are punished for
    lust & for hearkening to friars.  I do not think so.
    I think nobody is punished. I think it falls on them.

  ¶ Two houses — a plan:

        [ CAP. ]                         [ MOUNT. ]
           |          the streete           |
           |   servants — swords — heat     |
           +----------- PRINCE ------------+

  ¶ One thing seen (walking home by Leaden hall, a link-boy
    passing a doorway):
        a light in a vault.  Not day. A torch brought into
        a tomb & the tomb made a feasting chamber by it.
    keep.

  ¶ NOT YET.  The King first (ask Ric: Field for his Holinshed).
    This after.  Summer.  It wants heat.

  Item pd. for the booke  vi d.
  Item a quire of paper   v d.
  Item inke of Mrs Ansell ij d.
```

*Ledger note.* The prices are the canon's: Brooke 6d worn, a quire 5d, ink 2d the horn. The Holinshed loan begins on 19 March. Photo 9 shows the poem open under a candle with the cat's flank across the right-hand page.

#### 8.4.2 Notes page: S100 (the first page of the *Dream*)

**`S100` · 2025-10-28 (Tue) · 21:00 · `ansell_house` · drafts (foul papers), with a commonplace margin · "Titania's ruined seasons"**

*Rule.* The canon fixes the *Dream*'s first page on Tuesday 28 October: Titania's seasons, a speech written before the scene around it exists. The draft text is Q1 (1600) old spelling, **public-domain text in an invented setting**. The struck words are invented foul-paper revisions, not variants from any edition, and are labelled as such. **Re-key every line from Q1 1600 (EEBO-TCP) before use.** This is a draft surface, and no composition date is presented as a record.

*Merged from.* `gem` § 8.4. *Fixes:* `gem` dates it Saturday 25 October; the canon puts it on Tuesday 28 October. `gem`'s reckoning has "Taken at ye Theatre this weeke (Romeo, 4 dayes): my tenth part". *Romeo* is not played until 6 December, and a share is an eighth, not a tenth, so the line is rebuilt to the canon's October share band. `gem`'s cloves for the jaw are dropped: the tooth was drawn on 14 October and the jaw was tender only to the 21st. In `gem`'s margin note the dearth comes of fairies "wrangling over a little changeling boy". The note now says the dearth comes of a quarrel "and nothing we did", which echoes S024's "it falls on them". The changeling belongs to the play and turns up only when the scene is written. `gem`'s entry direction for the two fairies is cut: § 6 S100 has a speech with no head and no scene round it.

```
     28 Octob. 1595 — Tuesday, by candle.  St Helens.

  [top margin: a reckoning]
     It. a pound of tallow candles ................. iiij d.
     It. a quire of R. F.'s Rochell paper ........... iiij d. ob.
     Sat. last, of the company, my part ............ xxxviij s.
     my jawe: whole.  eat crust.

  [mem.]
     The nine mens morris cut in the turf behind the Shottery
     ditch, choked flat with black mud all that summer of '94,
     & the sheep rotting in the fold before shearing.  Give the
     dearth not to dukes.  Give it to the Queen of the fairies,
     chiding her jealous lord: as if our short loaves came of a
     quarrel between two of them, and nothing we did.

  ────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────
  [draft: the speech only; the scene round it not yet written.
   Q1 1600 text, to be re-keyed; ~~struck~~ words are invented]

  Quee.  These are the forgeries of iealousie:
     And neuer, since the middle Summers spring
     Met we on hill, in dale, forrest, or mead,
     By paued fountaine, or by rushie brooke,
     Or ~~on the~~ in the beached margent of the sea,
     To daunce our ringlets to the whistling winde,
     But with thy braules thou hast disturbd our sport.
     Therefore the windes, piping to vs in vaine,
     As in reuenge, haue suckt vp, from the sea,
     Contagious fogges: which falling in the land,
     Hath euery pelting riuer made so proude,
     That they haue ouerborne their Continents.
     The Oxe hath therefore stretcht his yoake in vaine,
     The Ploughman lost his sweat, and the greene corne
     Hath rotted, ere his youth attaind a beard:
     The fold stands empty in the drowned field,
     And crowes are fatted with the murrion flocke,
     The nine mens Morris is fild vp with mudde:
     And the queint Mazes in the wanton greene,
     For lacke of tread, are vndistinguishable.
     The humane mortals want their winter ~~cheere~~ heere,
     No night is now with hymne or caroll blest;
     Therefore the Moone (the gouernesse of floods)
     Pale in her anger, washes all the aire,
     That rheumaticke diseases doe abound.      [mine owne Ianuary rheume!]
     And thorough this distemperature, we see
     The seasons alter: hoary headed frosts
     Fall in the fresh lap of the crimson rose,
                                   ‸ — and then?  the Summer's crown
                                         set on old Winter's head.  mock it.
```

*Ledger note.* The weekly share (38s) is inside the canon's October band of 30–45s. Candles are his own (canon § 5.1). A tallow price of 4d a pound is T5, inside the source book's "tallow is cheap" (05) and unchecked (§ 9.4 B). Paper at 4½d the quire is the canon's trade price for Field's Rochelle crown paper. The note under the last line paraphrases the next lines, so the builder quotes only what is re-keyed. That 1594 was the wet summer is T1/T2 (source book 01 § 4 #5): the memory is of 1594, never of 1595. The "January rheum" is his cold of 13–19 January.

#### 8.4.3 Notes page: S116 (the year's book closed)

**`S116` · 2025-12-31 (Wed) · 20:30 · `ansell_house` · account book, the last opening of the year · "The account of this year"**

*Rule.* This is new fictional writing, not a transcription of any Shakespeare document. Shown here is the normalised transcript. The diplomatic image is in secretary hand with roman numerals, so £33 10s is written "xxxiij li. x s.". The account book keeps *expected* and *received* in separate columns from March (canon § 2, Notes).

*Merged from.* `gpt` § 8.5. *Fixes:* every figure is rebuilt to the canon. `gpt`'s opening £6 4s and closing £28 yield to the canon coffer (£24 10s on 1 January, £33 10s on 31 December). Its £24 sent to Anne becomes the canon's 60s a quarter (£12, of which 42s of the Lent quarter went to John's shops, so £9 18s of it reached Anne), plus the 30s over the quarter's that he put in Anne's lap on 8 August (W331), plus £5 of John's debts in Lent, the 20s barn gift and the £2 brief. Its lodging at 26s a quarter becomes the canon's £4 a year. Its "returned of the old advance" is dropped. The Quiney loan of £2, with £1 repaid, is canon. The court money stays unentered, as in `gpt`. The note to Anne about Susanna's book is dropped, because the Christmas pack went on 18 December and the note would come late. `gpt`'s line about the workmen is kept: it is the *Dream* thread of 22 November.

> **The account of this year, 1595**
>
> *In the coffer at the year's beginning:* £24 10s
>
> **Received**
>
> | | |
> |---|---:|
> | Of the company, my share of the weeks played | £51 14s |
> | Of my Lord Chamberlain at Richmond, for hose, my part | 10s |
> | Of the Queen's warrant of March, my part | £1 17s 6d |
> | Of Sir Edward Hoby's reward, my part | 12s 6d |
> | Of Richard Quiney, in part of his £2 | £1 |
> | **Together** | **£55 14s** |
>
> **Laid out**
>
> | | |
> |---|---:|
> | To Mistress Ansell, four quarters | £4 |
> | Firing and washing, the winter weeks | 12s 6d |
> | Board and victuals in London | £12 14s 4d |
> | Sent home: the Lent quarter by my own hand, 18s of it to Anne; three quarters by the carrier at 60s; to Anne in August, over the quarter's, 30s | £11 8s |
> | My father's small debts, paid in Lent (42s of it out of the Lent quarter's 60s) | £5 |
> | Toward the barn at Stratford | £1 |
> | To the brief for the fire | £2 |
> | Lent to Richard Quiney | £2 |
> | The journeys: the horse in Lent, the road in summer | £2 18s |
> | Books: Brooke, Daniel, the Plutarch, Southwell | 17s |
> | Paper, ink and lights | £1 4s |
> | Apparel, shoes and mending | £1 10s |
> | Gifts to the children, and the Christmas pack | £1 4s |
> | The tooth, and the cloves before it | 1s 2d |
> | Church and the dole | 5s |
> | **Together** | **£46 14s** |
>
> *In the coffer at the year's end:* **£33 10s**, counted twice.
>
> **Expected, not received:** my part of the three nights at Richmond. Not entered. The Queen's debt is better than another man's coin, says my lord; John has written it in his book and looks at it.
>
> Richard Quiney yet owes £1.
>
> Field's Holinshed: still here.
>
> The play of the wood: go on with the workmen. Do not make them fools before they have had leave to be men.

**Arithmetic check.** £24 10s + £55 14s − £46 14s = £33 10s. Laid out: 80s + 12s 6d + 254s 4d + 228s + 100s + 20s + 40s + 40s + 58s + 17s + 24s + 30s + 24s + 1s 2d + 5s = 934s = £46 14s. Received: £51 14s + 10s + 37s 6d + 12s 6d + 20s = £55 14s. *Lent:* the coffer's fall from £27 2s (25 Mar) to £19 6s (10 Apr) is £7 16s = horse, fodder and inns 38s + the Lent quarter's 60s carried home + 58s of John's £5 (the other 42s came out of the 60s, so Anne had 18s of it; W123, W029). John's £5 is the other baker 41s, the shoemaker 44s 6d and the whittawer's glover's reckoning 14s 6d.

*Ledger note.* The canon fixes only the coffer counts. The annual totals here (£55 14s in, £46 14s out, £51 14s in shares, with Hunsdon's 10s at Richmond on its own line) are new T5 values. They sit inside the canon's weekly bands and the source book's £50–£150 a year for a sharer, but builders should treat them as this page's figures and are free to vary sub-lines between the dated coffer counts. Hoby's 12s 6d is the canon's share of the £5 reward.

### 8.5 Screen descriptions

#### 8.5.1 Screen: S046

**`S046` · 2025-05-15 (Thu) · 10:20 · `fields_printing_house` · surface `proof` · "A sheet of the Plutarch on Field's line" · three segments, about 120 s in all**

*Merged from.* `fab` § 8.6 is the base. `gpt` § 8.6 supplies the third segment (Jacqueline's paper ends the view) and its legibility rule. *Fixes:* `gpt` lays the proof on a worktable while `fab` hangs it on a line. The merged screen does both, one after the other. `proof` appears only at Field's (canon § 2).

> **Segment 1: the hanging sheet (about 50 s).** A single large printed sheet, damp, hung by its top edge over a line. He reads it where it hangs, so it sways very slightly. Roman type, two pages of a folio forme side by side, with wide margins. At the head is a running title in italic capitals: **THE LIFE OF** on the left page, **IVLIVS CAESAR.** on the right. A decorated initial opens a paragraph, and small italic side-notes run down the outer margins. The ink is very black, slightly raised and unevenly impressed. At the foot of the left page one letter is printed upside down, a turned *n* reading as *u*. Around it, in brown ink, is the corrector's ring, with a stroke out to the margin and the right letter written small. Lower on the right page is a second mark: a caret, and "the" supplied.
>
> **What is legible.** On the right-hand page, the soothsayer's warning to Caesar of the Ides of March, and Caesar's answer on the day. A builder may make four to six lines legible only if they are quoted exactly from North's 1579/1595 text (EEBO). Otherwise the type stays texture. The capture never invents a fully legible page of edition-specific wording.
>
> **Segment 2: the correction (about 40 s).** Nothing is typed. His reading moves: the view drifts down the right-hand page and rests a long time on one side-note. A hand comes into frame, and it is not his: an inky thumb, Field's, taps the corrector's ring as if to say *that one's caught*. Field takes down the next sheet of the same forme and lays it on the worktable, where the turned letter is now right. William's own hands stay out of the wet ink. Behind the table the work goes on: a sheet lifted, a tool set down, a pressman passing.
>
> **Segment 3: the view ends (about 30 s).** Jacqueline Field's stack of quires comes into the foreground and ends the view. The screen changes because his task changes: he and Field now carry paper. There is no cursor, no zoom and no border; the sheet is simply covered.
>
> **Sound under it (if paired with the meeting).** The thump and creak of the press bar. Field: "Fourteen shillings to you, unbound, and my Holinshed home by Whitsun." Then, wiping his hands: "It's market day at home. I never pass a Thursday but I know it."

*Ledger note.* Field printed the 1595 North's Plutarch (T1); the month is unchecked. The 14s price is the canon's. That William reads the Caesar life this day is T5, and no play is drafted from it this year. The Holinshed is never returned (running joke 9). Photo 19 shows the sheets hung like washing, with a pressman's arm on the bar.

#### 8.5.2 Screen: M020 (the feast sonnet)

**`M020` · 2025-07-18 (Fri) · 05:30–10:00 · `ansell_house` · surfaces `draft` + `book` · "*Romeo* I.v: the pilgrim sonnet" · about 300 s, the morning after Heminges's call on the summer shares (W058)**

*Merged from.* `gem` § 8.5 (W020). *Fixes:* `gem` dates it Saturday 12 July. The canon puts Queen Mab at the Jackdaw on Tuesday 15 July, and the feast comes after Mab, so the screen is re-tied to the canon's M020, Friday 18 July, three days later (the final pass moved it off W058, which is Heminges's call at the Theatre the day before). `gem`'s "Brooke" lines ("With torch in hand a comely knight did fetch standard in place…") are not Brooke's text, so they are replaced by an instruction to quote Brooke exactly. Its labels `desk_draft` and `book_quarto` map to `draft` and `book` (crosswalk). The play's lines are given here as a **normalised transcript**. The diplomatic image re-keys them in Q2 (1599) spelling, and nothing is quoted in old spelling until it has been checked.

> **Left of the trestle board (`book`).** Brooke's small black-letter octavo of *Romeus and Juliet* (1562) lies held flat at the feast by the round pounce-box. A builder makes two to four lines of Brooke's feast passage legible, quoted exactly from EEBO-TCP, or leaves them as texture.
>
> **Centre-right (`draft`).** *Romeo and Juliet*, Act 1, scene 5 (the modern numbering is in this label only). An open bifolium of Field's Rochelle crown paper, with a small bunch-of-grapes watermark showing faintly against the window. It is two-thirds covered in a quick, slanting English secretary hand in brown-black oak-gall ink, with italic only for the speech-prefixes (*Rom.*, *Iul.*, *Nur.*). **Already done:** Capulet's welcome, the old cousins' talk of masking, Romeo's sight of her ("O she doth teach the torches to burn bright", normalised), and Tybalt's anger checked by his uncle.
>
> **What changes while we watch.**
> 1. *00:00.* Romeo's quatrain is already wet on the sheet: "If I profane with my unworthiest hand / This holy shrine, the gentle sin is this: / My lips, two blushing pilgrims, ready stand / To smooth that rough touch with a tender kiss." In the right margin he has set small rhyme-letters, *a b a b*.
> 2. *00:45–02:10.* The quill dips at the inkhorn and Juliet's answering quatrain goes down (*c d c d*): "Good pilgrim, you do wrong your hand too much, / Which mannerly devotion shows in this; / For saints have hands that pilgrims' hands do touch, / And palm to palm is holy palmers' kiss." He stops for about ten seconds. His right thumb taps four beats on the board's edge (his tell). Then the lines go down shared: one to him, one to her, then the couplet in his mouth.
> 3. *03:30.* He draws a light bracket down the left margin beside the fourteen lines and writes a small "14" against it. He adds the italic direction *Kisse.*, and goes straight on into a second quatrain that will not finish: it breaks off after two lines with *Nur.* and "Madam, your mother craves a word with you."
> 4. *04:45.* Pounce is shaken over the wet ink, and the sheet is tipped to let it run off. The view lifts to the window: morning light on the close, and a bluebottle at the casement.

*Ledger note.* The canon's *Romeo* drafting begins on 5 July, so a line of the play on this screen is allowed. The watermark is T5 (`gem`), and the use of italic for speech-prefixes is T2 `[ext]`. The quotations are to be re-keyed from Q2 (§ 9.4 C). Kemp's Peter is not yet written (casting T5).

---

### 8.6 Photo prompts

*All prompts.* What his eye held, at about 5 ft 7 in standing eye level. Never of himself: no face, mirror, reflection or shadow. Nobody poses or looks toward the viewer. Light is only sun, fog, candle, cresset or fire. The look is a good phone camera of 2025 in true available light, with shadows left dark: the editor's choice of Framing C (2026-11-17), which replaces the earlier "no modern lens character" line. Never aged, sepia, grained or painted, and no added captions. Text in frame follows the canon or is illegible. Never an execution, a corpse, blood, an event in progress that he did not see, or an impossible vantage (canon § 2, Photo).

**Photo 4 · `S006` · 2025-01-15 (Wed) · 22:10 · `ansell_house` door, looking west-south-west down Great St Helen's**

> A narrow London close in January 1595, at night, seen from inside the dark of a house doorway under a jettied upper storey, at a standing man's eye level, so that no shadow of the viewer falls into the frame. Hard frost: the cobbles and the central kennel glitter, each stone rimmed white. The full moon is out of frame, high behind and to the left, and lights the house-fronts blue-silver. On the left is the squat stone porch and twin gables of a medieval parish church, its churchyard elms bare. Beyond the close, above lower roofs, rises the long dark ridge and tall stone oriel of a great merchant's mansion, with a single warm light in one window. Timber-framed houses overhang on the right, shutters closed, a lantern guttering on a bracket. No people. A cat's prints cross the frost. In the foreground, a cart-rut puddle frozen white and opaque, holding a glint of moonlight but no reflection. Moonlight and one small flame only. No snow falling, no modern paving, no electric light, no cathedral dome, no famous portrait face. Still, cold, exact, ordinary: uncomfortable cold, not gothic spectacle.

*Merged from* `fab` § 8.7 photo 1 and `gpt` S006 (the frozen puddle and the "not gothic" instruction). *Fixes:* the moon is out of frame, because at 22:10 the full moon stands in the south-east, behind a viewer looking west. The doorway vantage keeps his moon-shadow out of the frame. The puddle is frozen opaque so that it cannot reflect him. `fab`'s breath-mist "as from the person looking" is dropped as a trace of himself.

**Photo 6 · `S011` · 2025-02-01 (Sat) · 11:20 · `tower_hill`, looking south-west over the Tower wharf to the river**

> The River Thames below the Tower of London on 1 February 1595, late morning, seen from the crowded slope of Tower Hill at a standing man's eye level, between the heads and shoulders of citizens seen from behind. Fifteen decorated barges row up on the flood tide toward the Tower stairs, close together. They carry streamers and painted banners, trumpeters in the bows, and young gentlemen of the Inns of Court in black gowns and white ruffs. White gun-smoke hangs low over the grey water from the Tower's ordnance answering them. Wherries bob in their wake. On the far bank, the low roofs of Southwark under pale, cold February cloud-light. At the left edge, a big man in a russet cloak, back to the viewer, leaning to watch how a gentleman in borrowed majesty steps out of a boat. Natural winter daylight, sixteenth-century materials only. No modern ships, no later bridges, no lettering.

*Merged from* `gem` § 8.6 prompt 1 and `fab-2a` S011. Burbage is the man in the russet cloak, seen from behind. *Fixes:* the vantage moves from `gem`'s wharf parapet to the hill (`fab`), where the canon's single `tower_hill` point is. "35mm" and "brass salute culverin firing on the wharf" are removed: the source book has "music, trumpets and guns", T1 (06 S011).

**Photo 14 · `S032` · 2025-04-01 (Tue) · 10:30 · `stratford_fire_ground`, Chapel Street looking north**

> A street in an English market town in early April 1595, eleven months after a great fire, seen at the eye level of a man walking. On the right of the street is a gap of three house-plots: blackened stone footings, a brick chimney-stack standing alone and smoke-stained, charred beam-ends, and new nettles coming through the ash. In the middle plot a new house-frame has been raised, fresh green oak almost yellow, pegged and braced, roofless against a soft grey sky. A carpenter sits astride the wall-plate, driving a peg, and calls down to someone. On the left are unburned timber-and-plaster houses with thatch, one with scorched upper plaster. A few paces ahead, three children are half-turned toward the new frame, caught mid-movement, their faces toward the frame and away from the viewer. A taller girl of about eleven in a russet gown and white coif points at the burned plots as if naming them. A boy of ten has a thin wooden toy sword over his shoulder and holds something small up to the light: a bent nail. A girl of ten has her arm flung out at the carpenter, mouth open in a shout. At the right edge, a woman in a grey gown carrying a new leather fire-bucket is coming from a baker's doorway. At the bottom edge of the frame, a man's own hand holds a small rush basket. A dog. Wet ruts in the road, a pale sun trying. Ordinary, bright, slightly cold, busy. No church in frame, no ominous light, no funeral symbolism, no lingering focus on any one child. No lettering.

*Merged from* `fab` § 8.7 photo 3, `gpt` S032 (Anne, the basket in his hand, the warnings) and `fab-2a`'s beats (Susanna names the houses, Judith shouts, Hamnet's nail, the carpenter's call). The sword is Burbage's lath sword, given on 28 March. *Fixes:* `fab`'s children "seen from behind, walking away from the viewer" become children half-turned and busy, to avoid any parting framing (grief care). `gpt`'s "three healthy children" loses its adjective: the children are simply there.

**Photo 24, two frames · `S059b` · 2025-06-14 (Sat) · 08:30 · `leadenhall`, then `ansell_house`: the morning after the butter riot**

> *Frame 1.* Leadenhall market in London on a June morning in 1595, seen at a standing man's eye level from the edge of the market floor under the great stone arcade. Wooden butter firkins are set out on a trestle stall as on any Saturday, and a market-woman in a white apron stands behind them. Leaning by the stall is a constable with a brown bill (a long-shafted polearm), bored, looking elsewhere. A scatter of straw. Citizens pass with baskets. Nothing is broken and nobody argues. Soft early sun through the arches. No crowd, no violence, no lettering.
>
> *Frame 2.* Back at the lodging: a plain wooden bread-board on a kitchen table, with a penny loaf lying on it. Cut into the board's edge is an old knife-notch, and the loaf falls short of it by a full thumb's width. Morning window light. Nothing else.

*Replaces* `gem` § 8.6 prompt 2, which put him at the Southwark butter riot on 13 June among the crowd. That breaks the binding rule (he is never present at a riot as it happens) and canon § 7 #3. The aftermath shot is the canon's, from `fab` § 7.7 shot 24 and `fab-2a` S059b. In the commonplace book under *Prices*: "butter iij d. by the prentices' law, iiij d. by the constable's, v d. by the seller's. the loaf: a thumb." Leadenhall is about 200 m from home, inside the canon's radius of fear.

**Photo 26 · `S065b` · 2025-06-24 (Tue) · 04:50 · `ansell_house` door, looking west along the close to the church porch**

> A narrow London close on Midsummer morning 1595, about an hour after sunrise, seen from just outside a house doorway under a jettied upper storey, at the eye level of a standing man. Every doorway along the row of timber-framed houses has been dressed in the night: green birch boughs tied over the lintels, bunches of fennel and yellow St John's wort, and on one step an earthenware pot of tall white lilies. Over the nearest door a small iron lamp in a bracket is still burning, its flame gone pale in the daylight. At the end of the close stands the squat stone porch of a medieval parish church, its door shut, nobody on the step. Low, warm sun from behind the viewer's right lights the upper storeys and leaves the cobbles and the central kennel in blue shadow, where a little grey ash has blown in from the street. Sparrows in the birch. No people, and no shadow of the viewer in the frame. Warm early light, still air. Period-accurate only; no lettering.

*Replaces* `fab` § 8.7 photo 2 (Old Swan Stairs, looking east at the bridge), which put him about 800 m from home inside the canon's radius of fear (§ 2, Location: within 600 m of home, 13 June – 5 July, except the Theatre road). The final pass (decision 1) takes the arc editor's version from § 7.7 shot 45: the Midsummer doorways in the close (`gem`'s birch and lilies, re-homed). The door-dressing is Stow's custom, `[ext]` (§ 9.4). The sun is placed behind the viewer, low in the north-east at that hour, so that his shadow falls out of frame.

**Photo 48, frame 1 · `S115a` · 2025-12-26 (Fri) · 17:30 · `richmond_palace`, the players' side**

> A plain service-side room at Richmond Palace on the evening of 26 December 1595, used by players as a waiting and tiring place before a court performance. Uneven light from a few tallow candles and a small fire, with colder shadows beyond the working group. On a trestle are a rolled actor's part tied with thread, a gilt property crown set well out of the way, and a mended doublet fastening being finished by someone's hands. Costume fabric is lifted off a dirty floor onto a chest. Through a half-open door at the back, a slice of a great hall lit by many candles: the corner of a canopy of state, empty. People seen from behind or in part, mid-task. At the frame's edge, the viewer's own hand rests on a bundle of papers. It feels like skilled people waiting to work, not a royal tableau and not a modern backstage photograph in costume. No view into the royal private rooms. No lettering.

*Merged from* `gpt` § 8.7 (S115) and `fab` § 7.7 shot 48 (the hall dressed, the canopy of state empty an hour before).

**Photo 48, frame 2 · `S115b` · 2025-12-27 (Sat) · 23:40 · `richmond_palace`, the hall floor after the play**

> The floor of a great hall at Richmond Palace late at night after a court play, Christmas 1595, seen from a standing man's eye level looking down and a little ahead. The boards are thickly strewn with rushes, sweet-flag and sprigs of dried rosemary, trodden and scattered. A boy of twelve in a plain shirt and hose is on his knees in the rushes, back three-quarters to the viewer, parting them with one hand and holding a short candle low in the other. The light makes a small warm pool, and the rest of the floor falls away into dark. At the edge of the pool, a yeoman's scarlet hose and shoe, standing by. Carved wainscot in the dim background. Nothing is found and nothing glints: the search is the picture. Candlelight only, no lens effects, no lettering.

*Replaces* `gem` § 8.6 prompt 3 (W054), which showed the Queen's pearl button glinting in the rushes. The T1 record says the button was *lost* "at a play at Richmond at Christmas 1595" (source book 01, 27 Dec), and `fab` has Sam search on 27 December and not find it. A photo of the button would make William its finder. The boy is `sam_latchet`. The court is on the second night, *Romeo*.

## 9. The canon ledger

*Sources.* **SB01–SB06** are the source book's sections 01–06 (`research/sourcebook/`); **SYN** is the synthesis. **`[ext]`** marks a detail from outside the attachments, to be verified before anything ships (§ 9.4). Where the bibles gave the same fact different tiers, the source book's tier is used, and the call is noted in the right-hand column.

### 9.1 Historical facts used

#### 9.1.1 The calendar and the frame

| # | Fact | Tier | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| H01 | 1 January 1595 is a Wednesday. Easter is 20 April, Lent runs 5 March–19 April, and Shrove Tuesday is 4 March. The Julian 1595 calendar is identical to 2025 | T1·calc | SB01 frame; SYN |
| H02 | Lady Day (25 March) is a quarter day and the start of the legal year. The quarter days are 25 Mar, 24 Jun, 29 Sep and 25 Dec | T1·calc | SB01 frame |
| H03 | Law terms: Hilary 23 Jan–12 Feb · Easter 7 May–2 Jun · Trinity 20 Jun–9 Jul · Michaelmas 9 Oct–28 Nov. Sunday adjustments are unchecked | T2·calc | SB01 frame; SB01 § 7 #12 |
| H04 | Moons, computed: full on 15 Jan, 9 Aug and 6 Dec; new on 27 Jun; all others as tabled | calc | SB01 § 2 |
| H05 | Daylight by date | calc / INF | SB05 § 3d |
| H06 | Fast days are Friday, Saturday and Lent. There is no compulsory Wednesday fish day | T1 | SB05 § 1c |
| H07 | Only two dated weather records survive: 23 July in Cornwall ("dark and cloudy") and 22 December in London ("foul weather"). The cold, wet summer was **1594**; no dated London weather is known for summer 1595 | T1 / T1–T2 | SB01 § 2, § 4 #5, § 7 #6 |
| H08 | 1595 was a low-plague year: no closure, no figures | T2 | SB02 § 1; SB04 § 4; SB01 § 7 #7 |
| H09 | Feasts and customs used: Twelfth Night, Candlemas, Shrovetide, Mid-Lent Sunday, Rogation, Whitsun, Midsummer bonfires and the marching watch, Lammas, St Bartholomew, Michaelmas, St Luke, All Saints and All Souls, Martinmas, Advent, St Thomas's dole | T1 dates / T2 customs | SB01; SB06 S065, S099, S113 |

#### 9.1.2 William and the family

| # | Fact | Tier | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| H10 | William baptised 26 April 1564; the traditional birthday is 23 April; the Garter feast at Whitehall falls on 23 April | T1 / T4 | SB01 § 2; SB02 § 1 |
| H11 | Susanna baptised 26 May 1583; Hamnet and Judith baptised 2 February 1585 | T1 | SB02 § 2a |
| H12 | Siblings' baptisms: Gilbert 13 Oct 1566, Joan 15 Apr 1569, Richard 11 Mar 1574, Edmund 3 May 1580. The parents' ages are estimates | T1 / T2 | SB02 § 2a |
| H13 | Anne and the children presumed in the Henley Street house; no document places them there in 1595 | T2 | SB02 § 1 |

### 9.2 Inventions to keep consistent

*All values are the canon's (§ 3–5) unless marked as from `fab-2a` or from a specimen in § 8. Every item is T5.*

#### 9.2.1 Names and ages

**Invented people** (17, all `[invented]`; web-checked 2026-11-15, all clear):

| `agnes_tipping` | Agnes Tipping | 45 | Hostess of the Jackdaw |

**Not people.** `gib`, the landlady's grey cat. `the_lady_unnamed` has no name, ever: "a married woman of the parish who plays the virginals", in her late twenties. ⛔ Never "kin to court musicians".

**Model ages for historical people** (T5, for voice and look only, ⛔ never in factual copy): Anne 39 · John 65 · Mary 57 · Kemp 35 · Phillips 34 · Pope 38 · Bryan 40 · Cowley 27 · Sincler 29 · Hamnet Sadler 34 · Judith Sadler 33 · Margaret Hoby 36 · Jacqueline Field 42. Historical ages and birthdays are as in canon § 3; Spencer's is "c.58 (est.)" (§ 9.4 B).

**Unnamed walk-ons** (no slug; `fab-2a`): the Cross Keys host and ostlers; two Gray's Inn students; an Inner Temple man; the Chamber clerk; the Southampton House porter; two boys hired by the week as Maria and Katharine; a water-bearer; the tireman; the Cheapside mercer. Do not name the mercer.

**Invented signs and houses.** `ansell_house` · the Jackdaw (Holywell Lane) · the Split Hazel (near Bishopsgate) · the Feathers (Richmond Green) · the Catherine Wheel (High Wycombe). **The Bear at Woodstock is `[ext]`**, not an invented sign: a long-established inn of that name, its existence in 1595 unverified (§ 9.4 D). The Oxford tavern is unnamed by rule, and the Banbury inn is unnamed unless the Reindeer is verified (E15).

**Phrases** (`fab-2a`). "On the thirty-first of April" is the company's word for never. "A man from Norwich" is the household's word for Verbeke from 12 June.

#### 9.2.2 The lodging

- **House.** A narrow three-storey timber house on the north side of the close, jettied over the lane, backing on a garden wall. Ground floor: the hall, the kitchen and Verbeke's back room. First floor: Mistress Ansell's chamber and Alse's closet. Second floor: William's front chamber. Above it, a lumber garret, which is Verbeke's hiding place on 12 and 29 June (`fab-2a`).
- **His chamber.** A west window over the close to the church porch, and a small north light. A board on trestles for a desk; a joined stool with the cushion Anne made; a standing bed with a flock mattress; the coffer (oak, two locks); one shelf of books; a candle-box; a pewter basin; a peg for the grey cloak; the brass inkhorn and pounce-box. The stair is narrow and cold as a well. **The latch** is unreliable all year and is mended on 22 December at no charge.
- **Terms.** £4 a year: 20s on each quarter day by noon (25 Mar, 24 Jun, 29 Sep), with Christmas paid early on 22 Dec. Firing and washing 6d a week from Michaelmas to Lady Day. Candles are his own. Board at her table 3d a meal. Ink from her at 2d the horn. She writes receipts in her late husband's law-hand (`fab-2a`).
- **Rules.** Callers are stopped at the foot of the stair. At Martinmas (11 Nov) he helps carry in the household's winter stores; there is no coal charge.
- **Books on the shelf.** Golding's Ovid · *Venus* · *Lucrece* · Brooke from 6 Mar · Field's Holinshed vol. 2, on loan from 19 Mar and not returned by 31 Dec · Daniel, bought 15 Apr · North's Plutarch at trade price from 15 May · Southwell's *Saint Peters Complaint*, bought Sat 17 May · a Chaucer through Florio, from October.

#### 9.2.3 Prices, wages and fees
